About this text

The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, known in Sanskrit as the Āryamahāparinirvāṇanāmamahāyānasūtra and in Tibetan as Phagpa yongsu nyangen le depa chenpo thekpa chenpo'i do, is a Mahāyāna sūtra set at the very end of the Buddha's life. It opens in the grove of twin sāla trees near Kuśinagara, on the bank of the Hiraṇyavatī River, where the Buddha, surrounded by a vast assembly of monks, announces on the full-moon morning of the last month of spring that his final passing is at hand, speaking so that every being understands him in its own language. Against this backdrop the sūtra presents his parting teachings, which are celebrated in the Mahāyāna tradition for proclaiming the permanence of the Tathāgata and the presence of buddha nature in all beings. It is Toh 120 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the general sūtra section. This lengthy, multi-fascicle version is traditionally understood to have been rendered into Tibetan from a Chinese translation, alongside a separate, shorter Tibetan version of the same sūtra.

Translation

In the language of India: ārya mahā pari nirbāṇa nāma mahā yāna sūtra. In the language of Tibet: The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa.

Fascicle one.

Homage to all the buddhas and bodhisattvas.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One (the Buddha) was staying in the city of Kuśinagara, on the bank of the Hiraṇyavatī River, in the grove of twin sāla trees near the land of the Mallas. There the Blessed One was surrounded and attended by eighty hundred-thousand koṭis of monks. On the morning of the full moon of the last month of spring, as the time of his parinirvāṇa drew near, he made himself understood as far as the Peak of Existence, in words that conveyed the meaning with perfect skill in the language of each and every being's own land. He declared: "Today the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, who has compassion for the whole world, the protector of the whole world, who regards all beings as he regards Rāhula, the great sage who has become the abode and ground of great refuge, will pass into parinirvāṇa." And such plain words came forth, answering the questions of all beings.

Moreover, at that time many kinds of light rays came forth from the Blessed One's own mouth: blue, yellow, red, white, the color of crystal, and the color of emerald. These rays reached throughout the great trichiliocosm and all the buddha fields of the ten directions, and struck the beings there: the hell-beings, animals, hungry ghosts, asuras, and the beings of the world of Yama. At that very moment all the darkness of the afflictions, all the lower realms, and all the unfavorable states were cut off.

Then, overcome by great sorrow, they cried out with one voice, "Alas, we are overwhelmed! We are overwhelmed!" They also wailed aloud, "Alas! Alas!" Stretching out their hands, they beat their heads; their bodies trembled, and they wept bitterly.

At that time the great earth, with its forests and its mountains, as far as the shores of the ocean, shook violently. Those beings took hold of one another and said, "Friends, hurry! Friends, hurry! Let us go, let us go to Kuśinagara, the city in the land of the Mallas! Let us bow at the feet of the Tathāgata, and entreat him to turn back from going into parinirvāṇa, and without haste to remain for an eon, or even longer than an eon."

Clutching one another, they said, "Alas, the world will become empty! Alas, the world will become empty! Alas, the ripening of the karma of beings' merit is exhausted! Terrible karma has come about! Venerable ones, hurry! Venerable ones, hurry! The Tathāgata, the perfectly complete Buddha, is about to pass into parinirvāṇa this very instant. Alas, the world will become empty! Alas, the world will become empty! If the Tathāgata does not remain, how will we fare, without a protector, without support, and destitute? We must ask him about whatever doubts and uncertainties we have."

These great śrāvakas were also present there: the elder Mahākātyāyana, the elder Bakkula, the elder Upananda, and others besides were present there. When the light struck them, they trembled and shook violently, they were shaken and deeply shaken, they were agitated and greatly agitated, they were alarmed and greatly alarmed, they were bewildered and utterly bewildered, they staggered and reeled, they were stupefied and utterly stupefied, they stiffened and grew rigid. In such states and others like them, they set out.

At that time eight million monks had finished chewing their tooth sticks and had come out. When the radiance of that light struck those elders, they hastily washed their faces and took hold of one another. All of these sons of the Tathāgata were arhats who had attained mastery, who had done what was to be done and accomplished their work, thoroughbreds, great leaders, who had attained their own aim through great emptiness, with retinues like great sandalwood and retinues like lions. Endowed with these and countless other qualities, all of them, early in the morning as the sun was rising, felt the hairs of all their limbs stand on end, red at the roots like the flowers of the lac tree, and their eyes filled with tears like blood. Stricken with feelings of the most intense suffering, out of help, happiness, and compassion for all beings, for the sake of those to be trained, and in order that the Dharma gateway of the various words of intended meaning concerning the aspect of emptiness, the supreme secret of the Great Vehicle, might be explained, they went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, circumambulated the Blessed One a hundred thousand times, and, joining their palms and bowing toward where the Blessed One was, sat down to one side.

At that time six hundred-thousand koṭis of nuns, headed by Subhadrā, the daughter of Kotaka, were all arhats whose defilements were exhausted, who had attained mastery, who had done what was to be done and accomplished their work, who had laid down the burden, free of afflictions, great leaders, who had attained their own aim through great emptiness. They were the nun Subhadrā, daughter of Kotaka, the nun Upanandā, the nun Sāgaramati, and others, six hundred-thousand koṭis of nuns. Early in the morning as the sun was rising, the hairs of all their limbs stood on end, red at the roots like the flowers of the lac tree, and their eyes filled with tears like blood. Stricken with feelings of the most intense suffering, out of help, happiness, and compassion for all beings, for the sake of those to be trained, and in order that the Dharma gateway of the various words of intended meaning concerning the aspect of emptiness, the supreme secret of the Great Vehicle, might be explained, they went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, circumambulated the Blessed One a hundred thousand times, and, joining their palms and bowing toward where the Blessed One was, sat down to one side.

The nuns seated in that assembly were also all bodhisattvas, supreme among humans, who had attained the ten grounds. For the sake of those to be trained, they bore the form of women, the shape of women, and bodies of flesh, blood, many sinews and bone. They were constantly engaged in meditating on the four immeasurables, and all of them were able, by their own power, to accomplish the aims of the great miracles that are the domain of the buddhas.

Furthermore, at that time the bodhisattvas led by the bodhisattva Sāgaradatta, as many as the sands of the river Ganges, had all attained the ten grounds, supreme among humans, and all bore bodies through skillful means. Among them were the bodhisattva great being Sāgaradatta, the bodhisattva great being Akṣayamati, and others. All these bodhisattva great beings wished to hear the Great Vehicle, had entered the Great Vehicle, hungered for the Great Vehicle, longed for the Great Vehicle, were free of stinginess toward the Great Vehicle, and were able to accord with the whole world. Thinking, "Having crossed over ourselves, we shall ferry others across," they had, for as many as ten million eons in the past, properly taken up the vows of the array of moral discipline and practiced the conduct of upholding them. Likewise they all thought, "Having been liberated ourselves, we shall liberate others." Likewise they thought, "We shall keep the lineage of the Three Jewels from being cut off," and "We shall turn the wheel of Dharma," and "We shall don the great armor." Likewise they were endowed with immeasurable qualities and regarded all sentient beings equally, as one would an only son.

At sunrise in the morning, the hair on all their limbs stood on end, reddish like the flowers of the lac tree, and their eyes filled with tears like blood. Stricken with feelings of the most intense suffering, out of help, happiness, and compassion for all beings, for the sake of those to be trained, and in order that the Dharma gateway of the various words of intended meaning concerning the aspect of emptiness, the supreme secret of the Great Vehicle, might be explained, they went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, circumambulated the Blessed One a hundred thousand times, and, joining their palms and bowing toward where the Blessed One was, sat down to one side.

Again at that time, the laymen led by the layman King Renowned for Stainless Light, as many as the sands of two Ganges rivers, had taken the five bases of training and were endowed with moral discipline, proper observance, and good qualities. All of them wished to understand things in their pairs: suffering and happiness, permanence and impermanence, the existence of a self and selflessness, the empty and the not empty, reality and what is not reality, refuge and what is not refuge, sentient beings and the absence of sentient beings, the stable and the unstable, the peaceful and the not peaceful, the conditioned and the unconditioned, the everlasting and the not everlasting, parinirvāṇa and what is not parinirvāṇa, and so on. They all wished to know their distinctions and their finer distinctions.

Among them were the layman called King Renowned for Stainless Light, the layman Excellent Giver, and others. These laymen wished to hear the Great Vehicle, and, having vowed, "Once we have heard it, we shall teach it to others," had taken up moral discipline. Having quenched their thirst with the Great Vehicle, they had vowed, "We shall quench the thirst of others who are thirsty." They longed for the Great Vehicle, were free of stinginess toward the Great Vehicle, and were all able to accord with the whole world. They wished to train with the thoughts, "Having crossed over ourselves, we shall ferry others across. Having been liberated ourselves, we shall liberate others. We shall keep the lineage of the Three Jewels from being cut off. We shall turn the wheel of Dharma." They had donned the great armor, held the trainings in reverence, and likewise were endowed with countless qualities. They all had compassion for all sentient beings, as one would for an only son.

They too, at sunrise, in order to build the pyre for the cremation of the Tathāgata, each came bearing five thousand pieces apiece of firewood of sandalwood, aloeswood, the finest pine, and sandalwood of the most excellent soil, together with fragrant fuel sticks of fine quality. Each kind of fuel stick was wound with golden thread adorned with the seven precious substances of seven kinds, so that it looked just like something in a painting. All were naturally fresh and finely worked. Each kind had a great mass of light of many sorts, blue in color, and here and there in places white, in places pale blue, in places reddish, in places yellow, all delightful to the eye. The fringes of the covering of every bundle were all made of the seven precious substances. Each of the many binding cords was interspersed with hues like vermilion, like the color of sesame flowers, and with blue and yellow. Each of the many binding cords was also interwoven with the finest pure gold brocade, renowned in many lands.

Each kind of fuel stick was furnished with sweet-smelling incense, flower garlands and saffron powder, covered with gold brocade, and adorned with silk streamers. They were placed inside sheaths of the seven precious substances, blue, yellow, pale blue, white, and the color of madder, wrapped in the finest cloth of the land of Kaliṅga, linen cloth, brocade and Chinese silk, adorned with many flowers, and anointed with sweet-smelling unguents.

Each cart carrying each kind of fuel stick was adorned with the seven precious substances; some were beautified with gold, and some, with blue, white, the hue of acacia blossoms and the hue of red lead, shone with a surpassing clear radiance. The yokes of those carts were also adorned with the seven precious substances, matchless, and were all harnessed to swift thoroughbred horses.

The horses, too, were swift with the force of the wind, and their coats were all the color of herons, of realgar, or of clouds. At the front and the back of each chariot of that design, victory banners and pennants were raised. All the banners were made of jewels and covered with golden nets. In front of the banners, too, they carried parasols whose ribs and shafts were made of stainless, radiant gems, all hung with tassels of pearls. In front of each parasol, too, they carried golden flowers, kumuda, utpala and white lotuses, with stems made of beryl and anthers made of diamond. Many bees hummed among them. They were fragrant, delightful, radiant, large and many, and with them they carried great festoons. In front of the great festoons, too, they bore great paintings depicting the deeds of the bodhisattva's birth stories.

On the right and left of each painted figure, too, dancers performed many kinds of dance from the Middle Country (Madhyadeśa). Among the dancers, instruments made of jewels were beaten and blown: clay drums, bronze drums, round drums, lutes, flutes, single-stringed lutes, great drums, conches and others. From those instruments of the dance, too, came words to this effect: "Alas, the world will become empty!" And the dancers sang in mournful tones.

In front of each of the dance instruments, too, they carried finely crafted jeweled caskets, fresh and lovely, all filled with powder of many kinds of fragrant, glittering flowers, and many other caskets, beautiful to behold, all filled with saffron powder. In front of these, too, they went carrying food enough for the Blessed One (the Buddha) together with his retinue, the Saṅgha of monks. All this food had been cooked over a small fire of red sandalwood, aloeswood and pine, and cooked with water from the Ganges River. It had sour, salty, sweet, astringent, pungent and bitter tastes, and it gave a fine complexion and did one good.

All the surroundings near the pair of sāla trees were spread with soft golden sand, and over it were laid cotton cloth, woolens, dyed woolens, cloth of mule hair, Kaliṅga cloth, and soft, white silk. The ground of that place, thirty-two yojanas all around, was smeared with saffron. Great lion thrones of gold were set out for the Blessed One and the Saṅgha of monks, adorned and made radiant with the seven kinds of precious things, vast as Mount Meru and lofty. They were covered with coverings all fit for them. They were surrounded by curtains with netting from which came the tinkling of golden bells and chimes and a jingling sound. Canopies of great bolts of fine Chinese silk were stretched over them. White parasols shaded them from the sun, and white yak-tail whisks fanned them. They were furnished with backrests in which makaras were set on elephant tusks, and from their mouths came strings of pearls, white as the rays of the full moon, hanging down as tassels. The ground all about was strewn with a shower of fragrant flowers.

Each of those laymen, too, thought: "To all sentient beings, to those who want food I shall give food. To those who want drink I shall give drink. To those who want a head, eyes, or any limb or member of the body, I shall give it just as they wish. To the poor, the destitute and the needy, apart from gifts of sensual pleasure, poison, harm, and gifts given without discernment, I shall provide whatever each of them wants, to their satisfaction." That is what they thought, and they thought of nothing else.

In the same way, all those companies of spiritual friends who abide in the conduct of bodhisattvas thought: "I shall serve a meal to the Tathāgata, and then he will pass into complete nirvāṇa. I shall serve a meal to the Tathāgata, and then he will pass into complete nirvāṇa." Each of them held only this in their hearts. The hairs on all their limbs stood on end, red at the roots like the flowers of the lac tree. Their eyes filled with tears like blood. Overcome by the most painful feeling, they set out with their own offerings, firewood and kindling, chariots, victory banners, parasols and pennants, and carrying vessels prepared with the finest food they went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and worshipped the Blessed One with incense, flower garlands, powders, fine powders, parasols, victory banners, pennants and vast canopies. They circumambulated him a hundred thousand times. Shedding tears from their eyes without ceasing, like rain, and showing grief in many ways, they cried out in loud voices: "Alas, the world will become empty! Alas, the world will become empty!" Rolling about at the Tathāgata's feet like a porpoise out of water, they made their request:

"Blessed One, will the Tathāgata, together with his retinue, the Saṅgha of monks, consent to accept the meal I have prepared? Blessed One, out of love for me, may the Tathāgata please accept this final offering!"

So they requested. Then the Blessed One, though he knew all, remained silent. Even when they requested it a second time and a third time, the Blessed One remained silent a second time and a third time. Then those assemblies of laymen lost all hope and sat without saying a word. They became utterly miserable, like parents rolling about in grief when their only son has died and has just been left in the charnel ground. So all those laymen, overcome with the deepest grief, rose, gathered up their offerings, and sat down to one side.

At that time, moreover, there were the laywomen who had taken up the five bases of training, who were endowed with moral discipline, with right conduct and with good qualities, and who held the true Dharma dear: Splendor of Life, daughter of a merchant, and other laywomen as numerous as the grains of sand in three Ganges rivers. In female form, every one of them was certain to free many tens of millions of beings, and every one of them reviled the household life, reflecting:

"This body is a mass compounded of eighty-four harmful constituents. This body is food for eighty-four tens of millions of kinds of worms. This body reeks like a king's dungeon dug deep in the ground. This body is soaked through with a stench worse than a rotting dog's corpse. This body leaks filth from nine openings. This body is plastered over with flesh, sinews, bones, blood and skin. It is like a palace-city: its hands and feet are outer gates, ramparts and battlements, its eyes are windows, its head is the pinnacle, and the mind dwells within like a king. This body, shunned by all the wise and delighting the childish, is the haunt of rākṣasas, the tens of millions of afflictions such as attachment, aversion and delusion. Like a reed, a castor plant, foam or a plantain tree, it has no core. Like lightning, or the ripples of a torrent rushing down a steep mountain, it is utterly unstable. Like an illusion, a mirage, an echo or the moon's reflection, it deceives. Like a tree on a riverbank undercut by water churned by a violent wind, it stands at the edge of a precipice. And since it is food for wolves, foxes, vultures and crows, who would take delight in bearing such a thing? It might be possible to take up all the water of the ocean on the tip of a hair and pour it into a cow's hoofprint, but this body, tormented by four hundred and four diseases, has a lack of essence and an impurity that mere words cannot convey. It might even be possible to use up this whole great earth by making it into pellets the size of juniper-berry stones, or of mustard seeds, or of the tiniest atoms, but as long as this body exists, its faults will never come to an end. Therefore, to protect the true Dharma, it should be cast away like spittle."

So it was that all those laywomen had trained their minds by meditating on great emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness. Among them were the laywoman called Splendor of Life, the laywoman called Śrīmālā, the laywoman called Viśākhā, and others. All of them wished to hear the Great Vehicle, and they vowed, "Having heard it, we shall teach it to others." They devoted themselves to guarding it. Each of them meditated intensely, reviling the female body as wretched and reviling the body as without essence. All of them thought, "We shall destroy the wheel of saṃsāra." All of them vowed, "Having quenched our thirst with the Great Vehicle, we shall also quench the thirst of others." They thirsted for the Great Vehicle and were free of stinginess toward it. They wished to train in harmony with the whole world. All of them wished to train with the thought, "Having been liberated, we shall liberate others. Having crossed over, we shall carry others across. We shall keep the lineage of the Three Jewels from being cut off. We shall turn the wheel of Dharma." They had donned the great armor and were steadfast in their observance of the trainings. They were endowed with many good qualities, and every one of them had compassion for all beings, as one has for an only child.

They too, very early at sunrise, holding one another by the hand, carried twice as much sacrificial kindling as the laymen had, to build the Tathāgata's cremation pyre. Because the Blessed One had not accepted their food, they fell into the deepest suffering, as if their own child had died. Weeping, they rose and sat down to one side.

At that time, moreover, there were the Licchavi youths, Pure Stainless Essence and the rest, the Licchavi youths of Vaiśālī, both those living inside the city and those living outside it, together with the minor kings of Jambudvīpa and their ministers, as numerous as the grains of sand in four Ganges rivers. Every one of them was skilled in protecting the true Dharma, endowed with moral discipline, with right conduct and with good qualities, and was like a hailstorm to those who disparage the true Dharma and to those who bear the signs of renunciation but are corrupt. All of them held this aspiration: "Whatever wealth we have, our stores of grain, our treasuries and our storehouses, all of it exists to make the ambrosia inexhaustible and to make the ambrosia endure. We shall be diligent in the true Dharma. We shall cut out the tongues of those who disparage the true Dharma and hold wrong views." So they solemnly vowed. They were resolved, thinking, "Those of corrupt discipline, who do not abide in moral discipline, we shall make wear white garments and set them to the work of householders." They vowed, "We shall serve those devoted to the true Dharma as we would serve a teacher." Though they had done everything to bring benefit and comfort to the Saṅgha, they did not think, "That is enough." They wished to hear the Great Vehicle and were steadfast in their vow, "Having heard it, we shall teach it to others." Every one of them was endowed with these and countless other good qualities.

Among them were the Licchavi youth called Pure Stainless Essence, the one called Purified of Obscurations, the one called Given by the Stainless Waters of the Ganges, and others. These Licchavi youths too held one another by the hand. Each of them carried, as fuel for building the cremation pyre, the finest pine, sandalwood and aloeswood, eight hundred and forty million pieces of sacrificial kindling, and offerings twice as great as those before. Proclaiming their offerings, they went before the Blessed One. Each Licchavi youth was furnished with eighty-four thousand elephants, eighty-four thousand horses, eighty-four thousand chariots and eighty-four thousand crystal jewels. Before each elephant were carried parasols befitting its greatness, victory banners, pennants and great silk canopies. Even the smallest of the parasols measured a full league in circumference.

Even the smallest of the great silk canopies was thirty-two yojanas wide. Even the shortest of the victory banners was a hundred yojanas tall. Even the shortest of the pennants reached a thousand yojanas. So they went before the Blessed One (the Buddha) with these offerings, bowed their heads at his feet, and offered him food, but the Blessed One said nothing at all. Since he did not accept it, they were filled with the deepest sorrow. Their hopes cut off, they rose and, through the power of the Blessed One, were lifted up to the height of seven palm trees, and there they remained.

Then, too, at that time ministers, merchants and householders of Jambudvīpa, as many as the grains of sand in five Ganges Rivers, came there as well. Every one of them had faith in the Great Vehicle, possessed pure moral discipline, rites and virtues, and was like a hailstorm to those who disparage the true Dharma. Among them were the king of householders called Vairocana, one called Jagatpāla, one called Dharmapāla, and others. All of them too outshone the earlier offerings with their own offerings and worshipped the Blessed One with a vast array of offerings. But because he did not accept their food, they were filled with the deepest sorrow. Their hopes cut off, they rose and, through the power of the Blessed One, were lifted up to the height of seven palm trees, and there they remained.

Then, too, at that time, apart from King Ajātaśatru, the king of Vaiśālī, Stainless Moon Essence, together with his attendants living inside the city and outside it, and the kings of all the cities, mountain villages, great provinces and valleys of Jambudvīpa, as many as the grains of sand in six Ganges Rivers, came with their fourfold armies. Each of these kings had eighteen million soldiers. They had elephants whose temples constantly dripped with musth, and horses swifter than the force of the wind. Their offerings, too, were twice as fine and twice as plentiful as those earlier offerings. Bearing such choice offerings, they came with their fourfold armies. Among their offerings, even the shortest parasol covered an area eighty yojanas wide. Even the smallest pennant reached a hundred thousand yojanas. Even an ordinary victory banner was thirty yojanas tall.

Thus Stainless Moon Essence, king of Vaiśālī, the one called Sunlight, and the other kings arrived at sunrise. They too were like a hailstorm to those who disparage the true Dharma and to the corrupt ones who bear the outward signs of renunciants. By their power they established beings in the true Dharma. Every one of them thought, "Whatever wealth, stores of grain, treasuries and storehouses we have are for the sake of preserving the true Dharma." Every one of them had firmly vowed, "We shall strive earnestly for the true Dharma. We shall cut out at the root the tongues of those who disparage the true Dharma." Every one of them longed for the Great Vehicle and was loving toward the whole world. Their offerings filled the entire region from Vaiśālī to the city of Kuśinagara. Because the shade of their parasols overlapped like scales, the light of the sun did not strike the ground. The fragrance of their food spread for four yojanas. Each of them begged the Blessed One three times to accept the food he had prepared, but he did not accept it, and they were unhappy. They rose and sat down to one side.

Then, too, at that time, apart from the queen of King Ajātaśatru, the queen of the king of Vaiśālī, Beauty of the Three Worlds, and the wives of the kings, merchants and householders of Jambudvīpa, as many as the grains of sand in seven Ganges Rivers, came there. Every one of them, in a woman's form, surely liberated many beings; each was constantly engaged in contemplation of the body, and each had a mind purified by the great emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness. Among them were the queen of the king of Vaiśālī called Beauty of the Three Worlds, the queen called Priyaṅku, and others. Every one of them had compassion for all beings, as one has for an only child. Holding one another by the hand, they carried fragrant kindling wood with which to light the Tathāgata's funeral pyre. With their own offerings they outshone all the earlier offerings and made offerings to the Blessed One twice as great. The height and breadth of the victory banners, pennants and parasols among their offerings were more than double those that came before. The fragrance of their food spread for eight yojanas. They bowed to the Blessed One and begged him to accept the food, but he did not accept it. Then they circumambulated the Blessed One, tearing out their hair and scattering their ornaments, weeping with harsh cries, filled with the deepest sorrow, like a mother whose only child has died. Through the power of the Blessed One, they sat down to one side right beside the twin sāla trees.

Then, too, at that time, hosts of gods, as many as the grains of sand in eight Ganges Rivers, led by the god called Wide Eyes, came there, and those gods spoke these words:

"Friends, look! Friends, look! The offerings that humans have brought are supremely excellent, and they wish to make every kind of offering and honor to the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha. Because he is to accept the last meal from humans, they are making their request to the Blessed One. Friends, we too should each make offerings to the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, and should present him with a meal at the very end. If only the Tathāgata would partake of the food that we offer, and then pass into parinirvāṇa! Friends, the appearance of a buddha is extremely rare. Rarer still is the consummate perfection of giving that is the gift of the last meal. Alas, if the Blessed One passes into parinirvāṇa, the world will become empty!"

All those gods longed to hear the Great Vehicle, and had vowed, "Having heard it, we shall teach it to others." They had vowed, "Having quenched our own thirst with the Great Vehicle, we shall quench the thirst of others who thirst." They yearned for the Great Vehicle. They had no miserliness concerning the Great Vehicle, and no jealousy, arrogance, concealment or deceit concerning it. To beings who repudiate the Great Vehicle they were like a hailstorm. They had taken up the moral discipline of guarding the true Dharma. They wished to train in harmony with the world. They wished to train with the thought, "Having crossed over, we shall ferry others across. Having been freed, we shall free others. We shall turn the wheel of Dharma. We shall keep the lineage of the Three Jewels from being cut off." They had donned the great armor, were steadfast in their vows concerning the trainings, and were likewise endowed with immeasurable qualities. Every one of them looked upon the whole world with compassion, as one treats an only child.

They too, at daybreak, in order to build the funeral pyre for the Teacher's parinirvāṇa, brought firewood and sacrificial kindling. All of it was divine substance, twice as fine as the kindling offered by those before them, fragrant, delightful, and rare among humans. Their chariots were yoked to the finest swift horses, covered with white silk, of a form and shape that could not even be depicted in a drawing, and surrounded by curtains hung with nets of bells and little bells. Vast canopies of silk, like great mansions, were raised on patterned poles. Dancers and delightful music led the way before them. They had arranged many delicious foods, and lion thrones with backrests of beryl whose crests were lit by the radiance of jewels, along with footstools made of gold. The ground, too, was adorned with all kinds of sweetly fragrant flowers, with loose blossoms strewn and set in order. Every one of them was followed by the fourfold army.

Each of them, unable to hold back tears like blood and tormented by the utmost suffering, went to the Blessed One for the benefit and happiness of all beings and out of compassion for them, and for the sake of those to be trained, so that there would be explained the supreme secret of the Great Vehicle, the manner of the Dharma door of the many kinds of words spoken with hidden intent concerning the aspects of emptiness. They bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One, made many kinds of supreme offerings and honors, and circumambulated him. For the sake of the Blessed One's retinue they spread their own upper garments on the ground. They tore off and scattered their own ornaments, tore at their own hair, wept with harsh voices, and with one voice addressed the Blessed One thus:

"Blessed One, please partake of the food we have prepared, and then pass into parinirvāṇa."

Though they made this request twice and three times, the Blessed One did not accept and said nothing. Losing hope, they rose and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the nāga kings dwelling in the four directions, the son of the nāga king Vāsuki, Nanda, Upananda and others, nāga kings as numerous as the grains of sand in nine Ganges rivers, came in great haste at daybreak, bringing offerings, each in keeping with his own wealth, that surpassed even the offerings of the gods twofold. They went to the Blessed One, bowed down, and requested him to accept their meal. But it did not turn out as they had hoped, so they rose and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Vaiśravaṇa and the other lords of the yakṣas, together with their servants and retinues, as numerous as the grains of sand in ten Ganges rivers, holding on to one another, outshone the earlier offerings of the nāga kings with their own offerings. They set out with a great host and arrived, bowed to the Blessed One, and requested him to accept their meal. But the Blessed One did not accept their meal, and so, displeased, they rose and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the garuḍa king called Friend of Foes and other garuḍa kings, as numerous as the grains of sand in twenty Ganges rivers, outshone with their own offerings the offerings of the yakṣa kings who came before. They bowed to the Blessed One and requested him to accept their meal. But he did not accept their meal, and so, displeased, they rose and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the son of Naradatta, lord of the gandharvas, and other lords of the gandharvas, as numerous as the grains of sand in thirty Ganges rivers, came bringing offerings far more excellent than all the earlier offerings. They made offerings to the Tathāgata, played all manner of sweet music, bowed down, and requested the Blessed One to accept their meal. But the Blessed One did not accept, and they too became displeased, rose, and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Sudarśana, lord of the kinnaras, and other lords of the kinnaras, as numerous as the grains of sand in forty Ganges rivers, arrived. The offering of each one of them surpassed even all those earlier offerings combined. But he did not accept their meal either, and so, displeased, they sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Mahāsudarśana, lord of the mahoragas, and other lords of the mahoragas, as numerous as the sands of fifty Ganges rivers, brought offerings twice as great as the previous ones. As before, the Blessed One did not accept their food, and, dejected, they sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Śambara, lord of the asuras, and other lords of the asuras, as numerous as the sands of sixty Ganges rivers, huge in body and of great radiance, every one of them free of the asuras' hostile disposition, came bringing offerings, each twice as great as the one before. They bowed to the Blessed One. When he did not accept their food, they became dejected, rose, and sat down near the twin sāla trees, lighting them up with their own splendor.

Furthermore, at that time the lord of the dānavas, Anudatta of the Stainless Waters of the Ganges, and other dānavas, as numerous as the sands of seventy Ganges rivers, huge in body and of great radiance, every one of them free of the dānavas' disposition, brought offerings twice and twice again finer than all the previous offerings. As before, the Blessed One did not accept their food, and, dejected, they lit up the space between the twin sāla trees with their own splendor and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Bhīṣaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, and other lords of the rākṣasas, as numerous as the sands of eighty Ganges rivers, every one of them free of the rākṣasas' disposition, lit up the area near the twin sāla trees with masses of great splendor, bowed to the Blessed One, and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Scent Keeper, lord of the fruit trees, and other lords of the fruit trees, as numerous as the sands of ninety Ganges rivers, brought hosts of offerings exceeding all the previous offerings twice and twice again. They adorned the ground with thickets of many creepers covered in shining flowers, alive with rutting male cuckoos and humming bees, and with unfolding leaves, and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Mahāmāya, king of the vidyādharas, and other kings of the vidyādharas, as numerous as the sands of a thousand Ganges rivers, brought offerings more than twice as great as the previous ones and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Sudarśanā and other goddesses who take any form they wish, as numerous as the sands of ten million Ganges rivers, brought offerings more than twice as great as those of the queens of the king of Vaiśālī. They heaped their own ornaments on the ground before the Blessed One and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Rambhā, Urvaśī, and other daughters of the gods, as numerous as the sands of a billion Ganges rivers, presented offerings far more abundant and splendid than twice and twice again the previous ones, and, as before, sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the host of bhūtas called Revolving Like Venus and others, as numerous as the sands of ten billion Ganges rivers, sat down to one side as before.

Furthermore, at that time the sons of the gods of the four world protectors, together with as many as the sands of a hundred thousand koṭis of Ganges rivers, brought offerings exceeding twice and twice again the previous ones. They bowed to the Blessed One and begged him to accept their food, but the Blessed One did not accept it, and as before they sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the four wind gods, gods who send forth the winds, together with as many as the sands of a hundred thousand koṭis of Ganges rivers, even out of season adorned the ground with trees, creepers, spreading trees, woodlands, ears of grain, flowering creepers, and coral, and made the glories of the flowers of every season flourish in all directions. With unfolding leaves and red roots they made the twin sāla trees look as though bathed in the yellow rays of the rising sun. Bringing offerings more than twice as great as the previous ones, they made offerings to the Blessed One and, as before, sat down near the twin sāla trees.

Furthermore, at that time the gods who send down the rain, as numerous as the sands of the Ganges, followed by great clouds and herons, poured down from amid flickering lightning streams of rain blue as a peacock's throat. With clouds slowly rumbling thunder, they rained down flowers mingled with a cool drizzle. They bowed to the Blessed One, and when he did not accept their food, they lost hope, and in order to put out the fire that would be kindled for the cremation of the Tathāgata's remains, they stayed right there in the sky.

Furthermore, at that time the perfume elephants Rāhu, Lungtang Hue, Clear Honey Hue, Fragrant with All Desires, and other perfume elephants, as numerous as the sands of twenty Ganges rivers, every one of them devoted to the Great Vehicle, because the Teacher was passing into complete nirvāṇa, pulled up with the tips of their trunks again and again, and carried, herbs of the snow mountains, lotus roots, and stalks each as large as the wheel of a great chariot, and, from great wildernesses, lotuses, blue lotuses, white water lilies, white lotuses, roots, fruits, and many different kinds of pebbles. Swifter even than the force of the wind, trumpeting like summer thunder, they came with great speed. They arranged hangings of plucked lotus petals on the lion throne, made offerings to the Blessed One, and when he did not accept their food, they lost hope and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the lion called Lion's Roar and other lions, as numerous as the sands of thirty Ganges rivers, every one of them a giver of fearlessness to all sentient beings, with the great prowess of lions carried flowers and fruits just as the great perfume elephants had, came swiftly, and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time geese, cranes, ruddy shelducks, kādamba geese, zalmo birds, wild geese, cuckoos, bālikas, kalaviṅkas, jīvaṃjīvaka pheasants, and others likewise carried flowers and fruits just as the great perfume elephants had, uttered their own cries, and sat down to one side.

Furthermore, at that time all the cows, buffalo cows and nanny goats on the face of the earth, their udders full of milk, came there in great haste. Near the twin sāla trees they saw a hollow, and into it they let flow, from their milk-filled udders, milk unmixed with grit, sand or salt and free of any taint of heat. They removed the flies from the milk and settled to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the sage Kṣāntivādin and the other sages dwelling in the four continents, all possessed of the five higher knowledges and all devoted to the Great Vehicle, as many as the sands of an immense multitude of Ganges Rivers, came by the power of their own miraculous abilities, carrying kindling for the fire offering and food. They bowed to the Blessed One and settled to one side.

Furthermore, at that time, apart from some of the Saṅgha of monks, such as Mahākāśyapa, and the elder Ānanda and others, every monk and nun living throughout the land came there, without a single exception.

Furthermore, at that time Mount Meru and the other great mountains, together with the mountains in the spaces between the world systems, as many as the sands of countless Ganges Rivers, also came there. They had trees and many creepers giving shade from the heat of the sun, spacious rocks casting broad shade, and streams of water pouring down from the cliffs, and they were filled with valleys of medicinal herbs, pebbles, roots and fruits. The great assemblies of the mighty sons of the gods, together with Mount Meru, also came to make offerings, bowed to the Blessed One, and settled to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the mighty sons of the gods of great radiance who dwell in the four great oceans and in the great rivers, as many as the sands of countless Ganges Rivers, sent ahead of them offerings twice as beautiful as the earlier ones. Just as the light of the sun outshines the light of a lamp, so their own radiance outshone the light of the sun. Just as the light of the rising sun makes all the leaves of the aśoka tree glow a brilliant red, so their own radiance made the waters of the Hiraṇyavatī River glow a brilliant red. They bowed to the Blessed One and settled to one side.

Furthermore, at that time the sky between the city of Kuśinagara and the twin sāla trees grew thick with mansions like the peaks of the king of mountains, every one of them white as swan's down. The ground was filled with parks and with ponds adorned with lotuses, more beautiful even than the parks of Uttarakuru in the north. In short, it became like the Nandana Grove and the Caitraratha Grove. Yet although those places appeared so delightful, the gods, asuras and humans wept bitterly, because the Tathāgata was about to pass into parinirvāṇa.

Furthermore, at that time the gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings said this to their retinues: "Friends, look! Friends, look! The offerings that gods, asuras and humans make to the Tathāgata are truly exceptional, and they are striving to offer the Tathāgata his last meal. Friends, we too shall each make offerings to the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha. We shall also offer him his last meal. If the Blessed One accepts the last meal offered by us, through that we will attain the great perfection of generosity."

Having spoken thus, they set out with countless multitudes of attendants, carrying offerings twice those that had come before, and abundant food. They also carried many flowers: māndāravas and great māndāravas, kekerukas and great kekerukas, mañjūṣakas and great mañjūṣakas, santānakas and great santānakas, radiant flowers and great radiant flowers, all-radiant flowers, flowers of boundless light, lovely flowers and great lovely flowers, delight-giving flowers and great delight-giving flowers, supremely fragrant flowers and great supremely fragrant flowers, flowers of wafting scent and great flowers of wafting scent, flowers of all-pervading scent and great flowers of all-pervading scent, flowers of boundless scent and great flowers of boundless scent, petals of divine substance, river flowers, and pārijātas, and other such flowers as well. They went to where the Blessed One was, and just as the brilliance of the sun outshines the glow of fireflies, so their own splendor outshone the light of all others. But the Blessed One did not accept their food, and they settled to one side.

Furthermore, at that time Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, together with his retinue of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, carried offerings twice the earlier ones: many kinds of flowers found in their heaven and suited to the occasion, pleasing flowers, greatly pleasing flowers, and sweet-scented flowers. They also emanated great mansions, white as lime, with flights of stairs and many rows of windows where pigeons played. They went to where the Blessed One was, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, and to one side, with palms joined, bowed and said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, since we are the guardians of the collection of the Great Vehicle sūtras, out of compassion for us please accept the food we offer." Although they made this request, he did not accept their food, and they settled to one side.

The second section.

After that, the six classes of gods who dwell in the desire realm came in succession, filling the sky above with offerings and with light that far surpassed what had come before, and went to where the Blessed One was. Among their offerings, even the ordinary parasols each measured in circumference the full extent of the four continents.

Their ordinary banners, too, reached to the edges of the four great oceans, stretching from the eastern ocean to the western ocean. When the wind stirred them, or the wind lifted them, sweet sounds came forth from those banners, like the melodious music of a lute swirling within. They bowed to the Blessed One and asked him to accept their food. But the Blessed One said nothing, so they lost hope and sat down to one side.

Then all the other beings of the successively higher realms, except those born at the Peak of Existence, came with great quantities of vast offerings in order to make offerings to the Blessed One, and, as the others had, they sat down to one side.

Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, surrounded and preceded by countless gods of the Brahmakāyika realm, outshone with his own splendor the splendor of all the gods. Bearing a great quantity of offerings, he went before the Blessed One. Their ordinary banners hung down from the world of Brahmā and reached all the way to the twin sāla trees. He too asked the Blessed One to accept his food, but the Blessed One did not accept it, and he sat down to one side.

Then Vemacitrin, lord of the asuras, together with a countless retinue of asura servants, with his own light dimmed the light of every god in between, except for Brahmā and Indra. Bringing a quantity of offerings suited to the occasion, and food suited to the occasion, he went before the Blessed One. His ordinary parasol was broad enough to cover a small world system. He too asked the Blessed One to accept his food, but the Blessed One did not accept it, and he rose and remained to one side.

Then Māra the Evil One, with a retinue of countless consorts, threw open all the gates of all the hells and gave the beings there water, food, and whatever else they wanted. He said to them, "Recollect the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha! Rejoice in the final offering!" And he comforted them, saying, "May you be happy!" Into the cities of the hells, full of weapons and blazing with great fire, he also poured down great waters like a great cloud, so that the hell beings felt a bliss they had never known before. Through the power of the Tathāgata, Māra's state of mind had become like this.

His retinue, too, all cast away their many kinds of weapons: bows, swords, hammers, axes, single-pointed vajras, kampana weapons, iron clubs, iron arrows, śataghnīs, arrows, javelins, single-pointed spears, and the like, and they devoted themselves to the Dharma.

His offerings were such as the earlier gods had not had. His ordinary parasol, in breadth, was as wide as a medium world system. Thus, endowed with great might, and fanned by his consorts with yak-tail whisks, he went to where the Blessed One was. He bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet, joined his palms, and said:

"Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks and the assembly, please accept the food that we offer. Blessed One, from now on we wish to make a vow for the sake of the Great Vehicle. Whenever any son or daughter of good family teaches the Great Vehicle, whether out of fear, or out of desire for gain and honor, or in hope of attainment, or because others urge them to, or to spare the feelings of others, for their sake I wish to proclaim a secret mantra that dispels all fear and all unhappiness:

tadyathā ṭhagke ṭaṭara ṭhagke rorure mahārorure ālamāla tāla svāhā

I have spoken this secret mantra on my own pledge in order to give confidence in the way of the Dharma to preachers of the Dharma who are troubled by confusion and fear, to intoxicate the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), to protect the true Dharma, and to protect myself and others. Those who recite it as a protective dhāraṇī-mantra amid the dangers of elephants, of thickets, of rivers, or of fire, by the mere recitation, just as a tortoise protects its own body, I shall protect from all those dangers, without guile or deceit. And since I will also make them powerful, Blessed One, please now accept this food." So he urged.

Then the Blessed One spoke thus to Māra the Evil One: "Evil One, do not make requests for the sake of food. These words of secret mantra, being for the benefit of all sentient beings, become a gift of Dharma, and so I accept them. A gift of material things is not like that. So, Evil One, do not make requests." Having said this, the Blessed One fell silent.

Two and three times, Māra and the daughters of Māra made their request to the Blessed One, but he did not accept. Losing hope, they sat down to one side.

Then at that time the god Maheśvara (Śiva), preceded by countless gods of immeasurable might, with his own light made the lights of Brahmā, Indra lord of the gods, the asuras, and Māra lose their brightness, like lumps of ink. Carrying vast offerings for the Blessed One, more than double and double again, he went there. Among his offerings, his ordinary parasol was broad enough to cover the great trichiliocosm. Sending such offerings ahead, he went before the Blessed One, circumambulated him many times, and bowed his head at his feet. Then, standing to the side of where the Blessed One was, with joined palms and bowing, he spoke thus to the Blessed One:

"My making offerings to the Blessed One is like a fly making offerings. Because I am like a fly, how could I have the strength to make offerings to the Blessed One? Even so, Blessed One, out of compassion for me, please accept this last meal."

Twice and three times he made this request, yet twice and three times the Blessed One remained silent. His hope cut off, he rose and sat down to one side.

Then, at that time, far to the east of this buddhafield, beyond world systems as numerous as the sands of countless myriads of Ganges rivers, there was a world system called Mind-Captivating Unceasing Sweet Sound. There dwelt the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha called Equal to Space. He addressed his eldest son, the śrāvaka Infinite Great Body:

"Son of noble family, go to the world system of Endurance. There the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha called Śākyamuni will before long pass into parinirvāṇa. Son of noble family, for his sake take wholesome, delicious food from this world system and go. Take great offerings as well, and say this to him: 'May the Tathāgata, the Blessed One, partake of this food and then pass into parinirvāṇa.' Whatever doubts and uncertainties you have, ask that very Tathāgata about them properly, and he will answer you."

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Infinite Great Body said, "Excellent! Excellent!" He circumambulated that Tathāgata and bowed his head at his feet. Then, surrounded and preceded by countless bodhisattva mahāsattvas of great body, he came here to the world system of Endurance.

Then, at that time, this great trichiliocosm moved and moved violently, shook and shook violently, was agitated and violently agitated, whirled and whirled violently, rumbled and rumbled violently, crackled and crackled violently, flashed and flashed violently, swayed and swayed violently. In these and other ways the great earth quaked violently.

Then, within that assembly, the gods, Brahmā, Indra, the world guardians, the asuras, Maheśvara (Śiva) and the rest, saw the ground behave in this way and shuddered. Terrified, their mouths and throats dry, they made ready to flee. Their own radiance could no longer be seen, and their light faded.

Then, from within that same assembly, Mañjuśrī, in youthful form, rose and spoke these words to that host of gods:

"Friends, do not be afraid! Do not be afraid! Far to the east, beyond world systems as numerous as the countless finest particles of the sands of countless myriads of Ganges rivers, there is a world system called Mind-Captivating Unceasing Sweet Sound. There the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha called Equal to Space now dwells, lives and abides. That Tathāgata called his eldest son, a śrāvaka, and said to him: 'Son of noble family, go to the world system of Endurance. There the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha called Śākyamuni is about to pass into parinirvāṇa. Offer the last meal to him and to the Saṅgha of monks. Whatever doubts and uncertainties you have, ask about them properly, and he will answer you.' That Blessed One sent this bodhisattva mahāsattva Infinite Great Body, and he has come here, surrounded and preceded by countless bodhisattvas. It is by his splendor that your light has faded. Rejoice in his coming! And look at the offerings that the Tathāgata Equal to Space makes to our Tathāgata!"

Then that whole assembly, as if seeing their own reflections in the surface of a mirror, saw that Tathāgata dwelling in a distant world system, and that world system as well.

Then Mañjuśrī, in youthful form, spoke these words to the assembly: "Just as you see this world system and this Tathāgata, so also look at the tathāgatas of the ten directions and the other world systems. Such is the greatness of the tathāgatas: even while remaining in their own abodes, they show their faces to you."

The assembly said, "Alas! Even a tathāgata of such greatness will pass into parinirvāṇa! Alas!" And they sat there in dejection.

Then they also saw the bodhisattva mahāsattva Infinite Great Body surrounded by immeasurable bodhisattvas. From every single pore of that bodhisattva's body a great lotus emerged. Each great lotus held seventy-eight thousand cities, and every one of those cities was as large as the land of Vaiśālī. Each was encircled in succession by seven walls and seven moats of the seven precious substances, and each was adorned in succession with seven rows of palm trees. They were prosperous, flourishing and happy, and full of joy. Those walls were all surrounded by great parapets of Jambū River gold, and between all the parapets grew many kinds of young trees bearing fruit and flowers. When the branches of those trees were stirred by the wind, there arose sounds, sweet and delightful like cymbals, charming and enchanting, five kinds of sound from each, and by those sounds the beings there played, rejoiced and took their delight.

All those moats were filled with cool water, clear as crystal, and within them, all around, those beings roamed joyfully in boats of jewels. Those waters were adorned with lotuses, blue lotuses and white water lilies, every one as large as a chariot wheel.

The perimeter all around the moats was also adorned with rows of parks, and in each park there were five ponds, each half a yojana square. The waters of those ponds were covered entirely with lotuses, blue lotuses (utpalas), and white water lilies (kumudas), of many colors and sweet fragrance, each as large as a chariot wheel, and they were adorned with rows of trees in full bloom. All around the parks, geese, ducks, and cranes savored the stamens of the lotuses. In each of those surrounding parks there were also houses, and each house had water four yojanas wide. Their walls were made of gold, silver, beryl, and crystal, and their floors of cat's-eye gem. All of them were lofty mansions hung with pearl necklaces, interspersed with jewel tassels and made radiant by them, adorned with great golden porticoes, windows, and latticework, with floors spread with golden sand. The ponds, too, all had walkways with golden stairways of eighteen steps, and all held water fragrant with divine sandalwood, adorned along their edges with rows of golden plantain trees.

Within each of those great cities there were eighty-four thousand kings. All of them were surrounded by countless, countless hosts of women, and, endowed with the five qualities of sensual pleasure, they played, rejoiced, and enjoyed themselves in delight. Other beings, too, were just the same: in other places, where the ground was as pleasant to the touch as kācalindi cloth, sprinkled with sandalwood water and strewn with scattered flowers, they played, rejoiced, and enjoyed themselves in delight. In those great cities there was no sound of any other vehicle, only the sound of the Great Vehicle.

On those great lotuses, too, were great lion thrones with legs made of gold, silver, beryl, and crystal, covered with cloth of Kaliṅga, befitting greatness, and entirely draped with the finest things in the three worlds. Those kings sat upon those lion thrones, and other beings studied the sūtras of the Great Vehicle. Before those kings, some wrote out the letters, some read, and some learned to recite.

Thus, although those beings seated on the lotuses that had grown from the pores of the bodhisattva mahāsattva Boundless Great Body possessed the joys of the five qualities of sensual pleasure, they gave them up entirely. Tormented by grief, they lamented, crying out, "Alas, alas!", and sat presenting hosts of offerings twice as great.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Boundless Great Body, surrounded by countless bodhisattvas, displaying such forms of splendor, came bearing great hosts of offerings and the most abundant food. Whoever perceived the fragrance of that food and of those lotuses became free of the afflictions. Through the great power of the bodhisattva, all the world systems became visible. The size of the body of the bodhisattva mahāsattva Boundless Great Body was equal to space, so that no one could see it.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Boundless Great Body went before the Blessed One, the Buddha, bowed his head at his feet, joined his palms, and said, "May the Blessed One please accept the food that I offer." Then the Blessed One remained silent. Although he requested a second and a third time, the Blessed One remained silent, and so that bodhisattva rose and sat down to one side.

In the same way, from the south, the west, and the north as well, there came bodhisattva mahāsattvas called Boundless Great Body, each with a great retinue. The size of their lotuses and cities was in each case twice as great as the one before, and they came equipped with splendor that was in each case twice as great. Having arrived there, they made offerings of lotuses to the Blessed One and, as before, sat down to one side.

In that very place at the foot of the twin sāla trees, across ground the extent of thirty-two maṇḍalas, there was no space at all where beings were not seated. There, those bodhisattva mahāsattvas with such great bodies, together with their own attendants, sat: some in a space the size of the tip of a reed, some in a space the size of the point of a needle, and some sat at ease in a space the size of an atom. The bodhisattvas who dwell in world systems as numerous as the particles of atoms also came in that same manner and sat down.

Of the beings of Jambudvīpa, apart from the venerable Kāśyapa, the elder Ānanda, and King Ajātaśatru, there was no one who did not come there. Even cruel people, venomous snakes, scorpions, spiders, and the like, down to those who practice the sixteen cruel occupations, came there. The dānavas and the asuras, who are always of hostile intent, all gave up their evil deeds and came to regard one another as a son, a mother, or a sister. All the beings dwelling in the great trichiliocosm loved one another as one would an only son, except for the icchantikas, those of great desire.

This Sahā world system, too, through the power of the Tathāgata, became free of clods, stones, gravel, pebbles and thorns. It was filled with trees entwined with jeweled vines, soft and smooth to the touch, and it became like the world system Sukhāvatī. All the beings who had gathered there, while remaining where they were, saw world systems as numerous as the particles of atoms, together with other buddha fields full of splendor, just as one's own reflection appears on the surface of a mirror.

Then the rays of light of many colors that had come forth from the Tathāgata's mouth, blue, yellow, red, white, crimson, crystal-colored and flame-like, did everything they had to do. Then they turned back, entered the Blessed One's own mouth and vanished. Their radiance outshone the light of the entire assembly.

Then the world with its gods, humans and asuras saw those rays vanish into the Blessed One's mouth. They were frightened, and their hair stood on end. "It cannot be that the Blessed One's rays would turn back and vanish without a cause. Without a doubt, their tasks in the ten directions are complete, and now the Tathāgata will surely pass into parinirvāṇa. If the Blessed One accepts food from no one at all, alas, has he abandoned the four immeasurables toward all beings? Has he resolved to remain fasting? Alas, have the sun and moon set? Has the ship of the sacred Dharma sunk? Alas, alas!" So they sat, lamenting.

Then they wounded their own limbs, and their blood turned the ground to mud. The Hiraṇyavatī River, too, flowed with blood.

Then, from among that assembly, the layman called Cunda, a smith's son from the city of Kuśinagara, rose together with fifteen smiths' sons. He composed his bearing, looked upon the world, draped his upper robe over one shoulder and set his right knee on the ground. He bowed with palms joined in the direction of the Blessed One, and, unable to hold back his tears, he bowed at his feet and said to the Blessed One:

"Blessed One, in order to deliver all beings without fail, I wish to offer the last meal to the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, together with the Saṅgha of monks. I have no protector, no support and no refuge. Since I am destitute and full of hope, I beg the Blessed One to accept the meal I offer and not to pass into parinirvāṇa.

"Blessed One, it is like this. Suppose some very poor householder or brahmin goes to another country. Rousing his enthusiasm, he gradually takes up farming. With a strong plow ox yoked to the plow, he clears his field of stones, pebbles and thorns, and then, full of great hope, he waits for rain to fall from a great cloud. In the same way, Blessed One, in the field of the body, with the plow ox of mind and speech free of the three faults and the four, and with the plow of knowledge, I have cleared away the stones, pebbles and thorns of the many kinds of afflictions, and now I wait for the rain of Dharma to fall from your great cloud of Dharma. When I invite you together with the Saṅgha of monks, then, to dispel my poverty and to benefit all beings, please accept the meal I offer. Since I have no protector and no refuge, I beg the Tathāgata to look upon me as he looks upon Rāhula."

Then the Blessed One, the omniscient Victor, the unsurpassed person, said to Cunda the smith's son, "Cunda, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha accepts your last meal, the great perfection of generosity."

Then those gods, humans and asuras heard that the Tathāgata, together with the Saṅgha of monks, had accepted the last meal offered by Cunda, the great perfection of generosity. They were filled with wonder and amazement, cried out loudly, "Excellent! Excellent!" and said to Cunda the smith's son:

"O Cunda, smith's son, how fortunate you are! Your gaining a human body has borne fruit. You have gained it well. Cunda, in this world, beauty like that of the udumbara flower is hard to find. Far harder than that is to find the appearance of a tathāgata. Harder still is to find faith. Far harder still is to find the chance to hear the Dharma. And far harder still is to find the chance to offer the last meal to the Tathāgata. Therefore, Cunda, smith's son, you who have attained the perfection of giving the last meal, we bow to you. When, on the full moon of the middle autumn month, the sky is without clouds and one sees the stainless, beautiful disk of the moon, only the moon is plainly visible. In the same way, because the Tathāgata has accepted the last meal you offered, you alone are plainly visible to all beings. We bow to you again and again. Cunda, though you have a human body, you are a god even among gods. You are like Rāhula, the son of the Tathāgata."

Having said this, they also spoke these verses:

"You are not a human; you are a supreme god,
Chief and best of humans, exceptionally noble.
Therefore we make this request of you:
The Teacher is about to pass into parinirvāṇa.
Cunda, out of love for all of us,
For the lasting benefit of beings,
Ask the Teacher here, the boundless protector,
To remain and teach the nectar without ceasing.
Freed from existence, he wishes to pass into nirvāṇa.
If you do not make the request today,
Know that all of us will be ruined.
So, for our benefit, make the request to the Protector."

Then, just as someone who sees both parents, who had died, come back to life and remain alive would be overjoyed, so too Cunda the smith's son was overjoyed. He stretched out his hands, bowed his head to the Blessed One, rose, and spoke these verses:

Oh! Today I have truly gained what is worth gaining.
My birth in the human world has borne its fruit.
Now I shall not be parted from gain and all the rest,
Nor shall I fall into the unbearable hells.
This great gain that I have gained here today
Surpasses even a heap of gold.
Since I have met the hero, the Sugata, the protector,
From now on I have no fear of an animal birth.
Faith in the Buddha, deep-rooted,
I have found today, like the udumbara flower.
I have wholly cast off the terrifying realm of the hungry ghosts,
And destroyed the dreadful seed of birth among the asuras.
To meet today the Tathāgata, whose coming is so rare,
Is like a mustard seed lodging on the point of a needle.
By offering him the supreme perfection of giving,
I shall always rise to the realm of the gods.
Like a lotus growing in water yet untouched by the water,
The great sage is untainted by worldly concerns;
By meeting him I have uprooted every seed of the peak of existence
And crossed, myself, the great ocean of saṃsāra.
Harder even than for a blind turtle in the ocean
To set its neck into the hole of a yoke,
Today I have met the Sugata and offered him a gift;
Thereby I have destroyed every accumulation of affliction.
Though the gods offered him fine food,
The Sugata did not accept it; from me
He accepted an offering like the eraṇḍa flower,
And so I am supreme in the world, endowed with glory and virtue.
Brahmā, Śakra (Indra), and all this world
Have come and reverently make offerings to me.
This attainment of the station of the bull among men
I have won in this very life.

Then, joining his palms, he said this to the Blessed One:

Protector, seeing you about to pass into nirvāṇa,
All this world is tormented by suffering.
Helplessly they cry aloud, "Alas!"
Do not forsake them; regard them always as your children.
Just as the lofty golden peak of Meru
Stands in beauty in the midst of the great ocean,
So you, lord of the Dharma, teaching the Dharma,
Please remain in beauty in the midst of the Saṅgha.
Just as, when the radiant sun has risen,
Its rays dispel the thin clouds,
So, great sage, with your great lamp of wisdom,
Dispel today the sorrow of the multitude.
These beings, their eyes swollen with weeping,
Have sunk to the depths of an ocean of sheer suffering.
Therefore engender in them the faith so hard to find,
Cut off their suffering, and remain for long.

Then the Blessed One said to Cunda: "Cunda, it is so. It is just so. The appearance of a buddha is as exceedingly hard to find as the udumbara flower. Faith too is hard to find. Hearing the Dharma too is hard to find. At the time of the Tathāgata's complete nirvāṇa, the perfect perfection of giving that is the last meal is also hard to find. Furthermore, Cunda, do not weep and do not grieve, but give rise to joy, thinking, 'I have attained the perfect perfection of giving, the last meal offered to the Tathāgata.' And do not make this request to the Tathāgata: 'Blessed One, please remain.' Realize the suchness of the Dharma of the buddhas. Enter into impermanence. Come to understand the nature of formations." Having said this, he spoke these verses:

Though life be very long, it will come to an end.
Here everyone will certainly die.
The world of the living is subject to destruction.
All prosperity inclines toward decline.
Youth is always surely overcome by age.
Fair form, free of illness, is destroyed by illness.
Life is torn out by the roots by death.
In all the world there is nothing permanent.
Power is impermanent, and life too is impermanent;
Beings forever turn upon the wheel of suffering.
In the three worlds there is nothing stable.
Within existence all beings cling to life.
Existence is subject to destruction and ever shaking;
Within it there is no happiness at all.
The fear of aging, death, sickness and decline
Torments them always on every side.
Existence is endless, unstable, without essence;
Know all of it to be foe and adversary.
Circling there, by the net of afflictions
The world is wrapped, like a silkworm.
In the world, that constant ground of harm,
Ever bound up with suffering like a tumor,
In this impure body and in those desires,
Who that seeks his good and wishes to be rid of suffering would delight?
There is no attachment to impermanent desires;
By proper reflection one is freed from desire.
Then, seeing rightly, one is parted from desire;
Having abandoned and cut them off, one becomes liberated.
I have crossed well the great river of existence.
I have passed beyond sufferings and entered into bliss.
Therefore I, infinite and free of elaboration,
Have cleared away all attachment and attained liberation.
For me there is no aging, sickness or death.
For me life never comes to an end.
Though I shall go, blazing like a fire,
Do not think that I shall cease to be.
See the Tathāgata as being like Meru.
Today I shall truly pass into nirvāṇa.
That supreme bliss is my true nature;
It is the domain of the victors, so do not weep.

Then Cunda, the smith's son, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, it is so. It is just so. Since nirvāṇa is inconceivable even to me, what need is there to speak of other beings of the Lesser Vehicle, who are like flies? Blessed One, oh! today I have become equal to the bodhisattvas, supreme among men, possessed of great fortune and worthy of offerings, such as the youthful Mañjuśrī, and to those whose defilements are exhausted. Blessed One, to give an example: just as a young man newly given full ordination, from the very day he goes forth, is counted as Saṅgha within the Saṅgha and is fully gathered into it, so too I, through the power of the Tathāgata, am counted among the bodhisattva great beings and fully gathered into their number. Blessed One, I wish the Tathāgata to remain for long; I do not wish him to depart into nirvāṇa like scorched food."

Then the youthful Mañjuśrī said this to Cunda, the smith's son:

"Cunda, do not say, 'I wish the Tathāgata to remain for long; I do not wish him to depart into nirvāṇa like scorched food.' One should indeed look at the nature of formations. Whoever looks in that way will perfect the meditation on emptiness. One who wants the sublime Dharma should train in that way."

Cunda said, "Venerable Mañjuśrī, then how could that be? The Tathāgata has become the supreme god of gods. If he were a formation, he would not be fit to be the god of gods. Formations are known to be like water bubbles, and they turn like the wheel of a chariot. The gods are renowned as supremely long-lived, yet the Tathāgata did not last even a hundred years. Therefore, on that view, the Tathāgata would not be fit to be the god of gods.

"For example, a certain man who was a village headman gradually became a great lord, surpassing even the power of the great ministers. He enjoyed the fruit of his own power, gained lawfully. But if his merit is then exhausted and he falls from his power, he is put to work by others and despised. Why is that? Because he has become poor. In the same way, if the Tathāgata were a formation, then, since that is a cause of death, he would not be fit to be the god of gods. Therefore, Venerable Mañjuśrī, do not regard the god of gods, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, as being like formations.

"Moreover, Mañjuśrī, do you conceive of the Tathāgata as having the nature of formations knowingly, or do you conceive of him so out of not knowing? If the Tathāgata were like formations, he would not be fit to be the god of gods who has attained mastery over the Dharma in the three worlds.

"For example, a certain lord of kings has a wrestler who is supreme among a thousand wrestlers. Because no wrestler at all can defeat him, he receives the name 'Victor over a Thousand.' Since he has no rival among wrestlers, if he is granted great authority by the king, would his fame be untrue when one hears that name of his, Victor over a Thousand? No: when one has qualities, one receives the name of one who possesses them. In the same way, the Tathāgata has conquered all the wrestlers: the māra of the afflictions, the māra of the aggregates, the māra who is a son of the gods, and the māra of intrinsic nature. So, like Victor over a Thousand, by virtue of his true qualities he is called in the three worlds the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha. Venerable Mañjuśrī, do not conceive of the Tathāgata as being like formations.

"For example, someone takes birth in a rich family of great wealth but is destined to be short-lived. Astrologers see this and declare, 'This boy is short-lived.' When his parents hear this, they think, 'He will be of no benefit to our household.' So they do not even count him and show him no love. In just this way, whether it is a man, a woman, a god, or even Brahmā, whoever is short-lived is held in contempt. If the Tathāgata, who is wholly devoted to the Dharma alone, were a formation, he would be despised by the whole world. Among all phenomena, that in which there is no falling back and no exhaustion is taught to be liberation. If things were as you say, that teaching would be meaningless. Venerable Mañjuśrī, do not say, 'The Tathāgata is a formation.'

"For example, there was a young woman who was poor, destitute, without protector, stricken with illness, suffering in the meantime, and burdened by her pregnancy. She went to an ownerless house, and there the girl gave birth to a child. But others who had been living there before drove her out of it. Just after giving birth, the afflicted woman carried her newborn infant and set out for a land with good harvests. On the road she was tormented by gadflies, flies, wind, the heat of the sun, and the harm of scorpions and snakes. She wanted to cross the great Ganges River, but she could not cross it. Still carrying the boy, she died in the Ganges. Through the special merit of her love for her child, after she was parted from her body in death she was born in the world of Brahmā. If one asks by what kind of special merit of love she was born there: she was born through the special merit of the thought, 'Even if I die, let this boy be brought across safe and unharmed.'

"In the same way, Venerable Mañjuśrī, a holy person who wishes to bring forth the sublime Dharma should not view the Tathāgata as a formation, and should not alter his perception either. Even if one does not have such an eye in one's body, one should hold the right view, thinking, 'The Blessed One is inconceivable.' One who teaches instructions that benefit all sentient beings should teach that the Tathāgata is unconditioned. One who brings forth the sublime Dharma should act just as the poor young woman strove, staking her own life, to rescue her child from the Ganges River. In order to employ the means of bringing forth the sublime Dharma, one should give rise to the thought, 'The Tathāgata is unconditioned.' If one sees the Tathāgata as unconditioned, one will realize the meaning of liberation, the aim that one must accomplish oneself."

"Venerable Mañjuśrī, it is like this. A certain man, worn out from travelling on the road, was lying asleep inside a house. Then a great fire blazed up from the roof of that house. Thinking, 'If I am burned by this fire, I am as good as dead,' and possessed of a sense of shame and of conscience, he wrapped himself tightly in his clothes, and he died with his mind still mindful.
