As the result of the merit of that thought of shame and conscience, he held the kingship of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three eighty times and the kingship of Brahmā a hundred thousand times. In the human world too he held the kingship of a wheel-turning monarch many times. He was never born in the lower realms, and many times over he obtained nothing but happiness, one state following upon another. In the same way, Mañjuśrī, you should be scrupulous, as excellent people are. Have a sense of shame! Do not make the Tathāgata out to be engaged in the ways of conditioned things.

"Rather than a monk perfect in moral discipline saying of the Tathāgata, who is unconditioned, 'The Tathāgata is conditioned,' it would be better even for him to become a tīrthika and die. Whoever says 'The Tathāgata is conditioned,' though the Tathāgata is unconditioned, speaks falsely. Those who say 'The Tathāgata is conditioned' will go to hell as if to their own home. Venerable Mañjuśrī, do not regard the Tathāgata as resembling conditioned things. From now on, as you wander in saṃsāra, turn away from ignorance and realize solely the knowledge that thinks, 'The Blessed One is unconditioned.' As the result of doing this and meditating on it, you will very swiftly come to possess the thirty-two marks, just like the Tathāgata."

Then Mañjuśrī, in youthful form, said this to Cunda, the smith's son: "Excellent, excellent! May you live long! This is how a son of good family should act. Son of good family, you know the Tathāgata to be permanent, everlasting, unconditioned and stable. Since you know in exactly this way, by doing away with the conditioned, you too before long will become just as the Tathāgata is. Later the Buddha himself will also give praise, saying that the two of us have thus spoken a praise of surpassing excellence.

"Cunda, hasten the Tathāgata's meal! Among all gifts, hastened giving is supreme. What is called hastened giving is this: in order that sprouts may arise from the seeds of every perfection of generosity, when monks, nuns, laymen or laywomen have set out on the road for some purpose and have grown weary, to give them whatever one has, what suits their needs, a gift given with care; that is called hastened giving. Cunda, in the same way, from whatever you have, let this last of meals, whether it be much or little, whether it be special or not, be served to the Tathāgata together with the Saṅgha of monks. The Blessed One is close to departing into complete nirvāṇa."

Then Cunda said, "Venerable Mañjuśrī, are you attached to food, or what, that you urge me on, saying, 'Serve the meal, whether much or little'? The Blessed One practised austerities for six years; can he not now hold out even for a moment? The Tathāgata is the body of Dharma, not a body of food, so does the Blessed One eat food?"

The Blessed One said, "Mañjuśrī, what Cunda has said is so. Cunda, excellent, excellent! You have great wisdom regarding the Great Vehicle. You exert yourself for the Great Vehicle."

Mañjuśrī also said this: "If it is so intended, may the Tathāgata's life be long!"

Cunda said, "Is the Blessed One pleased with me alone, or is he pleased with all sentient beings as well?"

Mañjuśrī said, "Is the Blessed One not pleased with you, with me, and with all sentient beings?"

Cunda said, "Do not say that! Wherever there is gratification, there is distorted perception, and that is where people like us operate. The Tathāgata has no pleasure or delight. For the Tathāgata does not have a love like that of a cow who, out of love for her calf, grazes and grazes and then comes back to the village; rather, he regards all sentient beings just as he regards Rāhula. Such is the domain of the Buddha's wisdom. Venerable Mañjuśrī, just as a donkey cart cannot possibly run alongside a chariot yoked to thoroughbred horses, how could people like us, who are like donkey carts, keep pace with the Tathāgata's wisdom and his words of hidden intent? It is like a garuḍa that flies for many leagues: abiding in the sky, it sees the fish and turtles in the water as clearly as one's own reflection appears on the surface of a mirror, yet children, because their eyes are not clear, cannot describe the power of its sight. In just the same way it is with the Tathāgata."

Mañjuśrī said, "Cunda, that is so. I merely raised that objection in order to speak of the greatness of the bodhisattvas."

Then at that moment rays of light of many kinds spread forth from the mouth of the Blessed One and struck the body of Mañjuśrī, in youthful form. Then Mañjuśrī, understanding this, said to Cunda, the smith's son, "Cunda, the Tathāgata is hastening to pass into complete nirvāṇa. Let the meal be served to the Blessed One in good time. Do not delay! Rays of light do not come forth from the Blessed One's mouth without a cause."

Then Cunda remained without saying anything.

Then the Blessed One said this to Cunda: "Cunda, I am certain to go into complete nirvāṇa. Let food be served to the Saṅgha of monks in good time." This he declared twice, and a third time.

Then Cunda said, "Alas! If the Blessed One does not remain, the world will become empty. All of us, weeping, implore the Blessed One."

Then the Blessed One said to Cunda, "Cunda, do not weep. Do not wound your body like this. Realize that the human body, like a plantain tree, has no core. Understand it to be like a mirage, a swelling bubble of foam, an illusion, a vessel made by a potter, and lightning. Know that the conditioned is the source of countless faults. Look at the nature of the conditioned."

Then Cunda said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, if out of love for me and for all beings the Tathāgata does not consent to remain for a long time, how could I not weep? Please do not depart and leave the world empty."

Then the Blessed One said to Cunda, "It is out of compassion for you and out of compassion for all beings that I pass into parinirvāṇa. This is the nature of reality of the buddhas, and it is the nature of the conditioned. Understand the verse that says, 'All formations are impermanent.' Realize that all phenomena are suffering, because they are mixed with formations. Examine all phenomena as selfless. Reflect that they are unstable, borrowed, and inverted. Know that this body is possessed of endless defects. Regard it as like foam, and do not weep. Do not despair."

Then Cunda said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, even when I see it in that way, the suffering is extremely great and unbearable. Yet I rejoice that the Blessed One passes into parinirvāṇa as a skillful means."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, Cunda, excellent! A son of good family should regard my nirvāṇa in just that way, as a skillful means. Cunda, know the departure of the buddhas to be like a haṃsa going off in play. Cunda, know the signs of what is long-lived and what is not long-lived. Cunda, all phenomena have the nature of illusion. Among all phenomena, the Tathāgata has the nature of skillful means, and is not stained by them; that is the nature of the buddhas, the blessed ones. Cunda, so that you may surely cross the ocean of existence, quickly bring the food. May gods, asuras, and humans as well, having made their own offerings to me, attain unmoving bliss. You too, give at the proper time to those who are worthy recipients. Do not delay. A worthy recipient, I myself, and what is called the Tathāgata are not different in meaning."

Then Cunda, for the sake of surely delivering all beings, bowed his head, shed tears, and said, "Excellent! Excellent! I shall do just as the Blessed One has said. How the Tathāgata is to be regarded after his parinirvāṇa is not the domain of us, who are like flies, nor is it the domain of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas."

Then, grieving and weeping, together with his servants and retinue, he circumambulated the Blessed One, performed a great act of offering, bowed to the Blessed One and to Mañjuśrī, in youthful form, and went to fetch the food.

The third fascicle.

Then, at that time, the great earth shook and shook violently, moved and moved violently, heaved and heaved violently, rolled and rolled violently, rumbled and rumbled violently, cracked and cracked violently, quivered and quivered violently, swayed and swayed violently. Doing these and other such things, like a person struck down by the affliction of fainting from a bile disease, this great earth, bounded by the poisonous ocean and reaching up to the world of Brahmā, quaked violently all at once. Then all those gods, asuras, and humans were bewildered, terrified, and their hair stood on end; and weeping, with one voice they offered these verses:

O Protector, we bow our heads to you
And make our earnest plea.
O Victor, leader of the herd,
If we are parted from you, we shall have no protector.
When we think that today you will pass into nirvāṇa,
We sink into suffering exceedingly deep.
O Sage, our hearts are lost to us,
Like a milk cow pining for the calf a thief has carried off.
Just as some poor man without a protector,
Struck down by illness and with no physician,
Doing as he pleases and eating harmful food,
Will surely die,
So these beings, struck down by the illness of the afflictions,
Without you, without the nourishment of your Dharma,
Will die like those who commit the faults of wrong view.
Therefore, for its benefit, do not abandon the world.
Like a city bereft of its king,
Like the poor when famine has come,
Like one tormented by thirst in the wilderness,
So shall we be after you have passed away.
Because you, who cherish beings, intend to pass into nirvāṇa,
The earth quakes violently and the directions are confounded,
The great oceans too are churned in turmoil,
And all of us are plunged in suffering and pain.
Great Sage, if today you pass beyond sorrow,
The very sun will fall to the earth,
The great oceans too will dry up,
And all of us, surely, will perish today.
Just as when a strong man drags an elephant
Through a narrow gateway to force it inside,
It is greatly distressed,
So all of us are greatly distressed.
Just as no one wishes an only child to die,
So we do not wish you to pass beyond sorrow.
If you do not hold back from passing into nirvāṇa,
All of us will be left destitute.
Seeing you set out on the road to nirvāṇa,
Even many animals grieve and lament;
How could we not lament?
Do not cast us away like spittle.
Just as the rising sun with its own clear light
Utterly destroys the darkness,
So you, possessor of miraculous powers, dispel the darkness of suffering
And dwell amid the Saṅgha like Mount Meru.

Blessed One, by way of example: suppose a certain king has a son who is dear and precious to him, and after having him trained in the treatises on statecraft, he commands, "Put him to death." In the same way, Blessed One, we too, the sons of the Blessed One who turns the wheel of the holy Dharma, have had right view produced in us. If we are not to be slain as that king's son was, then, Blessed One, for that reason please remain for a long time.

Just as someone who relies on treatises that dispute over scriptures becomes frightened, so do not make us frightened. Knowing reality as you do, do not let us sentient beings go to the hells. Satisfy us with the essence of the nectar of the holy Dharma, and, Tathāgata, please remain for a long time.

Suppose someone is first made to suffer in a king's prison because of harmful deeds, and another asks him, "What kind of pain are you experiencing?" He replies, "It is like this: when I am freed from the feeling of suffering, that will be nirvāṇa, and I shall experience happiness." In the same way, for all of us, if you remain, it is nirvāṇa, like being freed from the king's prison. For that reason, Tathāgata, please remain for a long time.

Blessed One, by way of example: a physician skilled in the eight branches of the science of life teaches the final tantra to his own son, but does not teach it to his disciples, fearing that they would become his equal. In the same way, O King of Dharma, great physician who has no stinginess, do not act like that with the final tantra of the Great Vehicle. Blessed One, please remain for a long time. Likewise, a charioteer skilled in the science of chariots teaches his own son the final tantra, all the techniques of striking a target by its sound, but does not teach them to his disciples. The same should be applied to the other fields of knowledge as well.

Blessed One, by way of example: if a child, an old person, or a sick person strays from the good road and goes onto a bad one, they will suffer greatly. In the same way, Blessed One, beings who go on bad paths will, after they part from the body, experience suffering in the hells. Therefore, Blessed One, guide of the path, please remain for a long time.

The same should be applied in full to the ninety-six wonders and the forty-five verses as well.

Then, at that time, the Blessed One said to the monks, "Monks, do not utter childish laments as gods and humans do; set about your diligence."

Then the gods, asuras, and humans, hearing the words of the Blessed One, which were full of wisdom and led to reality, were stricken with grief, like one whose only son has died and been cast into a charnel ground.

Then the Blessed One spoke to them in verse:

May you all surely be happy.
Do not grieve, do not weep.
This is the Dharma of the buddhas.
All of you, sit in silence.
Do not dwell in heedlessness;
Take delight in heedfulness.

"Whatever doubts, hesitations, or uncertainties you may have about empty and not empty, permanent and impermanent, refuge and not refuge, going and not going, support and not support, everlasting and not everlasting, stable and unstable, sentient being and no sentient being, real and unreal, true and untrue, nirvāṇa and not nirvāṇa, secret and not secret, two and nondual, and likewise about the many kinds of phenomena, ask me about each and every one of them, and I shall resolve them all. I shall also teach you the supreme nectar, and after that I shall pass into complete nirvāṇa.

"Monks, the appearance of a buddha is hard to find. Obtaining a human body is hard to find. Perfect leisure is hard to find. Perfect patience and moral discipline, unbroken and untorn, are also extremely hard to find. Attaining arhatship in the world is as hard to find as gold dust or the udumbara flower."

Monks, the earth is in every way the sustenance of all sentient beings. In the same way, my teaching of the Dharma in its many forms, the secret ambrosial food of the city of liberation, should be regarded as like it. For all sentient beings and for all my sons, the monks, I have firmly established many teachings of the sacred Dharma. Furthermore, for the benefit of all sentient beings, I shall proclaim the greatness of the Tathāgata's qualities, and after also setting down the discourse on the single syllable and its dot, I shall pass into parinirvāṇa.

When those monks heard that the Tathāgata was going to pass into parinirvāṇa, they were stricken with sorrow. Shuddering, their eyes brimming with tears, they bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One (the Buddha), circled the Blessed One many times, and then spoke to the Blessed One as follows:

"The Blessed One has taught suffering, impermanence and selflessness, saying, 'Among all footprints, the footprint of the elephant is supreme.' In just that way, by relying on, cultivating and repeatedly practicing the perception of impermanence, all attachment to the desire realm is exhausted, all attachment to the form realm is exhausted, all attachment to the formless realm is exhausted, all ignorance is exhausted, and all excessive pride is cut off completely, without remainder.

"Blessed One, it is like this: in autumn a farmer ploughs deeply and clears away all the tangled roots. In the same way, Blessed One, by relying on, cultivating and repeatedly practicing the perception of impermanence, all attachment to the desire realm is exhausted, all attachment to the form realm is exhausted, all attachment to the formless realm is exhausted, all ignorance is exhausted, and all excessive pride is completely exhausted, without remainder." The passage beginning "Among all kinds of farm work, the autumn farm work is taught to be supreme" is to be supplied in full, as it appears in the Elephant's Footprint Sūtra.

"Blessed One, it is like this: when a king is dying, he empties his prisons, freeing the beings who have been tormented by the hardships of prison, and only after that does he die. In the same way, Blessed One, please cut the bonds of ignorance of the beings confined in the prisons of the many kinds of ignorance, and only after that pass into parinirvāṇa.

"Blessed One, here is another example. When beings possessed by bhūta and piśāca spirits meet a great physician, they are freed from every affliction caused by those bhūta and piśāca spirits. In the same way, when they meet you, the whole assembly of śrāvakas is completely freed from the bhūta and piśāca spirits of the many kinds of ignorance, and comes to realize the Perfection of Wisdom itself.

"Blessed One, it is like this: a scented elephant, bound by its trainer with a great rope, snaps that rope and runs away. In the same way, Blessed One, we too have cut the great ropes of clinging to a self, clinging to what is mine, jealousy, conceit, stinginess and guile, and have come into the hands of the great elephant trainer, the Tathāgata. We shall completely clear away the fifty-seven elephants of affliction from all sentient beings.

"Blessed One, it is like this: one stricken with a chill fever who goes to a great physician is freed from suffering. In the same way, Blessed One, we, whose bodies were sick with the chill fever of the endless faults of affliction, wrong view and wrong livelihood, came to the great physician, the Blessed One, were completely freed from all suffering, and attained unmoving bliss.

"Blessed One, here is another example. A man drinks rice beer and becomes drunk. He no longer knows himself; he knows neither Dharma nor what is not Dharma; he does not even recognize, 'This is my mother, this is my sister, this is my daughter.' He babbles and vomits, and his whole body is smeared with excrement and urine. Later he sobers up, and understanding clearly what caused all this, he thinks, 'Liquor is in no way fit to drink.' Thinking, 'I have been freed from all wrongdoing,' he firmly impresses on himself that drinking liquor is in no way acceptable, and gives it up. In the same way, Blessed One, this world of living beings, like an actor, has been circling and wandering through saṃsāra since beginningless time. Not recognizing, 'This is my mother, this is my sister, this is my daughter,' beings act out desirous perceptions with mothers, sisters and daughters, and, just like one drunk on rice beer, they meet with suffering. Then those people who have a sense of shame, like one who has sobered up from drunkenness on rice beer, firmly impress on themselves that this is in no way acceptable, and so they pass completely beyond suffering.

"Furthermore, just as it is impossible for the castor plant to have a core, so too it is impossible for this body to have a self, a sentient being, a life, a person, a manuja, a mānava or an agent. Understanding it thus, we have cultivated and repeatedly practiced the perception of selflessness. It is like this: just as the heap of chaff thrown away by winnowers is not valued at all, so too there is no self in this body. It is like this: just as the khasral flower gives off no fragrance, so too there is no self in this body. In this way we have cultivated and repeatedly practiced the perception of selflessness.

"The Blessed One also declared, 'Monks, all phenomena are selfless; meditate in that way. Those who meditate in that way will abandon clinging to a self. When clinging to a self has been completely abandoned, nirvāṇa will be attained.' Blessed One, since all phenomena are thus selfless, we have cultivated and repeatedly practiced the perception of selflessness. Furthermore, just as no track of a bird can be found in the sky, so too, when one cultivates the perception of selflessness, one becomes free of every kind of view."

The Blessed One said, "Do you understand this way of meditating?"

The monks replied, "Blessed One, suppose one cultivates perceptions other than the perceptions of suffering, impermanence and no-self. A drunken man, reeling about, sees temples, towers, the ground, the sun, the moon, trees and mountains turning round, though they are not turning. In the same way, those worldly beings who have not cultivated the perceptions of suffering, impermanence and no-self should be regarded as like drunkards. Blessed One, we have cultivated them properly."

The Blessed One said, "Monks, now I shall explain the meaning of this example. You do not clearly know, regarding the meaning of the verse, 'This is the meaning, and this is the letter.' A drunken man sees temples, towers, the ground, the sun, the moon, trees and mountains turning round, though they are not turning. In the same way, those who are gripped by many inverted perceptions and are thoroughly deluded entertain the perceptions 'This is self,' 'This is permanent,' 'This is bliss,' and 'This is pure.' Here, 'self' means the Buddha. 'Permanent' means the dharmakāya. 'Bliss' means nirvāṇa. 'Pure' is a synonym for the Dharma.

"Monks, do not puff yourselves up with arrogance and pride, saying, 'Blessed One, we have cultivated the perceptions of suffering, impermanence and no-self.' If you cultivate the three in that way, then the three kinds of cultivation you have cultivated within my teaching will become meaningless. The three kinds of cultivation by which suffering is to be understood are of a superior kind, superior even beyond the superior.

"To perceive suffering as bliss is an inversion. To perceive bliss as suffering is an inversion. To perceive the impermanent as permanent is an inversion. To perceive the permanent as impermanent is an inversion. To perceive what is without self as self is an inversion. To perceive what has a self as without self is an inversion. To perceive the impure as pure is an inversion. To perceive the pure as impure is an inversion. Without truly understanding these four inversions, you have cultivated what was to be cultivated and practiced it much. You have cultivated bliss as suffering, the permanent as impermanent, what has a self as without self, and the pure as impure. Worldly people also have bliss, self, permanence and purity. Those beyond the world also have bliss, self, permanence and purity, and here the two are at odds with each other. 'The letter' is a term for the worldly. 'The meaning' is wisdom beyond the world."

Then the monks said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, the four perceptions and the other inversions that the Tathāgata has set forth, we have long since seen, and we have also cultivated them. Now, please tell us how we should understand them. Please, Tathāgata, remain for an aeon or for more than an aeon. Whatever the Tathāgata tells us, we wish to do just as he says. If the Tathāgata does not consent to remain, we would not wish to live together in one house with a venomous snake. If the Tathāgata does not remain, we will have no heart to go on living the holy life, and so, following you yourself, we too wish to pass into parinirvāṇa."

Then the Blessed One said this to those monks: "Monks, do not speak like that. Monks, I shall entrust the teaching into the hands of Kāśyapa. Like the Tathāgata, he will be a support for the monks. He will be a refuge for the monks and for sentient beings. Just as a king who is lord of many cities goes to one city and consecrates a householder as king, so I consecrate Mahākāśyapa in this world system.

"Monks, if you ask how you should cultivate the perceptions of suffering, impermanence, no-self and impurity: monks, suppose that when the summer season has come, people dam up water in a grove, and each, carrying his own swimming gear, plays in the water. Then someone places in that water a precious vaiḍūrya gem of the finest kind. Wanting that vaiḍūrya, they all throw away their floats and dive into the water. Whatever potsherds or pebbles are there, they pick up, thinking, 'This is the gem,' and each of them comes up saying, 'I have found the gem! I have found it!' Sitting on the edge of the pond, the perception arises in them, 'This is not the gem.' The water of the pond, too, is beautiful with the light of the gem, like moonlight, and seeing that beauty they come to perceive its qualities, thinking, 'Ah, the gem!' Then one among them, skilled in means and sharp of mind, truly finds the gem.

"In the same way, monks, whatever you have cultivated and practiced much, taking it to cover everything, saying, 'All is suffering, all is without self, all is impermanent, all is impure,' all of that has become mistaken and meaningless, like the potsherds and pebbles in the pond. Act like the man skilled in means. In each and every thing that you have cultivated by taking it to cover everything, in that very thing there are bliss, self, permanence and purity, and these four should be declared the non-inverted. Therefore, like the gem, cultivate the perception that the suchness of phenomena is permanent."

Then the monks said this to the Blessed One:

"The Blessed One (the Buddha) said, 'Monks, all phenomena are without self; meditate in this way. Those who meditate in this way will abandon the grasping at a self, and once they have abandoned the grasping at a self, they will attain nirvāṇa.' Since this is what you declared, what is the meaning of what you declared? Blessed One, we ask that the Tathāgata please explain its analysis."

The Blessed One said to those monks, "Monks, excellent, excellent! Monks, one who wishes to cut through his own doubts once and for all must ask just as you have. Excellent!

"Monks, suppose that in a certain city a king lacking in wisdom held the throne. That king had a bad physician, and a foolish one at that, who wielded great power and influence from on high and was honored by the king. He held that milk alone was medicine. Not knowing the basis of disease, he clung to a mistaken milk treatment and gave it to all people for every kind of wind disorder, and gave it as well for disorders of bile and disorders of phlegm. Yet although he did this, the physician remained the favorite, for the king too was a fool who could not tell the difference.

"Then another physician, skilled in the eight branches of the science of life and versed in the later treatises, newly arrived from another land. He went to that physician bearing a gift and said, 'Having heard your name, I have come in order to learn. Please be my teacher.'

"When the foolish physician heard this, he said to the newly arrived physician, 'For forty-eight years serve me with great diligence, enduring hardship. After that, study, and you will become a physician.'

"The newcomer agreed to this, saying, 'I shall do so.' Little by little he won the foolish physician's favor. Then he went to the royal palace and said to the king, 'Great king, you should always apply yourself to all the treatises. If you do so, the king will be worthy of being king.'

"The king replied, 'That is true, and it is pleasing to hear.' And so the king came to distinguish between the foolish physician and the other physician. He banished the bad physician from the realm and honored the other, good physician.

"Then that physician thought, 'Now the time has come,' and said to the king, 'Great king, if you are pleased with me, I would make a single request. If the great king is indeed pleased, please grant it.'

"The king said to the physician, 'Ask for any wealth whatever, even my right hand.'

"The physician said to the king, 'Great king, the milk treatment of that foolish physician is hālāhala poison. Since he did not understand this, please proclaim by decree throughout your entire realm: "Whatever milk treatment the former physician gave is hālāhala poison. Whoever drinks milk as medicine shall be beheaded." If this is not done, beings will die untimely deaths. I want them to live and to be free of disease. Your Majesty, that is the request I make.'

"Then the king said to the physician, 'If that is so, you are asking a small thing. I shall issue the decree.' And he issued it. Then, in accordance with the decree, it became the custom everywhere.

"Then the physician said to the king once more, 'Please issue the same decree again, a second and a third time.' And the physician cured people's diseases with the sweet, the sour, the salty, the pungent, the astringent, and the bitter tastes.

"Then, some time later, the king was struck by illness. He summoned that physician and commanded him, 'I am close to death. I will give you whatever you wish to request, as befits a king's means.'

"Then the physician said to the king, 'Great king, I spoke falsely. Milk is nectar. I ask that you take food mixed with milk.'

"Then the king said to the physician, 'Have you been driven mad by bile, or are you deceiving me? Earlier you said milk is poison, and now you say milk is the best of medicines. Alas, banishing the former physician has ruined me! Since he too praised the milk treatment, he was a great physician, not a bad one. Your giving the milk treatment is reason enough for you yourself to be banished.'

"The physician said, 'Great king, please do not think of it that way. For example, when one sees on wood or on a wall marks bored by worms that look like letters, the wise do not say, "The worm knows how to write." Even though they look like written letters, they do not take them for a marvel. In the same way, the milk treatment practiced by your former physician should be regarded like letters bored by worms, for he did not even know the distinctions among different kinds of milk.'

"The king said to the physician, 'What! How did that bad physician not know the distinctions among different kinds of milk?'

"The physician said, 'Great king, milk is both nectar and poison. What is called nectar is this: a cow whose calf is not overly strong; who does not drink the dregs of beer; who does not dwell in thickets, ravines, or muddy swamps; whose teats the cowherd has not roughly handled; who has not been fed on cut grass; who stays in her own herd and does not mingle with herds of buffalo; a cow endowed with these and similar good qualities. Her milk, drawn only in due measure without cutting off what remains to flow for the calf, pacifies every disease.'

"Then the king was amazed and said to the physician, 'Teacher, now I know your good milk treatment, and I know the bad one as well.'

Then the king issued a decree, commanding, "Drink milk!" When all the subjects heard that the king had commanded them to drink milk once again, they were thrown into confusion and said, "Has the king been seized by a graha? Or is he mocking us?" So the people of the city and all the subjects came before the king. When the king saw them, he said, "If this physician says that milk is like that, what objection do you have?"

Then the king, the people of the city and all the subjects were pleased. They paid the physician honor beyond compare and relied on treatment with milk. In just the same way, monks, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, charioteer who tames beings, unsurpassed, teacher of gods and humans, the Buddha, the Blessed One, appears in the world like the good physician. He goes before all the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), who are like the bad physician, thoroughly subdues them all and utterly defeats them, and pleases many kings. In order to vanquish the tīrthikas he declares, "There is no self. There is no sentient being. There is no life. There is no person."

The self taught by the tīrthikas is like letters carved by a worm. Therefore I taught all sentient beings the teaching that there is no self. Having issued the Dharma decree, "Selflessness is the word of the Buddha," then, according to the capacities of those to be trained and for the benefit of sentient beings, knowing the right time, on that very occasion of teaching that all dharmas are selfless, he truly teaches, like the good physician, that there is a self. It is not the self of worldly people, said to be the size of a thumb or the size of a grain of millet. Nor is it whatever worldly people view as a self. Here, all dharmas are called selfless. Yet all dharmas are not selfless either: the self is suchness itself; the self is permanence itself; the self is virtue itself; the self is the everlasting itself; the self is stability itself; the self is peace itself. Just like the good physician's milk, the Tathāgata too teaches what is endowed with suchness. Let the four assemblies strive in meditating on it!

Monks, furthermore, apart from matters of moral discipline, whatever doubts and hesitations you have, ask me about them, and when I have explained them well, you should be satisfied. I teach the great emptiness of intrinsic nature, which is free from all meditation. Monks, do not say, "The Tathāgata did not teach us the great emptiness of intrinsic nature." Monks, furthermore, apart from matters of moral discipline, whatever doubts and hesitations you have, ask me about them.

Then those monks said this to the Blessed One: "To put questions to the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is not within our domain; we are not able to do so. The Buddha, the Blessed One, is inconceivable; his meditative absorptions are inconceivable; your instructions too are inconceivable. Therefore we are not able to ask.

"Blessed One, it is like this. Suppose there is a man who is old and decrepit, worn out, a hundred and twenty-six years of age, stricken by illness, a sick man lying on his bed. Another man places a treasure in his hands and says, 'O man, take this treasure, and when I come back from abroad in ten or twenty years, give it back to me then.' Having entrusted it to him, he departs. The old man has no son. Not long after the man has left, the old man dies. When the man returns, although he has lost a great heap of wealth in this way, there is no one at all to whom he can say anything. In just the same way, Blessed One, whatever we ask apart from matters of moral discipline will not remain long among the śrāvakas, and we have no capacity to ask anything at all."

The Blessed One said to them, "What the monks ask will benefit all sentient beings. That is why I have opened the opportunity to ask questions."

Then the monks said this: "Blessed One, it is like this. Suppose there is a young man, twenty-six years of age, wealthy and possessed of a splendid retinue. Another man places a treasure in his hands and says, 'O man, I am going away on business. When I come back, give this treasure back to me.' That man hides the treasure well and guards it well, and when the other returns he gives it back. In just the same way, Blessed One, if the wealth of the holy Dharma well spoken by the Blessed One were entrusted to all the śrāvakas, such as the monk Ānanda, it would not remain long. Why is that? Because all the śrāvakas, such as Mahākāśyapa, are impermanent. If the wealth asked for by the bodhisattvas is entrusted to the bodhisattvas, it will remain for many hundreds of thousands of years, it will also increase, and it will benefit all bodhisattvas. The bodhisattva mahāsattvas are able to put questions to the Tathāgata, but we, who are like gnats, are not able to put questions to the Tathāgata." Having said this, they sat without saying anything more.

Then the Blessed One said this to those monks: "Excellent, monks, excellent! Monks, you speak thus because you are without defilement. You speak thus as arhats. Excellent! Monks, I too had already thought of the very point you have been thinking. If my Great Vehicle is entrusted into the hands of the bodhisattvas, it will remain for a long time, for two reasons."

Then at that time the Blessed One said to the assembly, "Sons of good family and daughters of good family, long-lived ones, apart from the three bases and apart from the basis of moral discipline, whatever indecision, hesitation and doubt you have, ask me about it." This he said twice and three times.

Then, from within that same assembly, through the power of the Tathāgata, a bodhisattva great being rose from his seat. He had been born in Stalk Village, and he was a son of a great and eminent brahmin family, of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa. He went to where the Blessed One was, bowed down, circumambulated him a hundred thousand times, and made great offerings worthy of the gods. Then he draped his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, and said this to the Blessed One: "If the Blessed One will grant me the opportunity to have my questions answered, I would ask the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, about a few points."

The Blessed One said, "Bodhisattva great being, son of a great and eminent brahmin family, of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa, ask the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, whatever you wish. I shall answer each question you ask, and so I shall gladden your mind."

Then the bodhisattva great being, son of a great and eminent brahmin family, of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, were it not for the power of the Tathāgata and the power of the roots of virtue of all beings, I would not presume to ask. I am like a gnat, while the Tathāgata possesses a great circle like sandalwood. He possesses a great circle like lions. He has a body as unbreakable as beryl and the great vajra. He has an unbreakable retinue. He has a great retinue endowed with an ocean of wisdom. There are also some bodhisattva great beings here who possess immeasurable qualities. If, through the power of the Tathāgata, I were able to put questions to the Tathāgata, who has so immeasurable a retinue, there would be much to do here. Through the power of the Tathāgata, I, who am like a gnat, wish to ask."

Having said this, he spoke in verse:

"O Sage, by what does one gain long life?
By what does one become like a vajra?
Through what does one attain the indestructible?
By what does one gain a firm body and great strength?
By what practice does one come
To reach the far shore of this sūtra?
How does one understand the varied minds
Of living beings, and then teach them?
Great Sage, in what way
Does one gain the vast and become a refuge?
How, though one is not an arhat,
Does one become equal to the arhats?
Blessed One, how is Māra,
The maker of obstacles, drawn into the retinue?
Knowing the words of Māra
And knowing the speech of the perfect Buddha as well,
How does the Guide, with joy,
Also teach the noble path?
Endowed with virtue and with what is right,
How does one teach without distortion?
Great Sage, in what way
Does one become a doer of good? Please teach this.
O Sage, how do bodhisattvas
See the element that is hard to fathom?
O Sage, how is it
That the half letter becomes complete?
How does one come to be like
The conduct of the wild geese and the zalmo birds?
How do Jupiter and Venus
Come to arise in the daytime?
O Sage, how do bodhisattvas,
While the mind is not yet produced,
Come to be fearless here?
How are they like the kata fruit?
How, when they are born in the age of strife,
Do they act without being stained,
And, like a lotus in a realm of faults,
Remain unsullied by faults?
How do bodhisattvas become
Great physicians for the lands?
How do they become ferrymen at every riverbank?
Please explain this to me here.
How do they come to be
Like snakes casting off their old skins?
How are they like a source of many jewels,
And like grain and like trees?
How do those steadfast ones,
Through the awakening of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas,
Display relics?
And how do they teach endless gateways?
How do the steadfast heirs of the Victor, firm in their vows,
Possess an unbreakable retinue?
How do they come to be
Like eyes for the blind of the world?
Great Sage, in what way
Do they display ten million many heads?
How, like the moon in its phases,
Do they teach the Dharma to the worlds?
In what manner, hidden like a rainbow,
Do they display complete nirvāṇa?
How, like gods and like Māra,
Do they become steadfast and heroic?
How, having realized the gateways of the Dharma,
Do they come to know the nature of reality as well?
How do bodhisattvas
Also become free of illness?
For the benefit of all beings,
How do they explain the intended meaning?
How is there virtue with remainder?
How does one come to abandon doubt?
O great intellect, in what way
Does one draw near to supreme awakening?
You who see the subtlest parts
Have spoken of the conduct of bodhisattvas.
The gateways of the many dharmas too,
O Guide, please declare them.
Healer of all beings,
Show the remedy to the two-legged.
Beyond these collections of the Sage
I have no other subject to ask about.
The domain of the steadfast bodhisattvas
Is that of omniscience."

Then the Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva great being, son of a great and eminent brahmin family, of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa: "Excellent, son of noble family, excellent! Son of noble family, it is excellent that you ask about the great secret sūtra of the Tathāgata in the way that omniscient ones question one another. Beyond world systems as many as the sands of countless Ganges Rivers, the omniscient ones who dwell each in his own world system, together with me, at the foot of the tree of awakening, displayed the play of awakening for the sake of awakening. Just as, by awakening alone, they displayed the play of awakening for the purpose of teaching the secret of the Tathāgata, so you too, for the benefit of all beings, are asking about the domain of omniscience, the way of qualities that bear a single name."

Then the one of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, though I do not presume to ask about the domain of omniscience, I do wish to ask a little."

"Blessed One, to give an example: for a gnat to reach the far shore of the sky and the ocean, or to presume to search out the waters of the ocean, is impossible. In the same way, Blessed One, I am like the gnat, while the Blessed One is like the sky and the ocean, endowed with profound wisdom and the Dharma. Blessed One, to give an example: just as a king's crown jewel, being precious, is received upon one's own head, so I too wish to receive rightly the Dharma that you speak. So that my wisdom may grow, I pray the Blessed One to speak. I pray the Blessed One to explain fully what is beyond compare. I wish to ask."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, listen! Listen with a mind that holds on to it, to the karma by which a bodhisattva, a great being, becomes long-lived, and the karma that becomes the cause of awakening. By the fruit of that karma the cause of awakening will arise. Once you have heard it, teach it to others as well. Son of noble family, I too performed that karma and taught it to others, and so I attained unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"Son of noble family, just as a person might enter a king's prison in order to free his only son, so a bodhisattva who wishes to be long-lived should cultivate the perception of all sentient beings as his only child. If he cultivates it in this way, he will give up all harming, and he will come to possess great loving-kindness, great compassion, great joy and great equanimity. He will give up taking life, and he will practice the Dharma. Son of noble family, moreover, he will establish countless sentient beings in the bases of training, and he will establish them in the path of the ten virtuous actions as well. If he is able, he follows all beings in order surely to rescue the sentient beings born in the hells, among the asuras, in the world of Yama, and among the animals, and the sentient beings who commit terrible deeds. Thinking, 'Once I am freed, I shall free others,' he comes to possess a mind that holds to this and keeps it in mindfulness. Through the ripening of these good deeds, and through the result that accords with its cause, bodhisattvas, great beings, become supremely long-lived, become lords of wisdom, and become deathless."

Then the bodhisattva, the great being of Mahākāśyapa's clan, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, with what in mind should a bodhisattva, a great being, cultivate the perception of all sentient beings as his only child? What is the meaning of what you have said? Blessed One, if a bodhisattva, a great being, must cultivate the perception of all sentient beings as his only child, that is a statement I cannot accept. Why is that? Blessed One, what of those sentient beings in this teaching who have corrupt moral discipline, who commit the acts of immediate retribution, and who repudiate the true Dharma? Should one cultivate the perception of an only child even toward sentient beings such as these?"

The Blessed One said, "You of Mahākāśyapa's clan, it is indeed so. I look upon all sentient beings as I do upon Rāhula."

The one of Mahākāśyapa's clan said, "Blessed One, then when the pure assembly of the fully ordained had gathered for the poṣadha of the fifteenth day, and a youth who was untrained, unpurified in body, and not fully ordained hid in a corner of the wall and listened, why did the Blessed One permit Vajrapāṇi, king of the yakṣas, to crush him to powder like fine dust? Alas, that killing by Vajrapāṇi was far too severe! If so, how does the Blessed One regard all sentient beings as like Rāhula?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, do not say that! That youth was an emanation. Vajrapāṇi, king of the yakṣas, merely displayed this in order to subdue and drive out those who repudiate the true Dharma. Whether someone repudiates the true Dharma or is full of greed, it is not right to kill him. I could not bring myself to take the life even of an ant.

"Son of noble family, moreover, I do not say, 'I shall destroy them,' in the way that kings or great ministers destroy those who repudiate the true Dharma; it is simply that the fruit of their own non-Dharma ripens upon them visibly. The all-knowing Tathāgata grants fearlessness for their sake and sends forth one or two rays of light. If those rays do not reach them, they will die, and in that case there is nothing the Tathāgata can do.

"Son of noble family, moreover, if you are able to look into what is hard to examine, I will show you yet another reason. In different lands, moral disciplines, rites and good qualities differ. Suppose that there, after the Tathāgata has passed into nirvāṇa, monks wish to bring forth the true Dharma, and the king, because there is no teacher, harms them. If another king then turns that king back, weakens his power, and that king comes to abide in the Dharma, I declare that the other king gains great merit. Why is that? Because he pacifies the basis of the first king's great wrongdoing.

"Son of noble family, moreover, just as a poisonous tree or a weed that grows in a field or a house must be pulled out by the roots, so I permit those of corrupt moral discipline and those who repudiate the true Dharma to be subdued like servants. Those who do not subdue them are very far from my teaching. Those who do subdue them are my disciples and my śrāvakas."

The one of Mahākāśyapa's clan said, "If the Tathāgata speaks in this way, then he does not regard all sentient beings as he does Rāhula after all, and the name by which he is called 'like toward the axe and the sandalwood' will also be lost. How could it be right to subdue those who repudiate the true Dharma? It is not right for the Tathāgata to permit such a thing."

The Blessed One said, "Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, suppose a householder, a merchant, a chieftain, a minister, or even a king were to place his beloved and cherished sons, who are exceedingly handsome and perfect in appearance, in the hands of a teacher, and were to say to that teacher: 'I entrust these sons of mine to you so that they may be trained in discipline, in ritual, in proper conduct, and in the sciences. You must teach them discipline, ritual, proper conduct, and the sciences. Train them! If three of these four sons of mine should die from beatings, threats, and punishments, while one stays alive and becomes well trained, then it is acceptable even that three die.' If three then died and one became well trained, would the teacher be said to have committed a sinful, nonvirtuous act?"

"No, Blessed One. In that case neither of them would incur a sinful act. And why is that? Because there is no hostile intention there. By acting in that way, much merit will increase."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, in the same way the Tathāgata regards beings who disparage the true Dharma just as he regards Rāhula, and just as sons are placed in the hands of a teacher, he places them in the hands of kings, ministers, and laymen. Then, if those kings, ministers, and laymen punish those heedless, childish ones, would the Tathāgata be said to incur a sinful act?"

"No, Blessed One. If not even the kings, ministers, and laymen incur a sinful act, how could the Tathāgata, who is free of all defilement, be defiled? They punished them unwillingly, in order to safeguard the very qualities and conduct of those beings."

The Blessed One said, "That, then, is cultivating the perception of an only child. Those who cultivate in this way will have cultivated the perception of an only child. Thus a bodhisattva, a great being, should cultivate the perception of an only child toward all sentient beings. If one does so, one will have cultivated meditative absorption toward all sentient beings. Through this one becomes supremely harmless and pure, and through this the bodhisattva becomes long-lived, supremely long-lived, and able to remember former lives."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "If they cultivate the perception of an only child toward all sentient beings, do bodhisattvas, great beings, become long-lived?"

The Blessed One said, "Certainly that is so."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "Blessed One, if I may offer an example: a storyteller, in the midst of an audience of spectators, tells all sorts of tales about the propriety of honoring one's mother. Then he goes home and harms his mother, who toils at the hardest labors, with sticks, broken bricks, clods, and stones. It is just like that. The Blessed One himself has said, 'If they cultivate the perception of an only child toward all sentient beings, bodhisattvas, great beings, become long-lived, supremely long-lived, able to remember former lives, deathless, everlasting, and stable.' Why, then, is the Blessed One himself so short-lived? Was he speaking deceitfully? Or, although the Blessed One regards all sentient beings as he regards Rāhula, does he not truly regard them so? Alas, the Blessed One has endured hardship upon hardship! Reckoned by human measure, he cannot even reach a hundred years. If the Tathāgata is this short-lived, did the Blessed One, long ago when he was a bodhisattva, cut off so many lives? Since his life is this short, the Tathāgata himself must surely have committed exceedingly terrible deeds."

The Blessed One said, "Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, do not lay such a charge against the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha. Do not lay such a charge! The Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is supremely long-lived. The Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is supremely stable, supremely peaceful, supremely everlasting, supremely permanent."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "Blessed One, in what way is the Tathāgata supremely long-lived?"

The Blessed One said, "Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, just as the great rivers, such as the Ganges, the Yamunā, the Sarayū, and the Hiṅgula, together with all the other streams, flow into the great ocean alone, in the same way, kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, every river of life there is in the realms of the gods and upon the earth flows into the ocean of the Tathāgata alone. In this way the Tathāgata is supremely long-lived.

"Furthermore, kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, just as the four great rivers issue from Lake Anavatapta, so too all the multitudes of the long-lived arise from the Tathāgata. Just as, among all permanent things, space is supremely permanent, so the Tathāgata too is permanent, like space. Just as, among all soothing things, ghee alone is supremely soothing, so the Tathāgata, who has become peaceful and cool, teaches a Dharma that is like ghee."

Kāśyapa of the Mahākāśyapa clan said, "Blessed One, if the Tathāgata's life is supremely long, then for the benefit of all sentient beings, may the Blessed One please remain for a long time, for an eon or even more than an eon, and like a great cloud, please let the rain of the sublime Dharma fall in abundance."

The Blessed One said, "Kāśyapa of the Mahākāśyapa clan, do not give rise to the notion that the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, come to an end. Kāśyapa of the Mahākāśyapa clan, here a monk, a nun, a layman or a laywoman, or even someone outside this teaching, who has gained mastery of the five superknowledges, can remain for an eon or more than an eon. Such a one walks in the sky and lies down in it, blazes like fire from the left side of the armpit, and likewise in other ways displays things such as emitting smoke. He also prolongs the span of his life for as long as he wishes. In short, he displays as much as he wishes and as much as pleases him. These are the qualities of one who has gained mastery of the five superknowledges. If even one who has attained the five superknowledges thus has power over the mastery of his own life, how could the Tathāgata, who has attained mastery, not possess such power? Why would the Tathāgata not remain for an eon or more than an eon? Understand that the Tathāgata is permanent, stable, and eternal. Understand, too, that in this world the emanated body is not a body of flesh, and that today the Tathāgata is casting off a body that depends on poisons and medicines. Strive also in the supreme teaching called 'permanent, stable, and eternal.'"

Kāśyapa of the Mahākāśyapa clan said, "If the Tathāgata is permanent, what difference is there between the worldly and the transcendent? Worldly people also say, 'Our Brahmā is permanent; he has existed since the beginning of time.' What difference is there from that? If the Tathāgata is permanent, there would be no difference."

Fascicle four.

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, suppose, for example, that a merchant or a householder had many cows of various coat colors, of many breeds and lineages, in a single herd and a single pen, many of them, as many as ten million. He entrusted those cows to cowherds and ate delicious butter, while the cowherds looked after the pen and churned curd and milk. Later, the owner of the cows came to the end of his life and died. After his death, bandits roaming the forest stole those cows, and because they had no wives, each of them churned the milk himself. Because it was milk churned by them, not even the smell of butter came from that milk. It did not turn into curd, nor into buttermilk, nor into cream, so how could it ever become butter? It even lost the very nature of milk, and they adulterated it with water, all the while thinking, 'I shall enjoy butter! I shall enjoy butter!'

"Worldly people who are rich in wealth and in teachings that have arisen as an outflow of the Buddha's Dharma are just like that. When the buddhas, the blessed ones, like the owner of the cattle pen, have passed into parinirvāṇa, worldly people who are like bandits twist aside moral discipline, ritual, discipline, the spheres of conduct, and the elements of sentient beings. They think, 'There is a sentient being, there is a life, there is a person,' and thinking, 'Liberation is like this, conduct is like this,' they do not know conduct, sentient beings, liberation, permanence, or suchness. Because they strive after other aims, they do not attain liberation, nor do they find moral discipline, ritual, the spheres of conduct, discipline, permanence, or suchness. Like the bandits' butter, worldly people do not attain the suchness of the Dharma. They hold that sentient beings emanated by Brahmā are permanent, and they hold, 'Mine is liberation,' and like the bandits' milk, they do not know suchness.

"Because they practice celibacy, because they abstain from others' wives, and because they serve their fathers and mothers, they obtain and enjoy a mere fraction of the happiness of the higher realms, just as the bandits had a mere remnant of spoiled milk. Yet they do not know what serving father and mother really is. They do not know what going for refuge to the Three really is. They do not know what abstaining from others' wives really is. They do not know what permanence really is. In this case, the emanation of the Buddha is impermanent and false, and so worldly people hold, 'The Tathāgata is impermanent, he is not stable.' Therefore, as the remedy for impermanence, one should strive in the teaching that the Tathāgata is permanent.

"Son of noble family, then a universal monarch who is fortunate enough to possess that cattle pen appears in the world, and through the power of the universal monarch's merit, those bandits do not make even a single cow disappear. Then the bandits roaming the forest abandon that cattle pen and depart. Then the universal monarch's precious householder takes charge of the cattle pen, and that precious householder, by his own intelligence, comes to know the method for the true nature of the milk of that pen, and through the merit of the universal monarch, the cows yield twice as much milk. In just the same way, when the Buddha, the turner of the wheel of Dharma, appears in the world and takes into his care all worldly beings, who are like the cattle pen, the word 'permanent' befits the Tathāgata alone. Worldly people who are like bandits, even though they abide by the name and the marks of the Buddha, do not attain it."

The śrāvakas of the Tathāgata are like a precious householder who tends his herd of cattle: they look after the herd of the sacred Dharma, and they draw the milk of the Dharma for the sake of beings who have merit. Son of good family, for that reason the Tathāgata is permanent, stable, and eternal. Worldly people have no permanent dharma, and I say that the word "permanent" belongs to the tathāgatas, the buddhas, alone.

So sons or daughters of good family should understand it in this way: "The Buddha, the Blessed One, is permanent. The Buddha, the Blessed One, is eternal." Those beings who meditate on the two syllables "permanent" will, before long, attain my state, lacking nothing and exceeding nothing. Son of good family, when meditation on the two syllables "permanent" dies out, at that time the Tathāgata will pass into parinirvāṇa. That is the nature of reality of the buddhas.

Kāśyapa asked, "Blessed One, what is the meaning of what you said, 'the nature of reality of the buddhas'? Blessed One, I wish to understand this discourse, so may the Blessed One please explain this statement in detail."

The Blessed One said, "'The nature of reality' means fully casting off the body. Know that this phrase, 'fully casting off the body,' is an expression of disparagement."

Kāśyapa said, "I ask whether the Tathāgata, having cast off his body, takes on a body again or does not."

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, do not speak like that. This is an inquiry into the nature of reality. Son of good family, consider the beings without perception. If they lack both the aggregate of form and the aggregate of perception, how do those beings abide? How do they exist? How do they appear? How does their perception operate? Such a question is the domain of a buddha. Son of good family, from now on do not ask, 'How are the form, the abode, the deportment, and the happiness of the gods without perception to be taught?' This is not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. It is the sphere of the buddhas.

"Son of good family, in the same way the body of the Tathāgata is the domain of the buddhas alone, and not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Where the Tathāgata abides and where he appears is not your domain either, so you should rest in the thought, 'The Buddha, the Blessed One, is a body whose skillful means are inconceivable.'

"Son of good family, meditate again and again on the perception of permanence with regard to the Dharma and to the Saṅgha of monks as well. Do not make distinctions among the three bases. Do not meditate on them under the aspect of being impermanent, unstable, not eternal, not at peace, or not liberation. I say that the going for refuge of those sons or daughters of good family who do not meditate in that way on the great nature of the three bases is pure. Thus the meditation on permanence is inconceivable.

"Son of good family, where there is a tree there is a shadow, and where there is no tree there is no shadow. In the same way, the Tathāgata is permanent and is a refuge. If he were impermanent, he would not be fit to be a refuge for gods and humans."

Kāśyapa asked, "Blessed One, how is it? In the darkness of night, should one say that the shadow of a tree appears, or that it does not appear?"

The Blessed One said, "Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, why should one say that the shadow of a tree that is there does not exist? Even so, the eye of flesh does not see it. In the same way, the Tathāgata too is permanent, stable, and eternal. Yet although he is eternal, just as the eye of flesh does not see a tree's shadow in the darkness of night, so too, because the Tathāgata enacts his parinirvāṇa, beings imagine that the Tathāgata is impermanent, even though he is permanent, stable, and eternal, just as a tree's shadow cannot be seen in the darkness of night.

"If your parents lack the perception that I, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha are permanent, I say that their going for refuge to the three is not pure. If you teach your two parents that the three bases are permanent, you will gain the name 'a bodhisattva great being, a layman who has gone for refuge to the three.'"

Kāśyapa said, "From this day on, I will declare to my family, down to the seventh generation, and to my two parents, that the Blessed One, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha are permanent. Oh, I will also train in the understanding that the Buddha, the Blessed One, is inconceivable. I will teach it to others as well. And those wicked beings who do not accept it as I teach it, and who speak of impermanence, I will treat like a hailstorm."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent, son of good family! A son of good family who protects the sacred Dharma must act in just this way. You have done well, son of good family. In this way you have entered upon non-harming. Through the great fruit of the karma of non-harming, bodhisattva great beings live long lives and also remember their former lives."

This concludes the first chapter, the Chapter on the Mass of Non-harming.

"Son of good family, now look upon the Tathāgata as a permanent body, an indestructible body, a vajra body, a body not made of flesh, a Dharma body."

Kāśyapa said, "If the Tathāgata passes into parinirvāṇa, I will not see him that way. If he passes into parinirvāṇa, I will see him as a body that can be destroyed, a body of ashes, a body of flesh."

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, do not say 'a brittle body like a human's, a body that can be destroyed.' Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, for many tens of millions of eons the Tathāgata has possessed an indestructible body. It is not a human body, not a divine body, not the body of any form of existence, and not a body of flesh. The body of the Tathāgata is bodiless, unborn and unceasing. It does not exist. It has no entering, no final end and no abiding. It is without consciousness and without activity. It is utterly pure. It is not anything at all, and it is not appropriated. It is without feet and without abode, without change and without experience. It is unmixed, unknowing and unknowable, and it cannot be cognized. It is inconceivable and unthinkable. It is without consciousness and free from mind. It is the very equality of mind, and it goes into the equality of mind. It has no descent, and coming and going are wholly cut off in it. It is uncut and cannot be cut. It gives no opening. It is not a substance and has not arisen. It is without conduct, without conceptual thought and without name. It is not established, not experience, and not manifest. It is without object, without basis, without darkness and without light. It is not peaceful, not utterly peaceful, and free from peace. It is pure and completely pure. It is nothing at all. It is without grasping, without attachment and without afflictions, free from afflictions. It abides by way of not abiding. It cannot be seized. It has no transference and cannot be transferred. It is not a phenomenon, yet it is not that it is not a phenomenon. It is not a field, yet it is not that it has not become a field. It is inexhaustible, it cannot be exhausted, it is free from exhaustion, it has no exhaustion. It is momentary, free from momentariness, and not momentary. It is undisturbed. It has entirely abandoned letters and dots, entirely abandoned sound, entirely abandoned explanation, entirely abandoned meditation, and entirely abandoned thought. It is not conjoined, and not unconjoined. It is measureless, and not measureless. It has no going and no coming. It is nondual. It has no undertaking and no exertion of power. It is without marks and cannot be realized through marks, yet it is adorned with the marks. It is utterly stable and utterly peaceful, free from torment. It does not fall. It cannot be seen. It is fully discerned. It is suchness. It is reality. By way of not liberating, it liberates all sentient beings. By way of not freeing, it frees. By way of not purifying, it purifies. By way of not releasing, it releases. By way of being a nondual teacher, it is the equal teacher. It is equal to the unequalled, equal to the immeasurable, equal to space, equal to the placeless, equal to the unborn, equal to the unobservable; it has become equal to the unequalled. It is peace, utter peace, close peace, without support. It is measureless. It transforms conduct. It has irreversibly cut off all fixed grasping. It has nothing beyond it. It teaches nonduality. By way of not cognizing and by way of not conjoining, it is the essential nature of intrinsic nature. It is not long and not round. It is not divided into aggregates, not divided into elements, and not divided into sense sources. It is without the conceptuality of the conditioned, and without any ground for the conceptuality of the conditioned. It has no increase and no decrease. It is immeasurable, and not immeasurable. It is keen, and not keen. It cannot be worshipped, and it is not that it cannot be worshipped.

"Thus the body of the Tathāgata possesses immeasurable and inconceivable qualities. No one has known it, seen it, or heard it; no one has distinguished it or cognized it. It does not exist. It cannot be expressed in words. It is not included within the world. It has not arisen from a cause, nor has it arisen from no cause. That body cannot in any way be cognized by conceptual or nonconceptual characteristics, by bases of characteristics, by characteristics, by enumeration, or by comparison. It has neither gone into nirvāṇa nor not gone. The Tathāgata possesses immeasurable qualities.

"Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, the body of the Tathāgata is something the Tathāgata himself comes to understand by way of the entities of signs; none of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are able to do so. How could the Tathāgata, who possesses such qualities and is not a body of flesh, fall ill, be harmed, or be destroyed like a clay vessel? Whatever display there is of destruction, of sickness, and of passing into nirvāṇa, it should be seen as being for the sake of those to be trained. Therefore, from this day on, bear in mind that the body of the Tathāgata is indestructible and hard like a vajra. Teach others as well: 'It is not a body of flesh.' Understand that the Tathāgata is the Dharma body."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "Blessed One, that being so, how could there be illness, impermanence or destruction for the Tathāgata, who possesses such qualities? From now on I wish to teach others too that the Tathāgata is the Dharma body, permanent, stable, everlasting and peaceful. How, then, is this body, indestructible like a vajra, accomplished? From what cause is such a body accomplished?"

The Blessed One said, "This body of mine is accomplished through the cause of the merit of protecting the true Dharma. Son of good family, a layman who protects the true Dharma need not take up the five bases of training, nor need he practice the discipline of a layman. To protect the monks who possess moral discipline, proper conduct and good qualities, he should carry bows and arrows, swords, and short broad-bladed spears."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "Those monks who do not sit together with guards, but stay alone on a seat spread at the foot of a tree, are the ones called monks. Those who sit together with guards are the ones called shaven-headed householders."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, do not speak of them as 'shaven-headed householders.' Here, a certain monk stays in a new lodging. After eating his food, he sits alone in a secluded place without speaking. He practices meditative concentration and contemplates the intrinsic nature of the body. To the beings who come to him he gives talks on generosity, talks on moral discipline, talks on merit, and talks on the karmic maturation of merit, and he teaches the Dharma of contentment and few desires. Yet he does not roar the lion's roar, he does not keep a lion-like retinue, and he does not teach in order to subdue those who wear the marks of a monk but are corrupt. Such a monk has not set out for the benefit of all people, nor has he set out for his own benefit. Because his afflictions are weak, he is merely someone who has moral discipline and practices pure conduct.

"Another monk, endowed with moral discipline and possessed of virtuous qualities, goes from one place to another, staying in new lodgings, and roars the lion's roar. For the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings he teaches the nine branches of the scriptures: the sūtras, the sung verses, the expositions, the verses, the inspired utterances, the accounts of what happened thus, the birth stories, the extensive teachings, and the marvelous phenomena. And he teaches: 'This sūtra declares that monks who keep improper possessions, such as male and female slaves, cattle, and buffalo, are to be punished, and this applies to our circumstances. In a certain great sūtra as well, the Blessed One had a certain great king punish renunciants who kept improper possessions, and he had those of corrupt morality wear white clothes.' When he roars the lion's roar in this way, those of corrupt morality may stir up trouble against him and even take his life, yet I call that monk one endowed with moral discipline, one who has set out for his own benefit and for the benefit of others.

"For this reason, in order to protect the holy Dharma, I permit kings, ministers, and laymen to protect the monks, and I also permit them to punish the shaven-headed householders. Among these, it is those of corrupt morality who are called shaven-headed householders; that is not a name for those endowed with moral discipline.

"Son of noble family, long ago in the past, countless eons ago, in this very city of Kuśinagara, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha called Nandivardhana appeared in the world. At that time this world realm was as vast as Sukhāvatī, and the beings of that time, like the bodhisattvas of Sukhāvatī, were never weakened by famine. That Tathāgata lived for many tens of millions of years and then passed into nirvāṇa in this very city and at this very place. After that, his teaching endured for many tens of millions of years. When forty years of the teaching's duration remained, at the time when the holy Dharma was about to decline, there appeared a monk called Buddhadatta, who had a large following, who roared the lion's roar, and who taught the Dharma of the nine branches of the scriptures. Those of corrupt morality, who kept improper possessions such as male and female slaves, cattle, and buffalo, who taught a false Dharma and whose minds were filled with hatred, heard of him and resolved to kill him. The faction of the immoral gathered together, took up all kinds of weapons in their hands, and went to where that monk was.

"At that time I was a king called Bhavadatta. When the king heard the news, he went to where the monk was in order to protect the holy Dharma, and he did battle with them. As a result, they were unable to harm the one who spoke the Dharma. But on the king's body there was not even a spot the size of a mustard seed that had not been pierced by swords, short flat-bladed spears, single-pointed vajras, and arrows.

"When the monk saw the king in that state, he said, 'Excellent! This is how one who protects the holy Dharma must act. You will become a vessel of immeasurable Dharma.' The king heard the monk's words, and after he died he was born in the world realm of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya. All the beings there who had rejoiced in this, and all those who had joined in the battle, also attained awakening. In Abhirati, the world realm of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya, there also appeared one called the Likeness of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya. The monk Buddhadatta also died, after the king's death, and became the foremost śrāvaka in the teaching of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya. The king became his second śrāvaka. Thus, when the holy Dharma is in decline, one should protect the holy Dharma.

"I was the one who was king at that time. That monk was the Buddha Kāśyapa, and he has passed into parinirvāṇa. Thus the fruit of protecting the holy Dharma is immeasurable. By that pure fruit I too became as though adorned with the many patterned eyes of a peacock's feathers, and I came to delight in an indestructible body, a Dharma body."

Kāśyapa, who was of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa, said, "The Tathāgata possesses a permanent body, like letters carved in stone."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, therefore one who wishes to protect the holy Dharma should protect any monk, nun, layman, or laywoman, wherever they may be. The fruit of a layman's protecting the holy Dharma is vast. Son of noble family, therefore the monks should travel with armed laymen escorting them in front. In this Great Vehicle, a layman is not to take up the five bases of training; rather, in order to protect the holy Dharma, he should take weapons in hand and protect the monks.

He asked, "What is the case here? Those monks who keep company and travel together with such laymen carrying weapons in their hands: are they teachers, or are they not teachers? Are they to be called immoral, or are they not?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, after the Tathāgata has passed into complete nirvāṇa, internal strife will arise and lands will be laid waste. At that time, when the age has turned evil, many will go forth and shave their heads because of the calamity of famine. They will drive out and kill monks who are perfect in moral discipline, in rites, in good qualities and in conduct. How then are monks who possess moral discipline to travel through villages, towns and mountain hamlets? In such times, when monks who possess moral discipline and have become teachers travel on the road, I permit those who carry weapons in their hands to go there with them, in order to protect their lives.

"I say that when laymen, householders, kings and ministers bear weapons in order to protect monks who possess moral discipline, that too is moral discipline. Yet they should not kill. Weapons are to be borne in order to ward off harm, and that is a moral discipline of supreme skill.

"A teacher is one who teaches the instructions of the Great Vehicle and the correct view; who has given up improper parasols, oil vessels and the various kinds of fruit; who does not attach himself to kings and ministers; who does not speak in flattering and pleasing words to donors for the sake of gain; who is perfect in deportment; who dispels the ways of unruly ascetics; who possesses moral discipline and wholesome qualities; who holds an intellect like the ocean; who does not desire any other appearance, attire, gain or honor; and who is content and shows that he has few possessions. Such a one is called a teacher. He turns back those of the Saṅgha in his own circle who desire a following, gain and honor.

"The Saṅgha, moreover, is of three kinds: the Saṅgha mixed with the immoral, the Saṅgha of the childish, and the Saṅgha that is so by intrinsic nature.

"Of these, he turns back the Saṅgha mixed with the immoral. The Saṅgha mixed with the immoral means those who simply go along with the immoral, who do not punish them, and who share their conduct with them for the sake of material things. Even though they possess moral discipline, by dwelling together with them they come to be counted among the immoral alone. This is called being mixed with the immoral. Because he turns back their improper ways, he is called a teacher.

"The Saṅgha of the childish means forest dwellers, those of weak merit and the dull. At the times of the invitation ceremony and of the poṣadha, they purify only their own circle and do not purify the immoral, but remain mingled with them. This is called the Saṅgha of the childish. Because he dispels the improper ways of these dull ones, he is called a teacher.

"The Saṅgha that is so by intrinsic nature cannot be destroyed even by millions of māras. It is the Saṅgha of bodhisattvas, pure by intrinsic nature. Those who turn back the two kinds of immoral Saṅgha are called teachers.

"One who is called a holder of the Vinaya knows each of the training rules in accordance with those who are to be disciplined. He knows the great severity, and he knows the lesser severity. He does not uphold as authoritative what is not Vinaya; he upholds the Vinaya.

"If it is asked how he knows what accords with those who are to be disciplined: there are bodhisattvas who go into towns in accordance with those who are to be disciplined. Apart from women, the halls of dancing girls, the houses of widows and the houses of prostitutes, they may go into the interior or the forecourt of any house they wish, and for them there is no fixed time for going there. A bodhisattva monk can bear to wander in towns for many years, but a śrāvaka monk cannot.

"If it is asked how he knows the great severity and the lesser severity: the great severity means that whenever the Tathāgata saw an occasion of misconduct arise, after seeing it he laid down a training rule: 'From now on do not commit such things as these four defeats. If you commit them, you cannot be ordained.' That is called the great severity. The lesser severity means that someone here commits one such offense but has not made that training rule firm; even when the Tathāgata sees this up to three times, he lays down another training rule.
