This is called fully remediable, and it is called the lesser severity.

"Moreover, he upholds the Vinaya concerning offenses with remainder. He does not uphold the Vinaya that arises from the benefits of improper gifts. He trains in the Vinaya. The immoral one has abandoned moral discipline while keeping the robes, and that is a grave matter. He does not uphold that one's Vinaya either; whatever appears in the Vinaya, with that he is supremely content. From the Buddha's scriptures he teaches what is suited to the circumstances, and what is gathered from them I also call Vinaya. Such a one is a holder of the Vinaya. He knows even a single syllable, and whoever knows a single syllable in this way is a holder of the Vinaya in the world. This is called a holder of the Vinaya with respect to the sūtras. In this way the teaching of the Tathāgata is immeasurable. The Tathāgata is inconceivable."

He said, "Blessed One, even to behold the Tathāgata is inconceivable. He is permanent, stable, eternal and at peace; he is inconceivable. I wish to teach this to others as well."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent, you who are of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa! The Tathāgata has entered upon holding a body indestructible like a vajra. So one should hold correct view and the training in reverence."

A bodhisattva great being who rightly sees the Tathāgata as possessing a body that is indestructible like a vajra sees the ultimate body clearly, just as a reflection naturally appears on the surface of a mirror.

This is the second chapter, called "The Mass of the Body Indestructible Like a Vajra."

"Furthermore, son of noble family, now grasp correctly the name of this sūtra, its qualities, and its manner. If beings hear the name of this sūtra, it is impossible that they should go to the four destinies. I shall teach the qualities and the manner of this great sūtra just as many buddhas have taught them."

He asked, "How do bodhisattva great beings correctly hold the name of this sūtra? How are they counted among those who hold the name of this sūtra?"

The Blessed One declared, "This sūtra is called the Great Parinirvāṇa. Its pure conduct is virtuous in the beginning, virtuous in the middle, and virtuous at the end. It is good in meaning and good in wording, unadulterated, perfectly complete, perfectly pure, and perfectly cleansed. It is renowned as a vajra-like treasure and as great.

"Son of noble family, just as the eight rivers, the Ganges, the Yamunā and the rest, flow only into the great ocean, so too victory over all māras, such as the māra whose nature is many millions of afflictions of every kind, will converge in this very Great Parinirvāṇa, this casting off of the body and dying. Therefore it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa.

"Furthermore, just as the sciences, such as the three medical sciences, each converge entirely in their own final tantra, so too all the various secret Dharma doors of intentional speech taught by the Tathāgata converge entirely within the Great Parinirvāṇa alone. Therefore it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa.

"Just as in summer a farmer plows with a great plow and sows seed, wishing for the seed to come to fruition, and once it has come to fruition has no further wish, because that is the culmination of all fruition, so too beings, even after they have meditated on the samādhis of all the sūtras, still wish for the essence of other sūtras; but the essence of this sūtra is the culmination of all fruition, and through it all beings will cross beyond all existences.

"Son of noble family, just as all footprints of whatever kind are included within the footprint of the elephant, and the elephant's footprint is called the best among them, called the supreme, called the foremost, so too, son of noble family, all the samādhis of this sūtra, whatever they may be, are included within the Great Parinirvāṇa alone, and among all the samādhis of this sūtra the great sūtra of Parinirvāṇa is called the best, called the supreme, called the foremost.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, just as sowing crops in autumn is called the best of all plowings, called the supreme, called the foremost, so too plowing with the great plow of permanence of the great sūtra of Parinirvāṇa is called the best of all sūtras, called the supreme, called the foremost. It should be applied likewise to everything; here it is applied to the means of the side of purity. The analysis found in the Saṃyukta sūtras of the Śrāvaka Vehicle should also be applied here.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, just as for one struck by a disease of excess bile, ghee and the medicine of the eight sweets alone are especially beneficial, so too for one seized by the disease of excess bile with regard to the Tathāgata's intentional speech, the sūtras of the Great Vehicle and the medicine of the eight sweets of the Great Nirvāṇa alone are especially beneficial. If one asks what the eight sweets are here, they are permanence, stability, eternity, peace, the nature of coolness, agelessness, deathlessness, stainlessness, and bliss. These are here called the eight sweets. Because it is the great sweet, it is therefore called the Great Parinirvāṇa.

"If bodhisattva great beings abide in that name, those qualities, and that manner, they display nirvāṇa in many forms. Therefore it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa.

"A son or daughter of noble family who wishes to pass into nirvāṇa through this Great Parinirvāṇa should strive at the thought, 'The Tathāgata, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha are permanent.' A son or daughter of noble family who wishes to practice the conduct of the Great Parinirvāṇa should strive at the thought, 'The Tathāgata is permanent.'"

He said, "Oh, Blessed One, the Tathāgata is inconceivable. The teaching of the Dharma is inconceivable. The Saṅgha, endowed with a wealth of qualities, is inconceivable. Likewise this sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa is also inconceivable. Those who act in accordance with its instruction are beings who hold it correctly. They are like stūpas. The others are to be lamented; they dwell in the wilderness of ignorance."

This is the third chapter, called "The Reasons for the Sūtra's Name and the Manner of Its Qualities."

"Son of noble family, here bodhisattva great beings teach four kinds of Dharma categories from the great sūtra of the Great Nirvāṇa. If one asks what the four kinds are, they are what is called one's own superior intention, what is called the superior intention of others, what is called the force of a question, and what is called the force of a circumstance that has arisen concerning a matter. These are the four.

"Among these, what is called one's own superior intention is when the Tathāgata teaches the Dharma on seeing something. For example, on seeing a pile of wood blazing and shining, he said: 'Monks who, on hearing the various secrets from the Tathāgata, tell others, "This was spoken by Māra," and who say of various words of their own invention, "These were spoken by the Tathāgata,"

Having done so, they say that the buddhas, the blessed ones, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha of monks are impermanent. Rather than say that, it would be easier to embrace this blazing pile of wood from every side and take it into one's lap. Those who say, "This was spoken by Māra," and those who hold to that perverse teaching speak falsely. Rather than speak the words "The Tathāgata is impermanent," it would be better to touch this blazing pile of wood all over with one's tongue and die. One should not even listen to such words. Those who are unable to affirm that the Tathāgata is permanent are to be grieved for. The buddhas, the blessed ones, are inconceivable. Likewise, one should hold that their teaching and the Saṅgha of śrāvakas who follow them are also inconceivable. What has been said up to "blazing pile of wood" is the category called "speaking according to one's own intent."

"Speaking according to another's intent" is this. Once, while the Blessed One was dwelling and teaching the Dharma, a certain young woman who had newly given birth came before the Blessed One, sat down to one side, and sat there thinking. Then the Blessed One, knowing her thoughts, taught the Dharma, saying: "It is like a woman who, loving her child and skilled in means, knows how much he is able to digest, and lets her little child lick butter from the tip of her finger."

Then the woman said: "Oh, the Blessed One has understood the thoughts of my mind and has spoken without omission or excess. It is exactly as you have instructed me. Blessed One, today I gave the infant butter from the tip of my finger, but it was too much, and so it has not been digested. What will happen, Blessed One? Will this boy of mine be long-lived, or will he be short-lived? Please, Blessed One, Tathāgata, tell me."

He said: "This son of yours will be happy and long-lived."

Then the woman, delighted, said: "By your truthful words I am greatly delighted. In just this way the Tathāgata looks to the capacities of those to be trained and, matching his teaching to their strength to digest it or not, teaches all phenomena as selfless and as suffering. If the Blessed One were to teach beings the secret of permanence without matching it to their greater or lesser strength, then, like butter that is not digested, beings would fall ill."

He said: "Sister, excellent, excellent! A daughter of good family should act just so. Sister, furthermore, when your son is able to take steps, then whatever rich foods are given to him, he will be able to digest them."

"Blessed One, that is true."

He said: "Will he then be satisfied with his first food?"

She replied: "Blessed One, he will not."

He said: "Sister, in the same way, when my śrāvakas are like a little child who eats only a little, then I teach, like the butter, the meaning of suffering, impermanence, emptiness, and selflessness. When, like your son grown up, they have become great śrāvakas, their strength has grown great, and they desire the Great Vehicle, then I too teach the great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, which is like a great meal of six flavors. What are the six flavors? The sourness of suffering, the saltiness of impermanence, the pungency of selflessness, the sweetness of bliss, the astringency of self, and the bitterness of permanence. Cooked with these six flavors over the firewood of the afflictions and the fire of illusion, this food, the delicious rice of the suchness of the Great Parinirvāṇa, my śrāvakas will eat.

"Sister, furthermore, suppose you were summoned by someone else, or wished to go to another town to tie someone's topknot. Would you leave behind your bad sons, take your good sons, and show them your secret treasures?"

"Blessed One, that is true. To bad sons who lack proper conduct and have no share in the wealth, I would not show the wealth. But to good sons who maintain the household, carry out the household's affairs, and are fit to be shown the goods, I would show it."

He said: "Sister, in the same way, when he is about to go into the Great Parinirvāṇa, the Tathāgata teaches the śrāvakas, without exception, the words of intended meaning concerning the various secrets, and so bestows faith upon his sons. Sister, suppose you went abroad. Would your bad sons think you had died, though you had not died?"

"Blessed One, that is true."

He said: "Then, when you return, they see you. In the same way, do not conceive of me as impermanent. Do not hold, as one would perceive a death, that today the Tathāgata will pass into parinirvāṇa. In the homes of those who always hold that the Tathāgata is permanent, stable, and everlasting, the Tathāgata dwells." This is called "speaking according to another's intent."

"Speaking by force of a question" is this. Here, someone asks the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha: "Blessed One, what should I do so that, without giving anything at all to anyone, I may gain the good report of being renowned in the world as a giver?" The Tathāgata said this to him: "Offer servants and maidservants to one who is free of attachment. Give daughters in marriage to one who always practices celibacy. Make one who does not eat meat eat meat. Make one who does not drink liquor drink liquor. Give food at the wrong time to one who eats at the proper time. To one who does not wear garlands and ornaments, give powders, perfumes, flowers, and incense. Know the right time for them and invite them as guests. If you do this, you will become greatly renowned as the king of donors in this world, but your merit will not increase."

The fifth fascicle.

Then the one of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa said this:

"Since urging people not to eat meat is of such great importance, would it not be wrong to allow those who do not eat meat to eat it?"

The Blessed One declared, "Son of good family, excellent, excellent! You have understood my intention. One who protects the true Dharma must act in just this way. Son of good family, from now on I do not permit my śrāvakas to eat meat. Since it is said that the alms food of the land is like the flesh of one's own son, how could I permit the eating of meat? I have taught that eating meat cuts off great loving-kindness."

He asked, "Blessed One, then why did you permit the eating of meat that is pure in three respects?"

The Blessed One declared, "I laid down this rule of meat pure in three respects so as to restrain the training step by step. Today I set it aside."

He asked, "Blessed One, with what intention did you teach the nine aspects of what is done for one's sake, and the avoidance of the ten kinds of meat?"

The Blessed One declared, "Those statements too were laid down in order to restrain people from eating meat."

He asked, "Blessed One, with what intention did you also speak of meat and fish as excellent food?"

The Blessed One declared, "I did not speak of meat and fish as excellent food. What I spoke of as excellent food is sugarcane, sāli rice, grain, wheat, barley, lentils and peas, brown sugar and white sugar, honey, butter, milk, and oils. I have taught that even the various garments that cover the body should be dyed a drab color. So what need is there to speak of craving the taste of meat?"

He said, "In that case, it would also be fitting to make rules against the five products of the cow, sesame, oil, tree sap, conch shell, silk, and the like."

The Blessed One declared, "Do not hold the view of the Nirgranthas, the naked ascetics. I now lay down a training rule for you with a different notion in mind. I have prohibited even meat of the three kinds. I have also prohibited what you know to have been done for one's sake. I have also rejected meat apart from the ten kinds. I have also rejected the meat of animals that have died.

"Whether one who eats meat is walking or sitting, all living creatures catch his scent and fear him. For example, when a person has eaten asafoetida or garlic, everyone becomes displeased and averse, and in a gathering of many people or in a crowd, those beings know, 'So-and-so has eaten it.' In the same way, all living creatures recognize a person who eats meat. Catching his scent, they are terrified with the fear of death. When he moves about, the beings that live in the water, on dry land and in the sky are frightened, and thinking, 'This one is coming to kill us,' they may even die of panic. For these reasons, bodhisattva great beings do not eat meat. For the sake of those to be trained, they may appear to eat meat, but since they do not even eat ordinary food, what need is there to speak of meat?

"Son of good family, when many hundreds of years have passed after my passing, when stream-enterers, once-returners, non-returners and arhats have ceased to exist, and the true Dharma has utterly perished, there will appear monks who hold the Vinaya and the Mātṛkā, full of various notions, who nourish their own bodies, cherish various kinds of meat, whose elements are disordered, who are tormented by hunger and thirst, who look frightful, who are like cowherds, like fowlers, who bear the mark of dyed robes, who practice the conduct of a cat, who claim, 'I am an arhat,' who are oppressed by many harms, whose bodies are soiled with their own urine and excrement, who feign a fine appearance like a sage, who are not śramaṇas but pretend to be śramaṇas, and who hold counterfeit scriptures that resemble the true Dharma. They will destroy the Vinaya I have laid down, the manner endowed with rite and proper sphere of conduct, the freedom from attachment to what is not allowable, and the words of the reality of liberation. Drawing on the Vinaya and the various sūtras, they will fabricate and speak whatever pleases them, saying, 'In our Vinaya the Blessed One said that the gift of the essence of meat is allowable.' Such śramaṇas, sons of the Śākya, will appear, making things up on their own and speaking in contradiction.

"Moreover, son of good family, there will appear those who accept raw grain that is not allowable, meat and fish, and prepare them as food; who keep vessels of oil, make parasols and make shoes; who attend on kings; who carry divination texts; who are devoted to medical practice; who accept male slaves and female slaves, gold, silver, coral, conch shell, crystal, pearl necklaces and fruits; who learn sciences, painting and sculpting, planting and cultivating, secret mantras, medicines and children's games, the branches of dance, magical illusion, applications for paralyzing, song and dance, garlands, tooth powders, the boiling of great medicinal oils, the wearing of perfumes and ointments, and affected gaits; and who are devoted to the practices of coquetry. Those who have abandoned these I call monks."

He asked, "Blessed One, then what is to be done? When monks, nuns, laymen and laywomen who live on what others give are in a land where food that has not been examined is plentiful, how are they to purify alms that are mixed with meat?"

The Blessed One declared, "Son of good family, I have taught that, so as not to contradict the Vinaya, one should wash it with water and then eat it. In such a land, food and drink that appear to be heavily mixed with meat should be avoided. Where one vessel merely touches another and the food itself is not mixed, there is no fault. Meat, fish, the meat of wild game, dried hooves, and things smeared with other traces of meat all become offenses requiring expiation; so I say. Earlier I taught this on an occasion when a particular purpose arose. Now, here, I have taught the harm that comes from eating meat. This is what I explain at length at the time of my passing into parinirvāṇa, and this is called teaching by force of a question.

Now, what is called teaching by force of circumstance is this. Monks, nuns, laymen or laywomen ask me: "When the Tathāgata first spoke on only a single point, why did the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, not speak these meanings and words to Prasenajit, king of Kosala? The way the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, taught that gateway of the Dharma was not well spoken." I say that if they speak in this way, it is a fault.

If it is asked, "What is meant by 'protection'? What is meant by 'Vinaya'? What is meant by 'prātimokṣa'?", then know that there are many other faults as well; listen to them.

If it is asked what prātimokṣa means: prātimokṣa means contentment, few possessions, and not accepting. Alternatively, it is called prātimokṣa because it heals with regard to both aims. Because it frees from wrong speech, it is liberation.

Protection is so called because it protects from falling into the four destinies through living by all that is improper, and from being cooked in all the fearful places, such as the Avīci hell.

Vinaya is so called because it brings attainment, because it disciplines, and because it teaches the rites. And because there is no deliverance in accepting impure goods, there is neither accepting impure goods nor giving impure goods. That is what is called Vinaya.

"Then why were the precepts of the Vinaya not laid down all at once?" At that time there were some who had committed the offenses of the four defeats. There were some who had committed the offenses of the thirteen saṅghāvaśeṣa. There were some who had committed the offenses of the thirty forfeiture transgressions. There were some who had committed the offenses of the ninety-one simple transgressions. There were some who had committed the offenses of the four to be individually confessed. There were some who had committed offenses against the many rules of training. There were some who had committed offenses against the seven means of settling disputes. There were some who constantly contravened all the trainings. There were some who committed the acts of immediate retribution. There were some who rejected the sūtras. There were some who had completed the characteristics of the causes that culminate in becoming icchantikas.

Here, monks who do evil deeds and have strayed from all dharmas, even when urged by others, are proud, presenting themselves as learned, and do not confess. Although it was taught that they could be restored, there were some who made themselves beyond restoring. Some conceal their faults the way a tortoise hides its body. Since, by keeping their own faults hidden for a long time in this way, further faults arise and evil accumulates, the Tathāgata, being omniscient, questions the evildoers on each occasion. Laying down one precept at the very beginning, he does not lay down all the precepts at once.

Kāśyapa said: "Does the Tathāgata, knowing this, send them to the utterly terrifying place of Avīci? To give an example: some travelers set out on a road without knowing the straight way. There one road goes off elsewhere, and they leave their road and take the wrong one. Though things go badly for them, they do not realize, 'We have taken the wrong road,' but holding the thought, 'This is the good road,' they press on, and they do not even see travelers coming from the other direction. In the same way, for beings deluded about the Dharma, who have not seen the suchness of the holy Dharma, the Blessed One ought to have shown the road at the very beginning. Since he did not first teach the gravity of transgressions, great and small, the monks have fallen into offenses. So may the Blessed One, the true wayfarer who perfectly sees the distinctions of the true path, who perfectly sees the benefits of the distinctive qualities of the path of Dharma, the god of gods, please teach the laying down of the precepts for that purpose."

The Blessed One said: "You who share the clan of Mahākāśyapa, do not speak that way to the Tathāgata. The Tathāgata perfectly sees the ten dharmas of distinctive qualities, and since he regards all sentient beings as he regards Rāhula, how could he make monks go to Avīci? If the Tathāgata saw such a cause in even a single sentient being, he would remain in hell for an eon or even longer in order to deliver that being without fail. How then could one endowed with compassion deceive his own children?

"You who share the clan of Mahākāśyapa, suppose there is a weaver in a town or a mountain village. He discards whatever section of the weave on his loom has gone astray. Or suppose there is a universal monarch. At the very beginning he does not train people in the ten virtuous paths of action all at once. Rather, whatever beings resort to what is contrary to Dharma, he subdues each of them with the precious wheel and establishes them in the holy Dharma. In the same way, the Buddha, the Blessed One, does not at the very beginning lay down the precepts of the prātimokṣa all at once. Rather, taking as occasion each circumstance that arose when some monk committed some offense, he lays down a precept. And when those beings who desire the Dharma see each of these, they gain faith, and these are set up as witnesses.

"Thus, having perfectly seen a matter that is extremely hard to examine, just as is the nature of the weaver and of the universal monarch, so too is the nature of the buddhas who turn the wheel of Dharma. In this way the buddhas, the blessed ones, are inconceivable. The teaching of the Dharma is also inconceivable. Likewise, the listeners and those who have faith are also inconceivable. This is what is called teaching by force of circumstance.

"For the sake of circumstances that arise, and because I am passing into parinirvāṇa, bodhisattva great beings teach the Dharma by these four modes of discourse. It should be understood that the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is what is called teaching by force of circumstance.

"As for what is called my own higher intention: since today I explain the teaching of my higher intention, it should be understood that what is called my own higher intention is also the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra."

As for "the superior intention of others": I understood the thoughts of the monks and taught, "I am permanent." For this reason the superior intention of others, too, should be understood to be the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra.

As for "in response to questioning": on your account, you who are of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa, a bodhisattva great being endowed with supremely subtle wisdom, I have taught words that no śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha has ever heard before: discourses on the single-syllable drop and various secret words of hidden intent. Therefore "in response to questioning," too, should be understood to be the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra.

Some people think, "Put this way it would be deceptive, and put that way it would not be deceptive." For example, a person may call it "space," may call it "emptiness," may call it "nothingness," and may call it "the unobstructed." When he speaks in this way, he does not deceive others. Likewise, if a son or daughter of good family, drawing on this Great Parinirvāṇa, has faith in this permanence taught by the Blessed One and expounds the four kinds of Dharma, they do not deceive others.

When a blazing lump of iron
Is struck, it gradually
Grows cold, yet the fire's heat,
Like something whose whereabouts no one knows,
So too those perfectly liberated,
Who have crossed the mire and river of desire
And attained unshakable bliss:
Where they have gone cannot be designated.

He spoke again: "Son of good family, do not grieve by holding the perception that the tathāgatas become nonexistent. 'Iron' and 'lump of iron' do not differ in meaning. Just as the iron blazing with fire grows cold, so there is no case of the tathāgatas becoming nonexistent. Just as the iron blazing with fire grows cold, the forms of the afflictions are pacified. But if, when the iron blazing with fire grows cold, it became nonexistent along with all its radiating heat, then the Tathāgata too would be impermanent. It is impossible, however, for that iron to become nonexistent along with its glow. Likewise, it is impossible for the Tathāgata, together with this great nirvāṇa, to become impermanent. Just as the fire in the iron grows cold, so the Tathāgata, having exhausted the afflictions accumulated over many countless myriads of eons, becomes permanent, stable, and everlasting. Thus the perfectly liberated Tathāgata is permanent, stable, and everlasting.

"'The mire of desire' means the afflictions. Because he crossed over that mire of afflictions through countless eons, no abode of the Tathāgata can be designated for the Tathāgata who has passed into parinirvāṇa. Therefore:

Those with perfect knowledge and liberation,
Who have crossed the mire and river of desire
And attained unshakable bliss:
Where they have gone cannot be designated."

He asked, "Blessed One, how can this be? Just as iron, even after it has grown cold, becomes hot again afterward from fire, so if the Tathāgata were like that, he would be impermanent."

He said, "The Tathāgata is permanent. Having himself become like fire, he instructs many beings who are like iron through the fire of wisdom, and brings their unreal afflictions to exhaustion."

He said, "Excellent, Blessed One, excellent! Please explain the permanence of the Tathāgata completely."

He said, "Son of good family, here is another example. A universal monarch goes among his circle of queens, and after a moment he comes out into another pleasure grove. He goes there and plays, and when that king has vanished from the circle of queens, one should not give rise to the perception, 'He has died.' Son of good family, in the same way the Tathāgata does not fall away from the nature of reality. As with the king, one should not give rise to the perception that he has passed away, thinking, 'In Jambudvīpa the Tathāgata has become impermanent.' Just as the king goes from the circle of queens to the pleasure grove and plays, so too the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, emerges from the very forms of affliction, which are like the circle of queens. Free of afflictions, the Blessed One goes to a pleasure grove filled with various dhāraṇīs and the various flowers of the limbs of awakening, and there the Tathāgata sports through the various displays of skillful means of his body, which is free of afflictions."

He asked, "I beg you to state the reason for the claim that the Tathāgata definitively crossed the ocean of afflictions over countless myriads of eons. At present, before our eyes, the Tathāgata is shown to have indulged in desires with Rāhula's mother and to have become Rāhula's father. Therefore the Tathāgata has not definitively crossed the river of desire over many myriads of eons."

He said, "Son of good family, do not speak like this: 'At present, before our eyes, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is shown to have indulged in desires with Rāhula's mother and to have become Rāhula's father; therefore the Tathāgata has not definitively crossed the river of desire over many myriads of eons.' The great parinirvāṇa is called the great permanence. Listen without letting your mind wander elsewhere, and once you have heard it, teach it to others. And do not be frightened by it.

"A bodhisattva great being who abides in this great parinirvāṇa places Mount Meru, king of mountains, so lofty and so vast, inside a mustard seed, and yet does no harm to the beings who live on Mount Meru. Those beings do not know or see it, and do not think, 'Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?' Other beings, however, know and see it correctly, thinking, 'This bodhisattva great being has put Mount Meru, king of mountains, inside a mustard seed and hidden it.' Then he sets it back in its own place. Such is the domain of a bodhisattva great being who abides in this great sūtra."

Son of good family, beyond even that, the bodhisattva great being who abides in the great parinirvāṇa places a great trichiliocosm, a world system of a billion worlds, inside a mustard seed, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this." Then he puts it back in its own place.

Son of good family, further, the bodhisattva great being who abides in this great parinirvāṇa places a great trichiliocosm in a single pore of his own body, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this."

Son of good family, further, the bodhisattva great being who abides in this great parinirvāṇa, while remaining in this world system, picks up the world systems of the ten directions on the tip of a needle as one would a juniper leaf, and casts them into another world system, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this." Then he puts them back in their own place.

Son of good family, further, the bodhisattva great being who abides in this great parinirvāṇa takes up the world systems of the ten directions in his right hand and, like a potter's wheel, hurls them far, far beyond as many world systems as there are fine particles of dust, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this." Then he puts them back in their own place.

Son of good family, further, the bodhisattva great being who abides in this great parinirvāṇa places the world systems of the ten directions within his own body, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this." Then he puts them back in their own place.

Son of good family, further, the bodhisattva great being who abides in the great parinirvāṇa places the world systems of the ten directions inside a single atom, and not a single being is harmed. Those beings neither know nor see it, and do not think, "Where have we gone? Where have we been carried? Where have we been put?" Other beings, however, know it correctly and see it correctly, thinking, "This one has done this." Then he puts them back in their own place.

Because the bodhisattva great being who abides in the great parinirvāṇa displays such great miraculous powers as these, in all their various forms, it is called the great nirvāṇa. Since not even all beings together are able to know of the bodhisattva great being who abides in the great parinirvāṇa, "He is like this, he is like that, he has done this, he has done such a thing," then how could you, son of good family, think, "The Tathāgata indulged in sensual pleasures and became the father of Rāhula"?

Son of good family, further, I, now abiding in the great parinirvāṇa, shall display a great miracle in the great trichiliocosm, in those world systems where there are a billion suns and moons and a billion Jambudvīpas. There, the great trichiliocosm should be set out in full in the manner of the Śūraṅgama Samādhi. In the great trichiliocosm, in some Jambudvīpas I appear as if passing into the great parinirvāṇa, yet I do not pass into final nirvāṇa forever in the great parinirvāṇa.

In some Jambudvīpas I appear to be conceived in the womb at the time of the parents' fertile season, and those two are fully convinced, thinking, "He will be our son." Yet for countless tens of millions of eons I have never once been born of sexual union. Because I have transcended a body born of sexual union, I am not a body of flesh; I am the Dharma body. This entering of a womb through sexual union is nothing more than a means of conforming to the world.

Son of good family, in some Jambudvīpas I also appear to be born as a child into the lap of a mother giving birth, and, like a child born in a pleasure grove, I stand up at once, face north, take seven steps, and say, "I shall become unsurpassed in the world with its gods, humans and asuras." Beings are fully convinced, thinking, "Never before have we seen a child take seven steps the moment it was born. Oh, our son is a son of the gods!" Many other beings also take it as a wonder. Yet for countless tens of millions of eons I have turned away from the state of a child, and so have never once been a child. I am the Dharma body, not a body of bones, sinews, flesh and blood, and this showing of myself becoming a child is nothing more than conforming to the world. Rising from the north, I take seven steps toward the southern quarter and say, "I shall become a field of merit for the whole world."

Taking seven steps toward the west, I declare, "Having abandoned birth, aging, sickness and death, I shall bring this to my last body." Facing east and taking seven steps, I declare, "I shall go at the head of all sentient beings." Taking seven steps in each of the intermediate directions, I declare, "Having vanquished the māra whose nature is the many kinds of afflictions, I shall become an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha." Taking seven steps toward the zenith, I declare, "Swiftly, like space, I shall keep my body unstained by any improper phenomenon." Taking seven steps toward the nadir, I declare, "To put out the hell fires of all the hell beings, I shall send down the rain of Dharma like a great cloud and bring them happiness. For those of corrupt moral discipline I shall be like hail."

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to wear a topknot, and sentient beings take it to be a child's topknot. Yet in the world with its gods there is no one at all who can take hold of my head and cut my hair. Since countless eons ago I have already brought the wearing of a topknot to its end, so it is impossible for a razor to cut my hair. This is merely conforming to the world, for a topknot is something worn only by children.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I also appear to go to the temple of Maheśvara (Śiva). There, when Maheśvara, Brahmā and the world guardians see me, they bow at my feet and remain with their palms joined. Since countless tens of millions of eons ago I have been the god above the gods of gods, humans and asuras, so I never go to the temple of Maheśvara. Appearing to go there is merely an act conforming to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to have my ears pierced, yet there is no one who can pierce my ears.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to wear earrings and bracelets bearing lions, and so sentient beings think, "This child is wearing earrings and bracelets with lions." Yet since countless tens of millions of eons ago I have turned away from childish things, and no earrings or bracelets with lions have been put on me. Appearing to wear them is merely conforming to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to go to the school where letters are learned, yet in the three worlds I have seen no one fit to be my teacher. I myself am the teacher of all sentient beings. Therefore my name is the Omniscient One. Countless tens of millions of eons ago I passed beyond staying in the school of letters and attained unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening. Nevertheless, sentient beings think, "This boy has gone to school." Understand that this is merely conforming to the world!

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I display elephants, horses, chariots, foot soldiers, all kinds of jewels, treasuries, and sovereign power over a kingdom, and I also appear to act as crown prince. Although since countless tens of millions of eons ago I have abandoned the enjoyment of sense pleasures, sentient beings think, "The crown prince is enjoying sense pleasures."

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to have become a wheel-turning king. Yet countless many tens of millions of eons ago I abandoned that and turned the wheel of Dharma. I cast it away like the flesh of my own son and gave up clinging to the enjoyments of kingship. Nevertheless, sentient beings think, "He is a wheel-turning king." This is merely conforming to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I appear to grow weary of the world on seeing an old man, a sick man and a dead man, to abandon all pleasures, which are like a theatrical show, and to go forth. Sentient beings think, "Alas, the young prince has gone forth!" Although I completed the work of going forth countless tens of millions of eons ago, appearing to go forth out of weariness is merely conforming to the world. My appearing to receive full ordination is likewise in order to truly liberate the world.

I also display stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning, arhatship and so forth, and the successive meditative absorptions, and sentient beings think, "Alas, he has very swiftly become an unsurpassed arhat!" Although I completed the work of an arhat countless tens of millions of eons ago, my appearing to sit on spread grass at the foot of the tree of awakening and to subdue Māra is in order to truly liberate the world. Sentient beings think, "Having defeated Māra, he has attained unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening." Yet countless tens of millions of eons ago I defeated the afflictions in all their forms, the divine māra, and the māra of intrinsic nature, and realized the supreme nectar.

I display the acts of defecating, urinating, breathing in and breathing out, yet in my body there is no circulation, no corresponding process, and no ripening. Sentient beings think, "He is like an ordinary human." This is merely conforming to the world. Sentient beings think that I eat food, yet my body is never weakened by hunger. This is merely conforming to the world.

Countless tens of millions of eons ago I accomplished the Perfection of Wisdom and fully abandoned craving, and yet I appear to lie down and sleep. Nevertheless my body has no sleep, no hiccups, no yawning, no aching back and no headache. Lying down to sleep is merely conforming to the world.

I appear to stand, to walk, to blink, to stretch my legs, and to engage in the bodily postures, yet my body has no such afflictions. I appear to wash my feet, to anoint myself with oil, to use water, to chew a tooth-stick, and to apply eye salve, yet my body has no such afflictions. Feet the color of lotus petals, the fragrance of the utpala lotus coming from the mouth, and eyes of incomparable beauty: these too are merely to conform to the world. Sentient beings take it that everything is just as it is with humans.

I appear to pick up rags from the rubbish heap off the ground and wash them, yet I have no need of that. Sentient beings take me to be a human being. Showing myself as the father of Rāhula, and showing Śuddhodana and Māyā as my father and mother, are also for the sake of the world. It is said, "Having experienced the pleasures of the world, he renounced them and went forth into the forest; the prince called Gautama, having distinguished between the desirable and the undesirable, between pleasure and what is not pleasure, seeks the bliss of nirvāṇa." This too is in order to deliver childish beings.

The Tathāgata is not born of sexual union. Showing that he became the father of Rāhula, and showing his own father and mother, are merely to conform to the world. Gods, humans, and asuras take him to be a human tathāgata.

Son of good family, in some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself again and again as if passing into complete nirvāṇa, yet, since my passing into complete nirvāṇa is permanent, I do not pass into complete nirvāṇa. At times sentient beings take it that the Tathāgata has died, but the Tathāgata is at all times permanent, stable, and everlasting. Showing the passing into complete nirvāṇa is the nature of reality of the buddhas.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself taking birth, and sentient beings take it that I have been born. Although I completed what was to be done countless tens of millions of eons ago, there too I show myself reaching the culmination, attaining the goal of every stage. This is merely to conform to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself as one of corrupt moral discipline who has incurred a defeat, and sentient beings think, "Alas, he has incurred a defeat!" Yet I have never at any time been negligent.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself drawing the blood of the Tathāgata with evil intent, killing an arhat, killing father and mother, and myself causing a schism in the Saṅgha, and sentient beings think, "Alas, this being commits the five acts of immediate retribution!" Yet I do not commit such acts, and in the world with its gods no one at all is able to split the Saṅgha.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself rejecting the true Dharma and as an icchantika, and sentient beings think, "Alas, this being rejects the true Dharma and is an icchantika!" Showing myself as an icchantika is for the sake of future sentient beings. Worldly people are always icchantikas, but a buddha is not an icchantika.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself upholding the true Dharma, and sentient beings think, "Ah, he takes the side of the true Dharma!" This is the intrinsic nature of the buddhas; since it is the nature of reality, there is nothing very wondrous in it.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself as if I were a māra creating obstacles, and sentient beings think, "Alas, it is Māra the Evil One!" Yet just as a lotus is not sullied by water, I am not sullied by the faults of a māra.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself attaining awakening in the form of a woman, and doubting sentient beings think, "A woman attains awakening." Though the Tathāgata is always male, in order to tame sentient beings he shows himself as both. In the same way he shows himself entering upon many forms as skillful means, and all of these too are merely to conform to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show myself taking birth in the four destinies, yet I do not perform the karma for that. Entering birth as an animal is also merely to conform to the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I show the form of Brahmā, and having shown myself obstructing the sentient beings who have faith in Brahmā, I establish them in the true Dharma. Yet I do not perform his karma, and sentient beings take me to be the lord Brahmā. In the same way, in the abodes of all the gods, I take on their forms and obstruct, and so establish all sentient beings in the true Dharma, and sentient beings take me to be the lord of the abodes of all the gods.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to deliver prostitutes, I show myself going to the houses of prostitutes, yet no fault of desire arises in me. Like a lotus in the water, I am not sullied by the faults of desires. In order to deliver sentient beings attached to desire from the river of desire, I appear at every crossroads and junction, yet I do not perform that karma, and sentient beings think, "This is a wanderer."

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to lead common women to the true Dharma, I go to places of music and entertainment, yet I do not perform that act.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to deliver children, I go to the schools where writing is learned, act as schoolmaster, and teach the children.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to deliver those beings, I drink liquor, play at dice, and delight in distractions. Although I do these grave acts, I do not commit that grave act, while sentient beings think, "This one does that act."

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to deliver vultures and crows, I go to the charnel grounds, yet no perception of looking upon them enters me there.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to establish the true Dharma, I also show myself as a householder.

In order to protect the upholders of the sacred Dharma and the world guardians, I am honored as a minister among ministers. In order to teach the sacred Dharma correctly to the young, I am honored as a youth among youths. In order to teach correctly the royal ways of the king of the sacred Dharma, I am honored as a king among kings.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, during an intermediate eon of disease, I give medicine to beings and so lead them to the sacred Dharma, and beings perceive it as an intermediate eon of disease. In some parts of Jambudvīpa, during an intermediate eon of famine, I satisfy hungry and weakened beings with food of ambrosia and so lead them to the sacred Dharma, and beings perceive it as an intermediate eon of famine. In some parts of Jambudvīpa I display an intermediate eon of weapons, and when beings gather and the time comes for them to kill one another, I lead them to the sacred Dharma, and beings perceive it as an intermediate eon of weapons. In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to teach the perception of impermanence to beings who hold the perception of permanence, I display an eon of burning, and beings perceive it as an eon of burning.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I teach speech and language to beings, and, drawing them on by means of speech and language, I bring them to certain liberation. In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to liberate beings with certainty, I transform myself into the four elements, and in order to remove the diseases of beings, I transform myself into every medicine and every tree.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa I also appear to go forth among all the wonder-working sects, and everywhere I serve as preceptor and am honored. Although the Tathāgata has, from countless tens of millions of eons ago, already reached the final end of the true meaning of going forth, he appears to go forth among all the wonder-working sects, in order to liberate them with certainty.

Although the Tathāgata always abides in the body of his intrinsic nature, in order to liberate all beings with certainty he also serves as master in all the arts and crafts, and displays all vidyā-mantras, secret mantras and magical emanations. Having crushed the arrogance and pride of all the wonder-working sects, he shows them correctly that they are without core, like cotton, and he leads them to the sacred Dharma. This is acting in conformity with the world. There, beings who hold the perceptions of views perceive, "This being is a worldly one," but the Tathāgata is not worldly; he is one who sees the Dharma that transcends the world.

In some parts of Jambudvīpa, in order to liberate with certainty by acting in conformity with the world, I also appear as a male or female servant, and beings perceive, "This is a male servant, this is a female servant." That is appearing in a low caste. In some parts of Jambudvīpa I have displayed all the activities of the world, and beings perceive, "This being is devoted to worldly affairs." Yet although he displays this, the Tathāgata does not perform those deeds; acting in conformity with the whole world is for the sake of liberating the world.

In the same way, in Uttarakuru, in Pūrvavideha in the east, in Aparagodānīya in the west, and in the twenty-five realms of existence as well, one should apply each of these in the same manner, by way of acting in conformity with the worldly; and the account of the great trichiliocosm too should be applied according to the manner of the Śūraṃgama samādhi.

Because the Tathāgata displays all great deeds, this is called the Great Parinirvāṇa. The bodhisattva great being who abides in it establishes beings in all great deeds, in great miracles and in fearlessness, and therefore he is called the great permanent one. One should not ask how he does it.

It should be understood that the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas act in conformity with the world, as in taking up the skillful means of appearing as the father of Rāhula. Because they crossed the river of desire many tens of millions of eons ago, the Tathāgata is therefore permanent, stable and everlasting.

He asked: "Here you have said that, just as no place can be designated where a lamp that has gone out has died, so too the state of the Tathāgata's nirvāṇa is like that. If that is what you teach, how is the Tathāgata permanent, stable and everlasting?"

The Blessed One said: "Son of good family, I have not said that nirvāṇa is like the state of a lamp. Son of good family, suppose some man or woman fills a vessel, whether large or small, with butter or sesame oil and lights a lamp. The flame of that lamp burns as long as there is sesame oil, and when the sesame oil is used up, the lamp goes out. In that example, the exhaustion of the afflictions is taught as resembling the going out of the lamp, while the Tathāgata is permanent, like the vessel. If the exhaustion of the afflictions is like the lamp and the sesame oil, does the vessel also cease to exist at the same moment as the lamp?"

He replied: "Blessed One, it does not. The vessel does not cease to exist. But then, is the Tathāgata also to be likened to the vessel? If the Tathāgata is like the vessel of sesame oil, would it not follow that you have declared that the Tathāgata too will perish?"

The Blessed One said: "The Tathāgata is not like the vessel. Whatever is supremely permanent, that is the Tathāgata; therefore nirvāṇa is forever supreme bliss. Furthermore, son of good family, what resembles the state of the lamp is the arhat. Therefore the attainment of arhatship, the state of nirvāṇa, is called the supreme fruit. All arhats are free of attachment to material things: they are without material things, they are final like the state of the lamp, and they delight in being without material things. The non-returner too I have taught, with words of hidden intent, to mean being without material things; but that is not the state of nirvāṇa."

He asked: "What is the meaning of what you have called the non-returner?"

The Blessed One said: "Whoever does not take up renewed existence is called a non-returner."

He asked: "Blessed One, it is called the non-returner. Yet if even the one who is a non-returner were still to take up renewed existence, why is it that the non-returner does not again take birth in this body, which is impure by nature and filled with a host of worms?"

The Blessed One said: "He does not take up that body, but he takes up a body as one takes a poison used as medicine. Therefore he is called a non-returner. The word 'non-returner' is a term for the body. The word 'returner' is a term for going back and forth."

He asked: "Blessed One, how is this? Is there something secret in the teaching of the Blessed One? Please do not keep it secret by saying, 'words of hidden intent, words of hidden intent.' Blessed One, how is this? Is the speech of the Buddha like a conjurer's mechanical device, or like the illusory woman of a mechanical device made by a magician?"

The Blessed One said: "There is nothing secret in my teaching. The speech of the Buddha is like the full moon of autumn: it is flawless, not sealed with signs, free of confusion, free of what is secret and of what is a great secret, and it is clear.

The sixth section.

"Son of good family, suppose there is a householder with servants and wives, who possesses ten million in wealth and has an only son who is dear, precious, cherished and pleasing. He calls the son back from the boy's teacher and, out of love for the son, diligently teaches him the half-letters himself. Even though the son has diligently learned the half-letters, can he explain the distinctions by way of the meaning of grammar? And even if he could explain them, would he come to know the meaning of grammar from having learned the half-letters? Does the householder keep secrets from his own son?"

He replied: "Blessed One, that is not so. Because of the son's skill in understanding, because his mind is sharp or dull as it is, and because he lacks skill in understanding, it comes to look as if something were kept secret from him. But whatever unhappiness, deceit and stinginess there might be toward him, that should be understood as secrecy. Whatever is endowed with the perception of an only son, out of faith and joy, is not called secrecy."

The Blessed One said: "Excellent, excellent, son of good family! Son of good family, you have done well. Whatever is endowed with hatred, deceit and stinginess is secrecy. Because of that, and out of love, that householder does not teach it. Just so, like that householder, I possess the cultivated perception of all sentient beings as an only son, yet because those childish ones cannot bear it, I do not teach them the words of my intent. Like the half-letters, I teach the nine-limbed scripture. Like grammar, I have taught the ten powers, the four noble truths, the noble eightfold path, and the grammar of the most extensive Great Vehicle.

"Further, son of good family, when the son has learned the half-letters and become fit for the science of language, if at that time the householder does not teach him the meaning of grammar, then, because he is being stingy, that is called secrecy. If he teaches him the meaning of grammar, the child will come to master grammar completely. Just so, as long as the śrāvakas have not become vessels for the Great Vehicle, I teach them the nine-limbed scripture. If I were to keep the training of the Great Vehicle secret from sentient beings who have become vessels, then it would be fitting to say of me, 'He has kept it secret, as with grammar.' Now I teach, as one teaches grammar, that the Tathāgata is permanent and stable, and like the householder, the Tathāgata brings his own children to completion. In this way, just as one brings a son to completion in all the sciences, so he brings them to completion.

"Further, son of good family, suppose the great clouds of summer roar with rumbling thunder and pour down great rain, and yet the seeds of some beings do not sprout. Is that the fault of the great clouds? Do the great clouds not rain? Just so, although today I teach the Great Parinirvāṇa, the great secret, without leaving anything out, those sentient beings who have not become vessels do not hear it."

And he said further:

"Those who have no full accumulation,
Who give no thought to food,
Like the birds in the sky,
Their tracks are hard to follow."

If one asks what the meaning of this teaching is:

"Who are those that have no full accumulation?
Who are those that give no thought to what they eat?
Whose tracks are hard to follow?
When one has gone, and gone to where?
Their tracks are hard to follow."

"Here, jewels and the like are the accumulation. The word 'full' is a term for a particular kind of particle. The word 'full' means 'gathered together,' and here it is a term for having acquired fine goods. 'Accumulation' and 'wealth' have one and the same meaning. Whoever fully accumulates wealth, that is called their full accumulation. Full accumulation is of two kinds: conditioned full accumulation and unconditioned full accumulation. The śrāvaka is of the type of conditioned full accumulation. The Tathāgata is of the type of unconditioned full accumulation.

"The Saṅgha is of two kinds: the conditioned Saṅgha and the unconditioned Saṅgha. The conditioned Saṅgha is the Saṅgha of śrāvakas. Since the Saṅgha of śrāvakas has no full accumulation at all, how much less could it hold on to bad goods such as male and female servants, or to treasuries and storehouses? Because it is not proper, it does not accumulate even so much as salt or a sesame seed. As for those who say, 'The Blessed One has permitted us to keep male and female servants and the like,' their tongues may well be struck dumb. Those śrāvakas are of the type that has no full accumulation; they are those who fully know food. Those who have no attachment to food are those who fully know food. Their tracks on the path of destinies are hard to follow. They very swiftly come to abide close to buddhahood."

"Their path is hard to trace," said the Tathāgata.

He spoke again: "If even in the conditioned Saṅgha there is no hoarding, how could there be any in the unconditioned Saṅgha? The unconditioned Saṅgha is the Tathāgata, so how could the Tathāgata teach a secret Dharma? Whatever is kept secret is hoarding. What cannot be traced is unmoving bliss; this is the meaning of nirvāṇa. In it there is not even space. It is not made by years, days and months. In it there are no comets, no hosts of stars, no mountains, no seasons and no clouds. It is the cutting off of the stream of birth. It is the abandoning of death, sickness, old age and the twenty-five states of existence. It is freedom from every form of suffering and from the afflictions. The Tathāgata, permanent, stable, blissful, unmoving and without abode, is parinirvāṇa. In order to go there, today the Tathāgata goes to the great city.

"'Great' and 'greatness' have one and the same meaning. 'Great' is a term for what surpasses others. Here, if a certain person comes to have an immeasurable lifespan, that person is counted as a great man, and if he also possesses the Dharma, that person is the highest of all men. Again, here a person becomes unique through the eight realizations of a great man. Among many great men, the one who has the eight realizations of a great man is the great man even among all great men. Because I have explained it in this way, it is called greatness.

"As for 'nirvāṇa with remainder,' that which heals is called nirvāṇa. Why is that? Son of good family, suppose a certain man is struck by a poisoned arrow and wounded by a falling stone. A great physician who draws out arrows treats that wounded man. He removes the poisoned arrow, makes him well and restores him unharmed, as he was before. Then the physician leaves the man and goes to stay in another land, another town or another house, wherever there are wounded people and wounds to be treated. In the same way the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha, the great physician who draws out arrows, heals the arrows of all the sentient beings of Jambudvīpa who for countless tens of millions of eons have been badly wounded, struck by the poison of the afflictions, attachment, aversion, delusion and the rest. He heals them with the medicines of the roots of virtue found in the various collections of the Great Vehicle sūtras. And having healed them, he turns them back from sorrow, and wherever there are wounded beings, there the Blessed One truly appears. Therefore the Great Nirvāṇa rests on the true meaning.

"'The Great Parinirvāṇa' is a name for the city of liberation in all existences. That he appears as a perfectly awakened buddha wherever there are those to be trained is the well-known sense of the word; it is the evident sense. The true meaning is what is called the Great Parinirvāṇa."

Kāśyapa asked, "Blessed One, what is this? Is it to be said that the physician, having healed all who are wounded, appears in whatever other land, city, district, royal palace or surrounding area there are wounded people?"

He replied, "Son of good family, do not say that he heals the sickness of every wounded creature. He does not heal other beings who cannot be healed. Why is that? Because their sickness is incurable. In the same way the Tathāgata heals every sickness except the incurable sickness of those of great desire, the icchantikas. Those who have passed into nirvāṇa are the ones who have been healed."

Kāśyapa asked, "What is liberation like? Does it have form, or is it without form?"

He replied, "One kind has form, and one kind is without form. That without form belongs to the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. That of the buddhas, the blessed ones, has form. That liberation is both form and formless, yet it cannot even be called form."

Kāśyapa asked, "Why is that?"

He replied, "Kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, it is like the bodies of the gods of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception: they are both form and formless, yet they cannot even be called form."

Kāśyapa asked, "If the gods of neither perception nor non-perception are both form and formless, and yet are not form, please explain how they exist and how they move about."

He replied, "This is the domain of the buddhas, not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, so leave it aside. In the same way, liberation too is both form and formless, yet it is not form; therefore it is the domain of the buddhas, not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas."

Kāśyapa said, "Blessed One, may the Tathāgata explain liberation by way of the Great Parinirvāṇa."

He replied, "Excellent, son of good family, excellent! What is called liberation is freedom from all bonds."

He spoke again: "Because what is called liberation is unborn, it is utterly free from all bonds. It is not born in the way a child is born from the coming together of father and mother. Liberation abides in its own domain. 'Unborn' means this: just as the essence of butter remains by nature in its own essential nature, so the Tathāgata did not arise from the union of father and mother, and yet, for the sake of those to be trained, the Tathāgata displayed both a father and a mother. Liberation is the Tathāgata, for it is the arising of what has not arisen. Suppose seeds of beans or peas are sown in the first month of spring, in the summer season, or in the last month of autumn; sprouts will come up from them. Liberation is not like that. What has not arisen is liberation. Liberation is the Tathāgata, the unarisen. The Tathāgata is simply unarisen, and the Tathāgata should not be thought of as a sprout that comes into being and then ceases to be."

The Tathāgata is by his very nature unarisen; therefore he is "unarisen." "Unarisen" means unmade. As for "unmade": people make cities, the spires of monastic dwellings, and gatehouses, but liberation is not like that. Whatever is unmade is liberation, and that is the Tathāgata.

"Unconditioned, unconditioned" means this. A potter makes a vessel, and afterward it comes to be destroyed, but liberation is not like that. Liberation has no birth and no cessation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata, and he has neither birth nor death. That which is unborn, which does not perish, which is without aging, without death, and unconditioned, is the Tathāgata. Therefore the Tathāgata goes to the Great Parinirvāṇa.

"Without aging and without death" means this. White hair, aging, sickness, the condition of death, and such notions of a conditioned nature are not liberation. That in which none of the things just mentioned exists is liberation, and therefore it is called "without aging." Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. In the Tathāgata there is no white hair, no aging, and no conditioned change. Therefore, since the Tathāgata has a body without aging, how could one regard him as dying?

"Without sickness, without sickness" means this. Its nature is such that there is no body in it that is afflicted by the four hundred and four adventitious diseases and burdened with many faults. Therefore liberation is without sickness. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. Since there is not even a trace of sickness in the Tathāgata, how could there be sickness in the Dharma body? Therefore the Tathāgata is without sickness.

"Deathless, deathless" means that in which there is no death, as there is for a human body. That is liberation. For it there is not even the expression, "After this much time it will come to the end of exhaustion," or, "It will come to the end of death." Freedom from death is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. It is impossible that one endowed with such qualities should be said to die. How could a body as hard as a vajra be impermanent? Therefore the deathless is the Tathāgata.

As for "afflicted, afflicted": liberation is not spoiled by foul, impure slime. Liberation is clear, like a white kumuda flower. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. His teaching of the various faults of desire should be seen as skillful means. Therefore the Tathāgata is not afflicted.

"Undefiled, undefiled" means "without a wound," and that is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata; therefore it is called undefiled.

"Without harm, without harm" means this. A hungry person who sees no one preparing food is harmed, but liberation is not like that. Whatever is without harm is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Peace, peace" means this. Worldly people call the image of a god "peaceful, peaceful," but that is false; it is not liberation. Whatever is peaceful by nature is liberation. Because it is the supreme peace of all sentient beings, it is called peace. Whatever is peace is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Bliss, bliss" means this. One who is afraid of bandits travels the road in safety, but liberation is not like that. In that which is blissful by its very nature there is not even the name of fear. Therefore uncontrived bliss is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. The one who brings bliss to all sentient beings is the Tathāgata.

As for "without rival, without rival," "without rival" means this. A king is suspicious and afraid for his life, but liberation is not like that; liberation is by its very nature, from the beginning, without rival. "Without rival" is like a universal monarch: there is no one else at all who could make him afraid; it is impossible. Likewise, for liberation there is no other phenomenon that could become its match; it is impossible. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. When the Tathāgata turns the wheel of Dharma, there is no other sound. Therefore the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha, is without rival. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Sorrowless, sorrowless" means that in which there is no sorrow. When a king's kingdom is seized by another, troublemaking king, and his realm is conquered and laid waste, he is struck by great sorrow; but nirvāṇa is not like that. Just as a king who cannot be overcome by others, having become free of enemies, is without sorrow, so too those who have attained liberation are without sorrow. Whatever is liberation is nirvāṇa. Whatever is nirvāṇa is the Tathāgata.

"Free from sorrow, free from sorrow" means this. A woman hears that her son has gone to war and been killed, and when she sees him come back she becomes free from sorrow; but nirvāṇa is not like that. Liberation is always free from sorrow. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

As for "dustless, dustless," "dust" means earthen dust and mud. In springtime, the wind rising in the afternoon stirs up the dust, but liberation is not like that. "Dustless" means this: just as a king's crown jewel is pure, so that which is by nature dustless is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Free from dust, free from dust" means this. Gold that has been thoroughly refined, free of sand and stone, has become by nature a precious substance; likewise, the nature that is purified of faults is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Not fragile, not fragile" means this. An earthenware pot of curd cracks, but liberation is not like that. Like a vessel of iron or of vajra, what is not fragile is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Having the nature of not being hollow, having the nature of not being hollow" means this. When the fruit of the castor plant is roasted and spread out on a rooftop in spring, it crackles all over and splits all over. Liberation is not like that. "Having the nature of not being hollow" means this. A pot of iron and vajra cannot be pierced even when a thousand charioteers shoot arrows at it. In the same way, that which has the nature of not being hollow is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Owing nothing, owing nothing" means this. A poor man runs into debt and falls into the hands of a rich man. Although he is tormented, he has nothing at all to repay with, and so he is let go. Liberation is not like that. "Owing nothing" means this. A man who possesses ten million in wealth is in debt to no one. In the same way, the supreme one who possesses many tens of millions of dharmas is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Having the nature of owing nothing, having the nature of owing nothing" means this. A poor man has no wealth anywhere in his own house, and the thought "I shall take out a loan" weighs on his mind. Liberation is not like that. "Having the nature of owing nothing" means this. A householder who possesses ten million in wealth does not depend even on the sun and the moon. Such a prosperous householder does not even hear the words "The sun is blazing" or "The moon has risen." Such a supremely happy householder, born into the lineage of a wheel-turning king, does not take loans from others to provide for later times. Nor do others say of him, "This man takes loans from others and lives on them." That is impossible. In the same way, it is impossible that liberation would take up some other abode of liberation. Therefore that which has the nature of owing nothing is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Harmless, harmless" means this. By indulging in what is too hot in spring, in what is too sweet in summer, and in what is too cold in winter, one is harmed by these faults, and harms arise from them. Liberation is not harmed by many illnesses in that way. Further, as for "harm": drinking milk after eating meat and fish leads to death. Liberation does not have a harmful nature. "Harmless" means this. By eating suitable food one tastes delicious flavors, and it is as though one has eaten the nectar of immortality. In the same way, liberation is by nature like the nectar of immortality. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Having a harmless nature, having a harmless nature" means this. A man full of pride, puffed up and intoxicated with pride, thinks, "I will not die," and tries to seize a snake or a tiger. Seized by it, he dies an untimely death. In the same way, one who is full of pride does not attain liberation. "Having a harmless nature" means this. Through the splendor of the precious bell-metal of a wheel-turning king, poisonous creatures of ninety-six kinds, such as beetles, snakes, kaṇabha insects and spiders, merely catch its scent from far away. Frightened by the natural scent of that precious bell-metal, they lose their poison. In the same way, liberation is by nature free of the twenty-five faults of existence. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Free of danger, free of danger": liberation is not like the blue and green colors of cardamom. "Free of danger" means this. Just as space is free of danger, liberation is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata. "Having the nature of being free of danger, having the nature of being free of danger" means this. A lamp on its stand becomes a danger. Liberation is not like that. "Having the nature of being free of danger" means this. Just as the light of the moon is no danger to any sentient being, liberation is like that: its character is to be no danger to any sentient being. Therefore freedom from danger is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Having nothing at all, having nothing at all" means this. A man has friends and enemies. Liberation is not like that. "Having nothing at all" means this. It is impossible that a wheel-turning king should have an enemy, and liberation is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata. "Having nothing at all" means this. When white cloth is dyed for some purpose, it turns blue or red. Liberation is not like that. "Having nothing at all" means this. It is impossible for the flower of the nāgakeśara tree to become foul-smelling or to turn blue. Liberation is like that: it is impossible to say that there is anything at all in it, just as that flower has only its one single scent. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Wondrous, wondrous" means this. A lotus growing in water is no great wonder. Liberation is not like that. "Wondrous" means this. Seeing a lotus flower blooming in the midst of fire gives rise to joy. In the same way, when there is liberation, joy arises. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Marvelous, marvelous" means this. A child's teeth grow in later. Liberation, however, does not change, whether its time has come or not. "Amazing" means this. It is impossible that icchantikas, those of great desire who have fallen into that state, should become buddhas. And it is impossible to say that when someone comes to have faith in the true Dharma or becomes a layman, at that time liberation is exhausted. That element is by nature marvelous. Therefore liberation is called marvelous. As in the case of the icchantika, the Tathāgata is inexhaustible. For one who focuses on the mind of the roots of virtue, phenomena do not arise, and that non-arising is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Immeasurable, immeasurable" means this. A heap of grain or a heap of śāli rice can be measured. Liberation is not like that.

"Immeasurable" means this: just as the water of the ocean is immeasurable, liberation too is immeasurable in the same way. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Of measureless nature, of measureless nature" means this: the karmic maturation of a single sentient being is of measurable nature, but liberation is not like that. "Of measureless nature" means this: just as the emergence of sentient beings from the bounds of saṃsāra is measureless, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Supreme, foremost and chief, supreme, foremost and chief" means that there is no other liberation that is supreme, foremost and chief beyond it. Just as there is no other great body of water like the great ocean, so too is liberation: liberation itself is supreme, foremost and chief. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Highest, highest" means this: just as above space there is no other space, nor any other thing that surpasses space, so above liberation there is no other thing that is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Utterly highest, utterly highest" means this: just as the lion's station is beyond all other beasts, so liberation is beyond all phenomena and is solely luminous. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Superior, superior" means this: just as among all the directions the north is called supreme, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Unsurpassed, unsurpassed" means this: just as among all lands, the land of the northerners is called the unsurpassed land, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Certain, certain" means this: for gods and humans death is certain, while life is not certain. Just as the death of gods and humans is certain, liberation is of a nature that is certain and abiding. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Not unstable, not unstable" means this: reeds, castor plants and bubbles are unstable, but liberation is not like that. "Unstable" is like the essence of butter. Liberation is not unstable. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Not of a perishable nature, not of a perishable nature" means this: flies settled on a wall, some sticking, some sitting, some moving about, some playing; liberation is not like that. "Not of a perishable nature" means this: just as on a wall where a painter has painted pictures they cannot settle and do not stick, so liberation is not tainted by any fault whatsoever. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

As for "limitless, limitless," liberation is not like a cluster of villages. "Limitless" means this: just as space has no limit, the limitless kind of bliss is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Indemonstrable, indemonstrable" means this: just as the track of a bird does not appear in the sky, so liberation is difficult to examine. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Profound, profound" means that it is of a nature entered into only with the greatest toil. Just as the virtues of parents who undertake hardships are most profound, since they are entered into with the greatest toil, so liberation is profound. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.
