"Very hard to see, very hard to see" means this: just as it is very hard for a person to see the top of his own head, so liberation is very hard to see even for all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Without dwelling, without dwelling" is a designation for houses and for space, but liberation is not like that. Here "without dwelling" is a designation for having no occasion: that in which the twenty-five harms of existence are absent is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Free of afflictions, free of afflictions" means this: whereas an emblic myrobalan can be held in a person's fist, in liberation, by comparison, nothing whatsoever can be grasped. "Free of afflictions" means this: just as nothing whatsoever can be grasped in an illusion conjured by a magician, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Flawless body, flawless body" means this: a human body may come to be free of moles, warts, leprosy, skin disease and blemishes, but liberation is not like that. "Flawless body" means this: just as milk is by nature of a single color, liberation too is of a single characteristic. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Pure, pure" means this: when the fruit of the ketaka is rubbed into water mixed with the stain of turbidity, the water becomes pure in nature, but liberation is not like that. "Pure" means this: just as rainwater in the sky is of a single taste and completely pure, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

As for "released, released," liberation is like the stainless moon on the fifteenth day, not like the moon covered by clouds. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Peace, peace" means this: a being stricken with an epidemic fever comes to be free of the fever, but liberation is not like that. As for "peace," liberation is like a being who is without illness and without harm. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Equality, equality" means this: when two rams butt one another their strength is equal, but liberation is not like that. Just as mother and child speak in harmony, liberation too is like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

As for "fully pacified, fully pacified," once one has attained that supreme abode, it is impossible to desire any other abode. "Not knowing satisfaction" means this: when one is weak, after eating the finest food the wish to drink buttermilk arises, but liberation is not like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

As for "severance, severance," whatever is the severance of desire within, which is like the noose of craving, that is liberation in the sense of severance.

That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"The far shore, the far shore": just as a river has two banks, the near bank and the far bank, liberation is not like that. Liberation is the far shore. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Like a river": only the river itself can serve as the example; in the world there is no other river that can serve as the example. "River" here refers to the ocean. Since among all rivers the ocean alone is truly the river, the ocean is counted under the term "river." It is called a river because it roars with a great sound. Liberation is like the far shore of the river that is the ocean. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Excellent, excellent": that in which there is no harsh taste like that of the kośātakī fruit. Liberation is not like the kośātakī fruit; liberation is like grapes. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

Just as a physician, having steadied the patient's mind with every remedy and medicine, dispels all illnesses, so too liberation is free from all faults. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Intense longing, intense longing": just as in a small village there is no room for great crowds of people to enter, liberation is not like that. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"The exhaustion of existence and of delight" is like being free of illusions and magical apparitions. Whereas among men and women who are given to sexual union there is talk passing back and forth, liberation is not like that. That which by nature has the character of its own essence, free from attachment, aversion, and delusion, is liberation. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Taming thirst, taming thirst": that in which there is not the slightest trace of craving. The absence of the craving of hungry ghosts is craving for the Dharma. Craving for the Dharma means compassion for all sentient beings. Liberation is not like the craving of hungry ghosts. To be free of grasping at self and at what is mine is liberation. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Cessation, cessation": this is liberation, in which there is no clinging to existence. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Abode, abode": this is liberation, which definitively delivers and protects from all existence. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Refuge, refuge": this is like one who, having gone for refuge to the Tathāgata, seeks no other refuge. Just as going for refuge to a king is not stable, liberation is not like that. Stable bliss is liberation. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Ally, ally": just as a forest wilderness is utterly frightening, liberation is not like that. It is like the ally that the king of beasts has and that other beasts lack. Just as the king of beasts has no fear of any of the terrors of death, so liberation is an ally. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Bliss, bliss": this is like an island of jewels. Likewise, liberation is a source of the Dharma. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Without peril, without peril": a man, terrified and frightened by a tiger, runs into a well, and then from inside the well he cries, "Who can get me out?" Liberation is not like that well. "Without peril" is like this: when a ship is wrecked, one finds another ship, crosses the great ocean completely, and comes to be safe and happy. In the same way, liberation is without peril. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"The destruction of abodes" means the exhaustion of occasion. Just as curd comes from milk, fresh butter comes from curd, clarified butter comes from fresh butter, and the finest essence of ghee comes from clarified butter, so liberation is the complete severance of all abodes, abiding in its own nature like the finest essence of ghee. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Subduing pride, subduing pride": just as a king, a lord of the earth, has pride that needs subduing, thinking, "I am supreme among all kings," liberation is not like that. Liberation is without pride and without grasping at pride. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Subduing intoxication, subduing intoxication": that in which not even the name of sexual desire exists is liberation. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Subduing ignorance, subduing ignorance": just as, passing from milk through one stage after another, each impurity and fault is exhausted and from that only the finest essence of ghee emerges, so from ignorance only knowledge emerges. That which is ignorance is liberation. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Solitude, solitude": that in which there is no other phenomenon as companion, just as in the elephant forest a single elephant roams alone and dwells alone. Liberation too is like that. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Complete abandonment, complete abandonment": this teaches the apprehension of selflessness, and it is entry into the tathāgatagarbha, the buddha-nature. Just as the stalks of the castor plant (eraṇḍa) and of the reed have no core whatsoever apart from their fruit, so all the bodies of gods, humans, and asuras, apart from the tathāgatagarbha, are all without core, like the castor plant and the reed. To completely abandon all coreless existence is liberation. Liberation makes itself known to oneself. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Rejection, rejection": just as one rejects uncooked food because the food is not digested, so it is the cutting off and abandoning of all existence. Thus liberation is wise. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"The qualities of pratyekabuddhas, the qualities of pratyekabuddhas": just as the flower of the nāga tree is not the flower of the pakhal, liberation too is like that. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Element, element": that element is the element of all sentient beings. That life is the life of all sentient beings. Liberation too is like that. That which is liberation is the Tathāgata.

"Source, source": this refers to that in which there is a gateway of emergence. Having meditated on selflessness, one enters the path of the self; that is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Virtue, virtue": it is like the way that, for those who are to be instructed, nothing equals the word of the teacher. "Virtue" means what is good; that is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Beyond the world, beyond the world": this is the supreme abode among all phenomena; that is liberation. Just as among all nourishing essences the finest is cow's milk, so what is beyond the world is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Unmoving, unmoving": just as the post that holds a door bolt cannot be shaken by the winds of the four directions, so liberation too is firm and unmoving. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata. The waves of the ocean are by nature unstable, but liberation is not like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Abode, abode": even a king's palace, a fine mansion hung with banners and without flaw, is something fabricated. Liberation is not like that. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Certain, certain": just as the gold of the Jambū River is flawless and can be worked into anything, so too is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata. Liberation is not like the conduct of children. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Thoroughly refined, thoroughly refined": just as one who has gained sovereignty over a kingdom is mighty and lives at ease, so the complete abandonment of the aggregates is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Final, final": this is the exhaustion of the impure afflictions. Liberation is like a man who has come out of the king's prison. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata, the bliss that is not contrived.

"Abandoning attachment, aversion, and delusion, abandoning attachment, aversion, and delusion": this is the casting off of the three. Just as a man bitten by a venomous snake vomits up phlegm in order to save himself, so the exhaustion of the phlegm of the afflictions is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Abandoning the afflictions, abandoning the afflictions": the exhaustion of the afflictions, which are like a venomous snake, is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Abandoning the aggregates, abandoning the aggregates": having cast off all suffering, great bliss is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"The exhaustion of attachment, aversion, and delusion, the exhaustion of attachment, aversion, and delusion": having exhausted the three and torn them out by the root is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Having abandoned all the aggregates, having abandoned all the aggregates": this is having abandoned all faults, because one sees well. That is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"The meaning of having abandoned all conditioned things, the meaning of having abandoned all conditioned things": this is liberation, the accomplishment of all virtue. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Cutting off paths, cutting off paths": having meditated on selflessness, one grasps selflessness in an unbroken way; having meditated on a self, one grasps a self in an unbroken way; and having meditated on both sides, one holds that whatever is the buddha element exists. That holding is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Emptiness, emptiness": this is when, though one searches, nothing at all is found. What is called "nothingness" is found among the Nirgranthas too, but liberation is not like that. As for what is called emptiness: take pots of honey, ghee, sesame oil, water, and curd. Whether there is curd in it or not, it is called a curd pot; and likewise, whether there is honey or water in it or not, it is called a honey pot or a water pot. Is it then called an empty pot? When those contents are absent, is the pot itself empty? If it has shape and it has color, how can it be called empty?

Liberation is not empty in that way. Liberation too possesses perfect form and color. Yet just as a curd pot, though it is not empty, is called empty because it has no curd, so liberation too, while not being empty, is called empty. If one asks how it can be called empty while it has form: it is called empty because the forms of the afflictions, the twenty-five designations of existence, suffering, worldly customs, rites, and the arising of spheres of activity are absent from it. Just as a curd pot is without curd, so liberation is called empty. Yet just as the form of the pot remains unmoved, in liberation there are great bliss, joy, permanence, stability, eternity, supramundane qualities, rites, and spheres of activity.

The permanence, stability, and eternity of liberation are not like the form of a pot. A pot will break, for it is merely something established by way of causes. Liberation, because it is not made, will not break. Whatever is liberation is the uncontrived element, and that is the Tathāgata.

"The exhaustion of craving, the exhaustion of craving": at the very moment of becoming a buddha, there is no craving or other desire such as "May I become Indra," or "May I become Maheśvara (Śiva)," or "May I become Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world." It is impossible for such a thing to be said to exist or to arise. Whatever is the exhaustion of craving, that is the Buddha. That is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

"Cessation free from attachment, cessation free from attachment": here, clinging to all craving, and desire without residue, with nothing remaining and nothing left over, together with all attachments of every kind, become pacified forever. This is liberation, the cessation of all bonds. Whatever is such a cessation, that is liberation. Whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata.

As for what is called "nirvāṇa, nirvāṇa": a herd of deer that has escaped from a hunter's hands, fearful and terrified of death, runs by whatever means it can, reaches a sage's hermitage, and there finds ease and catches its breath. Just as they catch their breath and find ease, so too many beings, with respect to inexhaustible nirvāṇa, slip free and escape from the hands of what conditions becoming and of all the foes that take the form of afflictions. Fearful and terrified of death, they run by whatever means they can and go to the hermitage of the sage of the Dharma, the dwelling place of the Tathāgata, the Sage, the refuge without equal, and there they catch their breath. The time when they catch their breath and find ease is nirvāṇa, the nirvāṇa that is inexhaustible.

Fascicle Seven.

Then the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said this to the Blessed One (the Buddha): "If that is so, then since there would be no arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, omniscient ones, they would not attain the supreme bliss of nirvāṇa by relinquishing the body, and those who go for refuge to the Three would not attain nirvāṇa."

He declared: "Suppose a man has recovered from a faint, and people go to him and ask, 'Have you recovered, and are you well?' Having recovered, he says, 'I have recovered and am well.' Just as he has recovered from his faint, so when one is freed from all twenty-five states of existence, one has attained the parinirvāṇa of a buddha. What is called supreme bliss is the many forms of supreme bliss found there, together with the perception of the inexhaustible. That is parinirvāṇa. That is liberation. That which experiences supreme stability, immovability, bliss and permanence is liberation. Whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata."

Then the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said this to the Blessed One: "Should one say that what is unborn and unceasing is liberation, and that it is the Tathāgata?"

The Blessed One declared: "Son of good family, it is indeed so. That is liberation. That is the Tathāgata."

He said: "Then, since the element of space too is unborn and unarisen, should one say that liberation is like it, and that the Tathāgata too is like it?"

He declared: "It is not so. Liberation is not like the element of space."

He said: "Then what is it like?"

He declared: "Son of good family, when the jīvaṃjīvaka bird or the kalaviṅka bird sings, would one say that it sings like a crow?"

He said: "No, Blessed One. The cries of other birds are not even fit to serve as an example for their song. The cawing of a crow does not come to even a hundredth part of the song of the jīvaṃjīvaka or the kalaviṅka, nor even a thousandth part; it cannot bear comparison by number, by fraction, by example or by cause. Those birds are beyond compare. Blessed One, the speech of the Tathāgata is beyond compare by way of cause."

He declared: "Excellent, son of good family, excellent! Son of good family, it is right that you should explain it so. Excellent! Therefore whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata. No other cause or example for liberation exists in the world with its gods, humans and asuras. Although it is without example, for the sake of those to be trained it is shown through examples: 'Its qualities are like this, they are like that.' The example of liberation is the Tathāgata. Why is that? Whatever is the Tathāgata is liberation, and whatever is liberation is the Tathāgata; here no division into two can be made, and no distinction can be made.

"Son of good family, even where there is no example, an example may be taken for the sake of a cause. One says, 'Ah, lovely-faced one, you are like the full moon,' and 'The fine elephant is like a snowy mountain.' But is the face like the moon, or is the moon like the face? Is the elephant like the mountain, or is the mountain like the elephant? Son of good family, in the same way, you should understand that liberation too, though without example, is here shown through examples by reason of a cause, in order to set beings free for certain. The suchness of the Dharma too should be understood as the suchness of the Dharma by reason of a cause."

He said: "Why does the Tathāgata teach in two ways?"

He declared: "Son of good family, here is another example. Suppose a certain man takes up a sword, thinking, 'I shall kill the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha,' and tries to kill him, while the Tathāgata does not do the slightest wrong in return, but remains undaunted and joyful. Would that man be called one who commits an act of immediate retribution, or not?"

He said: "In terms of the actual thing, one would not say that he had committed an act of immediate retribution. Why is that? Because the accumulated elements are not destroyed, in terms of the actual thing he does not commit an act of immediate retribution. In the body of the Tathāgata there is not even a trace of accumulated elements. Since it is the element of the Dharma (dharmadhātu), how could it be killed? Therefore, merely from forming the intention and from seeking to do it, he is said to commit an act of immediate retribution. Here, by reason of a cause, one should understand the suchness of the Dharma."

He declared: "Excellent, son of good family, excellent! I too had in mind to speak those very words. Son of good family, here is another example. Suppose a certain evil man thinks, 'I shall kill my mother,' and lies in wait in a field of millet and lentils in a wilderness. When his mother comes there, he says, 'You have not brought my broth properly!' and sharpens an axe to kill her. While he is sharpening the axe, his mother, frightened, hides inside a pile of straw. Then he takes up the axe and strikes the pile of straw, thinking, 'Now my mother is dead.' Fearing other people, he does not examine the straw carefully. Then the mother comes out from inside it and goes away. Then, thinking, 'My mother is dead,' he rejoices. Would he be called one who commits an act of immediate retribution, or not?"

He said: "One would not say that he killed his mother."

Again he asked, "Why is that? Is it the actual basis of the cause of a heinous act that one must ask about?"

The Blessed One said, "It is by destroying the aggregates of one's father or mother that one is said to commit a heinous act of immediate retribution. Therefore that person has not committed a heinous act. But since he began to give rise to the intention to kill his mother, it is like committing a heinous act. As long as the substance of the aggregates, the elements and the sense bases has not been destroyed, the heinous act is not said to be complete. Here one should understand the reality of phenomena by way of causes."

He said, "Excellent, Blessed One, excellent! In this way, by way of causes, one both understands and does not understand."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, in just the same way I speak of liberation by way of causes. In terms of causes, nirvāṇa, endowed with immeasurable qualities, is utterly incomparable. Likewise, going to the Great Parinirvāṇa is also endowed with immeasurable qualities. Today the Tathāgata will pass into parinirvāṇa, and therefore that is the meaning of the Great Parinirvāṇa."

He said, "The Tathāgata is not something that comes to an end. The Tathāgata should be regarded as one whose life knows no exhaustion."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, son of noble family, excellent! One who protects the true Dharma ought to speak in this way. One who wishes to resolve his own doubts ought to act in this way. Thus the bodhisattva great beings teach the Dharma in these four ways."

This concludes the fourth chapter, on bodhisattvas teaching the Dharma in four ways.

In this sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa there are four persons who have attained the way of the true Dharma, who bring forth the true Dharma, and who serve as a support. They appear in the world for the benefit of many people and for the happiness of many people. What are the four?

A person who is by nature fully afflicted appears in the world for the happiness of many people and for the benefit of many people; this is the first person. A person who possesses the fruit of stream-entry and the fruit of once-returning is the second. The non-returner is the third. The arhat is the fourth. These four persons are the supports.

Among them, the one called an ordinary being is this. Having himself come to possess moral discipline, proper conduct and good qualities, he brings forth the Dharma. Those who possess the root of the Tathāgata he causes to flourish from the very beginning, and he also understands the meaning. He explains the meaning, saying, "This Dharma belongs to those of few desires; this Dharma does not belong to those of great desires." He teaches the eight thoughts of a great being. When a fault has occurred, he has others confess it, in order to lift them out of it. And while practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, he is skilled in words spoken with hidden intent and brings forth the true Dharma. This is the first person. Although he is counted among ordinary beings, he is not an ordinary being who lacks learning. This ordinary being of the eighth rank, a bodhisattva great being, has been prophesied by the tathāgatas, with a secret prophecy, to become a buddha.

Among them, those called stream-enterers and once-returners are these. Having attained, it is impossible for them to be parted from inquiring into and upholding the true Dharma, and impossible for them to teach others self-fabricated words not spoken by the Buddha, various writings, or the acquiring of male and female servants. Although they are counted among stream-enterers and once-returners, they are not stream-enterers and once-returners. In this way the bodhisattva great beings who have attained the first, second and third grounds are prophesied, by a direct prophecy, to become buddhas in the abode of the buddhas.

The one called a non-returner is this. Having attained that Dharma, it is impossible for him to be parted from it. It is impossible for him to teach others improper, self-fabricated sūtras not spoken by the Buddha, with their various words and writings, or the acquiring of male and female servants and the like. It is impossible for the adventitious secondary afflictions to harm him, and impossible for the inner afflictions to harm him. It is impossible for him to conceal the element of the Tathāgata and teach the Dharma. It is impossible for the adventitious diseases that depend on the venomous snakes of the four elements to harm him. He is not one who speaks of a self, yet he also refutes those who proclaim no-self; he has fully abandoned the worldly self, and by worldly activities he also accords with the world. It is impossible for him to make any vehicle other than the Great Vehicle blaze forth. He does not hold a body of eighty kinds of worms and great defects. Having fully abandoned desirous thoughts, even if something arises in a dream, it is impossible for semen to be emitted from his body. When he sees the terrors of death approaching, it is impossible for him to be afraid. Such a person is called a non-returner, and is the third person.

Moreover, he is called a non-returner because he definitely does not return; yet that person is not said to have attained non-returning. He also has the qualities taught before, and even if he is born here, he is not stained by the faults taught before. That bodhisattva great being, although he moves about in the manner of coming and going, is counted among the non-returners. By the prophecy given the moment he generates the mind of awakening, he is prophesied to buddhahood, and immediately after generating that mind, without long delay, he becomes a buddha. Such a person is the third person.

The arhat whose defilements are exhausted is the fourth person. "Arhat" means one who is without afflictions, who has laid down the burden, who has done what was to be done for his own aim, who has accomplished the aim, and who has attained the ten grounds.

It further enlarges on the meaning: such a one is foretold with the prophecy of the forbearance toward unproduced phenomena. He manifestly emanates forms according to what he is able to do and forms according to what he wishes. And with whatever body he wishes to become a buddha, with that very body he becomes a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha. One endowed with these qualities is called an arhat. In this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, these four persons appear in the world for the benefit of many people and for the happiness of many people. Like the Tathāgata, they are teachers of gods and humans, and all four are authoritative persons. They have become a support for the world.

He said: "These four persons are not authoritative for me, nor are they a support. Why is that? When the Blessed One, on the occasion of the householder Ghoṣila, spoke about subduing Māra, he said: 'Even if a god, a māra, or Brahmā should come in the guise of a buddha's body like mine, the body adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, endowed with a fathom-wide aura, and with the face adorned by the ūrṇā hair, I permit you to subdue him. Say this: "Evil One, do not set yourself up like this as an arhat, in the form of an arhat. Evil One, even if you lie down in the sky, make fire blaze from your left side and pour down water from your right side, blaze like fire with splendor and give off smoke, I will not listen to your words. I will not trust you. Even if you teach something like the nine-limbed scriptures, I will not have faith in you."

Sons of good family, if you have such doubts, subdue even me. Sons of good family, it is like this: suppose that at night, in the darkness, the lady of the house or a maidservant notices that a thieving dog has come in, and says to it, "You thieving dog, get out quickly, before I kill you!" As soon as the thieving dog hears this, it runs off and from then on never comes to that house again. In the same way, you should subdue Māra the Evil One as one would the thieving dog, and say to him, "Evil One, do not act like this! Get out quickly, before I bind you with the five bonds!" The moment he hears this, he will turn back like the thieving dog. Those who subdue Māra in this way are the holy ones; they dwell in the very presence of nirvāṇa.'

Since the Blessed One declared this when he spoke about subduing Māra on the occasion of the householder Ghoṣila, why, Blessed One, do you establish the four persons as supports? I do not listen to them, and I do not trust them."

The Blessed One said: "Son of good family, it is so. What I said is exactly so. Nevertheless, what I said about subduing Māra, I said for the sake of the childish followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle who have the eye of flesh. I did not speak about subduing Māra for the sake of the followers of the Great Vehicle. Although among the followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle there are those who have the divine eye, they are counted as having what is called the eye of flesh. Although among the followers of the Great Vehicle there are those who have the eye of flesh, they are counted as having what is called the eye of a buddha. Why is that? Because they hold the reality of the sūtras of the Great Vehicle, the Buddha Vehicle.

Further, son of good family, suppose there is a hero, famed throughout the region, courageous and valiant, and certain cowardly men rely on him. When that hero teaches courage to the cowards, he instructs them: 'O man, if you wish to become a hero, walk upon a bed of sword points. Whenever you fight, fear no one at all. Whether it be a god or a fierce one, regard them as mere grass. Strive in courage and valor. When you see certain wicked troops who have changed into the garb of heroes, carrying heroes' weapons, drawing their swords from the scabbard, coming on as if to grind everything to dust, striking sword against sword so that sparks fly, and entering the marketplace raising war cries, do not flee.' When those who are wicked troops see these men, they take them for heroes and scatter like thieving dogs, while those who are good troops, fearing disgrace, hold their ground. Just as those wicked troops, frightened by them, scatter, so the Tathāgata too trains the followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle in heroism, teaching them: 'Do not fear Māra the Evil One, who is like a cowardly troop; be valiant, like one who has been taught courage, and Māra will be dismayed.'

Further, son of good family, some hero of a great family, or some son of a hero, though no one has taught him courage and he has no teacher at all, is courageous by his very nature; as is the way of a son of good family, he is courageous in keeping with his own heroic lineage as its condition. In the same way, followers of the Great Vehicle, whether men, women, boys, or girls, who on hearing the various words of the sūtras spoken with a hidden intent are not frightened or terrified, are those who in the past served and honored many buddhas. Therefore they understand this Great Vehicle and come to have firm faith in it. When they see a māra, or even ten million māras, the followers of the Great Vehicle are not frightened and do not regard it as the least bit remarkable. It is like a snake charmer who, by the power of antidotal medicine and of mantra, renders venomous snakes harmless and so has no fear of them. In the same way, even ten million māras are deprived of their arrogance by the splendor and power of the mantra that is the words of the Great Vehicle sūtras.

Further, son of good family, venomous nāga serpents frighten elephants, tigers, leopards, and dogs merely by looking at them. Some even die; some, fearing death, flee merely on hearing their sound. Yet there are also people who ride about on venomous nāga serpents, garuḍas, elephants, lions, and tigers.

Some people, when they see such nāgas, garuḍas, lions and rākṣasas, lose all their nerve. Like those timid people, the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are frightened when they see Māra. Māra has no fear of them, and he goes on doing the work of Māra. Those of the Great Vehicle ride upon Māra. They subdue his great splendor, his great strength and his courage, and they teach the Dharma. Those mighty māras, who are like the nāgas and the rest, are frightened, and at once they come to delight in the true Dharma. They even say, "From now on we will not obstruct the true Dharma."

Those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle are frightened by the forms that the afflictions take. Those of the Great Vehicle regard the afflictions as slight. In this way, those of the Great Vehicle are heroic. That, then, is the reason given earlier: it was for the sake of those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle that I taught the subjugation of Māra. Thus the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is supremely hard to accept.

Ah! Those who have faith when they hear this great sūtra and the words "the Tathāgata is permanent" are doing what is supremely difficult. They are as rare in the world as the udumbara flower. After I have passed away, this great sūtra will be very hard to find. In times to come, those beings who strive to hear this great sūtra will be shunned by many beings.

He asked, "For how long will it be shunned by beings? What kind of beings will bring forth the true Dharma?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, after I have passed away it will circulate in Jambudvīpa for four thousand years. After that it will disappear beneath the earth.

"Son of noble family, suppose there is a land where sugarcane, sāli rice, grain, honey, sugar, cow's milk and the essence of butter are plentiful. In that very land there are also others whose karmic ripening is inferior and who have made no merit. They say, 'If this bodily constitution of ours takes good food, it will fall ill,' and so they eat coarse grain, millet and wild millet. In that same land, some others do not even want to hear the names of coarse grain, millet and wild millet, and they eat only sugarcane, sāli rice and grain. In the same way, some other beings do not even want to hear this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra. Just as those who have made no merit eat coarse grain, millet and wild millet and dislike sugarcane and sāli rice, so those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle will dislike the Great Vehicle. Some others, who make merit by listening to it, will become followers of the Great Vehicle, like those who eat sāli rice.

"Son of noble family, suppose there are kings who live in wild mountain country, in ravines and poor lands. Although they do have sugarcane and sāli rice, they think, 'These grains are rare; it would not do for them to run out.' So they do not eat them themselves but eat coarse grain, millet and wild millet. They fill carts with sugarcane, sāli rice and grain and offer them to the great king. Then other beings there, though they had never before seen sāli rice and the like in their own land, eat them through the power of the king's wealth.

"Son of noble family, in the same way, wherever those four persons who act as leaders of the Dharma dwell, when even a single bodhisattva mahāsattva appears in the world, or wherever he may dwell, there, for his sake, they have the sections of the Great Vehicle sūtras written out for payment and cause them to be copied. When the copies are finished, they put the volumes into covers and honor them, whether for gain and respect, or for the practice of the Dharma, or to make a support for worship. They serve that bodhisattva mahāsattva, and many people come to enjoy the nectar of the Great Vehicle. Just as people eat sāli rice through the power of the king, so, through the power of that bodhisattva mahāsattva's majesty, beings will hear a drop of words, even a single syllable, that they have never heard before.

"Wherever the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra circulates, that place becomes vajra in nature, and those beings too become vajra in nature. So I declare. When ordinary beings of little merit hear the Great Vehicle, they do not grasp the meaning of the sections of the Great Vehicle sūtras. Those beings who hear it and do not grasp its meaning are to be pitied, like those who eat wild millet."

He asked, "After the Tathāgata has passed away, it will remain in Jambudvīpa for four thousand years and then disappear beneath the earth. After how long will it appear again?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, when eighty years remain at the end of my true Dharma, as the true Dharma is about to vanish, this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra will appear in Jambudvīpa. It will rain down the rain of the true Dharma for forty years, and then it will vanish."

He asked, "In the time when the true Dharma vanishes, when those who keep moral discipline have died out, when what is not the true Dharma spreads, when there is no practice, at the time this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa appears, what kind of people will listen to it? What kind will keep it? What kind will read it? Who will protect it, hold it, make offerings to it and teach it? What kind will have it written out? Blessed One, Tathāgata, Arhat, Perfectly Complete Buddha, please explain this for the benefit of all sentient beings. There are bodhisattvas who wish to train properly. If they hear this, they will practice so as never to fall away from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, excellent, excellent!

Son of good family, it is excellent that you ask about this matter. Son of good family, those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in the Hiraṇyavatī River will, in a later time, put the awakening mind into practice. They will not fail to put it into practice, and they will not reject the sūtras either.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River will, in a later time, not give rise to a mind that rejects this peerless teaching. They will have faith in it, and through their words they will make all people understand it in detail.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in two Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, again give rise to the mind set on awakening, and they will not reject this teaching.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in three Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture, listen to it, and ask about it, but they will not understand its meaning.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in four Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, again give rise to the mind set on awakening. They will not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture, listen to it, and ask about it, and they will understand one sixteenth of its meaning, but they will not understand its meaning completely.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in five Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture, look into it, and ask about it. Having written it down, they will also keep it themselves, and they will understand one eighth of its meaning, but they will not understand its meaning completely.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in six Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture and ask about it. Having written it down, they will also hold it, and they will understand one quarter of its meaning, but they will not understand its meaning completely.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in seven Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture and ask about it. Having written it down, they will also hold it, and they will understand half of its meaning, but they will not understand its meaning completely.

Those who have given rise to the mind set on awakening in the presence of as many buddhas as there are grains of sand in eight Ganges Rivers will, in a later time, not reject the peerless teaching. They will receive the scripture, listen to it, ask about it, write it down and hold it, keep it, read it, teach it, keep it safe, guard it, and explain it. Out of compassion for the whole world, they will make offerings to the volumes and honor them, and they will cause others to do so as well. They will regard it as one regards a revered teacher, and they will understand its meaning completely. They will teach that the Blessed One is permanent, stable, eternal, unmoving, and supremely blissful, and that all sentient beings possess the buddha-nature.

Thus, beings who in the past have served many buddhas will bring forth the true Dharma and will take the side of the true Dharma. Those who now give rise to the mind set on awakening will, in a later time, bring forth the true Dharma and take the side of the true Dharma, for at that time such beings will appear. Son of good family, in the future you must not regard in that way the beings who bring forth the true Dharma. Know that those who now give rise to the mind set on awakening will in the future also give rise to the mind set on awakening and bring forth the true Dharma.

Son of good family, there are those who, when I pass into nirvāṇa through the Great Parinirvāṇa, will feel no displeasure but will rather rejoice, thinking, "This Buddha who obstructs our gain and honor is going to die, and we are glad!" Thinking thus that the Buddha will die, they rejoice and feel no displeasure. Know that at that time they will grow lax, and that those who bear the mere marks of the Dharma robe will reject the sūtras.

Those sentient beings who are endowed with immeasurable qualities will have faith when they hear the Great Vehicle. For those sons of good family who long for the true Dharma, the countless evil deeds they have committed will be purified by no more than headaches, the torment of fever, piercing pains, and slander.

Furthermore, in a later time they will be despised by others. Their complexion will become poor, and their bedding, seats, and food will be poor. They will be without gain. They will be born into poor families, into common families, and into families of wrong view. They will suffer disturbance from kings, disturbance in the land, and disturbance among the things they hold dear. They will not meet virtuous spiritual friends. They will lack clothing, food, bedding, seats, and medicine and remedies for the sick. Their gain will be slight, and they will be afflicted by weakness from hunger and thirst. They will have to please common people. Kings, ministers, and the people of the palace retinue will not take up their talk on the Dharma, nor take the side of those who preach the Dharma. Even the ministers and officials whom they hope will be their allies and protectors will leave them without any recourse, and will not take the side of the true Dharma. Even if they are good-looking, they will not seem attractive; even if they are pleasing to behold, they will appear unpleasant to behold. Thus, those of my śrāvakas who teach the great sūtra in the world are doing what is difficult.

Son of good family, it is like even very dense frost, which remains until the sun has risen, but as soon as the sun rises, it melts away completely. In the same way, son of good family, the countless evil deeds of those beings also remain until the sun of the Great Parinirvāṇa has risen, but when the sun of the Great Parinirvāṇa rises, those evil deeds will be completely purified.

Son of good family, here is another example. Suppose someone wishes to go forth from home into homelessness, but has not yet taken even the novice's precepts. At that time a certain person invites the Saṅgha of monks, headed by the Tathāgata, to a feast. That householder also puts on the Dharma robes and goes into the town together with the Tathāgata's śrāvakas, and when he is given a seat, he is counted among the Saṅgha of the Tathāgata's śrāvakas. In just the same way, son of good family, a bodhisattva mahāsattva who trains in this sūtra is counted among the bodhisattva mahāsattvas who have attained the ten grounds.

Those who stand outside this only hear it: they are lured by material things, or they want something to talk about, or they desire gain, honor and verses. Yet if they do not reject the sūtra, then, apart from those who reject it out of pride, those other beings will gradually attain the perfect and complete awakening of the buddhas, the arhats.

Son of good family, therefore the four persons taught earlier are the ones to rely on. They could not possibly teach of a sūtra not spoken by the Buddha that it was spoken by the Buddha; there is no occasion for that. Therefore they are the ones to rely on. If even a single one of these four persons is present, make offerings to him and protect him.

He asked, "How should one make offerings to him?"

The Blessed One declared, "That person gives up his own life to bring forth the sacred Dharma. Therefore learn from him how to protect the sacred Dharma, and once you have learned it, make offerings to that teacher. In the Śrāvaka Vehicle too I taught:

Whether he is old or whether he is young,
The one from whom you come to understand the Dharma,
Like a brahmin tending his fire offering,
To him you should bow with reverence.
Whether he is old or whether he is young,
The one from whom you come to understand the Dharma,
As one does toward the god Mahendra (Indra),
To him you should bow with reverence."

He asked, "It is right to make offerings to a teacher. But it is not right for an elder monk endowed with moral discipline to bow to a junior monk, and this needs to be examined. If an elder monk learns from a junior, must he then bow to the junior? If he must bow to the junior, then that elder would no longer be a monk endowed with moral discipline.

There is something further to raise. Suppose the elder monk is immoral and the junior is endowed with moral discipline, and one comes to understand the reality of the Dharma from the elder. Must the junior, even though he has understood, bow to the immoral elder?

There is something further to raise. If one comes to understand the Dharma from someone who lives as a householder, must one bow to that householder? It is not right to show such excessive deference. Householders are not worthy of the offerings of those who have gone forth, nor are they fit recipients of gifts. In this teaching, it is right for the young to make offerings to their elders. What is called an elder worthy of offerings is one who has received full ordination and who abides in the Vinaya, the rites and the proper sphere of conduct. The Blessed One has also said that the immoral are like stones in a field. So would you now also say:

Whether he is old or whether he is young,
The one from whom you come to understand the Dharma,
As one does toward the god Mahendra,
To him you should bow with reverence?

These two statements need to be examined. Otherwise, to act in this way would become an occasion for censure. Blessed One, are you deceiving the monks who are endowed with moral discipline? There are many faults in this. Since the Blessed One has declared in many sūtras that the immoral are to be punished, did the Tathāgata say this with some special intention?"

The Blessed One declared, "The verse

Whether he is old or whether he is young,
The one from whom you come to understand the Dharma,
As one does toward the god Mahendra,
To him you should bow with reverence

I taught for the sake of the bodhisattvas of the future. I did not teach it for those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle.

Son of good family, as I taught earlier, there will come a time when the sacred Dharma declines, when those endowed with moral discipline dwindle, when the faction of the immoral flourishes, when definitive liberation and freedom from attachment are exhausted, and when many take possession of improper goods such as male and female servants. At that time, even a single one of the four persons will go forth from home into homelessness within a Saṅgha that possesses many improper goods such as male and female servants. He will know the distinctions between what is allowable for them and what is not, and between what accords with the Vinaya and what does not. He will know their negligence and the bases of their many faults, and he will know that this conduct is not the conduct of the Blessed One. Then, having regard for place and time, he will learn the nine branches of scripture from the junior monks. Although he knows that they have incurred defeats, he thinks, 'I must bring forth the sacred Dharma,' and so he remains silent, thinking only of that. He appears in the world solely to protect the sacred Dharma.

Son of good family, suppose that in some country a king dies and none of his sons is capable of being king. A caṇḍāla from that very king's realm, who has cymbals that resound like thunder, seizes the kingdom by force together with other caṇḍālas and rules as king. That caṇḍāla is also wealthy by nature. Then, while the caṇḍāla rules as king, the brahmins and householders living there think, 'It is not right for us to be ordered about by a caṇḍāla,' and they abandon the country and go to a land where one of royal caste rules as king. Some other brahmins and householders cannot bring themselves to abandon their own country, and just as a tree withers right where it grew, they too, like trees, stay on in that very country.

Then that caṇḍāla thought, "Many people will flee," and he had caṇḍālas guard all the roads. To hold on to the people who remained, he also proclaimed: "Whatever pure brahmin who has mastered the Vedas wishes to enjoy half of my kingdom, let him serve as my royal chaplain (purohita)!" The great drum was beaten in the city in this way for seven days. Yet the pure brahmins said, "Who would serve as priest to a caṇḍāla?" and no one would agree.

Then that caṇḍāla gave this command: "If no one will agree to do this, then all the brahmins shall eat food together with caṇḍālas in caṇḍāla households, and sleep there! If they will not agree, I shall force them to do that as well. But if someone agrees, I will give him even half of the kingdom. And the nectar of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, which makes whoever drinks it become a god, and which is rare in the world, he shall drink together with me and become a god."

Then a certain young brahmin, pure, wearing a topknot, master of the Vedas, a youth living the celibate life, heard that command. He undid his own topknot, went to the caṇḍāla, bowed to him, and said, "Whatever Your Majesty commands, I shall do." Then that king installed him as royal chaplain. The brahmins mocked him, saying, "This wicked one has agreed to be chaplain to a caṇḍāla! This wicked one!" Then, just as he had said, the king gave him half of the kingdom and ruled together with him.

Then on one occasion the brahmin said to the caṇḍāla king, "Great king, I abandoned the ways of a brahmin and agreed to serve as your chaplain, thinking that I would teach you the sciences one by one, yet you still do not trust me."

The king replied, "Why should I not trust you? I trust you, who have such fine qualities. Now let us drink this vase of nectar of the king who died, sharing it equally, half and half."

Then, after the minister had drunk first, he skillfully poured another drink into the nectar, and when the king drank it, he fainted and lay there as if dead. Then the brahmin made the son of the former king, a capable prince, king, and said, "Take this kingdom and do not let it go to ruin. The caṇḍāla is not fit to be king of this kingdom; you yourself are fit to be king. It is impossible for a caṇḍāla to rule as king."

Then he gave the caṇḍāla a medicine to revive him from his faint, and when he had brought him round, he banished him and inflicted on him the punishment for rebels. When that brahmin had thus restored the kingdom and banished the caṇḍāla, he did not fall from his brahminhood. The brahmins and householders said, "This is a bodhisattva, a great being," and they cried, "Excellent! Excellent!" and honored him.

In the same way, after I have passed away, a bodhisattva will use every kind of means in order to bring forth the true Dharma. In a Saṅgha that hankers after bad goods and is given to bad conduct, he will even appear to conform to the immoral. There will be one who is able to take from those immoral ones their robes, their books, and their requisites and give them to monks who keep moral discipline. There will be one who is able, relying even on administrators, chiefs, and grain collectors, to do away with those who accept the eight improper things. There will be one who is able, just as the brahmin did with the caṇḍāla king, to join with them in granting leave, in the poṣadha, and in joining palms, while carrying out acts that afflict them and subduing those who go against the Dharma. And there will be one who is able to turn even the lax into a pure Saṅgha and to teach the collections of the Great Vehicle sūtras for the benefit of many beings. It was for the sake of such a bodhisattva, a great being, that those verses were spoken.

When he acts in this way, because he brings forth the true Dharma, the brahmins and householders are pleased, and thinking, "Though he is a brahmin, he has established the kingdom and the true Dharma," they honor him. Whoever teaches in this way is truly a bodhisattva, and since he acts in conjunction with skillful means, one should not find fault with him. If one finds fault with him, one will in turn incur a fault oneself: it becomes like an offense that ought to be confessed, and yet, under the sway of pride, one does not confess it. Although that bodhisattva teacher may appear exceedingly lax, he incurs no fault. This intentional statement was taught for the sake of bodhisattvas:

Whether he be old or whether young,
the one from whom you come to understand the Dharma,
as a brahmin does his fire of offering,
to him bow down with reverence.
The one from whom you come to understand
the Dharma taught by the perfect Buddha,
as is done to the god Mahendra (Indra),
to him bow down with reverence.

These lines were not taught for the sake of śrāvakas; they were taught for the sake of bodhisattvas.

He asked, "Blessed One, what of this? May such lax bodhisattvas be given full ordination, or may they not?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, do not speak like that. That itself is their full ordination, and whatever offenses of theirs they confess are confessions that are accepted. Son of good family, it is like an old, cracked pond, from which all the water leaks away. Why is that? Because the people who act in accordance with the Dharma dwindle, while the people who do not act in accordance with the Dharma increase."

If someone repairs that pond, water will no longer leak out of it. In the same way, the bodhisattva also sees that there are no monks endowed with moral discipline, that the practice of the Saṅgha has declined, and that laxity toward the vehicle has spread. Because monks then perform full ordination, the invitation ceremony at the end of the rains (pravāraṇā), the restoration ceremony, and acts of homage with joined palms and bowing together with the lax, moral discipline and ritual leak away, like a leaking pond. But, son of good family, the bodhisattva who wishes to bring forth the true Dharma, and the person endowed with moral discipline, are like the repair of the pond: it is they who are truly fully ordained. Having purified themselves through stainlessness and confession, they repair it by means of the very reality of the sūtras and the Vinaya, as one repairs a pond.

The eighth fascicle.

He asked, "In the sūtra spoken by the Blessed One on the simile of the four kinds of mango fruit, how should those endowed with moral discipline and those whose discipline is corrupt be recognized?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, those endowed with moral discipline and those whose discipline is corrupt are to be recognized from the great sūtra, the Mahāyāna sūtra of the Great Nirvāṇa."

He asked, "How are they to be recognized?"

The Blessed One said, "Suppose a certain householder farmer, at the very beginning, clears away the stones and weeds from his fields of sāli rice, grain, and millet, and makes them clean. From then on, until the crop has come to fruit, he counts it all as field with his fleshly eye. But when it has come to fruit, he knows precisely the difference between husk, empty ear, fruit, and kernel, thinking, 'This is grain; this is not grain.' In the same way, the eight great matters are the faults of the Saṅgha. Having removed them like the stones and weeds of the field, one thereafter counts, with the fleshly eye, those within the Saṅgha among the monks endowed with moral discipline. Whoever it may be, so long as he has not become faulty and the fruit of his qualities has not become evident, one cannot tell with the fleshly eye, 'This is fruit; this is bad grain.' In the meantime, like the field, they are all counted equally as members of the Saṅgha. Why is that? Because the fault of a field is its stones, and the fault of the Saṅgha is the eight great matters. The Saṅgha that is free of the worst of faults, which are like venomous snakes, is called a field. Whatever monk has become like a pure field is to be honored like a god and made a recipient of offerings. Before he has come to fruit, the fleshly eye cannot discern faults and qualities.

"Furthermore, son of good family, suppose that within the enclosure of a village there are many kāraka trees, and among them there is also a single tinduka tree. That tinduka tree bears fruit, and those kāraka trees bear fruit as well. Then a poor young girl who gathers fruit picks the tinduka fruit, and picks about ten times as much kāraka fruit, thinking it is tinduka fruit. She fills a basket with them and sells them in that same village. Then children, thinking that both kinds of fruit are tinduka fruit, buy them, eat them, and die. Then the discerning people who wish to eat tinduka fruit question the young girl carefully: 'Sister, from which direction did you get these?' She shows them the place, and when she has shown them, they see that there are many kāraka trees there and only a single tinduka tree. Realizing, 'These were taken from there,' they search for the tinduka fruit, and failing to find it, they are frightened and terrified of the kāraka fruit. Out of that very fear they reject the fruit and go back without buying.

"In the same way, son of good family, in whatever Saṅgha the eight great matters are knowingly practiced, if monks endowed with moral discipline are living among those who practice them there, and one sees them there like a tinduka tree standing in a kāraka grove, then even though they are endowed with moral discipline, a wise layman should not bow to them and should not make them objects of offering. If he wishes to bow to them, he should first ask, 'Do you accept the eight great matters, or do you not accept them?' and only then bow. He should ask, 'Do you perform the invitation ceremony and the acts of the restoration ceremony together with those who accept them, or do you not?'

"Suppose they reply, 'The eight great matters of the Saṅgha are accepted, for the Blessed One too, out of compassion for all beings, accepted gold and silver in the grove of Prince Jeta.' And suppose some among them do not accept them, saying, 'We do not perform the invitation ceremony, the restoration ceremony, or acts of homage with joined palms and bowing together with those who accept them. We do not even drink their gruel, and we accept neither gain nor honor from them.' And suppose others say, 'If even the Buddha, the Blessed One, the god of gods, accepted them, what is there that cannot be tolerated in the Saṅgha's accepting them?' Even if they speak in this way, one should say, 'Surely you have not performed the invitation ceremony, the restoration ceremony, or acts of homage with joined palms and bowing together with them?' Those who will not heed such words are the ones who know the true sūtras. Those who accept, who are like the children, should be known to be beings of the hells; those beings are like the children who died."

Furthermore, son of noble family, it is like this. In the incense markets of a great city, merchants sell māduraka incense, many other kinds of incense, and medicines. The buyers there do not know what the medicine that comes from the snow mountains is like, so they ask, "If you have even a little māduraka incense from the snow mountains, give it to us." The merchant takes something made to imitate incense from the snow mountains, says, "This is incense from the snow mountains," and hands it over. They fully believe, "This comes from the snow mountains," and so they accept the counterfeit incense. In just the same way, whether the monks are of corrupt discipline or possess moral discipline, the layman who has only the eye of flesh thinks of them, "These are the true Saṅgha of śrāvakas. These are the true Saṅgha. These are the harmonious Saṅgha," and he should bow to them and should give them gifts. For a layman with the eye of flesh, it is hard to tell whether something is genuine or counterfeit, just as it is hard in the incense market with the medicine from the snow mountains. Likewise, those who lack the divine eye cannot tell the difference, and so, just as with the medicine from the snow mountains, they cannot discern, "This one has moral discipline. This one has corrupt discipline. This Saṅgha is free from the faults of the eight great matters." Who possesses moral discipline and who has corrupt discipline is the domain of those who have the divine eye. It is not the domain of those who have only the eye of flesh. But if one sees that they are impure, then one should not bow to them and should not give them gifts until they have confessed the offenses they have committed that can be confessed. As for monks who accept the eight great matters, until they have confessed their own confessable offenses and so have become pure, like the medicine from the snow mountains, one should not bow to them simply because one trusts that they wear dyed robes. Nor should one give them gifts.

Then this was said: "Excellent, Blessed One! Excellent! This word of the Blessed One is well spoken, and I shall receive it on the crown of my head like a precious vajra. On this the Blessed One has said: 'Monks, these four are the reliances. What are the four? Monks, your reliance is the nature of reality (dharmatā), not the person. Monks, your reliance is the definitive meaning, not the provisional meaning. Monks, your reliance is wisdom, not consciousness. Monks, your reliance is the meaning, not the letter.' And it was well spoken when you said, 'For us these four reliances are authoritative; the four persons are not authoritative.'"

The Blessed One declared: "The nature of reality means the parinirvāṇa of the Tathāgata. That the Tathāgata is permanent, stable, and everlasting: this is the nature of reality of all buddhas. I say that those who see the Tathāgata as impermanent do not know the nature of reality. I say that they are mere persons. I say that they are not authoritative. The four persons other than these are authoritative. As for the four persons who are skilled in words spoken with hidden intent, who know the secret reality of the Tathāgata, and who know that he is permanent, it is impossible for them even to give rise to the thought, 'The Tathāgata is impermanent,' because those four persons are themselves tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas. If I say that other beings too, persons skilled in all the words of hidden intent that the Tathāgata is permanent, are authoritative and have become reliances, then how could those four not be reliances?

"'Monks, your reliance is the nature of reality' refers to the direct knowledge of the śrāvakas, and it means, 'Do not forget the nature of reality!' The nature of reality means that one should strive toward the truth that the Tathāgata is permanent, and not strive toward persons. It means that if persons of corrupt discipline who are greedy for material gain say, 'The Tathāgata is impermanent,' those persons are not authoritative.

"As for 'Monks, your reliance is the definitive meaning, not the provisional meaning': the definitive meaning is the Great Vehicle teachings. When one hears the words of hidden intent, the secret of the Tathāgata, it is called definitive because there is no fault of doubt. It means having crossed the great ocean of wisdom and having come forth from it. The definitive wisdom, the Great Vehicle, refers to unobstructed wisdom. Obstructed wisdom is that of the followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle. Definitive wisdom is called great wisdom and the Great Vehicle. Because those bodhisattvas who rightly realize that the Tathāgata is permanent make it certain, the words of bodhisattvas are authoritative, whereas the provisional meaning is not so. 'The Tathāgata has a body of flesh, and he passed into parinirvāṇa like a fire whose fuel is exhausted': this is the Śrāvaka Vehicle, and it is not authoritative. Those sūtras were taught according to those who were to be trained. The Śrāvaka Vehicle is like half-formed letters, and it makes distinctions; it does not serve as a master. The followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle are counted among the provisional meanings. Whatever knowledge does not comprehend the greatness of the Tathāgata as taught from wisdom is the Śrāvaka Vehicle. Therefore the provisional meaning is not authoritative. Those four persons are authoritative, for they are the definitive meaning and not the provisional meaning. Therefore they have become reliances, and they are the definitive meaning.

"'Monks, your reliance is wisdom' is a figurative expression of the Tathāgata. The Dharma body is not something to be measured. The body of skillful means is not something to be measured.

When one considers the body of skillful means, in what way are there aggregates, elements and sense sources in the body of the Tathāgata? Yet if there were none, where would the mass of relics have come from? Therefore, because of the relics, stūpas arise in the world, but the Dharma body does not arise.

The statement "the Tathāgata is a body of flesh" is taught from what consciousness examines, and consciousness is not a valid authority. That is why it is said that consciousness is not a valid authority. Those beings who conceive of him in that way are not valid authorities either, and sūtras of that kind are not valid authorities either.

In the saying "Monks, your reliance is the meaning," "meaning" is the clear meaning. "The clear meaning" is the illuminating meaning. "The illuminating meaning" is the meaning that is not feeble. "The meaning that is not feeble" is the meaning that the Tathāgata is permanent. "The meaning that the Tathāgata is permanent" is the meaning that the Dharma is permanent. "The meaning that the Dharma is permanent" is the meaning that the Saṅgha is permanent. That is the meaning, and that is the reliance.

"Not the letter" refers to self-fabricated writings connected with discontent, failing to reduce one's possessions, great desire, hypocrisy and flattery, and to sūtras of various words not spoken by the Buddha. They claim that the Blessed One, out of love for us, so that his children would not be afflicted when famine came, spoke training rules permitting male and female servants and the like and many kinds of stores of provisions, and that he said: "I permit male and female servants and the like, the amassing of gold, silver, cattle, buffalo and grain, trading, plowing fields, and so forth." Such statements are called "the letter," and sūtras and Vinaya of that kind are not valid authorities. Therefore the persons who recite the letter are not valid authorities either, and that is why it was said, "Monks, your reliance is the meaning."

"Meaningless" refers to the view that in the three bases there exists impermanence, such as destruction, falling, scattering and disintegration. This is called meaningless, and this is called the letter. Therefore it was said, "Monks, your reliance is the meaning, not the letter."

Those on the side of the meaning are the followers of the Great Vehicle. Therefore the followers of the Great Vehicle are valid authorities, but those outside this who rely on the letter are not so. Therefore, although they are not a reliance, they have been set forth for the sake of those who have only fleshly eyes.

He asked, "Should the four persons spoken of earlier be called reliances?"

The Blessed One said, "Noble son, that is so. The four should be known to be reliances. I say that the four who are not those are māras."

He asked, "Why is that so?"

He said, "Because they uphold sūtras spoken by Māra."

He asked, "What is that like?"

He said, "It is like the four māras who rise up against the Tathāgata and say, 'This was spoken by Māra, not by the Buddha. These beings accord with Māra, not with the Buddha.'"

He said, "Blessed One, I wish to understand what is spoken by Māra and what is spoken by the Buddha."

The Blessed One said, "Noble son, when seven hundred years have passed after my passing, Māra the Evil One will break my teaching apart piece by piece and destroy it. When the true Dharma declines, Māra the Evil One, who is defiled, will transform himself as if he were undefiled, and, just as one conceals the signs of what one is doing, he will destroy it in the guise of a monk, the guise of a nun, the guise of a layman, the guise of a laywoman, and in the guise of a stream-enterer, a once-returner, a non-returner, an arhat, a pratyekabuddha and a buddha. He will speak thus: 'The Buddha died and passed from the abode of Tuṣita, and when King Śuddhodana and Queen Māyā came together, he was born from the act of sexual union, in accordance with the particular time of her menses.' But it is impossible for the Tathāgata to be born from sexual union. They say that he was born in name only, and that by the virtue of austerities practiced in the past he came to be worshiped and praised by the world with its gods, humans and asuras, and that he says, 'I became a buddha by giving away wives and various kinds of desirable things as gifts.' Such statements should be known to be sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

"The Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, by the force of those to be trained, shows himself as though he were born, but he is not born from sexual union. Statements that say, 'The Tathāgata's showing himself as though he had both a father and a mother is conforming to the world,' should be known to be sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Tathāgata.

"Whatever beings take as valid authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be in accord with Māra. Whoever take as valid authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

"The statement that when the Tathāgata appeared in the world he was shown taking seven steps in each of the directions and intermediate directions is not to be believed; sūtras and Vinaya of that kind should be known to be spoken by Māra. Statements that say, 'The Tathāgata's being shown, when he appeared, taking seven steps in each of the directions and intermediate directions is the Tathāgata's assuming a body of skillful means,' should be known to be sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha.

"Whatever beings take as valid authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be in accord with Māra. Whoever take as valid authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

"The Tathāgata went to the temple of the gods, and when he arrived he paid homage at the feet of the gods, while the gods did not bow their heads at the Tathāgata's feet. After making offerings to the gods he became the Tathāgata and attained manifest awakening. The gods came first and he came later, and that is why he is called the manifestly and perfectly Awakened One." Statements such as these should be known to have been spoken by Māra.

"When they saw the Tathāgata going to the temple of the gods, all the gods, Īśvara, Maheśvara (Śiva), Brahmā, Śakra (Indra) and the rest, joined their palms reverently and touched his feet with the crowns of their heads." Statements like this should be known to be sūtra and Vinaya spoken by the Tathāgata.

Those beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be in accord with Māra. Those who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

"The Tathāgata, when he was crown prince, indulged freely in sensual pleasure with the daughters of the neighboring kings." Statements such as these should be known to be sūtra and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

"The Tathāgata cast aside even women like the divine maiden Rambhā as if they were spittle, and went forth from home into homelessness." Sūtra and Vinaya of this kind should be regarded as spoken by the Buddha.

Those beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be in accord with Māra. Those who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

"The Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly awakened Buddha, out of compassion for the whole world, accepted, in Prince Jeta's Grove, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, precious metal, gold, male servants, female servants, cattle, buffaloes, goats, sheep, chickens, pigs, dogs, cats, snakes, mongooses, copper, brass, jewels, pearls, conch, crystal, coral, gold dust, silver, household utensils of every kind, fields, markets, scales, engaging in trade, boys, girls, treasuries and storehouses." Statements like this should be known to be sūtra and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

"While he was dwelling in Prince Jeta's Grove, among the reeds, in the grove of castor plants, at the foot of a picumarda tree, he spoke concerning the brahmin Sheep Glory and Prasenajit, king of Kosala, and declared: Those who keep precious metal, gold, male servants, female servants, cattle, buffaloes, goats, sheep, chickens, pigs, dogs, cats, snakes, mongooses, copper, brass, jewels, pearls, conch, crystal, coral, gold dust, silver, wealth, and household utensils of every kind; who keep fields, markets, scales, who engage in trade, who keep boys, girls, treasuries and storehouses; who roll up their own sleeves and prepare food, who cook and have others cook; who practice omens, the science of bodily marks, divination texts, the recitation of chants, and the reading of bird omens; who observe Rāhu, the sun, the moon, Venus and the courses of the other planets, the flower chariot, the lines of the hand, and dream omens; who examine the marks of men, women and neuters; who teach the sixty-four arts, the eighteen fields of learning, the work of craftsmen and all the crafts; who tell tales; who sprinkle scented powders; who wear flowers, incense, perfumes, garlands and unguents; who kiss heads; who are hypocritical, flatter, know no contentment, have great desires, delight in bustle and love to chatter; who prepare wealth, grain, meat, fish and poisons; who keep scented sesame oil, medicine tubes, parasols, shoes, palm-leaf fans, water vessels, satchels and clubs; who gather fruit; who indulge in tooth powder, medicated butter, fine refined powders, kakkola and pungent fruits; who rely on the king and rely on others; who cannot bear heat; who keep silence; who are much given to thinking like women; who talk a great deal, weep, judge good and bad, and disparage others; who affect a special gait; who wear white clothes and fine cloth; who busy themselves with building mansions and lofty shrines and the like; who acquire what is not allowable and bad articles; who proclaim the praises of donors; who indulge in liquor; who go to the houses of prostitutes: to those who do such things, who are not holy and who are of bad conduct, I permit that white clothes be given and that they be made to do worldly work. Or else I permit them to be driven out of the region, as stones are cleared from a field of śāli rice." Sūtra and Vinaya of this kind should be known to be spoken by the Buddha.

Those beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be in accord with Māra. Those who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

The Tathāgata does not go to any of the abodes of the gods, of Brahmā, the bhūtas, the Desiccator, Kumāra, the Grandfather of the World, Kātyāyana and the rest, in order to crush their pride; he does not go, and it is impossible that he should go. He does not go forth among any of the wonder-workers. He is not a teacher of the imaginings of all beings. He does not keep any secret mantras or vidyā-mantras. He does not engage servants, male servants or female servants, and he does not arrange reconciliations or quarrels. He does not hold men, women, physicians, kings or ministers dear. If the Tathāgata were like that, he would not be the Tathāgata, and he would have to be called one of wrong view.

"The Tathāgata is like the axe and the sandalwood tree: he neither punishes anyone nor does anyone any good. Therefore the Tathāgata is called one who sees the middle path." A statement such as this should be known as a confusing sūtra spoken by Māra.

"The Tathāgata goes to all the abodes of the gods. He goes forth into homelessness among all the heterodox orders. He shows the thoughts of all sentient beings. He makes use of secret mantras, vidyā-mantras, and medicines of every kind. He goes into assemblies and into places of music. He gathers in servants, maidservants, and all sentient beings. He also displays the various bodies of youths, maidens, the queens' retinue, palace attendants, country folk, householders, brahmins, hungry ghosts, the destitute, men and women. And when he goes here and there to free the whole world from suffering, he understands without being stained by views, just as a lotus is not soiled by water. This is how he acts in accord with the whole world." A statement such as this should be known as the sūtra and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha.
