Those sentient beings who take as authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known as conforming to Māra. Those who take as authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known as bodhisattvas.

"Thus in our Vinaya, misdeeds, wrongdoings, light offenses, heavy offenses, and gross offenses are by their very nature to be abandoned, and therefore the Blessed One said that they are to be abandoned. Such things accord with our Vinaya but do not accord with your Vinaya, so why should we go against our own Vinaya and adopt yours? Your Vinaya is a fabrication, while our Vinaya was spoken by the Blessed One. It is well known that the Blessed One made nine seals from the nine-limbed scripture; the Blessed One sealed it with those nine seals. We have never before heard so much as a single sound, word, letter, or dot of what is called the Vaipulya. How many sūtras did the Blessed One teach? Did he teach ten sūtras? In our scriptures there is not even the sound of the word Vaipulya. All of these were made by Devadatta, who ruins every meaning and then argues. The Vaipulya was fabricated by its own authors and is therefore not authoritative, whereas our Vinaya is sublime. Ours is like this! Ours is like this!" This rejection of the Vaipulya compositions in order to ruin the teaching should be known as the sūtra and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

"In the future, too, monks will appear who speak a false Vinaya and false sūtras, saying, 'Our Vinaya is like this. In our Vinaya the Blessed One said such and such.' From the nine-limbed scripture of the Great Vehicle, I taught the Later Section, the Great Vehicle Vaipulya. Those who practice together with me in my presence are not ones who speak a false Vinaya and false views. My teaching has abandoned everything improper; it is completely pure, stainless, like the full moon. I have taught the meanings of each sūtra, each Vinaya, and each basis of training in numbers like the sands of the Ganges River, and these too I have taught through many different verbal synonyms. Whoever says, 'Whatever bases of training are not found in our Vinaya, those the Blessed One did not teach,' a fault will arise for every one of them. 'Even though we are unable to uphold them all, whatever concerns contentment and devotion to few possessions, and whatever is fit to be the word of the Buddha, all of that he taught in the manner of the Great Vehicle, out of benefit and compassion for all sentient beings. Therefore we too shall expound what is called the Vaipulya.' I am the teacher of those who make such a pledge; of those who do not, I am not the teacher. They have not gone forth for my sake; all of them are sons of tīrthikas." A statement such as this should be known as the sūtra and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha. Proclaim it thus!

Those sentient beings who take as authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known as conforming to Māra. Those who take as authority the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known as bodhisattvas.

"The Tathāgata does not possess immeasurable qualities. He is impermanent. He has passed into complete nirvāṇa. He has become empty. He teaches selflessness. He does not act in accord with the world." A sūtra that says such things should be known as the sūtra and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

"The Buddha, the Blessed One, is inconceivable and possesses inconceivable, immeasurable, countless qualities. He is permanent. He is stable. He is everlasting. This is what I say." One who speaks thus should not be said to have incurred defeat, like a palm tree whose top has been cut off. What need is there to mention all four of what the Blessed One called the four defeats? If even any one of the four defeats occurs, one becomes like a broken stone. This was said in the complete text of the Last of the Later Sections, in the passage on superhuman states. Why is that? It refers to claiming, "I have attained the fruit."

What I taught in the Later of the Later concerning the case of a monk brought down in regard to the superhuman qualities is this. Suppose a certain monk lives here who has few desires and is content. When the king or the king's great ministers see him, they think he has attained the fruit. They praise him by many means, composing it in verse, saying, "This venerable one, once he has parted from the body and died, will become a buddha." That monk hears this from them directly and says, "Do not praise a person who has not attained the fruit as if he had attained it. The Buddha has censured the craving for greatness. The Buddha has praised keeping silent. As long as I live I take no delight in such things, so do not set me in the craving for greatness like this. I myself know that I have not attained the fruit."

The wise king thinks, "This venerable one has attained the fruit; he is a buddha," and proclaims it among the learned throughout the world. He also has his queens' retinue and his princes learn the sūtras as though they were poetry. Even when this is done, much merit grows for the king and ministers who give the praise. I say that this monk is simply one who leads the holy life and keeps moral discipline, and that he has not incurred a defeat. Nor has he committed the offense concerning superhuman qualities. I say he keeps moral discipline and has few desires. Even though he has been praised in this way, his mind is clear with faith and he accords with the Buddha's scriptures, and I say that such a monk has not the slightest fault.

Moreover, a certain monk here teaches the great sūtra of the tathāgatagarbha. He says that all sentient beings have the buddha-element, and each carries that element in their own body. Once sentient beings have exhausted every form of the afflictions they become buddhas, except for those of great desire (icchantikas). Then some king or king's minister says to him, "Monk, will you become a buddha or not? Do you have the buddha-element or not?" The monk replies, "Venerable sir, who could know whether I will become a buddha or not? Even so, the buddha-element is present in my body." Then the other says to the monk, "Monk, if you are not an icchantika, think, 'I shall become a buddha.'" And the monk says to him, "Venerable sir, that quality is certainly present in my body." If he says this, and someone asks whether that monk should be said to have committed the offense concerning superhuman qualities or not, the answer should be: "Although he said the element is present, it is present only in those endowed with generosity, moral discipline, knowledge, and the Dharma."

Moreover, after going forth a monk may reflect, "Ah, will I become a buddha or not?" Or he may think, "It is clear that I shall attain buddhahood." If he thinks this, much merit grows for him, even though he has not attained the fruit. If it were not so, every monk would be guilty of claiming superhuman qualities. And why? Because for eighty koṭis of eons I gave up keeping unsuitable possessions. I lived in supreme contentment with few requisites and meditated that the tathāgatagarbha is present. Having awakened fully and perfectly, I became the Buddha, the Blessed One, endowed with great compassion, who teaches a hundred thousand sections of the Dharma.

Teaching of this kind should be known as the sūtras and the Vinaya spoken by the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha. Those sentient beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known as followers of Māra. Those who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known as bodhisattvas.

Now suppose it is said: "There are no four defeats. There are no thirteen offenses entailing suspension from the Saṅgha. There are no two undetermined offenses. There are no thirty downfalls requiring forfeiture. There are no ninety-one simple downfalls. There are no four offenses to be confessed individually. There are no many trainings. There are no seven means for settling disputes. There are no misdeeds. There are no grave offenses. There are no acts of immediate retribution, and there are no icchantikas. If monks went to hell for transgressing the training rules just listed, when those rules do not even exist, that would be the teaching of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). For those to whom these rules do not apply, it follows that they will go to the higher realms. The Blessed One proclaimed the training rules in the Vinaya only to frighten people. Whoever wishes to engage in unchaste conduct should give up the dyed garments and the Dharma robes and put on white clothes. Having done so, he may think to himself, 'Because my own good qualities are few, I indulge in sensual pleasures of little worth, but there is nothing wrong in it. Even while the Blessed One was alive, monks indulged in sensual pleasures and still reached the higher realms and liberation. So have I not entered into the nature of reality? This has existed by custom since ancient times.' A monk who reflects in this way will attain liberation even if he has incurred a defeat. It is the same for transgressing the five precepts and for keeping every kind of unsuitable possession. The Tathāgata did say that by committing a misdeed one will burn in hell for eighty thousand years, counted even in the years of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, or longer still. Yet that too was only to frighten. Nor is the fault of a grave offense, one degree lower than a defeat, anything severe. Many holders of the Vinaya have spoken falsely about it, but the Tathāgata said that it carries no force."

Sūtras and Vinaya of this kind should be known as spoken by Māra.

Among all the bases of training, a misdeed (duṣkṛta) is a minor one. Yet even when such a minor misdeed has occurred, someone denies it, saying, "It did not happen to me," and hides the offense he has committed as a turtle hides its limbs. One should not associate with a person like that. A person who has broken the single rule against speaking falsehood has abandoned the world beyond, and there is nothing he will not do. This means that there is no improper conduct whatsoever that he would not resort to. Since the Buddha's teaching is subtle in this way, how could it fail to restrain grave offenses? If someone does not restrain even that much, he is utterly unrestrained.

If one asks what one holds to in guarding moral discipline: the buddha element exists. With the single conception of becoming a buddha in the manner of the ninefold scripture, to say, "All sentient beings have the buddha element," and to say, "In the extensive sūtras (vaipulya) there is the supreme selflessness," is not to reject the sūtras in the manner of the ninefold scripture. Otherwise, it would be so.

He said, "If the ninefold scripture teaches definitively that all sentient beings have the buddha element, how does that not become a view of a person? How should I hold it, just as I have heard it? If it were so, it would also amount to claiming a superhuman quality."

The Blessed One replied, "Just like someone who says that in the ocean there are no jewels of various kinds endowed with the eight qualities is someone who says that in the great ocean of wisdom of the extensive ninefold scripture there is no buddha element. This speech of the Buddha is not their domain. The Buddha's speech, by way of the reasons and signs that all sentient beings have buddha-nature, is evidently not the domain even of all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. In the Great Vehicle, there is no destruction of self and life force either. Therefore the Buddha is called the Middle Way.

"Those who say, 'There is the buddha element in my body, and I will attain buddhahood, but I must exhaust the afflictions,' are my śrāvakas. As for the other kind, I say that he has committed the offense of claiming a superhuman quality. Because I have taught in this way, someone says, 'Since the Blessed One sees from the buddha ground at which the holy Dharma is attained, I too shall become a buddha, a blessed one.' If one asks whether one who says this commits the offense of claiming a superhuman quality, he does not commit that offense. I say that he will gradually become a buddha. Thus the speech of the Buddha is subtle and profound."

He said, "Here some king may say, 'How is it that a monk is said to have committed the offense of claiming a superhuman quality?' An answer to this is needed."

The Blessed One replied, "Here there are monks who, for the sake of gain and honor, or for material things, put on false manners and flatter. They think, 'How should I act so that others will know me as one who lives on alms? If I act in that way, I will obtain gain.' Having become deluded by nature and greedy for praise, they think, 'Though I have not attained the fruit, I shall make others know that I have attained the fruit. I shall make others know that I am a stream-enterer, a once-returner, a non-returner, an arhat. I shall get the laymen sitting before me to speak words by which all people will know that I am such a one.' Attending in this way, not for the sake of attaining the Dharma, but for gain, honor, veneration as a teacher, prostrations, and material things, they put on the manner of arhats in looking at one another, in half-closing their eyes, and in their gait. They carry their robes, their outer robe, and their alms bowls. They sit on new beds and seats, thinking, 'I shall make people say of me that I am a good monk, an ascetic monk. Moreover, I will have a retinue and disciples. Then, together with whole companies of followers, I will obtain requisites of food to eat, to chew, to lick, and to drink. Women too will honor me.' With such a conception in mind, they make it known to household members and servants. A monk or a nun who conceals things in this way, I say, has committed the offense of claiming a superhuman quality.

"Again, here some monk, with the thought, 'I shall bring forth the holy Dharma,' sits on beds and new seats and thinks, 'Though I am not an arhat, I shall make it known that I am like an arhat, so that all people will think, Oh, this monk is truly virtuous and good. Because I am known in this way, and many monks become my retinue, even non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) will become lay followers. Those of corrupt morality will also be humbled. Then I shall bring forth the holy Dharma. I shall show the greatness of the Tathāgata. I shall proclaim the extensive sūtras. I shall surely free all sentient beings. I shall distinguish what is Vinaya from what is not Vinaya. In me there is the seed of becoming a buddha, called buddha-nature, and I shall attain buddhahood. I shall destroy the forms of affliction. You laymen also have the buddha element; we shall all become buddhas and attain the ground of the Tathāgata. We shall exhaust the forms of affliction.' One who thinks in this way is a bodhisattva by way of his conception of the holy Dharma, and I say that he has not committed the offense of claiming a superhuman quality.

"If even by committing a misdeed one is cooked in hell for eighty thousand years and more, reckoned in the years of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, what need is there to speak of a grave offense? In this Great Vehicle, one should not associate with one who has committed a grave offense."

"Grave offense" means this. Suppose that, in order to look after a stūpa of the Blessed One, a flower garland belonging to someone else has been tied on it, and it has grown old. If a monk takes it because he needs the thread, and, being shy of the monks, takes it without asking them, he becomes guilty of a grave offense. And if he takes it thinking, "Something like this makes no difference at all," one should still not associate with such people. If someone tears down a stūpa with the intention of stealing, he too becomes guilty of a grave offense, and one should not associate with such people.

Suppose that here some householder or minister, with a mind set on the Dharma, wishes to honor the relics, or needs bricks, and so tears down an old stūpa and takes its bricks. If that householder or minister then finds gold or silver in the stūpa and places it in the hands of some monk, and that monk takes it, makes it his own property and quarrels over it, that is not good for the monk. Those lay followers abide in the Dharma. That monk cannot be trusted, and one should not associate with him. He should be known as a eunuch. He should be known as a hermaphrodite.

"Hermaphrodite" means one who has both sexual organs. When male desire arises, such a person has sexual relations with women, and when female desire arises, with men. That is why such a person is called a hermaphrodite, and one should not associate with that person either. If, then, one should not associate even with people like these, how could one even look upon those who commit offenses against the five bases of training?

Fascicle nine.

First of all, monks, following the Dharma of a renunciant, should have compassion even for ants. Following the Dharma of a renunciant, they should also give up any drink that smells of liquor. Following the Dharma of a renunciant, they should not give rise to the thought of lying, even in a dream. Following the Dharma of a renunciant, they should not give rise to the thought of meeting a woman, even in a dream.

He asked, "How is it? If one meets a woman in a dream, does that amount to the fault of unchaste conduct?"

The Blessed One said, "It does not. If one who has intelligence and mindfulness does not give rise to the thought of unchaste conduct toward foul-smelling sense pleasures, then, because he has reviled the afflictions and talk of women, his conceptual thinking has been completely cut off, and so it is impossible for that thought to arise. Even if he meets a woman in a dream, when he wakes the next morning he feels regret, like a monk in a time of famine who has eaten food made of his own son's flesh. How then could the notion 'That is pure' ever arise in him? And even if the thought does arise, he should crush it again."

A Dharma teaching that says such things should be known to be the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha. Those sentient beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be siding with Māra. Those who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

Suppose it is said: "The Blessed One has permitted sitting in the heroic posture, remaining silent, leaping from precipices, eating poison, fasting, binding oneself, killing oneself, having others carry out malicious deeds, reciting vidyā-mantras and secret mantras, and practicing the arts of concoction. The Blessed One has permitted the sons of caṇḍālas, eunuchs, hermaphrodites, and all those with defective limbs to go forth. The Tathāgata gave up the five products of the cow, honey, sesame oil and the like, silk, conch, shoes, grain and the like, and, seeing that all trees have life and perception, passed into complete nirvāṇa." What says such things should be known to be sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra.

Dharma activities such as remaining in the heroic posture and walking are not included in this. "Eating poison, leaping into fire, fasting, leaping from precipices, binding oneself, killing oneself, having others carry out malicious deeds, reciting vidyā-mantras and secret mantras, and practicing the arts of concoction: these I have not permitted. The five products of the cow, honey, sesame oil and the like, silk, conch, shoes, grain and the like I have permitted, without perceiving them as meat, with the perception that no tree has life, and without perceiving them as having minds. Those who see it otherwise are śrāvakas of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Those who act in accordance with my word are my śrāvakas; this I declare. I do not say that the four elements have life and perception." Sūtras and Vinaya that say such things should be known to be the sūtras spoken by the Buddha.

Those sentient beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by Māra should be known to be siding with Māra. Those sentient beings who take as authoritative the sūtras and Vinaya spoken by the Buddha should be known to be bodhisattvas.

"Son of noble family, I have now explained in detail the difference between what Māra speaks and what the Buddha speaks."

He said, "Blessed One, only now have I truly entered upon the Buddha's word."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent, son of noble family! You have done well, son of noble family; the wise should act just so."

He also said, "Son of noble family, 'This is suffering' is not to be called the truth of the noble ones. If one asks what that suffering is, what is called suffering exists also in cattle, donkeys and the like, and in hell beings. If that suffering were the truth, it would be their truth as well. As for what is called suffering, oh, what is called the knowledge of suffering is this: the Tathāgata is permanent; he is the Dharma body, not a body of flesh. To know the greatness of the Tathāgata in this way is the knowledge of suffering. Until they have attained that, sentient beings imagine that what is not Dharma is Dharma, and so for a long time they experience suffering in the wheel of saṃsāra, a place of intense suffering, and then go to the lower realms."

When one knows that the Tathāgata is permanent, then however one hears those two syllables, one will go to a happy destiny, and after that one will also attain liberation. One then realizes for oneself, "This is the bliss of miraculous power. It is through the mere cause of having heard the words 'the Tathāgata is permanent' that I have come here. For as long as I did not know the Tathāgata to be permanent, for all that time I circled among sufferings." To say, "Now I know suffering," this is the truth. The meditation on suffering of others is meaningless.

As for "This is the origin of suffering": not knowing the suchness of the Dharma as suchness, people accept many impure goods, such as male and female servants, for the needs of the Dharma, and they take what is not Dharma to be Dharma. In this way they cause the true Dharma to decline and to disappear. Because they do not know the means by which the true Dharma is made to endure, they hold that there is no such thing as the true Dharma perishing, and as the result of that they wander among the sufferings of saṃsāra. Afterwards they attain the higher realms and liberation. To know, "Because I did not know the reality of the origin of suffering, by which the higher realms and liberation are attained, I became a cause of the destruction of the true Dharma, and through wrongly spoken words I have been afflicted for this long": this should be called knowing the origin of suffering. That of others is not like this.

As for "This is the cessation of suffering": it is not the kind of meditation on emptiness which, meditating on utter emptiness in which everything is exhausted, makes buddha-nature nonexistent. If meditating on the cessation of suffering were that, then even all the heterodox have an emptiness that runs contrary to the meaning, and they too would be said to have the cessation of suffering. All sentient beings have buddha-nature, but it is not clearly manifest. When the afflictions are exhausted, then you will see the entry into buddha-nature. When you come to know, "Through the karmic fruit of generating a single thought, I attain the permanence and bliss of the lord of the Dharma," that is to meditate on what is to be meditated on as the cessation of suffering. By meditating on buddha-nature as selfless and meditating on utter emptiness, sufferings do not cease; it becomes like a moth flying into the flame of a lamp. Not so: sentient beings who meditate on buddha-nature as existing stop the aspect of affliction even while the afflictions are still present. Why is that? Because of the condition of buddha-nature. Knowing this is called, according to the word of the perfectly complete Buddha, the true word of the cessation of suffering. Anything else is meditating on what is not meditation.

As for "This is the path leading to the cessation of suffering": it refers to the Tathāgata, the Dharma, the Saṅgha and liberation. Thus, the thing that has become inverted with regard to them through the four inversions is liberation. To think that in liberation too there is nothing at all is in this way to dwell in illusion and to wander among the sufferings of saṃsāra, and through the fruit of that wrong meditation one comes to experience suffering for a long time. "Now, through the fruit of generating a single thought that the Tathāgata, the Dharma, the Saṅgha and liberation are permanent, stable, everlasting and indestructible, I attain the fruit of permanent sovereignty. Having held the four inversions, suffering, selflessness and impurity, taking what is not Dharma to be Dharma, I experienced the fruit of wrong views." This is called the path leading to the cessation of suffering.

Those who meditate on impermanence in order to accomplish the meditation on the three bases as permanent are meditating on a false meditation. Those who meditate on them as permanent in this way are my śrāvakas; I say that they know the Four Noble Truths. These are to be understood as the Four Noble Truths.

It was said: "Blessed One, only now do I understand that one should meditate in the manner of this path of the Four Noble Truths."

The Blessed One declared: To perceive suffering as bliss is an inversion. To perceive bliss as suffering is an inversion. Here, to say "the Tathāgata is impermanent" is wrongdoing and suffering. To say "the Tathāgata has passed into parinirvāṇa like a fire whose fuel is exhausted" is wrongdoing and suffering. To meditate oneself, thinking "it is not suffering," and to say "the Tathāgata is impermanent," this is an inversion. To perceive bliss as suffering is an inversion: to think, "If one meditates that the Tathāgata is permanent, one may come to hold a view of a person," and so to think, "this meditation is suffering," is the inversion of those who perceive bliss. This is the first inversion.

To perceive the impermanent as permanent is an inversion. To perceive the permanent as impermanent is an inversion. Here, to think, "If one meditates on utter emptiness, one's life will become short," and therefore to meditate on impermanence as not empty, thinking, "Through the fruit of that meditation I will become long-lived," that too is an inversion. This is the second inversion.

To perceive what is without self as self is an inversion. To perceive what has a self as without self is an inversion. Here, to meditate on selflessness, saying, "Worldly people say there is a self, but unlike the worldly, in the Buddha's word there is no self; there is not even the name of buddha-nature," this is the third inversion.

To perceive the impure as pure is an inversion. To perceive the pure as impure is an inversion. Here, the Tathāgata is permanent; yet to take what is not a body of flesh to be a body of flesh, bound together with flesh, blood and sinews, and to say "it is impure,"

To think that the Dharma and the Saṅgha too will disappear once liberation is attained is the inversion of taking the pure as impure. My whole body is impure, and since there is nothing at all that can be called pure, to meditate thinking, "My body is pure," is grasping the impure as pure. To think, "I too shall go to where there is nothing at all," is to meditate on the pure as impure. It is a factor that causes delusion, and this should be understood as the fourth inversion.

He said, "Blessed One, only now have I come to hold right view; until now I held wrong views. Blessed One, how is it? In the twenty-five kinds of existence, should we say that there is a self, or that there is not?"

The Blessed One said, "'Self' means the tathāgatagarbha. The buddha-element is present in all sentient beings. But it is obscured by the various forms of the afflictions, so although it is present in themselves, sentient beings cannot see it.

"It is like this. Suppose that in a great city, in a poor household, there was an inexhaustible treasure of gold. A poor woman lived there, and she did not know that there was a treasure beneath the ground of her own house. Then a man skilled in means said to the woman, 'Mother, come here. I will pay you wages. Go and work in my field.'

"She answered him, 'Sir, I will not go. If you show my son the treasure, then I will go.' So she spoke words grounded in reason.

"He said to her, 'Mother, as soon as my son is born of you, I will give you an inexhaustible treasure of jewels and gold.'

"Then she said to him, 'How could you ever give that? You are only making fun of me.'

"Then he said to her, 'There is a treasure in your own house. But if even you yourself do not know it, how could anyone else know to look for it?'

"Then she entreated him, saying, 'I wish to obtain it.' So he dug the treasure out from inside that house and gave it to her. When she saw it she was filled with wonder and went to him for refuge.

"In the same way, son of good family, the tathāgatagarbha is present in all sentient beings. They simply cannot see it, just as the poor woman had a treasure of her own. Son of good family, I shall rightly teach that the tathāgatagarbha is present in all sentient beings. The poor woman did not know of her great treasure and had to be shown it. In the same way, sentient beings neither know nor see that the tathāgatagarbha is present in all beings, because it is obscured by the forms of the afflictions. The Tathāgata shows it to them, and, delighted, they go to the Tathāgata for refuge."

He said, "Blessed One, now I understand that the buddha-element exists. I shall regard the Tathāgata, who gives the instructions, as like that man skilled in means, and I shall regard all sentient beings as like the poor woman."

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, here is another example. Suppose there was a woman whose son, still very small, fell ill. The woman called a physician, and the physician gave the child medicinal ghee mixed with sugar. Then he instructed the mother, 'Lady, do not give the child the breast until the medicinal ghee has been digested.'

"So that the child would not suckle, the woman smeared both her breasts with a paste of ground neem leaves. She told her son, 'My child, my breasts are smeared with poison. My child, do not suckle.'

"The little boy was thirsty and wanted to suckle. But when he smelled the neem leaves on her breasts, he cried, and he stayed apart by himself until the course of treatment was over.

"When the treatment was finished, the woman washed both her breasts with water and said to her son, 'O my child, now you may suckle.' But the boy thought there was still poison. He would not suckle, and he sat there crying, refusing even to look.

"Then she spoke to him again: 'Earlier your medicinal ghee had not yet been digested. To keep you from suckling, I smeared both my breasts with neem paste. I did not let you suckle because I feared it would harm you and you would die. Now you may suckle.' She let him suckle, and slowly he began to suckle.

"In the same way, son of good family, earlier, for the sake of those to be trained, I acted as one does when a child's medicinal ghee must be digested. I wanted the monks to destroy worldly views. I wanted to show rightly the greatness of what transcends the world. I wanted to show rightly that the worldly notion of a real self is false. And I wanted the body to be purified through meditation on selflessness. So I said, 'Monks, meditate on all phenomena as selfless. When you meditate in this way, you will abandon clinging to "I." When clinging to "I" has been completely abandoned, you will attain nirvāṇa.' Just as the child's mother smeared her breasts with neem paste, so I too said, 'Meditate on all phenomena as selfless, as emptiness.'

"Later the child's mother washed her breasts and said to her son, 'Earlier I did not let you suckle, so that your medicinal ghee could be digested. Now suckle.' In the same way, in order to turn beings away from worldly ideas, I taught as I did and said, 'There is no self.' Now I teach, 'The tathāgatagarbha exists.'

"Monks, do not be frightened as the child was. The child looked into it and then suckled at his mother's breast again. In the same way, monks, you too should examine this, thinking, 'We have the tathāgatagarbha,' and strive in meditation. This is what I have now taught."

He said, "Yet when a child is just born, it has little understanding, or none at all. It does not make sense that it should then become clear-minded. If there were a self, it would make sense for the child to have understanding and intelligence from the start. And if its understanding is weak, it does not make sense that it should later become clear. Therefore I say that there is no self. Why is that? If the self were permanent, it would make no sense for one who has been born to die."

"If the tathāgata-element were permanent, why do differing distinctions appear in it? Distinct differences of birth also appear, arising from the distinctions of karma by which one becomes a brahmin, a kṣatriya, a vaiśya, a śūdra, or a caṇḍāla, or comes to a birth among animals. If there were a self, a self without distinctions would be sameness itself, and in that case there would be no element in it. If there is an element, why does one engage in the ten paths of nonvirtuous action, such as killing, stealing the wealth of others, and sexual misconduct? Why does one become intoxicated by drinking liquor? If the element of sentient beings were permanent, then the deaf too ought to hear with their ears, the blind too ought to see, and the mute too ought to be eloquent. If the self, the element of sentient beings, were permanent, in which part of the self do the masses of phlegm, of the embryonic mass, of blue and of yellow reside? And in what place among them does that self reside? Or does it reside everywhere, like moisture? If that were so, then when a man's ears and nose are cut off in battle, the limbs of that self too ought to be impaired, or it ought to become maimed."

He declared: "Son of noble family, suppose a certain king's wrestler had a precious vajra fastened between the eyebrows on his forehead, and that wrestler contested his strength with another wrestler. As the two were wrestling in the customary way, the other wrestler butted him with his head, injured his forehead, and drove the ornament between his eyebrows entirely inside. The wrestler did not feel it, and thought, 'Where can I have put my vajra?' He summoned a physician and had him treat the wound. That physician was skilled in means and sharp of mind. Knowing that the wound had come from the jewel, he thought, 'The vajra has sunk inside the forehead and is lodged there.' He asked the wrestler, 'O man, where has that jewel of yours gone?' The wrestler said to the physician, 'Alas, physician, I must have lost the vajra that adorned my brow. Oh dear, I do not know whether I put it in a box or where I put it; even when I search in the chest I cannot find it. Oh dear, curse it, it is gone! If it has vanished like a bubble on water, has it become like an illusion?' Then the physician said to him, 'O man, do not grieve like this. That vajra of yours has sunk into the wound and is shining there amid the flesh, blood and pus. You were simply so absorbed in wrestling that you did not feel it.' Though the physician said this, the wrestler did not believe him, and said to the physician, 'Then why did it not come out with the pus and blood? Or is it caught on a sinew? Do not say such things.' Then the physician drew out the vajra and showed it to him, and when he saw it he was amazed.

"In the same way, all sentient beings possess buddha-nature, but sentient beings do not know it. Through the fault of relying on nonvirtuous friends, and being overwhelmed by attachment, aversion and delusion, sentient beings are born as hell-beings, animals, hungry ghosts, asuras, caṇḍālas, brahmins, kṣatriyas, vaiśyas and śūdras. Because they do not know it, through the power of their own faults and through attachment, aversion and delusion, sentient beings become dull-witted, deaf, blind, maimed, withered on one side, stained with various impurities, with yellow eyes, with the color of lentils, with the color of kuraṇṭaka flowers, with reddish-yellow eyes, and take various forms in the twenty-five kinds of existence.

"Just as that wrestler, though the vajra was in his own body, through impaired perception imagined it lost, so worldly sentient beings, not realizing the reality of the self, falling under the sway of nonvirtuous friends, and not understanding words spoken with a hidden intent, meditate on no-self even though the self exists. Those who do not meditate on no-self meditate on a worldly self. Just as those who say 'a worldly self exists' do not realize the reality of the self, so it is too with the supramundane: through relying on nonvirtuous friends and not knowing the final import of words spoken with a hidden intent, even while meditating 'there is no self, there is no self,' they do not know the reality of no-self. They think: 'Where is the self? Where does it reside? Is it like a pot inside a pot? Or does it pervade like moisture? Or is it like the ornament on the bewildered wrestler's brow?' To them, like the physician, the Tathāgata declares: 'The self does not reside everywhere; like the precious vajra it is luminous, and it is obscured by the aspects of the afflictions.' When he has declared this, sentient beings come to believe it, and all that remains is to exhaust the afflictions. Then, like the vajra, they will see the buddha-nature. Son of noble family, in this way the buddha-nature is immeasurable.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, on the snowy mountains, for example, there is an elixir-element called the Bee Essence. Though it is hidden by a dense forest of gelpa trees, it is revealed by its fragrance. In the past a certain wheel-turning king, for its sake, set up pipes on every side, and from that elixir-element there came, from the mouths of some pipes, a sour juice; from some, a salty taste; from some, sweetness; from some, pungency; from some, bitterness; and from some, astringency. Such an elixir-element hovers in the air within the forest. From that single-tasting elixir arise various different tastes, yet these tastes do not mix with one another, and that elixir-element remains all-pervading, like the disc of the moon."

"Although it remains there in this way, when other beings enter that forest of gelpa trees and dig with hoes, beings of little fortune do not obtain it, whereas a wheel-turning king obtains it by the power of his merit. In the same way, son of noble family, the tathāgatagarbha is like that elixir-element, and it remains obscured by the various forms of the afflictions. Although, as the cause of becoming a buddha, it has a single taste, through the karmic maturation of beings it turns into many tastes, and males, females and neuters come into being.

"That tathāgata-element is the very nature of the person. Therefore, when a life is cut off, the living being is not killed. If the living being were killed, the life-principle would be made utterly nonexistent, and it is impossible for the life-principle to become utterly nonexistent. Here, what is called 'the life-principle' is the tathāgatagarbha. That element cannot be destroyed, killed or made utterly nonexistent, and until buddhahood is attained it cannot be seen as completely pure. Therefore there is no person who kills living beings."

Kāśyapa said, "One cannot say that there is none. If there were none, it would follow that killing living beings is no wrongdoing."

The Blessed One declared, "One should say that there is. How so? Destroying the outer, accumulated element, which is like the gelpa tree, is called taking life. Therefore, through taking life, the killer goes to the lower realms because of the mass of wrongdoing. And through the maturation of bad karma, beings take rebirth as brahmins, kṣatriyas, vaiśyas, śūdras and caṇḍālas, as birds and as wild animals, under the names of the three different sexes, with different dispositions, and in the twenty-five states of existence.

"Worldly people conceive of the self as being the size of the fruit of the śyāmāka plant, the size of a grain of rice, the size of a thumb, and so on. That is a false conception. The transcendent conception is the conception that the buddha-element exists, and that is the conception of ultimate truth.

"Again, son of noble family, suppose a man who knows how to work a mine digs for treasure with a hoe or some other digging tool. He can grind all the stones and gravel to powder, but he can never cut a vajra even slightly. Just as no weapon whatever can cut the precious vajra, in the same way, son of noble family, the tathāgatagarbha, the self of the one who takes birth, cannot be cut by any weapon, even by tens of millions of gods and māras, whereas the accumulated element can be ground to powder like stones and gravel. The tathāgatagarbha, the life-principle, is like the precious vajra. So when people's lives are cut off, their living is destroyed, and therefore, by destroying the accumulated element, living beings are killed.

"Son of noble family, in this way the Buddha's teaching of the Vaipulya is supremely subtle. It is the supreme nectar, and it is also the supreme poison."

Kāśyapa said, "Blessed One, with what intention did you declare that the Vaipulya is both the supreme nectar and the supreme poison?"

The Blessed One declared, "Son of noble family, do you wish to know this intentional statement?"

Kāśyapa said, "Blessed One, I wish to know the supreme utterance of intentional statements."

Then the Blessed One spoke these verses:

"Some who drink nectar do not die;
Some, if they drink it, die.
Some, even drinking poison, do not die;
Some, if they drink it, die.
In this Great Vehicle,
Wisdom free of attachment is nectar,
And that supreme, unequaled wisdom
Is also taught to be poison.
For one who eats after digestion,
The essence of ghee, honey
And sugar are like nectar;
For one who eats undigested, they become poison.
Likewise the Vaipulya, the supreme nectar,
For those unskilled in intentional statements
And possessed of childish minds,
Becomes a poison hard to bear.
Pratyekabuddhas and śrāvakas too
Are always just like that:
For them the Great Vehicle, the supreme medicine,
Is like drinking cow's milk.
The fearless bodhisattvas,
Those steadfast, supreme among humans,
Surely go forth by the Great Vehicle.
Kāśyapa, having understood it thus,
Go now, today, for supreme refuge
To the deathless, the permanent, the expanse of the element.
That element of the supreme nectar
Is my element, the element of beings.
If you always go for refuge to me,
The three refuges become the element itself.
Because the element is present in my body,
Enter into this very self!
The Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha
Are the nature of my element;
These three are the supreme jewels."
So the Blessed One spoke in verse.

Kāśyapa said:
"How one goes for refuge to the fearless one,
I have not understood.
Nor do I know the Three Jewels;
How does one go to what is without self?
Even if one goes for refuge to the Buddha,
How does one find relief?
For what reason do people
Go for refuge to the Dharma?
By what does one become free,
And by what does one not?
If one goes for refuge to the Saṅgha,
How do the three refuges become supreme?
In the future, how does one attain
Buddhahood itself, perfect and true?
For the sake of what is to come, the three bases of refuge,
What are they like?
I see them as a ladder;
Apart from wisdom there is nothing else.
When no son has yet been born,
How can one even speak of a 'son'?
If a son is conceived, he will be born,
And then thought and action have their purpose too.
Sage, what you teach
The unintelligent do not understand;
All of them, in the prison of saṃsāra,
Wander round as on a single wheel.
Therefore they do not become laymen
By the mere name alone.
Sage, explain it to me in detail;
Please cut through my doubts swiftly.
Tathāgata, endowed with great wisdom,
Please show what your intention is."

The Blessed One declared:
"Bodhisattva, great being,
Mahākāśyapa, holy and supreme,
I shall teach that intention too;
I shall cut through your doubts.
By going for refuge to the Buddha,
One attains the state of a layman.
None of those gods
Are then his refuge.
If one goes for refuge to the Dharma as well,
Here one gives up doing harm.
If one goes for refuge to the Saṅgha too,
Likewise one becomes a layman."

Therefore those outside of this
Are not worthy of offerings to him;
And so those who are without fear
Will obtain the three bases of refuge.

He replied:

Therefore to the three entities that overcome fear
I shall go for refuge.
This is the path of the holy ones;
This itself is the nature of reality of the buddhas.
Great Sage, at all times
The three bases are sameness itself.
I too, just like you,
Shall be unsurpassed in this abode.

This path is praised by the Sugata;
To go there is to abide truly.
From it the all-seeing eye too surely arises.
This path is praised by the Sugata;
Therefore I too shall enter
This path of the buddhas, this supreme and vast path.
Even if Māra should bestow ambrosia this very day,
I shall not go for refuge within the realms of existence.

Then the Blessed One said to him: "Do not hold it as the childish followers of the Śrāvaka Vehicle do. Go for refuge to the one alone. In this wisdom there are no three refuges, nor is there any gathering of Dharma and Saṅgha. The Buddha himself is also the Dharma and also the Saṅgha; the Tathāgata himself is the three bases. In the Śrāvaka Vehicle the three bases are set out in order to remove the worldly views of the childish. If you conform with worldly people, then go to the three bases of refuge in that way, and resolve thus: 'Having gone for refuge to the Buddha, may I become of one body with him. Then, having become a buddha, may I carry out the vast deeds of the Tathāgata. Having become equal to the Tathāgata, I shall not bow with joined palms to the buddhas. May I become like a great refuge for all sentient beings. Without giving up the Dharma body, I shall pay homage to the relics of the Buddha and to stūpas. May I become like a stūpa for all those sentient beings who do not wish to pay homage. May my body become an object of homage for all sentient beings. Having gone for refuge to the Dharma, may I become the Dharma body. May I lead all sentient beings who cultivate dharmas that are not reality, passing them along one after another as an arrow is passed from one to the next. Having gone for refuge to the Saṅgha, may I become like a refuge for those saṅghas that are like flies. By way of engaging in going for refuge, may I become like the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha for sentient beings. May I become like an eye for those saṅghas that are like the blind. May all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, who are like flies, come to me for refuge.' Make your resolve in this way.

"Son of good family, in this way the bodhisattva great beings appear to the childish like an inexhaustible heap of evil, while to the wise those very same ones are like buddhas. For example, when a true hero fights in battle, he thinks: 'I shall defeat all the heroes and make them all come to me for refuge.' And for example, when the time comes for the succession of the king, a prince, surpassing all the other princes, thinks: 'I shall be king. I shall become lord of all the princes. I shall make them come to me for refuge. I shall not give rise to an inferior resolve.' Whether they be heroes, kings, or ministers, those of such a nature, who are exceedingly supreme and preeminent, accomplish deeds befitting their own qualities. Son of good family, in the same way the bodhisattva great being possesses a mind that will go beyond the three bases. Therefore, like a prince and a hero, generate courage without fear. I have taught that the three bases are not distinct, and that among the three the Tathāgata is supreme. Just as among all the parts of the body the head is chief, so the Buddha is chief. And just as the head does not remain apart from the body, nor do the hands remain apart, nor do the feet remain apart, in the same way the Buddha does not remain apart, the Dharma does not remain apart, and the Saṅgha does not remain apart. Nevertheless, the division into distinct ones is taught like the rungs of a ladder in order surely to deliver the childish. In the reality of the Dharma, do not view the three as divided into distinct ones. Do not hold to words that bring confusion, as the childish do. Make your mind sharp like a razor-edged sword. Become endowed with great wisdom."

Then the bodhisattva great being, the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, said to the Blessed One: "Great Hero, Blessed One, even though I understood, I asked in order to request that you explain the stainless conduct of bodhisattvas. Blessed One, oh! You have spoken excellently of the conduct of bodhisattvas, the unsurpassed Great Vehicle, the Vaipulya, which is like a razor-edged sword. Oh, it has been well set forth! Having heard the explanation of the conduct of bodhisattvas, I too wish to teach it to all sentient beings. Whatever sentient beings they may be, those who have strong faith in this sūtra in order to realize the tathāgatagarbha are themselves the three refuges. They go for refuge to themselves and have no need of the three bases. Why is that? Because the tathāgatagarbha, the buddha-element, is present. Having examined thoroughly, one should declare, 'The buddha-element is present in my body.' Having understood in this way, such a one will not even go to the three refuges, for that itself becomes the refuge of the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and it is a place to which the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas always pay homage. Therefore one should undertake the practice of the Great Vehicle. Thus the buddha-element, the thirty-two major marks, and the eighty excellent minor marks are inconceivable."

The Blessed One said: "Excellent, son of good family, excellent! One of sharp wisdom must act in this way. Son of good family, furthermore, I shall explain the entry into the tathāgatagarbha. If the self were stable and everlasting, there would be no end to suffering; and if there were no self, all pure conduct would be meaningless."

If all phenomena are without self, then that becomes a view of annihilation. If the self is stable, that becomes a view of permanence. If all phenomena are impermanent, then that becomes a view of annihilation. If all phenomena are permanent, that becomes a view of permanence. If all phenomena are nothing but suffering, that becomes a view of annihilation. If all phenomena are bliss, that becomes a view of permanence. If one meditates on everything as permanent, one will come to grasp at annihilation. If one meditates on everything as annihilated, one will quickly come to grasp at permanence.

Leeches, for example, quickly feed on a person's head and quickly let go of the other spot. In the same way, if one meditates on permanence, one grasps at everything and so becomes like the leech. Therefore, one should meditate on the other phenomena that are nonvirtuous as suffering. One should meditate on the other phenomena that are virtuous as bliss. One should meditate on the other phenomena, such as the forms of the afflictions, as without self. As for nirvāṇa, because it has no basis and no abode, one should meditate on the other phenomena, such as buddha-nature, as having a self. One should meditate on the other phenomena, such as wealth and enjoyments, as impermanent. One should meditate on the other phenomena, such as the qualities of the Tathāgata, as permanent. The three bases and the reality of liberation are not like the feeding of the leech.

Know that the speech of the Buddha is this Middle Way. The Buddha, the Blessed One, taught the Middle Way, which abandons both extremes, annihilation and permanence. Fools are confused about the speech of the Buddha. They quickly fall into views of the two extremes, just as those whose stomach heat is weak eat a great deal of butter. "It exists" is not established, and "it does not exist" is not tenable either.

Physicians, for example, even when the bodily elements such as bile are disturbed and at odds with one another, pacify the bile in a bile disorder, remove the wind where wind predominates, and clear away the phlegm in one who suffers from phlegm. For a combined disorder they apply the remedy for a combined disorder. They do not set the elements at odds with one another, and they also bring about well-being. In the same way, like a physician, gradually remove the endless diseases that are the forms of the afflictions, and heal the true element as well. The true element is buddha-nature, which is called the buddha element. Therefore, abandoning all other elements, apply yourselves diligently to what is permanent and stable.

The wise should not be attached even to existence. "It does not exist" also comes from speaking falsely. And remaining silent, one should not assert "it exists" either. Nor should one dispute; one should know these, by their intrinsic nature, as the nature of reality.

Fools who do not understand words spoken with a hidden intent constantly dispute, because I have taught suffering even though such seeds of bliss are present in my body. Grasping at everything, the childish think and imagine, "His body is not stable." When impermanence is taught, the childish grasp it as, "It is impermanent, like a potter's vessel." The wise, knowing that such seeds of the Dharma body are present in my body, do not grasp at everything.

When selflessness is taught, the childish take it as, "The teaching is that there is no self." The wise know that a self exists conventionally, and they have no doubt. When it is taught that buddha-nature is empty, the childish cultivate the crooked view of annihilation, which is terrifying. The wise know that what is permanent, stable and eternal exists as a mere illusion. When liberation is taught, the childish take it as, "Having attained liberation, the buddhas cease to exist." The wise know that buddhas come and go, and they say, "The lion among men has come."

The childish take as two the statement, "Conditioned by ignorance, formations." The wise know that ignorance and knowledge are not two separate things. The childish take as two the statement, "Conditioned by formations, consciousness." The wise know that formations and the absence of formation are not separate, that they are not two. Likewise, the childish take everything virtuous and nonvirtuous as two separate things. The wise know them to be not two, so it is said. The childish take the blameworthy and the blameless as two. The wise know them, likewise, to be not two, so it is said; that is the nature of the wise. The childish take what are called the path and the non-path as two. The wise know them to be not two, so it is said; that is the nature of the wise. The childish take as two the statement, "All phenomena are black and white." The wise know them to be not two, so it is said; that is the nature of the wise.

The childish meditate on all phenomena as suffering and bliss, two separate things. The wise meditate on them as not two, so it is said; that is the nature of the wise. Those of childish mind meditate on buddha-nature as, "All conditioned things are impermanent." The wise know it to be not two, so it is said; that is the nature of the wise. The childish, when the Buddha speaks of selflessness, take all phenomena as being without self. The wise know that self and selflessness are not two; that is the nature of the wise.

The immeasurable buddha-nature praised by the buddhas I have taught in the Sūtra Gathering All Merit, and it should be understood that self and selflessness are not two. Son of noble family, remember how in the Sūtra Gathering All Merit, and in that very Light of Entering Nonduality from the great Perfection of Wisdom Sūtra, I taught that self and selflessness are not two.

Fascicle ten.

For example, from milk comes curd, from curd comes butter, from butter comes ghee, and from ghee comes the cream of ghee. In that case, does the curd arise simultaneously with the substance of the milk? Or does the curd arise by itself? Likewise, does the butter arise simultaneously with the substance of the curd? Or does the butter arise by itself? Likewise, does the ghee arise simultaneously with the substance of the butter? Or does the ghee arise by itself? Likewise, does the cream of ghee arise simultaneously with the substance of the ghee? Or does the cream of ghee arise by itself? Or does that richness come from something else? If it comes from something else, then from what does it come?

If curd arose without milk, milk would be unnecessary. If that were so, curd would possess an intrinsic nature of its own; yet both the cause of curd and its characteristic marks are evident. Therefore it does arise in succession, one from another; and yet one should not say that it arises in succession, one from another, simultaneously with those substances. And if it does not arise simultaneously with those substances, does that mean it is utterly nonexistent?

The ghee that comes from the cow does not come from anything else, and that nature is present in all of them. Yet it is not manifest, because it remains obscured by faults and intermingled with them. Through a fault in the cow's food, the milk tastes of a foul smell of blood; when the cow eats sweet grass, the milk comes out sweet; when it eats pungent grass, the milk comes out pungent. In the snow mountains there is a kind of grass called Unctuous. When the cow eats it, the milk comes out purely of the nature of the cream of ghee: neither white, nor blue, nor red, nor bluish gray, it comes out purely of the nature of the cream of ghee.

Just as milk thus becomes ill-colored or well-colored according to the different kinds of grass, so too, although ignorance and knowledge are not two, through the fault of the karma of sentient beings that very thing which is knowledge becomes like ignorance. All virtuous and nonvirtuous phenomena, too, are simply nondual.

Therefore, just as was taught before, buddha-nature has a nature like the cream of ghee, but the faults of the forms of the afflictions make it appear otherwise.

For example, the water of the ocean serves only the single purpose of saltiness, and yet one should not say that it serves only the single purpose of saltiness, for within the ocean there is also what is called the ocean of milk. In the snow mountains, too, because there are medicines, the mountains are spoken of in terms of their good qualities: although there is poison in the snow mountains as well, medicine is mentioned as the chief thing. Likewise this body is similar: although it holds the poison of the four elements, which are like venomous snakes, it contains the king of medicines, buddha-nature. Therefore buddha-nature is an element uncontrived from the very beginning, while the afflictions are adventitious. Whoever wishes to become a buddha should destroy the forms of the afflictions.

For example, when thunder rumbles in the sky, mushrooms swiftly spring up, whereas in spring there is not even the name of mushrooms; they are brought forth by the thunder. In the same way, buddha-nature, obscured by the forms of the afflictions, appears as though it were selfless. But those who hear this great sūtra, immediately upon hearing this Great Parinirvāṇa, give rise, like the mushrooms, to the knowledge of buddha-nature. All other sūtras and all samādhis are like the season of spring: through hearing all those sūtras and all those samādhis one does not truly know that buddha-nature exists. Just as mushrooms spring up under the summer rains, so from this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra come forth all the secret words of the hidden intent. Immediately upon hearing this sūtra, just as mushrooms spring up under the summer rains, all sentient beings will truly know that buddha-nature exists. Because it unfolds the great meaning, it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa. Those who train in this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra are the ones who repay my kindness.

It was said: "Oh, Blessed One, buddha-nature is extremely difficult for all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas to see, extremely difficult to comprehend, and extremely difficult to accept."

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, so it is. Son of good family, so it is. I too say exactly that."

It was asked: "How is it difficult to see?"

He said, "Son of good family, suppose there were a hundred blind people. Wishing to see with their eyes, those blind people seek out a great physician who is an eye doctor. That physician treats their eyes and removes their cataracts. Then he holds up one of his fingers and says to each of them, 'Man, look at my finger! It will then be clear whether or not your treatment has succeeded.' Yet although the treatment has been carried out, however he holds up his finger, those people do not see it. Then he shows it to them two or three times, and they see it dimly, as though not seeing it.

"In the same way, son of good family, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas who have attained the ten grounds and have undergone every treatment of purification are also like this. Although they have been purified in this way, they are like those people: when I have shown them two or three times that buddha-nature exists within themselves, they see it dimly, as though not seeing it. And having seen it, they say: 'For so long we too have been turned about by the single wheel of selflessness. How then could śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas ever see it?' Son of good family, in this way, entry into the treatise on my element is difficult to see."

"Son of noble family, here is another example. Suppose a certain person sees geese flying in a line across the sky. Having seen them, he falls into doubt and thinks, 'Is this just the sky, or are those geese flying?' When he examines carefully, he sees, though only dimly, that they are geese in flight. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds think, 'We see only dimly that each of us has the tathāgatagarbha (buddha-nature).' How much less, then, could any śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas see it!

"Suppose a person traveling on a road has his bile disturbed, so that his sense of direction is confused. When he examines carefully, he sees the road and the shapes around him only dimly. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds, when they examine carefully, see only dimly that in their own bodies there is the uncreated element, the tathāgatagarbha.

"Suppose a certain traveler, tormented by thirst, finds no water in a remote wilderness. As he sits there longing for water, he sees white cranes perched on the branches of trees and thinks, 'Is this a mansion, or is it the sky?' When he examines carefully, he sees, though only dimly, that they are white cranes. In the same way, son of noble family, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the tathāgatagarbha is present in their own bodies, present as it truly is.

"Son of noble family, suppose a person sailing in a ship on the ocean sees, from many leagues away, a white mansion within a city, and thinks, 'Is this the sky, or is it a mansion?' When he examines carefully, he sees it only dimly. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the buddha element is present in their own bodies, present as it truly is.

"Son of noble family, suppose a young prince living a life of pleasure has spent the night until it fades watching performances of music. When he gets up, his eyes are dazzled by the sunlight, so that when he looks carefully he sees even his own belt only dimly. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the buddha element is present in their own bodies.

"Suppose a certain attendant of the king, on a pitch-dark night thick with clouds, leaves the royal palace to go to his own home. In a flash of lightning he sees a herd of oxen coming toward him, and he thinks, 'Is it a cloud? Or a house? Or a gatehouse? Or a building with an upper story?' When he examines carefully, he sees them only dimly. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds, though the buddha element is present in their own bodies, see only dimly, thinking, 'The tathāgatagarbha is present in my body.'

"Suppose a certain monk, conscientious in keeping moral discipline, looks at water in which there are no living creatures, yet by his own mind sees creatures in it, and he thinks, 'Are these living creatures? Or is it dust? Or is it dust that has suddenly blown in, or is it a creature?' When he examines carefully, he sees only dimly. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the tathāgatagarbha is present in their own bodies, present as it truly is.

"Suppose a person is walking here and there early in the morning when clouds have suddenly gathered, and he sees some children and thinks, 'Is that an ox? A buffalo? Or a vulture?' Only afterward does he see, dimly, that they are children. In the same way, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the tathāgatagarbha is present in their own bodies, present as it truly is.

"Suppose an image of a bodhisattva painted on a large cloth is spread out in the darkness of night, and people with eyes examine it and think, 'Is this an image of the Buddha? Or a piece of red cloth? Or is it an image of Maheśvara (Śiva) and Brahmā?' Only afterward do they see, dimly, that it is the image of a bodhisattva. In the same way, son of noble family, even the great bodhisattvas who have attained the ten grounds see only dimly that the tathāgatagarbha is present in their own bodies, present as it truly is.

"Son of noble family, thus the tathāgatagarbha is extremely hard to see. It is the domain of the Tathāgata, not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Son of noble family, thus my teaching is to be understood by the wise; it does not accord with the whole world."

He asked, "If that is so, how can those who have only fleshly eyes see this subtle aspect?"

The Blessed One said, "How could even the gods of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception see it? This is the domain of the Buddha alone, not the domain of any śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas. They should develop faith by following this sūtra. If they do so, they too will come to have right view. Son of noble family, thus all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas too should have faith, in the manner of this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, that the tathāgatagarbha is present in their own bodies, and they should strive to accord with the sūtras. The tathāgatagarbha is the domain of the Tathāgata, not the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas."

He said, "Blessed One, among worldly people too there is the designation of a person as the element of sentient beings."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, suppose there were two men who were friends. One was a king's son and the other a poor man. Out of mutual affection the two would go to each other's homes and play together. Then the poor man saw the king's son's sword, which was of a beautiful color, and after that the king's son took the sword and went off to another country.

"Later, while the poor man was sleeping at night in a shelter for the homeless, he talked in his sleep, crying out, 'Sword! Sword!' The town watchmen heard his words and reported it to the king. The king had the man summoned and said, 'Hey, what is this sword of yours like, and where did it come from?'

"The man told the king what had happened and said, 'Your Majesty, if I have stolen it, then please have these hands and feet of mine cut off. I only saw it while I was playing together with my friend. I never so much as touched it.'

"Then the king said to him, 'What are that sword's shape and color like?'

"He replied to the king, 'Your Majesty, that sword is like a goat's horn.'

"Then the king released the man, saying, 'Man, do not be afraid, do not be afraid! Go quickly, wherever you please. Even had you once seen that sword here in this royal palace, you would still not have seen it. How then could a king's son have found it in a market in his own country? It is hard to find even for a king.'

"Then the king asked his purohita and his ministers, 'Have you seen that sword?' and then he died. Though all the great ones searched for the sword, they did not find it. Then another king's son was installed on the throne, and he found that sword. The others ruled as kings without ever finding it. Thus the kings who succeeded one another did not see that sword, and they imagined, 'That sword is like this, like such-and-such. It is like a sheep's horn. It is like a goat's horn.' Some imagined it however they pleased.

"In the same way, the bodhisattva great being too appears in the world like that king's son of the far-off land: he teaches the reality of the self and then passes away. Then those of small intelligence think, 'All sentient beings have a self like this.' Seeing wrongly, they teach it to those without wisdom, like something dreamt in a dream. Worldly people too, hearing it put that way, imagine that there is a self-element in their own bodies, yet they do not know the nature of that self. Thus, having heard the bodhisattva's words, they grasp them wrongly, and just as the sword was imagined to be like a goat's horn, so they imagine it in many ways. Worldly people falsely imagine that something called a thumb-sized person, or something the size of the fruit of the śyāmāka plant, or the size of a grain of rice, dwells in the heart and blazes. That view of theirs is a wrong view handed down from one to another. It does not lead to benefit, and it does not lead to happiness. Therefore I taught selflessness. Even so, worldly people still wrongly imagine that there is a self.

"Son of noble family, therefore now, in my teaching, like the true sword, I shall explain the buddha-nature, saying, 'The self is like this.' Whatever is well spoken and accords with the Dharma should be understood to be transcendent, even though it belongs to worldly people. Among the things of the world, the supreme dharma in every respect should be understood to be an emanation of a bodhisattva. Know that whatever treatises, vidyā-mantras, or secret mantras there may be, all of them were spoken by the perfect Buddha."

He asked, "How did the Blessed One teach the alphabet?"

The Blessed One said, "As for the whole alphabet, the half-letters were emanated first, and through the letters they become the dhāraṇī of vidyā-mantras, of secret mantras, of the suchness of phenomena, and of all the aggregates. Moreover, I emanated it so that one who learns the alphabet, the way of the true Dharma, will distinguish between Dharma and non-Dharma. It was not for the sake of non-Dharma."

He asked, "What is the meaning of what is called a letter?"

He said, "The fourteen sounds are called letters, because all the letters are certain. It means imperishable. If one asks what is meant by the indivisibility of what are called the fourteen sounds of the letters, it means non-leaking. 'Letter' means the Tathāgata. The Tathāgata is the indivisible vajra body. In the Tathāgata there is nothing called leaking from the nine openings, nor are there even the nine openings themselves. Therefore the meaning of non-leaking is the Tathāgata. Because it is certain, it is called a letter. It means uncreated.

"ā means auspicious; it is a term for the three foundations. Again, ā is a designation for the wisdom of the noble ones. If one asks who the noble ones are: those in the world who are rich, who are without attachment, and who are content with whatever is suitable, and those who definitively liberate all sentient beings from the whole ocean of existence, are called noble ones.

"ā teaches religious custom. Since it is called engaging in accordance with rites, with the sphere of conduct, with discipline, and with the world, its meaning is not different. Again, if one asks what meaning ā unfolds: it is that, by relying on the noble ones, all sentient beings come to know that rites, the sphere of conduct, father and mother, the teacher, and discipline are precious, and come to know, 'This is the nature of reality.' Those who are noble ones are the sons or daughters of noble family who have entered the Great Vehicle. Monks endowed with moral discipline and those bodhisattva great beings are called noble ones. Again, ā teaches law. Law means saying, 'O man, come here! You have done what is improper. From now on, do not act like that!' It means calling such improprieties to mind at that time.

"i means 'this.' This teaching is the holy life, stainless and utterly pure like the full moon.

"This teaching is the Blessed One's. Do this! This is meaningful. This is meaningless. This was spoken by Māra. This was spoken by the Buddha." It means that one should come to know things in this way, thinking thus.

ī stands for Īśvara, the Lord. My teaching belongs to Īśvara, to Maheśvara (Śiva), and to Brahmā, and it is extremely rare. Īśvara is the protector of the Dharma. Because he protects the Dharma, he is a king, and therefore he is called a world guardian. This great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra was requested by the lords for the sake of other beings. It was upheld by the lords. It was expounded by the lords. The lords make the sublime Dharma blaze supremely. It was for the sake of the lords that I taught it. And what did I teach? I taught the sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa. Therefore it is the Vaipulya of those who possess lordship, and those who are jealous of it are to be rooted out, as weeds are cleared from a field. Because it unfolds many meanings such as these, I taught ī.

ū stands for the Later Section. This collection of sūtras teaches the last of the later sections of the meaning of all the sūtras. I have taught the single letter, the single drop, that none of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas had ever heard before. This great collection of sūtras is supreme and sublime. Just as the beings of Uttarakuru are endowed with merit, so those who listen to this great collection of sūtras have gone beyond the world; they should be known as bodhisattvas, great beings. Therefore it is called "great," which is the meaning of "the Later Section." Therefore I taught ū in this way.

ū means something like a cow's udder. Just as a cow's milk is delicious, the taste of this sūtra is just the same. Those who reject the teaching of buddha-nature in this collection of sūtras are like cattle. Just as the ungrateful who intend to kill themselves are deeply to be pitied, so beings who reject buddha-nature and teach selflessness should be known as deeply to be pitied. Therefore it is called ū.

e means that the statement "the Tathāgata has passed into nirvāṇa" is the nature of reality of the buddhas. Therefore it is called e.

ai means the Tathāgata. Since ai means both coming and going, and since he has come just as he has gone, it is called ai.

o stands for the afflictions. Because they become medicine, one passes from being a human to becoming a Tathāgata. Therefore it is called o.

au stands for the Great Vehicle. Since au is the last of all fourteen sounds, the Great Vehicle is called the last of the later sections of all the tantras. Therefore it is called au.

aṃ means turning back everything that is improper in my teaching, and giving up gold and silver. Therefore, in the sense of turning back, it is called aṃ.

aḥ stands for the supreme vehicle. Therefore it is called aḥ. In the same way, the culmination of all the sūtras and all the tantras spoken by the Buddha is the Great Vehicle.

ka means looking upon all sentient beings with compassion, as upon Rāhula. Since one should think, "I shall work for the benefit of all sentient beings," it is called ka.

kha stands for the secret of the Tathāgata. Because those without discernment cannot know it, it is called kha.

ga stands for the essence. Because all sentient beings possess buddha-nature, it is called ga.

gha means that the Tathāgata is called permanent. Therefore the Tathāgata is permanent.

ṇa means that formations are called impermanent. Therefore it is called ṇa.

ca stands for conduct. Because all sentient beings are to be brought to full maturity, it is called ca.

cha means that the Tathāgata dwells radiantly beneath the single parasol of the white dharmas. Therefore it is called cha.

ja means that, like ja, liberation does not decay. Therefore it is called ja.

jha means swiftly shaking off the afflictions. Therefore it is called jha.

ña is a term for knowing the Dharma of suchness. Therefore it is called ña.

ṭa means that, like ṭa, the Tathāgata displays his incomparable body in certain parts of Jambudvīpa. Therefore it is called ṭa.

ṭha stands for the Dharma body. Because, like ṭha, it is fully complete, it is called ṭha.

ḍa means that the Saṅgha is called youthful. Because it will not decline, it is called ḍa.

dha stands for shamelessness. Because one is ungrateful toward one's teachers, it is called dha.

ṇa means that the outsiders are not noble compared to this. Therefore it is called ṇa.

ta means turning back from falling into any form of existence. Therefore it is called ta.

tha stands for ignorance. Because one is wrapped up in it like a silkworm, it is called tha.

da means "Take delight in striving in the Great Vehicle!" Therefore it is called da.

dha means that the three bases are as firm as Mount Meru and will not decline. Therefore it is called dha.

na means that the three bases are extremely firm, like the post that holds the bolt of a city gate. Therefore it is called na.

pa stands for perversion. Because one becomes utterly confused about the three bases not declining, it is called pa.

pha stands for the destruction of all worlds. To say, "When the world is destroyed, the three bases will be destroyed," is to destroy one's own fruit, for one falls away from practice. Therefore it is called pha.

ba is a synonym for the powers of the tathāgatas: because they possess the ten powers, it is called ba.

bha means that the bodhisattvas carry the burden of the true Dharma. Therefore it is called bha.

ma means that to carry one's own burden without fail is the way of the bodhisattvas. Therefore it is called ma.

ya: because the bodhisattvas must accomplish the four aims, it is called ya.

ra: because, having dispelled the fear of attachment, aversion and delusion, one enters the Dharma, the meaning of suchness, it is called ra.

la: because, having moved upward from the Śrāvaka Vehicle, one is to take up the Great Vehicle, which is itself unmoving, it is called la.

va: because the supreme bodhisattvas teach all the world's spells, secret mantras and medicines, it is called va.

śa means being free of the three thorns. Therefore it is called śa.

ṣa means complete: because whoever hears this great sūtra hears the whole of the Vaipulya, it is called ṣa.
