sā means the mind that brings forth the true Dharma. Therefore it is called sa.

ha means joy and grief, as one thinks, "Ah, I have reached the end of saṃsāra!" and "Alas, the Tathāgata is passing into parinirvāṇa!" Therefore it is called ha.

kṣa means Māra. Even millions of māras cannot divide the Saṅgha, yet the Tathāgata displays acting in conformity with the world. Again, kṣa means displaying a mother and so on in order to conform to the whole world. Therefore it is called kṣa.

With the four letters ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ, the four meanings are elaborated: showing the Buddha, the Dharma, the Saṅgha, and the remedy. Conforming to the world is the remedy, as for example when Devadatta divided the Saṅgha. When the division of the Saṅgha is displayed, other forms too are emanated in order to lay down the bases of training. When the wise see that skillful means being shown, they should not be frightened, but should have confidence, thinking, "This is conforming to the world." Therefore they are called ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ.

Those that arise from the two lips, those that come from the root of the tongue, those that come from both mouth and nose, the long ones, and those drawn out at length, are combined together with meaning. By combining the letters together, they come to function. The tongue, the teeth and so on are elements of the body, and that element is by nature completely pure. Likewise, all the various aggregates, elements and sense sources are included alike in the pacification within the suchness of the Dharma. By being combined through the splendor of that element pervaded by the tathāgatagarbha, they come to function. Therefore they are called the half-letters.

All treatises, all secret mantras and all dharmas are designations made by combining the alphabet. Therefore one sees the Tathāgata and liberation as they truly are. To abandon the very notions of uttering letters and of conceptualizing, and the notions of letters and syllables, is the Tathāgata and liberation. Therefore liberation is release in every respect and utter release.

Those who rightly understand, thinking, "That very Tathāgata appeared solely for the sake of the meaning of the dharma of the half-letters," are called knowers of letters. Others are called knowers of half-letters. If one asks who are without letters, they are those who rely on what is not the Dharma. Even if they rely on the Dharma, all those who do not distinguish the Tathāgata as permanent, the three bases, eternal liberation, sūtra and non-sūtra, Vinaya and non-Vinaya, what Māra has said and what the Buddha has spoken, all of them I call "without letters within."

"Therefore, son of noble family, you should learn the half-letters in these ways. If you do so, you will be counted among those called knowers of letters."

He said, "Blessed One, I shall be counted among them. Understanding it in this way, I shall train in the half-letters. Blessed One, only now have I found a supreme teacher such as you. Blessed One, only now have I entered the school of writing and reckoning."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent, son of noble family! One who wants the true Dharma must aspire in this way. Son of noble family, what are called zalmo are of two kinds: the zalmo of suffering, impermanence and selflessness, and the zalmo that keep company with geese. The latter refers to birds."

He asked, "How are suffering, impermanence and selflessness called zalmo?"

The Blessed One said, "It is because some dharmas are impermanent while some are permanent, some are suffering while some are happiness, and some are selfless while some have a self. For example, sugarcane, sālu rice, grain, millet, wild millet, beans, lentils, peas and the other kinds of grain, from the time of flowering until the time of fruit, are included within impermanence. Once they are free of husk, they come to benefit all beings and are counted as grain. Therefore they are called permanent. Why is that? Because it has become their nature."

He asked, "Blessed One, how is it, then? Do those things not cease to exist later? Is the Tathāgata called permanent in the same way that they do not cease to exist later?"

The Blessed One said, "Although one may use the example that the Tathāgata is like Mount Meru, even Mount Meru, the king of mountains, will be destroyed by the eon of destruction. Do not hold the Tathāgata to be like that. Apart from nirvāṇa, there is nothing whatsoever in all the world that will not be destroyed. Therefore, in a manner that accords with the world, follow conventional designations."

He said, "Excellent, Blessed One, excellent!"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, in the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, the fruit of cultivating the meditation of all the sūtras and all the samādhis, is included within impermanence for as long as sentient beings have not heard it. Once they have heard this precious sūtra, sons or daughters of noble family, even though they still have afflictions, are included among those without afflictions, and they also come to benefit all sentient beings. Why is that? Because they know that the tathāgatagarbha, the buddha-nature, exists within themselves. And once they know it to be supremely permanent, they will enter into it.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, take for example a mango tree or a palmyra tree. From the time of its flowers until the time of its fruit, it is included among impermanent phenomena. Once its fruit has ripened, it comes to benefit all sentient beings and is included among permanent phenomena. Son of noble family, in the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, the fruit of cultivating the meditation of all the sūtras and all the samādhis, is included within impermanence for as long as sentient beings have not heard it. Once they have heard this precious sūtra, sons or daughters of noble family, even though they still have afflictions, are included among those without afflictions, and they also come to benefit all sentient beings. Why is that? Because they know that the tathāgatagarbha exists within themselves. And once they know it to be supremely permanent, they will enter into it.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, take for example a gold mine or a copper mine. Until the time it is dug, it is included within impermanence. Once the grit, the stones and the impurities are gone, it comes to benefit all sentient beings and is included among permanent precious substances. Son of noble family, in the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, the fruit of cultivating the meditation of all the sūtras and all the samādhis, is included within impermanence for as long as sentient beings have not heard it. Once they have heard this precious sūtra, sons or daughters of noble family, even though they still have afflictions, are included among those without afflictions, and they also come to benefit all sentient beings. Why is that? Because they know that the tathāgatagarbha exists within themselves. And once they know it to be supremely permanent, they will enter into it.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, take for example sugarcane or sesame. Until the time they become juice and oil, they are included within impermanence. Once the sugarcane pulp and the sesame dregs are gone, they come to benefit all sentient beings and are also included among permanent substances. Son of noble family, in the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, the fruit of cultivating the meditation of all the sūtras and all the samādhis, is included within impermanence for as long as sentient beings have not heard it. Once they have heard this precious sūtra, sons or daughters of noble family, even though they still have afflictions, are included among those without afflictions, and they also come to benefit all sentient beings. Why is that? Because they know that the tathāgatagarbha exists within themselves. And once they know it to be supremely permanent, they will enter into it.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, all rivers, for example, are gathered into the ocean. Son of noble family, in the same way, all the sūtras, all the samādhis, and the Great Vehicle are gathered into this sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa. Why is that? Because in it the tathāgatagarbha is clearly taught in its utterly complete and final form, and whatever is supremely ultimate is called the cause. Therefore it is said that some phenomena are impermanent and some phenomena are permanent."

He said, "It is stated that the Buddha, too, is pierced by the thorn of grief. Yet the grief of the perfect Buddha is not like the grief of humans, nor like the grief of gods, nor like the grief of Māra. The Buddha does not grieve even in the way of one born as an animal. What, then, is it like?"

The Blessed One said, "What, then, is the perception of those beings who are without perception? If they had no perception, it would not be right for them to be alive, yet those without perception are alive. How could those who have no aggregates, elements or sense sources have perception? For example, although gods dwell in trees, it is not evident whether those gods dwell in the branches of the trees or in their roots. In the same way, the teaching too is profound, and the Tathāgata too is profound. To grieve constantly out of loving-kindness, as one would for Rāhula, is like the grieving of the gods of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception. These things are the domain of the buddhas; such is the way of the buddhas. What the nature of reality of the buddhas is like, what their bodies are like, what the speech of the self-arisen ones is like, and what their minds are like, only the buddhas know. How could there be grief in the very nature of mind? And yet, if there were no grief, that would contradict the existence of the Vaipulya teaching. If one were to say that there is no grief, the statement 'He regards beings as he regards Rāhula' would become false. The statement 'All phenomena are false' was taught as inconceivable."

In the same way, the Buddhas, the Blessed Ones, are inconceivable. The Dharma too is inconceivable. The teaching too is inconceivable. So this is the true nature of reality of the Buddhas. Wherever there is space, there is no house to dwell in, no pillar, and no dust; it remains simply in the essential nature of space. Seeing this, those of childish nature should transform their minds so that they become just as space is.

Since this is inconceivable, how could there be sorrow in it? And if there were no sorrow, the saying that he regards beings as he regards Rāhula would become meaningless. The sorrow of the Tathāgata is like space. An illusion has no further mind behind it; in the same way, the statement that the Tathāgata has sorrow is no more than a manner of illusion. So how could there be sorrow in one who has passed into parinirvāṇa? Sorrow exists for what is impermanent, for it comes from the minds of such beings. Therefore the Tathāgata is permanent. If sorrow exists for what is impermanent, then, since the Tathāgata is not impermanent, he cannot be understood either as having sorrow or as having none. The Tathāgata is free from sorrow.

The lowly know the lowly, but the lowly do not know the middling. The middling know the middling, but the middling do not know the superior. The superior know the lowly, the middling, and the superior. The śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas know no more than their own stage. The Buddhas understand everything. Therefore the perfectly complete Buddhas are called those who have no obscuration to their wisdom.

So it is that they display illusions of many kinds in the realms of the gods and upon the earth. Knowing that the Buddhas, the Blessed Ones, teach in this way in accordance with the world, even childish beings with fleshly eyes should abide in the knowledge of the wise, in order to attain unobstructed wisdom. So "sorrow" and "freedom from sorrow" are what the perfect Buddha has examined; they are not the domain of the childish. Therefore some dharmas have a self and some dharmas have no self, and this is taught as being like the waterfowl.

Furthermore, son of noble family, the sphere of activity of the Buddha should be shown to be like geese moving on. For example, in summer, when the waters of the rivers are greatly swollen, geese and waterfowl move away from the plains and make their nests on firm ground in order to tend their eggs, and they go to wherever their feeding grounds are. That is their nature. In the same way, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, too, having been born, establishes many tens of millions of beings in the true Dharma. Like the geese and the waterfowl, once he has accomplished the benefit of all those there are to be trained, he appears wherever there are others to be trained.

So it is said that some dharmas are suffering and some are happiness. Whatever formations there are, they are suffering and perishable. Whatever is nirvāṇa is supreme happiness; because formations have been abandoned, it is endowed with happiness.

He asked, "How is that?"

The Blessed One said, "It is just as it is."

He asked, "How is it happiness for beings who have attained nirvāṇa?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, in my teaching, aging and death arise from formations. Heedfulness is the abode of the deathless. Heedlessness is the abode of death. Those endowed with heedfulness do not die. Those endowed with heedlessness are like those who are forever dead. Whatever is endowed with heedlessness is formation, and that is supreme suffering. Nirvāṇa is the deathless. Those endowed with heedlessness have become formations, and they are supremely suffering. Whatever is nirvāṇa is deathless, I say. Those endowed with heedfulness have not become formations; although they engage with formations, they are without aging, without death, and indestructible, I say.

"Now, who are those endowed with heedlessness, and who are those endowed with heedfulness? Worldly people are endowed with heedlessness and are like the dead. Those who have gone beyond the world are without aging and without death. Those endowed with heedfulness have attained nirvāṇa, the supreme happiness. Therefore I have taught that some dharmas are suffering and some dharmas are happiness.

"As for some dharmas having no self and some dharmas having a self: a person standing on the ground who lacks the divine eye does not see the track of a bird flying through the sky. In the same way, those who dwell in the afflictions do not themselves see that there is a supreme essence within them. Therefore I taught, with words of hidden intent, that there is no self. People who lack the divine eye do not see the self, even though there is a self within them; this is due to the afflictions, I say. Those dharmas that are formations of an afflicted kind are called impermanent. Therefore I have taught that some dharmas are impermanent. For this reason it is said:

One endowed with wisdom, standing on the mountaintop,
Clearly sees the childish, standing on the ground.
One who stands upon the palace of wisdom,
Though free of sorrow, sees that beings are in sorrow.

It is like this: having cut off the afflicted kinds of mind, the wise one dwelling on the mountaintop sees the childish, who are bound up with the afflicted kinds of mind."

He asked:

"Who are those who stand upon the roof of wisdom's house?
Who in the world is free of sorrow?
For one free of sorrow, passed beyond sorrow,
How could any sorrow come to be?

If the Buddha, the Blessed One, has passed into parinirvāṇa, how do the childish see him? And how does he dwell upon the palace of wisdom? A person standing on a mountaintop is able to see, but that is possible only if he is present. So how does one who has passed into parinirvāṇa see?"

The Blessed One said, "The palace of wisdom is nirvāṇa. The one free of sorrow is the Tathāgata. Those in whom sorrow predominates are beings. They are full of sorrow, while the Tathāgata is free of sorrow. 'Mountain' means liberation, because it is firm like Mount Meru. 'Ground' means the formations.

All childish beings are those who stand on the ground. "The wise one" refers to the perfect Buddha, because the Tathāgata is free of sorrow and steadfast. Among those who sorrow, the Buddha is the one without sorrow, and he removes the barb of sorrow from sentient beings.

It was asked: "If the Tathāgata has not passed beyond sorrow, then he still has sorrow, and he would be called one who is not perfectly awakened."

He declared: "There is a reason. Wherever there are those to be trained, there the perfect Buddha appears, and there he shows himself as if taking birth. Therefore the Tathāgata is permanent, and the Buddha is said to be like the shelduck and the zalmo bird."

The eleventh section.

"Son of noble family, consider the moon. When it appears in the daytime, in some places it seems to be setting, and sentient beings then take it that the moon has set. Yet the moon does not cease to exist. It rises over another continent, and there too the moon has not been born. Sentient beings take it that the moon has risen. Though the moon seems to set, it is not destroyed, and though it seems to rise, it is not born. It is only that it cannot be seen because Meru, king of mountains, hides it. By its nature the moon has always already arisen, for such is the nature of luminaries.

"In the same way, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, in some parts of Jambudvīpa within the great trichiliocosm world system appears to be born from the union of a father and mother, and sentient beings take it that he has been born. Yet for the Tathāgata there is no birth. In some parts of Jambudvīpa he appears to pass into parinirvāṇa, yet for the Tathāgata there is no death. Sentient beings take it that he has died. Though the Tathāgata seems to set and to be born, there is neither birth nor death for him. By the power of those to be trained, he moves about just like the moon.

"In some continents the moon appears as the fully rounded full moon, and sentient beings take it that the moon is full. In some continents the moon appears as a new crescent shaped like the letter ta, and the people of Jambudvīpa take it that today is the first day of the moon. Then they take it to be a half moon. Then, thinking that it is the fifteenth day, they take it to be the full moon. Some take it to be a half moon. The moon becomes half, yet it neither rises nor sets; the moon appears in these ways because of the condition of Mount Meru.

"Son of noble family, in the same way, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, in some parts of Jambudvīpa appears to pass into parinirvāṇa, and sentient beings take it that the Tathāgata has passed into parinirvāṇa. In some parts of Jambudvīpa, like the new moon, he appears to be born, and sentient beings take it that this child has been born. Like the moon on its second day, they take it that he goes before the gods. Like the moon on its third day, they take it that he goes to the school where writing is learned. Like the moon on its eighth day, he shows the going forth. Like the full moon, just as its light dispels darkness, he destroys millions of māras and abides in radiant brilliance. Like the waning moon, he shows the parinirvāṇa. Just as the moon is encircled by the planets, he is adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks. There, some take it that he has set and some take it that he is born, yet there is neither birth nor cessation, for the Tathāgata, like the full moon, is permanent at all times. Therefore the Tathāgata is called permanent and steadfast.

"Consider again the moon. It appears everywhere: in water, in wildernesses, in houses and cities, in basins filled with water, in wells, and elsewhere, even as far as a thousand yojanas, in every place where sentient beings go, it appears in all of them. There the childish think in their minds, 'This moon follows behind me alone.' When they see it inside the house, they wonder, 'Now that it appears here, is this the same moon, or is it some other one?' Among them, some children take the moon to be the size of a basin. Those of middling understanding take it to be about the size of a cartwheel. The best know that the moon is round and about fifty yojanas across. Animals see the moon as the size of a firefly; animals of every kind of birth examine the moon with their own consciousness and so see it each in their own way.

"In the same way, when the Tathāgata appears in the world, he appears everywhere. Some sentient beings take the Tathāgata to be like the moon, thinking, 'Out of love for me he dwells in the innermost part of my house.' Animals see him as having a body like that of their own kind of birth. Mute sentient beings see the Tathāgata as mute. The deaf and the maimed also see him as like themselves. Those of different languages, perceptions, and scripts also take him to be like themselves, thinking, 'He speaks my language, he agrees with my perceptions, he eats my food.' Those with large bodies also take it that the Tathāgata is like them. Some take him to be a śrāvaka or a pratyekabuddha. Some see him as having gone forth among all the various sects of wonder-workers. Some take it that the Tathāgata has appeared in the world for their sake.

"Just as the moon, abiding in its own nature, pleases all according to their individual natures, so too the Tathāgata, while being the Dharma body, assumes a body of skillful means like a medicinal tree against poison and delights sentient beings. Through acts in accord with the whole world, he shows many thousands of birth stories. Therefore, because he shows birth everywhere, the Tathāgata is called the moon of the birth stories. Therefore the Tathāgata is permanent and steadfast."

Son of noble family, here is another example. When the moon and Rāhu, lord of the asuras, meet, worldly people think, "The moon has been seized by the planet." Yet Rāhu does not seize the moon, nor is he able to eat it. All that happens is that Rāhu's hand covers the moon so that its light does not appear. The moon suffers no wound, and no affliction either. To the extent that Rāhu's hand covers the moon, to that extent the moon's light does not appear. When Rāhu releases the moon, it looks as if it had come out of his mouth, and people think, "The moon is suffering." While the moon remains just as it is by nature, it shows itself as being together with Rāhu. This is the way of the world. Even millions of Rāhus could not kill or destroy the moon; it is the moon itself that displays things in this way.

In the same way, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha himself, makes a display like the moon. For the sake of the future, in order to teach the deed of immediate retribution that is drawing blood with malicious intent, he also displays such things as the extreme of the icchantikas and the various kinds of schism in the Saṅgha. Not even a hundred thousand koṭis of māras could draw blood from the body of the Tathāgata. In the body of the Tathāgata there is no blood, no bone, and no sinew, and not even a trace of suffering. Sentient beings think, "The three foundations are ruined! The Tathāgata has been wounded." Although the Tathāgata abides just as he is by nature, he makes such a display in accord with the world.

It is like this. Among people, one person strikes another with a whip or a stick and draws blood, or with the intention to kill he kills him. After that, the Tathāgata, intending to make that deed weighty, lets blood be drawn from the Tathāgata with malicious intent; then, understanding that person's mind, he truly teaches the deed of immediate retribution. In this way the Tathāgata, for the sake of the future, teaches the distinctions of karma.

For example, a certain physician teaches his son the various names of roots: "These are the five roots; this is the bitter one." He introduces them by way of the distinctions among the roots. The boy, having been trained in this way, comes to know the distinctions among the roots. Then, when the physician has died, the boy says, "Alas, father! Alas, father! You taught me the distinctions among the roots: this root is like this, the leaves are like this." In the same way, the Tathāgata too emanates manifestations of all the bases of training. He teaches what is curable and what is incurable, the deeds of immediate retribution, the rejection of the sūtras, and the extremes of the icchantikas, meaning, "In the future it will be like this." Then, after the Tathāgata has passed into parinirvāṇa, the monks, like the physician's son, will know, "The Tathāgata declared this in the sūtras: this deed is such and such."

For example, when an eclipse comes over the moon, people know that the moon is eclipsed once in six months. The gods above know the moon's eclipse as happening as if every day. Why is that? Because their year periods are long and the year periods of humans are short. Son of noble family, in the same way gods and humans think, "The life span of the Tathāgata is short." Just as the higher and higher gods perceive the moon being eclipsed moment after moment, in the same way, to the higher and higher gods, he displays, moment after moment, even millions of passings of the Tathāgata into parinirvāṇa. One should know that he displays even millions of the māras of the afflictions, of the aggregates, of the gods, and of the Lord of Death; that he displays even thousands of births; and that he displays many acts in accord with the world. Thus the Tathāgata is immeasurable, and therefore he is permanent and stable.

For example, the moon is pleasing to the minds of all sentient beings and beautiful to behold, and for that reason it is called "the moon." It is not pleasing to the minds of those who have come under the sway of desire and who indulge in attachment, aversion, and delusion. In the same way, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is by nature pleasing to the minds of sentient beings who long for the Dharma, and supremely beautiful to behold. To others, who have hostile minds, he is not pleasing. Therefore the Tathāgata is like the moon.

For example, the sun changes and shifts through three seasons, traveling through the three times of winter, summer, and spring. In winter the sun moves very quickly. In summer the sun moves at a middling pace. In spring the sun lingers long as it moves, and its light is very hot. The Tathāgata likewise displays three kinds of birth in the three worlds. Among short-lived sentient beings he displays a supremely short life span, and sentient beings think, "The Buddha's life span is short." To those of the Śrāvaka Vehicle he displays a short life span, like the winter sun. To bodhisattva great beings he displays a middling life span, like the summer sun. To buddhas he appears to remain for an eon or more than an eon, like the spring sun.

Son of noble family, from this Great Vehicle I shall truly teach words of hidden intent, the rain of the Tathāgata's secret, sacred Dharma. In the future, whoever teaches the beneficial instructions of the sūtra collection of the sacred Dharma, the Great Vehicle, are like clouds in the summer sky; they should be known to be bodhisattva great beings. Śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, when they hear the words of hidden intent, are like those struck by a winter chill. For that reason I taught a short life span and that which will certainly be experienced. From the vast abode of the buddhas, where the life of the Buddha is taught, I taught permanence and stability, like the spring sun.

Like the sun, I never fall anywhere, and I also follow the nature of the sun and moon. In this way the Tathāgata appears perfectly in the three worlds. This is the nature of reality of the buddhas.

Furthermore, son of noble family, when Venus, Jupiter, Mars, the comet, the other planets and the rest move together with the sun and the moon, sentient beings cannot see them. Yet these do not turn back, nor do they fall, because their light is very faint and is outshone by the far greater light of the sun and moon. In the same way, the Tathāgata always appears as if born together with the host of planets that are the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and also shows himself passing into parinirvāṇa. Yet those śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are neither impermanent nor unstable. Although they are permanent and stable, sentient beings cannot see them, just as with the planets by day. Being like the sun in this way is the nature of reality of the pratyekabuddhas.

For example, when the sun and moon are covered by clouds they are not seen, and sentient beings take it that they do not appear; yet the two of them do not thereby cease to exist. Son of noble family, in the same way, when the Tathāgata does not remain in the world, it seems, like the sun and moon covered by clouds, as though the three bases are being destroyed. Yet the three bases do not cease to exist. Though they remain permanent, stable and everlasting, they are not stained by faults. This is the nature of reality of the buddhas.

Son of noble family, for example, on the day of the new moon, when the moon is neither waxing nor waning, at a time when neither sun nor moon is present, a comet called Endowed with Fierce Splendor rises, and in a single instant vanishes without the whole world noticing. Son of noble family, in the same way, when the buddhas do not remain, the pratyekabuddhas too arise like the comet rising on the day of the new moon, establish many tens of millions of sentient beings in the sacred Dharma, and then vanish. The comet, too, is always present, but sentient beings cannot see it. Likewise, although the pratyekabuddhas are always present, sentient beings cannot see them. To reveal themselves perfectly and to make themselves unseen: this is their nature of reality.

Son of noble family, just as, for example, when the sun rises the fog disappears, in the same way, when this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra appears, even by merely hearing it the heaps of evil from the defeats and from the deeds of immediate retribution swiftly disappear. Thus this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, this inconceivable domain, is immeasurable, because it teaches the buddha-nature. Therefore sons of noble family and daughters of noble family should strive in this way in understanding the sūtra, thinking, "The Tathāgata is permanent. The sacred Dharma will not decline. The Saṅgha will not cease to exist." If they do so, they too, like me, will live in the state of a buddha.

He asked, "Blessed One, what are bodhisattva great beings like?"

The Blessed One said, "Whether they have generated the mind or have not generated the mind, they become bodhisattva great beings."

He asked, "How is that?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, just as, for example, ice frozen in winter cannot be melted even by the heat of the sun, in the same way, as regards the fruit of cultivating what is to be cultivated in all the sūtras and all the samādhis, even those who have generated the mind do not attain awakening, and those who have not generated the mind do not attain awakening either. Why is that? Because, like the winter sun, their diligence is weak.

The supreme fruit of cultivating what is to be cultivated in the sūtras is like this: when the summer sun is hot, even before the sun has risen, it melts even ice that froze hard during the night, and it warms even the great ocean. In the same way, whatever sons of noble family or daughters of noble family, as soon as they hear this sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, whether they take it up out of rivalry, or are made to do so by others, or are urged on by others, or do so for the sake of gain, honor, or verses, even if they think, 'I have no need of awakening,' or have no wish to generate the mind, the cause of awakening will nonetheless enter every pore of the body of such a person, like the summer sun. Therefore I say that those sons of noble family are bodhisattva great beings.

Thus this great sūtra of inexhaustible awakening is a great heap of merit, and therefore it is called great. It is first, vast and splendid like the summer sun, and therefore it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa. Why is that? Because it teaches that the buddha-nature is permanent.

Furthermore, son of noble family, the light of the stars and of fireflies does not surpass the light of the sun and moon and shine out brilliantly. The light of the sun and moon surpasses all lights and shines radiantly. In the same way, the other lights do not surpass the light of the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, the cultivation of what is to be cultivated in all the sūtras and all the samādhis, and do not shine out brilliantly. The light of the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra surpasses all lights and shines out brilliantly. Entering the pores of all sentient beings, even if they think, 'I have no need of the awakening mind,' and have not generated it, they will certainly obtain the cause of awakening. Therefore it is also named the Great Parinirvāṇa."

He said, "Please explain the reason for this statement, that even if they hold that they have no need of the awakening mind, it enters every pore of the bodies of all sentient beings and the cause of awakening is actualized."

"Blessed One, in this teaching there are those who commit defeats, those who commit the acts of immediate retribution, those who reject the sūtras, and also icchantikas. If, even without generating the mind of awakening and while holding it to be unnecessary, they still come to have a cause of the awakening of the awakened, then what distinction is there in what the Buddha has taught? Otherwise it should be explained that those four do not exist. Or else it should be explained that by this their evil is purified."

The Blessed One said, "Apart from the icchantikas, everyone, merely by hearing this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa resound in their ears, comes to have a cause of awakening. Those who have honored countless buddhas are able to hear the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, and because their roots of virtue are not meager, they obtain a cause of awakening. Therefore, because it rightly teaches buddha-nature, this great sūtra is of great power."

He asked, "How does one who has not generated the mind of awakening, and who holds it to be unnecessary, come to have a cause of the awakening of the awakened?"

The Blessed One said, "Those who hold generating the mind of awakening to be unnecessary and who disparage the sūtras are threatened in their dreams by a great rākṣasa of terrifying form, who says to them, 'Quickly generate the mind of awakening, before I kill you!' Then, frightened and terrified in their dream, they wake, and in the morning they generate the mind of awakening. After they die, whether they are cooked in the hells, or are born from the world of hungry ghosts into the heavens, they remember it, and after that they become bodhisattva great beings. Thus this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa is of great power: even in those who hold awakening to be unnecessary and have not generated the mind, it produces a cause of awakening. That is the cause; that is the condition. In this Great Vehicle nothing is without a cause. Therefore my teaching is called 'spoken by the Buddha.'

"Furthermore, son of noble family, when a great cloud sends down rain from the sky, the great earth becomes filled with water. Yet on high ground, even when heavy rain has fallen, not even a little water remains; it flows down to wherever the land slopes, and having flowed there it stays, and it benefits all beings. In the same way, the rain of the true Dharma falls from this great sūtra, yet in the icchantikas no cause of awakening arises; it cannot possibly arise. In others it produces one, like water on sloping ground.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, suppose a great cloud were to rain a thousand times on scorched seeds of lentils, chickpeas, peas, or grass. Still they could not grow sprouts; it is impossible for sprouts to come from them. In the same way, even if icchantikas hear this Great Parinirvāṇa, they cannot possibly obtain a cause of awakening, because their roots of virtue are exhausted, and so they are like scorched seeds.

"Son of noble family, when a kataka fruit is put into muddy water, it removes the mud and makes the water clear. In the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa, like the kataka fruit, removes the mire and turbidity of the karma of beings' defeats and acts of immediate retribution and sets them on the cause of awakening, the icchantikas excepted. Yet the kataka fruit only makes turbid water clear; even if it were worked for a thousand years, it could not draw water out of the mire itself. In the same way, since the roots of virtue of icchantikas are exhausted and they have become baseless, even if they hear this Great Parinirvāṇa for ten million years, they are utterly unable to produce a cause of awakening, because they have no virtue.

"Son of noble family, consider the antidote tree, the great king of medicines. Whether it is mixed with buttermilk, molasses, ghee, milk, or water, or made into powder, paste, or soup, or smeared on the mouth, or burned as a fumigant, or applied to the eyes, or smelled, seen, or touched, it removes the diseases of all beings. That tree does not think, 'It is fine if my leaves are taken, but not if my roots are taken,' or 'It is fine if my roots are taken, but not if my leaves are taken,' or 'It is fine if my trunk is taken, but not my bark,' or 'It is fine if my bark is taken, but not my trunk.' Although it forms no such thoughts, it is simply medicine, and it removes all diseases.

"Son of noble family, in the same way this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa removes the diseases of evil karma of all beings, whether they are those who commit defeats, those who commit acts of immediate retribution, or those outside this teaching. Even in those who do not hold awakening to be necessary and have not generated the mind, it produces a cause of awakening. Why is this? Because the medicine of the antidote tree for all evils is the great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa. Whether their bodies have been anointed or not, if they merely hear the name of this sūtra and know that this sūtra exists, all the diseases of evil will be cleansed.

"But the incurable disease called anargala cannot be cured, whatever is done. Why is this? Because it is a disease that ends in death; it is not a disease of untimely death. In the same way, the icchantikas cannot be placed in awakening by this sūtra, because they are without a cause and are dead.

"Just as poison does not take hold in the palm of a hand that has no wound, but does take hold in one that has a wound, so here too a cause of awakening does not arise in the icchantikas. Here, 'wound' means the cause of awakening; 'poison' means supreme bliss; and the palm with no wound is the evil icchantika.

"A vajra jewel of great splendor, for example, cannot be broken by anything, and it smashes every other kind of thing, yet it does not cut a sheep's horn."

In the same way, even this great Sūtra of Nirvāṇa cannot establish in awakening the icchantikas who commit extremely evil deeds, because they have no cause for it, just as a vajra cannot pierce a ram's horn.

For example, the sallakī tree, the doṅka tree, the kanakaprabha, the silk-cotton tree, the valmīki, the karṇavalya, the skandastava, the ketakī, the pattraka, the vīraṇa and the rocana may be felled, chopped into pieces, carried off and laid in a house. Yet as soon as they meet the moisture of water they quickly sprout. Their leaves unfold, they bear clusters of buds, they put out branches and stems, and they come to be counted among what are called trees. In the same way, in this teaching too, those who commit defeats and those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution have become like a tāla palm with its crown cut off. Yet as soon as they hear this sūtra, even though they do not hold that they must generate the resolve, those supreme persons will be born into awakening, like the sallakī tree, the doṅka tree and the rest.

For example, khadira and tinduka trees, once they are cut, do not grow again, like burnt seeds. In the same way, icchantika beings, even though they hear this sūtra many times, are not born into awakening, like burnt seeds.

For example, though rain has fallen from a great cloud, water does not collect on ground that is slick. In the same way, though the rain of Dharma falls from this cloud of the great sūtra, the cause of awakening does not take hold in the icchantika, because he is slick like a vajra.

The icchantikas do not see the good that has been done; they see only the evil of reviling and disparaging.

"Well done" means awakening. "Does not come" means does not meet. "Intention" means virtue. If one asks to whom the virtue that far surpasses intention does not come: good karma does not come to the icchantika. If one asks to whom a virtuous mind does not come: the one known as an icchantika is a being without virtue, under the sway of pride; it does not come to that icchantika. If one asks what the root factor is: it is the rejection of the sūtras. Therefore the rejection of the sūtras is a thing to be dreaded; be afraid of it!

For that reason the wise and steadfast
Are afraid, since on the great road there are conditioned things.
The childish have no fear of the great road;
Through the power of pride they go to an unbearable destiny.
Therefore those of poor understanding do not attain it,
Like monkeys grasping at the moon.
Like a lord of men setting out upon the great road,
The wise and steadfast will attain it.

If one asks by whom what has been done is not seen: it is not seen by the evil icchantika. Until the very end of saṃsāra he does not see what has been done. I have taught its meaning in brief; since it is supremely to be dreaded, be afraid of it!

If it were possible that at some time all sentient beings, of one accord and one-pointed mind, should fully and perfectly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, then it might also be possible for the evil icchantika to fully and perfectly awaken to supreme awakening. That is what is meant by not seeing what has been done. Hero, you should understand it in this way.

If one asks whose deeds are not wasted: the deeds of the Tathāgata are not wasted. If all sentient beings dwelling in saṃsāra were to fully and perfectly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, then the deeds of the Tathāgata would be wasted. For then, having passed forever into final nirvāṇa, the Buddha would become impermanent, like a lamp, or like a fire whose wood is used up.

In the same way, the evil deeds of the icchantika are reviling and disparaging. If a bodhisattva abandons the merit that is the cause of awakening, that too is called reviling and disparaging the Great Vehicle. If he does not abandon it, he will then bear the name "perfect Buddha," for that is the nature of reality of the buddhas.

Milk and evil deeds
Do not turn at once;
They are like fire covered with ash,
Which burns a child who steps on it.

The icchantika, a lone blind man who imagines he is an arhat, wishes to travel the great inexhaustible path. Imagining that he is an arhat endowed with loving-kindness, he wishes to refute the Vaipulya.

One who is held to be an arhat, having refuted the Śrāvaka Vehicle, says: "I am a bodhisattva, a teacher of the Vaipulya. All sentient beings possess the qualities of buddha-nature. There is the Buddha." And he prophesies their buddhahood, saying: "You and I must shatter the forms of affliction like a water pot. Without doubt, one must cultivate awakening. Such is the instruction of the sūtras."

For example, a king's messenger, pure, loyal, skilled in speech and of great majesty, stakes his own life among enemies and delivers the king's words in full. In the same way, the wise one who cherishes the Vaipulya stakes his own life among the childish and prophesies buddhahood, because all sentient beings have buddha-nature.

The forest dweller who rejects the sūtras, the childish fool who imagines he is an arhat, is looked upon as though he were an arhat and as though he were a great being. That evil icchantika monk lives in the forest and reckons himself to be like an arhat. He says: "Monks who live in the forest do not accept invitations from others for the four requisites. The Vaipulya was spoken by Māra. The Blessed One is impermanent. The Dharma and the Saṅgha too will cease to be. Such omens of the disappearance of the true Dharma are appearing." And he says, "This is well explained in the Great Vehicle." One who speaks in this way what is not the true Dharma does not turn at once, like milk; but like one who steps onto fire covered with ash, these childish icchantikas will later be burned.

Thus the Buddha's teaching, the Great Vehicle, is subtle, and this sūtra is like the kataka fruit.

Son of good family, a closed lotus flower opens when the light of the sun touches it. In the same way, as soon as they hear this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, even all the sentient beings who hold that they need not generate so much as the mere name of the awakening mind will, whether they wish it or not, have the cause of awakening enter every one of their pores. The icchantikas too have the tathāgatagarbha, yet in them it remains thickly covered, as though veiled. A silkworm winds itself all around, and once it has sealed itself in, it cannot come back out. In the same way, because of the fault of their karma, the tathāgatagarbha cannot be brought forth from within the icchantikas. Therefore, to the very end of saṃsāra, they do not obtain the cause of awakening.

Son of good family, lotuses, utpalas, kumudas and white lotuses grow up out of the mud, yet the mud does not stain them. In the same way, in this great sūtra those of the Great Vehicle, even though they still have afflictions, are counted among those without afflictions. Why are they not stained? They are not stained because of the blessing of the tathāgatagarbha.

When a cool breeze rises, it enters every pore of all sentient beings, whether they wish it or not, and soothes all their heat. In the same way, this great sūtra also enters every pore of all sentient beings and, whether they wish it or not, establishes them in the cause of awakening. The only exception is the icchantikas, who do not hold that generating the awakening mind is needed.

Son of good family, a physician skilled in the eight branches of the science of life heals all the sick, yet he cannot heal those who are incurable. In the same way, all the sūtras and samādhis heal all who are stricken with the disease of attachment, aversion and delusion, and remove every thorn of the afflictions, yet they cannot heal those who commit defeats or those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution.

A physician who knows the Later Section of the eight branches of the science of life heals the diseases of all sentient beings, yet even he cannot heal a disease when the time of death has come. In the same way, this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra also removes every disease of affliction in all sentient beings and sets sentient beings in the cause of awakening. The only exception is the icchantikas, who are like those whose time of death has come.

Those blind from birth do not know even the light of the sun and moon, so how could they see clearly? A great physician treats the eyes of those whose sight is dim, and they come to see, but the one blind from birth is the exception. In the same way, this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra treats the eyes of all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, who are like the blind, and swiftly lets them see with the eye of the Great Vehicle. It sets in awakening even those who commit defeats and those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution while holding that they need not generate the awakening mind. The only exception is the icchantikas, who remain utterly blind from birth.

Son of good family, a great physician who knows the Later Section of the eight branches of the science of life removes the diseases of all sentient beings by the treatments of pouring medicine into the nose, giving it to drink internally, rubbing it on the body, and fumigating with smoke. There are those who have no protector, and those who think, "We need no medicine," and so do not resolve to seek it. He lays them in houses or among the grasses, and by the power of mantras, medicines and antidotes he removes their poisons and diseases. When pregnant women wish to give birth but cannot deliver, by the power of antidotes he lets them give birth easily, and he keeps the child free of disease as well. In the same way, when this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is made into a book and kept in the homes of any sentient beings, they too are made happy by the power of the Great Vehicle, and through this great sūtra they even become bodhisattva mahāsattvas. It sets in awakening even those who commit defeats and those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution while holding that they need not generate the awakening mind. The only exception is the icchantikas, who are like the dead.

He asked, "Those who commit defeats and the terrible deeds of immediate retribution are like a palm tree whose crown has been cut off. If they hold, 'We need not generate the mind set on awakening,' then how, without generating that mind, will the cause of awakening arise in them?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of good family, whether in a dream or after death, when they are being boiled in the hells, they think, 'We have done what is not good. If there were some way to be freed from here, then, having seen this terrible fear, wherever we are born, there we would generate the mind set on awakening.' Such a thought of bringing forth the sublime Dharma bears great fruit.

"Consider a child who, once his strength has grown, comes to understand himself and says, 'When I was in my mother's womb, this physician, by the power of mantra and antidote, kept my mother well, so this life of mine is due to your kindness. Alas, my mother carried me in her womb for twelve months and was sorely afflicted, and then she gave birth to me with great hardship. She washed me when I was smeared with the slime and blood of the womb, she gave me milk when my lips and mouth were wet and dribbling, and she nursed me by every means she had. She did what is supremely difficult. Now, since I was born through your kindness, I must make offerings to you as to a guru, and I shall act accordingly.' In the same way, those who commit defeats and those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution, at the time of death, through the fruit of not having forgotten mindfulness, will be born in the world of the gods. Then, those who commit defeats and those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution, again through the fruit of not having forgotten mindfulness, become a cause of awakening in the hells or in the human world. The only exception is the icchantikas."

For example, a physician, or a physician's son who knows the Later Section, uses antidotes and the power of vidyā-mantras to subdue venomous snakes, harmless ones, the nāga called Bird-Choker, and others. He brings them under his control as though they were on his own path, and he makes them free of poison, except for nāgas that are exceedingly fierce. In the same way, this great sūtra, which is like an antidote, also subdues those who commit defeats, and the sinful, supremely fierce nāgas who commit the acts of immediate retribution and hold that there is no need to generate the mind of awakening. It establishes them in the great state of awakening. Therefore, because it brings about the happiness of all sentient beings, the Great Parinirvāṇa is called "the Great Antidote, whose nature is good qualities." The exception is the fierce and terrible nāgas who are icchantikas.

For example, a physician prepares a poison medicine that draws out darts, a king among medicines, and smears it on a great drum. If that drum is beaten within the palace grounds, then by the power of its sound the darts of everyone who hears it will all be drawn out. But for those there who are close to the time of death, they will not come out. Why is that? Because their karma has a terrible ripening. In the same way, wherever the sound of the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is heard, it draws out the darts of attachment, aversion and delusion from all the sentient beings who dwell within the palace grounds of formations, and it also makes them free of formations. Those who dwell within the palace grounds may think, "The darts will not come out," but how could they ever think, "The darts will come out"? Likewise, the others also have no thought that the darts of attachment, aversion and delusion will come out; their minds dwell on desires. In the same way, it produces the cause of awakening even for those who commit defeats and those who commit the acts of immediate retribution and hold that there is no need to generate the mind of awakening. The exception is the icchantikas, who are like those who have come to the time of death.

Son of noble family, for example, in the darkness of night the sentient beings of Jambudvīpa do not set about work such as plowing. They stay where they are and wait for the sun to rise. Then, once the sun has risen, they set about plowing and the rest. In the same way, those of the Great Vehicle, having heard and meditated on all the sūtras and samādhis, wait for the sun of the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra to rise, just as beings who want to plow wait through the night. Then, having heard the words of intended meaning in this great sūtra, they begin their work in the field of the sublime Dharma. And just as, once the fields have been plowed, a great rain falls, the crops grow and the year turns out well, so too, once the field of the sublime Dharma has been plowed, the rain of Dharma heard from the great cloud of the great sūtra of the tathāgatagarbha falls. Then the crop of the great prophecy grows, bringing liberation from the eight kinds of epidemic, happiness, good years and delight. Just as grain comes to full ripeness in the first month of spring, so it will be like the full ripening of the crop of the great prophecy given to the eighty great śrāvakas in the great Sūtra of the White Lotus of the Sublime Dharma. The exception is the icchantikas.

The twelfth fascicle.

Son of noble family, for example, a great physician sends pills of medicine or antidotes many leagues away for those seized by bhūtas or bitten by snakes, saying: "Take this medicine quickly, and once you have taken it, give them the treatment. If you do this, the grahas of gods, nāgas, yakṣas and rākṣasas there will disappear. If you do not take it, I must go myself, for it would not do for it to be wasted in the meantime." Then, as soon as the patient sees the physician or the physician's messenger from far off, all harm is pacified by the splendor and power of that mantra, and he too becomes well. In the same way, suppose a monk, a nun, or anyone else outside this teaching knows, or hears from another, that a written copy of this powerful great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is kept in a certain place. That becomes for them a cause of awakening. And however many who commit defeats or acts of immediate retribution, and are seized by the grahas of bhūtas and piśācas, may hear of it, all of them, like those who see the great physician, will be freed from harm and will become bodhisattva great beings. Why is that? It comes about through the power of the great sūtra on the permanence of the Tathāgata. That being so, how could those who read this great sūtra written in a volume, or hear it, or write it out, fail to become bodhisattva great beings? The exception is the icchantikas. Just as the deaf do not hear sound, so the icchantikas cannot hear this sūtra, because they lack the cause for it.

Furthermore, son of noble family, for example, a physician who knows the Later Section of the eightfold Āyurveda sees a great king and predicts: "Great king, you will be struck by an illness grave enough to bring you to death." The king said to him, "How do you know that a serious illness will arise, without having seen it?" Then the physician said to the king, "If you do not believe me, take some medicine and it will become clear." Even so, the king would not agree to drink the medicine. Then the great physician, by the power of his knowledge, made hemorrhoids break out at the king's anus.

Then the king saw those hemorrhoids and was frightened and dismayed. He shuddered and thought, "The physician's words were true." Knowing that the hemorrhoids had appeared, he thought, "Oh, how wondrous is the physician's power of knowledge!" and he honored that physician as one reveres one's father and mother. In the same way, this great sūtra of the Great Parinirvāṇa easily places in the cause of awakening even all those sentient beings who hold that there is no need to generate the mind of awakening, just as the great physician did for the king. Whether in their dreams or at the time of their death, the cause of awakening will enter those beings without their knowing it. The icchantikas are the exception. Even the great physician, thinking, "This cannot be cured," does not treat a fatal disease accompanied by terrible karma. The icchantika is like one who is bound to die.

Son of noble family, consider this example. A physician who knows the Later Section of the eight branches of the science of life first teaches his son the branches of that tantra, the medicines that grow on the plains, in the water, and on the mountains. While the son is only learning the first branches, he does not teach him the Later Section. When he has finished learning the eight branches of the science of life, then he teaches him, from the Later Section, the methods for accomplishing mantras and medicines. In the same way, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, also first teaches his sons, the monks, the first branches: purifying the forms of the afflictions, meditating on the body as without essence, and the three kinds of meditation that are like the medicines of the plains, the water, and the mountains. Here, "the plains" means the many kinds of bodily suffering. "The water" means that this body is impermanent, like a bubble on water. "The mountains" means the selflessness of the forms of the afflictions. Therefore the Tathāgata teaches, "This body is without self." If the monks have not seen the meaning of the nine-branched scriptures, he does not teach them this supreme Great Vehicle. But now, like the great physician, to those monks who have completed the work of understanding the meaning of the nine-branched scriptures of the Tathāgata, he teaches, from the Later Section of buddha-nature, that buddha-nature is permanent. Thus, when they hear the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra taught by the Tathāgata, it becomes the cause of awakening even for those beings who hold that there is no need to generate the mind of awakening, except for the icchantikas.

Son of noble family, in this way the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is inconceivable. Just as the great physician is supreme among physicians, so this sūtra is the supreme king of sūtras.

Furthermore, son of noble family, in order to ferry beings across, a boat comes from the far shore to the near shore and goes from the near shore to the far shore. In the same way, the Tathāgata, the Great Parinirvāṇa, having passed into parinirvāṇa, goes forth to ferry beings across, like a boat going to the far shore. Wherever there are those to be tamed, there he displays himself as a perfect buddha. Therefore, just as a boat goes wherever it wishes to ferry beings across, so too is the Tathāgata. For this reason he is called the Great Boatman. Hence the Tathāgata is permanent and stable. When there is a boat, the boatman ferries people across; the Tathāgata exists permanently, and he will go on ferrying beings across and taking birth.

Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this example. When a great ship sailing on the great ocean meets with wind, it travels straight ahead and covers many leagues in a single instant. If it does not meet with wind, it stays in one place for many years, or else it capsizes. In the same way, when beings who have fallen into the great ocean of ignorance meet with the king of winds, the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, they swiftly reach the far shore of awakening, like the ship. Those who do not meet with this king of winds, the sūtra, are lost in the wheel of saṃsāra, like a ship capsizing in the great ocean.

Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this example. When there is no wind in a perilous place on the great ocean, beings in dire straits think, "This is surely death." Then, without their knowing it, the king of winds carries them off in an instant, and they think, "Oh, how wonderful it is that we have been saved!" In the same way, this king of winds, this great sūtra, places in the cause of awakening, without their knowing it, those beings dwelling in the great ocean of ignorance who hold that there is no need to generate the mind of awakening.

Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this example. When a snake sheds its old skin, has it died, or has it ceased to exist?

He replied, "No, Blessed One, it has not."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, in the same way, the Tathāgata too, like one shedding an old skin, casts off his body of skillful means, the old trunk of a poisonous plant. Does the Tathāgata thereby become impermanent?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One. Just as a snake changes its skin, in some Jambudvīpa he casts off one body of skillful means and displays another body of skillful means."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, therefore the Tathāgata is permanent.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this example. A goldsmith smelts gold from a gold mine, and once it is free of impurities, whatever form of fine ornament one wishes and commissions the goldsmith to make, he makes it just so. In the same way, the Tathāgata, by skillful means, emanates forms of whatever kind those to be tamed in the twenty-five states of existence wish for, and so liberates beings. Therefore the body of the Tathāgata is limitless and permanent.

"Furthermore, son of noble family, consider this example. A mango tree, or a jambu tree, changes under the influence of the three seasons. Sometimes its buds burst open and its leaves sprout.

At times their leaves flourish and their shade is cool. At times they shed their leaves and look like dead wood. When that happens, are those trees to be called dead?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One, they are not."

The Blessed One said, "Noble son, in the same way the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, for the sake of those who are to be trained, displays three kinds of bodies of skillful means in the three worlds. He appears as though he were born, as though he were living and remaining, and as though he passed into parinirvāṇa. When he displays these, is the Tathāgata therefore impermanent?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One, he is not."

The Blessed One said, "Noble son, therefore the Tathāgata is permanent. Noble son, in this way both the body of the Tathāgata and the words of the Tathāgata's hidden intent are hard to understand.

"For example, suppose a certain king commands his minister, 'Bring the saindhava!' The word saindhava is used for salt, for a drinking vessel, for a sword, and for a horse. All four of these are called saindhava. Since one word has many meanings, when the king says 'saindhava' while he is taking his meal, the minister understands that saindhava means salt, and offers him salt. When the king says 'saindhava' after he has finished his meal, the minister understands that it means a drinking vessel, and offers him a drinking vessel. When the king says 'saindhava' as he is going to the pleasure garden, the minister understands that it means a horse, and offers him a horse. When the king says 'saindhava' at the time of battle, the minister understands that it means a sword, and offers him a sword. In this way the ministers grasp the four kinds of the king's words of hidden intent.

"Noble son, in the same way, this Great Vehicle also contains what are called the four kinds of impermanence. Followers of the Great Vehicle are like the minister, and they should understand these in the same way. When the Tathāgata has appeared in the world and teaches that the Buddha passes into parinirvāṇa, followers of the Great Vehicle should understand, 'He is teaching impermanence,' and they should cultivate the perception of impermanence. When the Tathāgata teaches that the true Dharma will decline and perish, they should understand, 'He is teaching suffering,' and they should cultivate suffering. When the Tathāgata tells the monks and nuns, 'My body is afflicted by great and endless illness,' and 'The Saṅgha will be destroyed,' they should understand, 'He is teaching selflessness,' and they should cultivate the meditation on selflessness.

"When the Tathāgata teaches that liberation is signless, empty, and nothingness, they should understand that he is teaching that liberation is free of the twenty-five kinds of existence. That is why it is called empty. Because there is no suffering in it, it is the complete absence of suffering, it is supreme bliss, and it is signless. Because there is nothing in it that is impermanent, unstable, not eternal, or not of a peaceful nature, liberation is permanent, stable, eternal, and peace, and that very liberation is the Tathāgata.

"When the Tathāgata teaches that all sentient beings possess buddha-nature, then one should cultivate the meditation on true permanence. I say that whichever monks meditate in this way are my śrāvakas. I say that, like the king's minister, they are skilled in words of hidden intent. If even the ways of a king contain words of hidden intent, how could my teaching lack words of hidden intent? Many wise people should understand it in this way. My teaching does not accord with any of the world.

"Noble son, further, consider palāśa, aśoka, and karṇikāra trees. When rain is scarce, their flowers become poor and their fruit becomes poor as well. The other flowers that grow in water and on dry land also become short-stemmed, poor in color, weak in luster, and faint in fragrance. In the same way, in the future, flowers and medicinal plants will decline in luster, fragrance, and so forth.

"Noble son, likewise, after I have passed away, in a future time when an evil age arrives and the true Dharma is perishing, because sentient beings will have little merit, just as flowers lose their luster when rain is scarce, so only about half of the verses that explain in the world the meaning of the verses of hidden intent of this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, and only about half of its words and its scattered phrases, will be handed down. Evil monks, too, lacking the strength themselves, will cast aside the words of hidden intent, the secret verses, the words of nectar, and will recite other things instead. Like thieves who carry off loads of chaff and throw away jewels of great worth, lax monks will throw away the verses of this sūtra, its words of hidden intent.

"Alas! A future time will come that is supremely dreadful and supremely terrifying. Alas! The sentient beings of the future will have very little good fortune, and they will not listen to this great sūtra. Those who do listen to it are bodhisattvas, great beings. They will grasp the meaning of reality and will not cling chiefly to the letters. They will teach others exactly as much as the meaning of the sūtra is, and will not teach what is meaningless.

Son of noble family, it is like this. A cowherd woman, wanting to make a profit, adulterates the milk by mixing in water and molasses. She carries the jug of milk away from the cattle pen and sells it to a fellow cowherd woman. That fellow cowherd woman in turn adulterates the milk with twice as much water and sells it to a dealer. That dealer in turn adulterates it with twice as much water and sells it to a fellow dealer. That fellow dealer in turn adulterates it with twice as much water. Then, in a city or a marketplace, a man who has come to buy wants to make milk porridge for the festival of setting his son's topknot. Thinking, "This is hard to come by," he buys the milk. He cooks the porridge with that watered milk, and that supreme food turns out weak in flavor. Even so, among all tasty things the essences of the cow are foremost, and among them milk is the finest, so that supreme food is still a thousand times more delicious than pungent fare.

Son of noble family, in the same way, after I have passed away, before many hundreds of years have gone by, when eighty years remain of the time the true Dharma endures, this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra will circulate in Jambudvīpa. At that time, while the sūtra is circulating, evil and utterly negligent monks, who rob the flower of the true Dharma of its fragrance, will tamper with the volumes. When they read this sūtra, they will drop the negating syllables such as na, ma and ku from the secret words of hidden intent, whose meaning and wording are hard to understand, and recite something else in their place. Those monks, who keep improper possessions in order to secure their own gain, are on the side of Māra. Just as the cowherd woman adulterated the milk with molasses water, they will corrupt this sūtra.

Furthermore, through people not knowing how to read, through bad preachers of the Dharma, through the faults of poor writing, through people not abiding in the Dharma, through their not serving their teachers with respect, through its being slighted by bad people, through unfavorable times, through the long passage of time, and through the faults of transmission from hand to hand, what circulates will have only about half of its words and meaning, and no more than a small remnant of the sūtra will survive. The supreme food cooked with milk bought second hand from the dealer turned out flavorless, and yet that supreme food is still the best of all tasty things. In the same way, though only a little of this great sūtra will remain, it is still a thousand times more important than all the sūtras of the Śrāvaka Vehicle. Why is this? Because all sūtras and all samādhis are the power of parinirvāṇa, it is called the supreme Great Parinirvāṇa. It is a great name.

Son of noble family, furthermore, a son or daughter of noble family should meditate so as to take on the nature of a man. Why? Because the nature of woman is the source of everything contrary to the Dharma, being its cause. For example, the urine of a fly will never moisten the great earth, nor ever saturate it. In the same way, women are never sated with desire. Suppose the great earth were rolled into pellets the size of juniper berries or of mustard seeds. Even if a woman were to indulge her desire, full of attachment, with as many men as there are pellets, she would not be sated, just as fly urine does not moisten and saturate the earth. Women are not sated with desire.

Son of noble family, furthermore, listen to how women are not good. Even if a woman were to indulge her desire, full of attachment, with as many men as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River, or with numbers beyond reckoning, multitudes upon multitudes, she would not be sated, just as fly urine does not moisten and saturate the earth. Women too are not sated with desire.

Son of noble family, furthermore, the great ocean is never filled, though the rain falling from great clouds and the waters of the rivers pour into it. In the same way, women are never sated with desire.

Son of noble family, furthermore, when flowering trees such as the aśoka, the dongka tree and the pāṭala are in full bloom, the bees are never sated with the nectar of the flowers. The bees drink the nectar skillfully, delicately and deeply. In the same way, women drink up men's wealth, their desire and their semen, and like the bees they are never sated.

Therefore, son of noble family, having heard this Great Parinirvāṇa, one should cultivate a mind free of attachment to the nature of woman, and cultivate the resolve to take on the nature of a man. Why? Because this sūtra rightly teaches the nature of a man, which is the tathāgatagarbha. The nature of a man is what is called a person. The nature of the Tathāgata is the man. Even those in the world who are men are not of the nature of a man, because they do not know that they themselves possess the tathāgatagarbha. Those who do not know the tathāgatagarbha, I say, are women. Those who rightly know, "I possess the tathāgatagarbha," belong among the supreme of men. Even if they are women, they belong among the supreme of men. Thus this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra is immeasurable, because it teaches the tathāgatagarbha. A son or daughter of noble family who wishes quickly to realize this point of the sūtra should strive at the tathāgatagarbha.

He said, "Blessed One, it is so. Blessed One, it is indeed so. I too, now, in order to meditate on the tathāgatagarbha, abide in the nature of a man. It has been made plain. I have understood the hint."

The Blessed One declared, "Excellent, son of noble family, excellent! One who conforms to the world should view it in this way."

He said, "Blessed One, this is not conforming to the world."

The Blessed One declared, "Excellent, son of noble family, excellent! Just as the bee feeds on the flowers of the trees, so should one partake of the food of the profound Dharma."

"Son of noble family, here is another example. A little fly's urine cannot soak the great earth. In the same way, in the future this great sūtra will be like a fly's urine: it will circulate in the world only a little. At the end, when the true Dharma is being destroyed, it will be used up, as a fly's urine sinks away into the great earth. This is the seventh sign of the disappearance of the true Dharma, and the wise should know that there are other signs as well.

"Son of noble family, here is another example. At the close of summer, the first month of winter is called autumn. When the autumn season arrives, the clouds let fall their rain to ripen what has not yet ripened, and then they quickly scatter. In the same way, this great sūtra, like the autumn clouds that rain down and quickly scatter, will go to the south and pour down the rain of Dharma of all the words of intended meaning of the Great Parinirvāṇa. Then the bodhisattva great beings of the south will know that the true Dharma is being destroyed. Like clouds when winter draws near, they will go to Kashmir and sink into the earth, and all the true Dharma of the Vaipulya sūtras of the Great Vehicle, the supreme nectar, will disappear. The bodhisattva great beings, the best of humans, should understand: 'Although this sūtra known to the Tathāgata has now come into our hands, the true Dharma will disappear.'"

He said, "So that all sentient beings may easily understand that the element of the tathāgatas, the pratyekabuddhas, the śrāvakas and the bodhisattvas is not different, may the Blessed One please explain it clearly and thoroughly."

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, suppose a householder or a householder's son owns many herds of cattle. He has cows of different colors, and among them are also blue cows, and he has a single herdsman graze them. Then on one occasion, in order to make offerings to his own god, the householder gathers all the herds together and has them milked. Seeing that the milk is all white, like the color of a conch shell, he is amazed and thinks, 'These cows here are of different colors, yet the milk of these blue cows is not blue, and the milk of the white cows is just the same: it is like the color of a conch shell.' Later he understands, and thinks, 'This should be seen as having come about from the cause of the maturation of the karma of beings.'

"Son of noble family, in the same way the nature of all śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and tathāgatas is like milk. Why? Because they have exhausted the defilements. All sentient beings take the nature of śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and tathāgatas to be different, and their forms to be different, but they too will gradually come to know, just like the householder.

"Son of noble family, just as from a gold mine or a copper mine there comes forth the very nature of gold, free of stone and grit and beyond price, in the same way all śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and tathāgatas are like gold, one in value and one in nature. Why? Because they are free of the afflictions, which are like stone and grit. Therefore there is no distinction in this buddha wisdom. That all sentient beings have the buddha element is also like this. Just as the householder came to understand the milk, they too, having heard the sūtra of the tathāgatagarbha, will exhaust every form of affliction and realize the buddha element."

He said, "Blessed One, what does this mean? Are all sentient beings and the tathāgatas not different? If the Tathāgata were to teach in the way he has just taught, there would be a great fault in it, so I ask you now to state the reason. Leaving aside all sentient beings and the pratyekabuddhas, if that is so, why do Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana pass into parinirvāṇa by the parinirvāṇa of the śrāvakas? Why do the pratyekabuddhas pass into parinirvāṇa by the parinirvāṇa of the pratyekabuddhas? Why do the bodhisattvas pass into parinirvāṇa by the great parinirvāṇa of the bodhisattvas?"

He replied, "The Great Parinirvāṇa of the buddhas does not belong to the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas or the bodhisattvas. In this Great Parinirvāṇa there is no place for anyone other than the buddhas."

He asked, "How is that?"

He replied, "Over a great many countless ten millions of eons, the Tathāgata displays the appearance of a tathāgata. If one asks what difference there is, then, between the tathāgatas, the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the bodhisattvas: son of noble family, I have taught that the arhats have no place in the secret nirvāṇa of the Tathāgata, and that is what is without place. Therefore, in the manner of this great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, the Great Parinirvāṇa is without any place. Therefore it is called the Great Parinirvāṇa."

He said, "It seems they are indeed different."

He replied, "It is as it appears."

He said, "The śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the bodhisattvas will also pass into parinirvāṇa by the Great Parinirvāṇa. Just as rivers flow down into the ocean, that is also their destination. Therefore they too are called permanent."

He replied, "They are, in my sense, supremely permanent."

He asked, "Blessed One, how can the element be both not different and different?"

He replied, "It is different."

He asked, "How so?"

He replied, "For example, cow's milk is supremely sweet, and the element is like that. The element of the śrāvakas and the pratyekabuddhas is like curd. The element of the bodhisattvas is like butter. The element of the buddhas is like the essence of butter. Thus the Tathāgata, who teaches the instructions of the Great Parinirvāṇa, has taught it as fourfold."

He asked, "What is the element of all sentient beings like?"
