The Blessed One said, "Like milk that is mixed with blood just after a calf has suckled, that element, mixed with the blood of the afflictions, pervades the tathāgata element, which is like the essence of butter."

He asked, "The Blessed One has prophesied that a caṇḍāla living here in the city of Kuśinagara will, in this world system, become one tathāgata among a thousand buddhas, the Tathāgata called Utterly Joyful, and that through a single generation of the mind he will quickly become a buddha within such and such a time. Since he has been prophesied to buddhahood, why have the two, Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana, who will become buddhas even more quickly, not been prophesied to buddhahood?"

The Blessed One said, "Among śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and bodhisattvas, there are some who make aspirations for a long time in order to uphold the sacred Dharma, and there are some who make aspirations for swiftness. By the power of the latter's aspiration, I prophesy them very swiftly to the supreme state of buddhahood.

"Furthermore, noble son, suppose a great merchant went to a city of caṇḍālas to sell a priceless jewel. When the caṇḍālas, who could not tell its worth, saw that jewel, they would ridicule it and disparage it. Even though the merchant told them, 'It is precious,' they would not understand, and would say, 'This is glass.' Noble son, in the same way, even if I were to prophesy the śrāvakas swiftly to buddhahood, to the state of a buddha, I would be like the merchant selling a rare gem. Those who are like the caṇḍālas are the foolish śramaṇas who will appear in the future: lazy, having abandoned the roots of virtue, destitute of livelihood, tormented by hunger and thirst, afflicted by famine, caring only for their own bodies, seeking after a livelihood, and unsteady. When they hear that my śrāvakas have been prophesied, they will mock and revile them, and, not understanding, they will slander them, saying, 'They are immoral.' To them, these śrāvakas of mine are not śramaṇas, just as it was with the caṇḍālas and the merchant. Therefore, noble son, I prophesy to buddhahood those beings who have aspirations for swiftness. Those who are engaged in upholding the sacred Dharma I prophesy, as they wish, to the state of buddhahood after a long time."

He asked, "How do bodhisattva mahāsattvas come to have a retinue that cannot be divided?"

The Blessed One said, "If the supreme bodhisattvas, thinking, 'I shall uphold the sacred Dharma,' make that practice firm, they will have a retinue that cannot be divided."

He asked, "From what do wicked and terrifying beings, whose tongue, palate, and throat are burnt, come about?"

The Blessed One said, "Those who do not know the three foundations become wicked and terrifying beings whose tongue, palate, and throat are burnt. For example, when humans and animals with burnt tongues eat the six tastes, namely sweet, sour, salty, pungent, bitter, and astringent, they cannot distinguish one taste from another. In the same way, ignorant beings who do not know the three foundations to be permanent are wicked and terrifying, and are called those whose tongue, palate, and throat are burnt.

"Furthermore, noble son, I say that those who do not know the Tathāgata to be permanent are the foremost of the blind. I say that those who know the Tathāgata to be permanent, even though they have fleshly eyes, have the divine eye. Furthermore, noble son, I say that those who, having heard this sūtra, know and understand the Tathāgata to be permanent have the divine eye. I say that those who, even though they have the divine eye, do not know the Tathāgata to be permanent, have fleshly eyes. Even for many more tens of millions of eons, they will not be able to see or show what their own head and tail are like. Therefore, because they are without the meditation on the Tathāgata as permanent, I say they have fleshly eyes.

"Furthermore, noble son, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the Perfectly Complete Buddha is like a father and mother to the whole world. Why is that? Because he teaches the Dharma to all beings, two-legged and many-legged, each according to their own domain, and with a single word all beings understand, each according to their own domain, and they think, 'Ah, he is teaching the Dharma so that we may realize our own natures and perceptions.'

"Furthermore, noble son, suppose a householder or a merchant speaks as if he were mute with his own son who is sixteen months old, and by that means the son learns human speech. Would that father then be called mute?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One. It should be seen that he does so for the sake of the son."

The Blessed One said, "Noble son, in the same way the Tathāgata too speaks as if he were mute. If he displays such a form in order to establish all beings in the sacred Dharma, would the Tathāgata then be called mute?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One. Since the Tathāgata always roars the lion's roar, his speaking as if mute should be regarded as acting in accordance with the world."

Then, at that time, various rays of light spread forth from the Blessed One's own mouth, and those rays struck Cunda, the son of the smith. He thought, "Ah, a gift that hereafter can no longer be offered to the Tathāgata is rare indeed." Very hastily he took food enough to satisfy the Tathāgata together with the Saṅgha of monks, and, together with his own retinue, he hastily brought vessels of stainless Jambū River gold adorned with tall, radiant jewel bosses, and some silver vessels adorned with various precious stones, filled with food combined with various relishes, together with many articles of offering. Coming out from the midst of the city, he set out for where the Blessed One was.

Then the mighty sons of the gods surrounded Cunda, the smith's son, and said to him, "Cunda, do not offer the food yet. Leave the Tathāgata be for a moment."

Then the Blessed One (the Buddha) once again sent forth his rays of light. The rays struck the sons of the gods, and they let Cunda go.

Then all the gods and others gathered there made a great offering to the Blessed One. Together with Cunda they went before the Blessed One and said, "If the time for the monks' midday meal has come, please give your permission."

Then the monks, knowing that the time for the midday meal had come, composed themselves in good deportment, took their alms bowls in their hands, and sat down.

Then Cunda, the smith's son, set out lion thrones of every kind, beyond what words could describe, as though fit even for the world realm Sukhāvatī. He also prepared the food, and then said to the Blessed One, "May the Tathāgata please remain for an eon, or even longer than an eon."

The Blessed One said, "Cunda, if you want me to stay long, the time has come for the final perfection of generosity. So offer it quickly!"

Then all the gods and humans and the bodhisattvas said, "Oh, how fortunate Cunda is! See how the Tathāgata has resolved to eat the food that he has offered! We have no such good fortune. The food we prepared has been for nothing."

Then from all the pores of his body, from each and every pore, the Blessed One manifestly emanated a buddha. In each assembly he showed a tathāgata together with a Saṅgha of monks, all appearing to partake of the meal. The Tathāgata himself ate the food offered by Cunda. The food Cunda had prepared from eight nalita measures of Magadhan śāli rice, by the Tathāgata's power, was also made to fill the whole Saṅgha of monks with food.

Then Cunda too became joyful, thinking, "The Tathāgata, together with the Saṅgha of monks, has eaten my gift and will pass into complete nirvāṇa."

Everyone in that assembly thought, "The Tathāgata has accepted the food we offered and has not accepted Cunda's. Having eaten the food we offered, the Tathāgata will pass into complete nirvāṇa." And they became supremely joyful.

In that great assembly, where the tathāgatas sat partaking of the meal, the offerings appeared in spaces no larger than the tip of a splinter. Some appeared to be seated on spots no larger than the point of a needle, and to have food and offerings all alike.

Then at that time the gods, humans, and asuras wept and wailed, crying, "Having eaten the food that each of us offered, the Tathāgata is passing into complete nirvāṇa. From now on, to whom shall we offer food? We are parted from our protector and shall be without a protector."

Then the Blessed One spoke these verses to that whole assembly.

The thirteenth fascicle, the last.

"Since this is the very nature of the buddhas,
Do not grieve and do not weep.
When I pass beyond sorrow,
Ten million eons will have run their course.

Hear from me of my passing beyond sorrow,
Which is permanent, blissful and supreme.
I do not eat food,
And I have no itching or burning at all.

I shall explain the rejoicing
That brings benefit and happiness to all beings.
Having heard it, come to know
That very permanence of the perfect Buddha.

When owls and crows
Dwell together on a single tree,
And like siblings and like children
Always please one another,
Then, as on Rāhula, his son,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good.

When snakes and mongooses
Dwell together at the foot of a single tree,
And like siblings and like children
Always please one another,
Then, as on Rāhula,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good.

When the flowers of the saptaparṇa
Take on the scent of vārṣikī jasmine,
And from karaka trees
Tinduka fruits come forth,
Then, as on Rāhula,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good.

When the sinful icchantikas
Fully and manifestly awaken to awakening,
And, having attained supreme bliss,
Have cast off every fault,
Then, as on Rāhula,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good.

When all sentient beings
Come to dwell in harmony of intention,
Perfect buddhas in supreme awakening,
Having cast off every fault,
Then, as on Rāhula,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good.

When with a mere drop of a fly's urine
The earth, bounded by the four oceans,
Together with its forests and mountains,
Is filled to the full,
Then, as on Rāhula,
Will the Tathāgata gaze on beings,
And the one supreme among all beings
Will die, passing beyond sorrow for good."

"Therefore, you who possess the Dharma and revere the Dharma, do not grieve! Do not weep! The Tathāgata has mastery over himself. So do not hold the perception of impermanence, but see rightly that he is permanent, eternal, and supremely stable. Protect yourselves with this concealment and protection of the secret mantra of the three permanent bases. Through the secret mantra of the three permanent bases, even trees burned by lightning will grow again, and come to have branches, sprouting buds, and unfolded leaves. If, among the fourfold assembly, people hear from one another this talk of rejoicing that the three bases are permanent, then even if they hold that there is no need to generate the awakening mind, because the three bases are supremely permanent, it will arise as a cause of awakening. This is called rejoicing in the Buddha."

"Those monks or nuns who teach beings this rejoicing that arises from the permanence of the Three Jewels are worthy to eat the alms of the land. The others are not worthy. By the truth of the Dharma through which what this rejoicing brings forth becomes happiness throughout all the world, may those who long for the sublime Dharma be free of obstructions and free of harm!"

Then the world with its gods, asuras and humans came to understand that the Tathāgata is permanent. With minds of great joy, virtuous minds, minds free of obscuration and minds without partiality, their faces like blossoming lotuses, they performed a great divine offering, scattering flowers, powders, incense, scented ointments and the like. They also played cymbals and other instruments.

Then the Blessed One said to the bodhisattva mahāsattva, the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, "Son of noble family, what marvel have you seen here today?"

He replied, "Blessed One, I have seen a marvel such as I have never seen before. I saw the food offered by each assembly being eaten by a tathāgata, one for each, together with a saṅgha of monks. In this assembly, even on spaces no larger than the tip of a needle, on seats arranged just so, many tathāgatas of great size sat at their ease, and I saw them seated there teaching the thirteen verses of rejoicing. All beings do not know this magical display; each one thinks, 'The Blessed One is seated here eating the food that I offered.' The supreme among humans, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas such as Mañjuśrī the youthful, truly understand this extraordinary wonder: that of the food Cunda prepared from eight nālikas of Magadhan śāli rice, not even a speck was eaten by the saṅgha of monks, and that the Tathāgata did not eat the food. They think, 'This was done merely as a skillful means.' The assemblies of śrāvakas do not understand, 'Oh, the Tathāgata is immeasurable and permanent!'"

Then the Blessed One said to Cunda the smith's son, "Cunda, what marvel have you seen?"

He replied, "Blessed One, before, I saw many tathāgatas endowed with the thirty-two supreme marks and the eighty excellent minor marks. Now each of them has transformed himself, and here they appear as bodhisattva mahāsattvas of great bodies. The Tathāgata has become a single body, like a medicinal tree, and is surrounded by the bodhisattvas."

The Blessed One said, "Those former buddhas were emanations, made in order to benefit all beings, to make them happy, to delight their minds, and to make their merit inexhaustible. Apart from the bodhisattvas, beings do not know this magical display. Thus the conduct of bodhisattvas is immeasurable. Thus the bodhisattva mahāsattvas are immeasurable. Bodhisattvas, the supreme among humans, have the power of a buddha's miraculous transformations. Cunda the smith's son, too, is a bodhisattva mahāsattva who has attained the ten grounds. Such is the ultimate culmination of the supreme among bodhisattvas."

The kinsman of Mahākāśyapa said, "Blessed One, it is just so. I rejoice in Cunda, the supreme human among the bodhisattvas. Blessed One, now it is fitting that you grant permission for the explanation of the sūtras. Blessed One, how is it? Do all the sūtras have a meaning with remainder, or a meaning without remainder?"

The Blessed One said, "They have a meaning with remainder, and they also have a meaning without remainder."

He said, "In the lines,

Giving is praised everywhere;
Giving is nowhere disparaged,

how is this explained as a meaning with remainder? What is the meaning of the verse? What is the difference between those who possess moral discipline and those whose moral discipline is corrupt?"

The Blessed One said, "Except for one, giving is praised everywhere."

He asked, "What is the exception?"

The Blessed One said, "Whoever is reckoned in this sūtra as having corrupt moral discipline."

He said, "Blessed One, today I ask the Blessed One only this; please tell me quickly."

The Blessed One said, "Except for the icchantikas, giving is praised everywhere."

He asked, "What are icchantikas like?"

The Blessed One said, "Whether a monk, a nun, a layman or a laywoman, whoever rejects the sūtras with evil words and afterward does not even ask forgiveness has entered the path of the icchantika. Those who have incurred the four defeats and those who commit the five acts of immediate retribution, who, although they know, 'We have entered a fearful state,' do not regard it with fear, do not take the side of the sublime Dharma, make no effort with the thought, 'We shall uphold the sublime Dharma,' and even speak in dispraise of it, have also entered the path of the icchantika. Those who say, 'There is no Buddha, there is no Dharma, there is no Saṅgha,' have also entered the path of the icchantika, I declare. Except for those icchantikas, giving is praised everywhere."

He asked, "Then who are those called people of corrupt moral discipline?"

The Blessed One said, "Those who commit the four defeats, those who commit the five acts of immediate retribution, and those very ones who reject the sūtras."

He asked, "Blessed One, how is it? Are they forever nothing but people of corrupt moral discipline?"

The Blessed One said, "There is a reason here, and therefore some are so permanently and some are not so permanently. There are those who do not dwell together with those who bear the sign of the dyed robe, who regard it with fear, thinking, 'Alas, we have incurred a defeat; we have committed a wrong,' who practice with the thought, 'We shall uphold the sublime Dharma,' and who think, 'We shall speak the praises of those who hold to the side of the sublime Dharma.'

"The factions of what is not the Dharma should be subdued; the incomparable sūtras should be recited." Those who strive with this thought, I do not say that they are of corrupt moral discipline. Why is that? Son of noble family, just as when the sun rises the passing dust is cleared away, so too, when the sun of this great sūtra rises, the countless heaps of evil committed by the sentient beings who cultivate it in this way are exhausted. Thus bringing forth the true Dharma has great fruit.

As for those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution and those who reject the sūtras: suppose they become remorseful and anguished, thinking, "Alas, this rejection of the sūtras that we have committed will be our ruin; we have done wrong," and they give rise to the thought, "Our refuge is this very sūtra; we see no other place to cling to." Then for half a month, or a month, or for however long it lasts until they have gone for refuge to the sūtra, what is given to them is of little fruit. When they have gone for refuge to the sūtra, then they too become worthy recipients of offerings. If one who commits a deed of immediate retribution also feels remorse and thinks, "Alas, I have done what is improper; I shall bring forth the true Dharma," then he too should be known as not being a perpetrator of a deed of immediate retribution, and even if only a little is given to him, it bears fruit. But what is given to those others, the utterly immoral, has not even the slightest fruit.

Son of noble family, listen further to what one who commits a defeat should do. If one who commits a defeat comes to understand the suchness of phenomena, the tathāgatagarbha, and thinks, "I shall bring forth the true Dharma," then what is given to him becomes exceedingly excellent.

Son of noble family, suppose a pregnant woman, when her country was in turmoil, went to another land and gave birth to a baby boy inside a temple. When she had recovered, she took the boy, and after her own country had become peaceful she set out to return. She came upon a great river, and while crossing that water together with her child she became exhausted in the water. Thinking, "Better that I should die; I shall save this boy," she strove for his sake, and she died together with her son. Although she had previously committed extremely dreadful deeds, through the fruit of her love for her son and her thought of saving him she was born in the higher realms. In the same way, those among the perpetrators of defeats and the perpetrators of deeds of immediate retribution who bring forth the true Dharma, although they previously committed evil deeds, through the fruit of bringing forth the true Dharma become, like that woman, worthy recipients of the offerings of all sentient beings. So great is the fruit of bringing forth the true Dharma.

He asked, "Blessed One, if someone who has fallen into being an icchantika later confesses before the three bases and asks forgiveness, will what is given to him be of great fruit?"

The Blessed One said, "Son of noble family, do not say that. Suppose some person ate a mango fruit and threw the stone on the ground. Afterward he thought, 'The kernel inside may be tasty,' so he discarded the outer stone and ate the kernel, and finding its taste unpleasant he threw it away again. Then he thought, 'Alas, I have ruined the seed and spoiled it.' He put the seed back inside the stone, dug the earth, placed it in, and poured milk and butter over it. But even if a great rain were to fall, the ruined and spoiled seed would not grow; it cannot possibly grow. Then, thinking, 'This seed has been spoiled and will not grow,' he grew weary and sat there without saying a word. In the same way, the icchantika has no seed; it is impossible for an icchantika to give rise to the thought of confessing and asking forgiveness. Therefore, for such an icchantika, giving is not of great fruit in every case. The fruit of what is offered to arhats is also distinct. The fruit of what is offered to pratyekabuddhas is also distinct. The fruit of what is offered to the tathāgatas is also distinct. Therefore the fruit of giving is not great in every case."

He asked, "Why has the Blessed One taught the meaning of this verse in two ways?"

The Blessed One said, "It arises from a cause. Formerly in Rājagṛha there was a layman without faith who delighted in the Nirgranthas. At the time when he asked me what giving leads to, in order to guide him I taught him the verse that says, 'Giving is praised everywhere.' That was taught for the sake of the bodhisattvas, as words whose meaning is to be interpreted; the meaning of the verse was not taught as the same at all times. The word 'all' should be understood as fitted to the occasion, with words left unstated. The bodhisattvas, supreme among humans, should root out the immoral just as one clears a field.

"Further, son of noble family, I formerly taught this verse:

All rivers and all waters flow crookedly;
All forests are nothing but trees;
All women are deceitful;
All lords are happy.

The minds of women are deceitful and frightening."

Thereupon Mañjuśrī Kumārabhūta rose from his seat, bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, and addressed the Blessed One in turn in verse:

Not all waters flow crookedly;
Not all forests are trees;
Not all women are deceitful;
Not all lords are happy.

"Therefore, the Blessed One's teaching of that Dharma door in that way was not well spoken. I beg you to tell me what reason there is for this."

"In this great trichiliocosm world system there is a separate continent, the western one called Godānīya. There is a river there named Flowing Straight, which runs straight like a carpenter's chalk line from the western ocean to the eastern ocean. If the Blessed One has declared, on the authority of the Vaipulya scriptures, that the word 'all' has a meaning that allows for exceptions, then in which sūtra of the Vaipulya scriptures is this river called Flowing Straight explained? May the Blessed One, who speaks the truth, please tell me.

Suppose, for example, that a guide who knows a mine of treasure, but is not skilled in mining, says, 'There is no gold here,' even though there is gold in that very place. From his saying so, one should understand that he knows only certain particular parts of the place.

The saying 'All forests are nothing but trees' also has a meaning that allows for exceptions, for there are many kinds of forest, such as forests of gold and of beryl. In the same way, the saying 'All women are deceitful' also has a meaning that allows for exceptions, for there are many women who possess moral discipline, who are free of desire, and who are without avarice. The saying 'All lords are happy' also has a meaning that allows for exceptions. The word 'lord' refers to universal monarchs and to the buddhas, who are supreme lords; yet they too fall under the power of death and transmigration and are subject to exhaustion. These lords such as Brahmā are also subject to falling. Therefore I say that they are not lords. The lord who is permanent and stable is nirvāṇa, the lord of supreme bliss."

The Blessed One said, "Mañjuśrī, suppose a physician is treating the illness of a householder's son. He boils medicine in ghee and has him drink it, and the patient asks to drink a great deal of ghee. The physician then says to him, 'Although one who has strength may be allowed to drink a great deal of ghee, for he is able to digest it, you have no strength, so do not drink too much ghee. Ghee does cure illness, but if you drink a lot of it, that very thing will make you die suddenly.' When he says this, has that physician, who wishes to help and is compassionate, spoiled the true meaning?

In the same way, the Buddha, the Blessed One, in order to dispel the pride of kings, ministers, and queens, and to produce in the queens of Prasenajit, king of Kosala, fear of committing adultery with others and disenchantment with it, the Tathāgata, like a physician, spoke this verse beginning 'The waters of rivers flow crookedly,' with reference to King Prasenajit. In saying that rivers flow crookedly, the Tathāgata is not mistaken. Should he ever be mistaken, heaven and earth would be overturned. Therefore, in using the word 'all,' he spoke of what allows for exceptions as though it allowed for none. Mañjuśrī, excellent leader of the herd of bodhisattvas, it is excellent, excellent that you thus expand wisdom for the benefit of all sentient beings."

Then Mañjuśrī the youthful again addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"What one has done and left undone
One should examine; but what others
Have done and left undone,
And the faults of others, one should not examine.

That the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, taught that Dharma door in this way was not well spoken. Why is this so? I beg you to tell me the reason you said it. In the ninefold scripture the Blessed One said: 'Here the Nirgranthas are all hell beings, and the ninety-six heterodox schools have entered a bad path. All the children of the Buddha have entered upon nirvāṇa. All who are upright, who possess moral discipline and ritual observance, and whose senses are tamed, go to the higher realms and have the Dharma as their domain.' Thus he spoke in praise of himself, in disparagement of others, and in censure of others. Since the Blessed One has in this way spoken of the faults of others, how is it that this is not called the faults of others?"

Then the Blessed One said this to Mañjuśrī the youthful: "Son of noble family, if you ask with reference to whom and for what reason I taught, 'One should not examine the faults of others,' it was taught with reference to Ajātaśatru. The Buddha, the Blessed One, does not teach what contradicts the meaning without a cause; he teaches with a cause. Formerly, after Ajātaśatru had killed his father, he came before me and asked a question in a contemptuous manner: 'What is this? Is the Blessed One omniscient, or is he not omniscient? If he is omniscient, why did he ordain Devadatta? If you saw that for many thousands of lifetimes he has followed after you and done you harm, how did you, the omniscient one, not see that now too he would come to attack you?' When he said this, I taught him the verse, 'One should not examine the faults of others.' And I said, 'Now, great king, purify the great and violent poison of the act of immediate retribution that you committed in killing your father. O king, without looking at the faults of others, understand it in this way.'

Furthermore, one should indeed examine faults: one should make it one's main concern to examine the flaws of those who rely on moral discipline and ritual observance. That is the teaching of the Buddha. Those who examine the faults of others should know that what oneself and others have done and left undone belongs to oneself and to others. Those who in this way always see correctly what others have done, I declare, are my śrāvakas."

Then again the Blessed One said this to Mañjuśrī the youthful:

"All are afraid of punishment;
To all, life is dear.
Taking oneself as the measure,
One should not strike, one should not kill."

Then Mañjuśrī the youthful said this to the Blessed One:

"Not all are terrified of punishment,
nor is life dear to all.
Taking oneself as the measure,
one should understand how to act through skillful means.

Therefore the Dharma door that the Blessed One (the Buddha) taught in that way was not well spoken. For:

Arhats and wheel-turning kings,
the precious elephant, the precious woman,
the precious householder, and the horse:
these cannot be harmed by anything at all.

In the world with its gods, humans, and asuras, no one dares to harm these. Even fierce and powerful enemies cannot possibly harm them. They are not frightened even by gods, by māras, or by Brahmās. Therefore these beings are called 'those who cannot be harmed by anything.' A man who is wholly heroic at the front line of battle, a wife who is wholly unswerving toward her husband, a lion, a thoroughbred horse of Sindhu, and a monk endowed with moral discipline: even though these can be harmed, they are not afraid of anything at all.

"As for the words 'All are terrified of punishment,' here the meaning carries an unstated qualification, so it is a condensed statement. The words 'Taking oneself as the measure' also carry an unstated qualification, for it is well known in the world that arhats are not the same as oneself. Suppose someone of small understanding thinks, 'This teaches that arhats perceive a self and that arhats perceive a life-force.' Such a person has placed the arhats in a lowly state. By doing so, through the evil view that perceives a life-force, the fruit of delusion, he will swiftly be born in Avīci hell. Moreover, even if one were to form the intention to kill arhats, it is impossible for them to be killed by such beings. Even if many beings turned against them, they would not dare to kill them."

The Blessed One said, "Out of compassion for living creatures, I regard them as like myself. With respect to harmlessness, the arhat should be regarded as immeasurable and incomparable, and one should not form the intention to kill such beings. Thus the Buddha does not, without a reason, teach what contradicts the meaning. This verse was taught with reference to a deer hunter of Rājagṛha. Having killed deer, he invited me to an offering of food that included meat. I accepted his invitation and, regarding him as I regard Rāhula, I spoke this verse:

All are terrified of punishment;
to all, life is dear.
Taking oneself as the measure,
do not strike, do not kill.

Look far ahead and live long! Come to understand the meaning of the sign of harmlessness!

"Excellent, excellent, Mañjuśrī, bull among men among the bodhisattvas! It is excellent that, for the benefit of all sentient beings, you have asked about this statement made with a hidden intention."

Then Mañjuśrī the youthful addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"If one makes offerings to both father and mother
in the manner of worshiping a mother,
and is also respectful toward one's teacher,
how could one go to the terror of Avīci?"

Then the Blessed One spoke in verse:

"Here, craving is called the mother,
and delusion is called the father.
Whoever honors these two things
goes to the terror of Avīci."

Then the Blessed One again spoke this verse to Mañjuśrī the youthful:

"All that is under another's power is suffering;
all that has power over itself is happiness.
All that is under the power of pride is endless;
all joy is good."

Then Mañjuśrī the youthful said to the Blessed One:

"The Dharma door that the Blessed One taught in that way was not well spoken. Suppose that here a certain householder entrusts his son to a teacher of children. What then? Is that boy not called 'under another's power'? If one says of him that he is not under another's power, it contradicts the meaning, and if one says that he has power over himself, that also contradicts the meaning. This matter is extremely hard to examine. Suppose that here a certain prince has become incorrigible. He will not learn anything at all and wanders about as he pleases and at his own whim. If one calls him 'one who has power over himself,' it greatly contradicts the meaning, while if one says he is under another's power, it does not contradict the meaning. This matter is extremely hard to examine.

"Furthermore, the expression 'under power' is used because if he came under his own power he would commit evil deeds and perish. So this statement carries an unstated qualification. Therefore it is not the case that all that is under another's power is suffering, nor that all that has power over itself is happiness.

"The words 'All that is under the power of pride is endless' also carry an unstated qualification, and it is not the case that all that is under the power of pride is endless. Suppose that here someone goes forth under the power of pride. What then? Is that renunciant called 'one under the power of pride, endless in all'? He is without pride, endowed with merit, possessed of moral discipline and proper conduct, and guards his senses. Therefore it is not the case that all that is under the power of pride is endless.

"The words 'All joy is good' also carry an unstated qualification, and not all joy is good. In this teaching, those who have incurred a defeat, even though they themselves see it as permissible to sit together with those who bear the sign of dyed robes, will be born among the beings of the hells. To call such joy good would contradict the meaning. Making those who keep committing defeats wear white clothes is accomplishing the very best of what is good, a vast benefit. Those who have committed a defeat are called not good, and those who bring about ruin are called not good."

"Therefore, if not all joy is good, with what intention did the god of gods, the Buddha, the Tathāgata, say that?"

Then the Blessed One said this to Mañjuśrī the youthful: "A buddha does not speak against the meaning without a cause. He speaks with a cause and with a condition. In Rājagṛha, Subhadrā, the daughter of the householder Kotaka, went to her father's house and conceived the wish to go forth. She went to the Tathāgata, thinking, 'To be a woman is to be under another's power, while to be a man is to be one's own master.' She took refuge in the Tathāgata, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha of monks. The Blessed One understood her thought, and so he spoke that verse: 'All that is under another's power is suffering.'

"Excellent, excellent, Mañjuśrī, bull among men among the bodhisattvas! It is excellent that you have asked me about these words spoken with a hidden intention, for the benefit of all beings."

Then the Blessed One again addressed Mañjuśrī the youthful in verse:

"All depend on food.
All who are free of avarice waver.
Through a fault in food one falls ill.
To keep company with those of pure conduct is happiness."

Mañjuśrī said, "Blessed One, what is this? After eating Cunda's offering, did you fall ill through a fault in the food?"

Then the Blessed One said this to Mañjuśrī: "Not all beings depend on food. Not all who are free of avarice waver. Not everyone falls ill through a fault in food. Nor is keeping company with those who practice the Dharma always happiness. Mañjuśrī, when you are struck by illness, then I too am struck by illness.

"Arhats and pratyekabuddhas,
Bodhisattvas and tathāgatas,
Are always without food;
That is the very nature of the buddhas.

"Arhats, pratyekabuddhas, bodhisattvas, and tathāgatas do not eat food, yet they accept many hundreds of thousands of offerings. They also teach the instructions on how offerings are to be received. In order to deliver beings with certainty from the ocean of the hells, the animals, and the hungry ghosts, they praise the perfection of generosity, yet they do not eat.

"One should not regard the Buddha as being like all other beings, saying, 'The Tathāgata became weak by practicing austerities for six years.' Those who have crossed the river of desire are steadfast; those heroes do not move within the domain of human beings. In the same way, the power and the domain of my śrāvakas are also inconceivable.

"The words 'All beings depend on food' should be understood as a statement that admits exceptions. The words 'All who are free of avarice waver' also admit exceptions, for there are some holy beings who, sparing toward themselves, go to an unwavering happiness. The words 'Everyone falls ill through a fault in food' also admit exceptions, for there are also sudden illnesses, such as being pierced by a thorn. The words 'Keeping company with all who practice the Dharma is happiness' also admit exceptions. Among worldly people too there are many kinds of practices that accord with those who hold to a dharma, and they have various undertakings. But if someone were to keep company with those whose bodies are tormented by various kinds of fasting, would he find happiness? If that were so, it would contradict the meaning.

"Therefore, understand that a buddha does not teach against the meaning without a cause, but teaches with a cause and with a condition, according to those who are to be trained. The supramundane Dharma is supreme happiness.

"This verse was taught in connection with a brahmin called Sheep Glory, of the city called Ujjayinī. The brahmin Sheep Glory was keeping a four-day fast when he came to me, and in order to dispel his view about the four-day fast, I taught this verse beginning 'All beings depend on food.'"

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva, the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what does 'all, without exception' mean? And what does 'all' mean?"

He replied, "The sense of 'without exception' is as in the statement, 'Everything on the side of virtue is permanent and is bliss.' Thus a son of good family who longs for the Dharma should reason out that some statements admit exceptions and some do not."

He said, "Oh! The Tathāgata regards all beings as he regards Rāhula. I am overjoyed."

He replied, "Excellent, excellent, son of good family! One must examine things in fine detail in this way."

He said, "Blessed One, now please explain how the merit of the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra arises."

He replied, "Because this domain of the Buddha is inconceivable, not even all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are able to speak of the name of this sūtra, even as mere words, to those who listen."

Then the gods, humans, and asuras, with one voice, addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Inconceivable Buddha, who teach the inconceivable Dharma,
The Dharma and the Saṅgha rely on you always.
Therefore, until the monks Kāśyapa and Ānanda
Have come, Protector, we beg you to remain.
Ajātaśatru, king of Videha,
Your supreme friend, great and of like mind,
Has also not yet come; do not depart for good.
Omniscient one, cut through our doubts."

Then the Blessed One said this to them:

"My supreme eldest son Kāśyapa
And wise Ānanda will cut through your doubts.
Look upon my face, and know the permanent
And the impermanent; do not grieve."

Then that whole assembly made great offerings to the Blessed One and made aspirations for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. There, beings as numerous as the grains of sand in countless myriads of Ganges Rivers generated the mind of awakening and attained the state of the first bodhisattva ground.

Then the Blessed One spoke to Mañjuśrī the youthful and to the great and eminent brahmin, the kinsman of Mahākāśyapa. Having empowered Cunda the smith's son for awakening, he said to these three bodhisattvas:

"Sons of noble family, be heedful! Be heedful! I have been struck by a severe illness of the back. Young men, I wish to lie down for a while within the palace of skillful means, so let me lie down. Mañjuśrī, you must teach the Dharma to the whole assembly. Roar the lion's roar! Until the elders Mahākāśyapa and Ānanda arrive, I entrust this teaching to you. When they come, entrust it to the two of them."

When he had spoken these words, the Blessed One, for the sake of those to be trained, showed himself as one who is ill, and lay down on his side in a separate sleeping chamber.

This completes the chapter called "Requested by the Assembly of Gods, Humans, Asuras, Kinnaras, Vidyādharas, Rākṣasas, and Others," from the great Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra, which has twenty-five thousand chapters. This sūtra is the heart of all the scriptures of the sacred Dharma. Like the Later Section, it is a unique source of teachings that are difficult to realize, and it teaches the tathāgatagarbha.

It contains three thousand nine hundred ślokas. It was translated and finalized by the Indian preceptors Jinamitra and Jñānagarbha and the chief editor and translator, the monk Devacandra.

Translator's notes

This Tibetan version was translated from the Chinese. The cry rendered "we are overwhelmed" is literally "covered." The Chinese parallel has a cry of pain at this point, and the precise sense of the Tibetan is uncertain. "Thoroughbreds" renders a term normally standing for Sanskrit ājāneya. Kotaka, the name of Subhadrā's father, is romanized as the Tibetan spells it, since its Sanskrit form is not certain. The name of the lay leader (Tibetan: "King Renowned for Stainless Light") and that of the layman "Excellent Giver" could not be confidently identified with Sanskrit forms, so their meanings are translated. "Hue of acacia blossoms" renders the obscure color term bab la, tentatively taken as the babla (acacia) tree; this is uncertain. "Pyre for the cremation" interprets gdung zhugs la bzhen pa as building the funeral pyre for the Tathāgata's body. The animal to which the grieving laymen are compared is the śiśumāra, usually understood as the Gangetic dolphin or porpoise; "out of water" is added to make the image clear. The line "I shall serve a meal to the Tathāgata, and then he will pass into complete nirvāṇa" could also be read as each layman wishing to be the one whose meal is the last before the parinirvāṇa. Several of the textile names (dyed woolens, cloth of mule hair) are uncertain. The personal names Splendor of Life, Pure Stainless Essence, Purified of Obscurations, and Given by the Stainless Waters of the Ganges are translated from their Tibetan meaning, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The line about the body reeking "like a king's dungeon dug deep in the ground" renders an obscure Tibetan phrase whose exact image is uncertain. Several personal names could not be identified with confidence and are rendered by meaning: the king of Vaiśālī "Stainless Moon Essence," the king "Sunlight," the queen "Beauty of the Three Worlds," and the god "Wide Eyes." Jagatpāla and Dharmapāla are reconstructions of "Protector of Beings" and "Protector of the Dharma." Vairocana is the standard equivalent of the Tibetan name. The word rendered "arrogance" in the list of faults (jealousy, arrogance, concealment, deceit) is written དུས་ཆེ་བ, which makes little sense; it is taken as a slip for a word meaning pride or haughtiness. The garuḍa king's name, དགྲའི་མཛའ་བོ, could not be confidently identified with a Sanskrit form and is translated by meaning as "Friend of Foes." The gandharva lord's father, མིས་བྱིན, is tentatively given as Naradatta. Several names are rendered from their meaning because no Sanskrit form could be identified with confidence: the fruit-tree lord "Scent Keeper", the bhūta host "Revolving Like Venus" (taking ba sangs as the planet Venus), and the perfume elephants "Lungtang Hue", "Clear Honey Hue" and "Fragrant with All Desires". The dānava lord's name may in fact be two names, "Stainless Waters of the Ganges" and "Anudatta". The asura lord's name is rendered Śambara, taking bde mchog as a rendering of Śambara rather than the tantric deity. "Zalmo" and "bālika" are bird names that could not be identified. The phrase ja hong khar 'dug pa, describing the sun's rays on the sāla trees, is obscure, and its sense is given only approximately. "An immense multitude of Ganges Rivers" renders a Tibetan large-number term that denotes a specific vast numeral. Śakra's flowers are taken as those growing in his heaven, though the phrase could also mean flowers available there at the scene. The weapon written in Tibetan as "śa ta ka ni ha" appears to transliterate a Sanskrit weapon name; it is rendered as śataghnī, a "hundred-killer" missile, which seems the likeliest identification. "Kampana" for མཚོན་ཀམ་པ་ན is likewise a transliterated weapon name. The names of the eastern buddha, bodhisattva and world system are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be established with confidence. "Equal to Space" may reflect Gaganasama or Ākāśasama. "Infinite Great Body" corresponds to the bodhisattva called "Boundless Body" in the Chinese parallel. "Mind-Captivating Unceasing Sweet Sound" takes mu tug as "unceasing." The text calls Infinite Great Body first a śrāvaka and then a bodhisattva mahāsattva; both are kept as given. "World system of Endurance" renders the name of our world, Sahā. The bodhisattva's name, rendered here by meaning as Boundless Great Body, has no securely attested Sanskrit form; the Chinese parallel calls him Boundless Body. "Thirty-two maṇḍalas" is translated literally; the Chinese parallels give a measure of distance here, so "maṇḍala" may stand for a unit of area. In the list of birds, the syllable after "cranes" does not fit as written and is probably a copyist's slip; it has been left out of the translation. The phrase "those of great desire" is taken as the Tibetan rendering of icchantika, as the context and parallels indicate. "The three faults and the four" most likely refers to the ten nonvirtuous actions: three of body, four of speech and three of mind. The Tibetan phrasing is compressed, so the exact grouping of faults with body, speech and mind is left as the text gives it. Skuल་བྱེད is rendered as Cunda, the smith's son known from the parinirvāṇa narratives. This Tibetan version (Toh 120) was translated from Chinese, which accounts for some unusual phrasing. The line rendered "Within existence all beings cling to life" is terse and its exact sense is uncertain. In "ever bound up with suffering like a tumor," the word read as "tumor" could also mean "rice grains" or "fruit"; the sense is uncertain. The simile "like scorched food" is literal; its intended sense in this context is unclear. The term rendered "a great lord" here translates the same Tibetan used elsewhere for Maheśvara (Śiva). In this simile it plainly means a powerful worldly lord, not the deity. The fourth māra is given in this text as "the māra of intrinsic nature" rather than the usual māra of death. It has been translated as written. The word for "rescue," which in wrathful rites means "liberate," has its ordinary sense here. The phrase rendered "can he not now hold out even for a moment" reads gzhes mi spyod, whose exact sense is unclear; the rendering follows the context of Cunda's objection. "Whether it be special or not" reads khyed par, taken as khyad par. "Please do not depart and leave the world empty" renders the terse humble phrase འཇིག་རྟེན་སྟོངས་ཀྱིས་མ་མཆིས་སོ, taking མཆི as "go" in humble speech; it might also be read as a statement that the world will be left empty. "Like a haṃsa going off in play" is a tentative reading of བྱ་བཞད་གཟས་ལ་འགྲོ་བ; the word གཟས is unclear. "Borrowed" for བརྙན་མ could also be understood as "a mere reflection." The verb མི་གཞེས in the verse "If you do not hold back from passing into nirvāṇa" is unusual; it has been read in the sense of desisting or refraining. གཡར་ངན, rendered "grieve and lament," is likewise an uncommon term. "Final tantra" renders རྒྱུད་ཕྱི་མ (uttaratantra), the concluding and most advanced section of a technical treatise, here applied by analogy to the final teaching of the Great Vehicle. The phrase rendered "the discourse on the single syllable and its dot" is obscure in the Tibetan. It may refer to the anusvāra dot, or more broadly to a teaching condensed into a single syllable. The word rendered "conceit" is uncertain in the Tibetan. "Khasral flower" keeps the Tibetan plant name, because the species could not be identified with confidence; the point of the simile is that the flower has no scent. Manuja ("born of Manu") and mānava ("son of Manu") are standard terms in lists of names for a supposed self. སྡུག / མི་སྡུག in the four inversions is rendered "pure / impure," corresponding to śubha / aśubha, the fourth quality (purity) of the dharmakāya in this sūtra's doctrine. The opening question of the Blessed One is terse; it is rendered as asking whether the monks understand this way of meditating. སྤོ in the list of things seen turning is rendered "towers"; its exact sense here is not certain. "Versed in the later treatises" renders རྒྱུད་ཕྱི་མ, probably the supplementary section of a classical medical treatise. Two items in the list of the cow's qualities are uncertain: "whose teats the cowherd has not roughly handled" (ནུ་མ་མ་བཞིབས) and "who has not been fed on cut grass" (རྩྭ་བརྔས་པ་མ་གསན). Both are given by best sense. The king's question "khyed gsher ci yod" is idiomatic and not fully clear; rendered "what objection do you have?" The term rendered "millet" is the Tibetan khre rgod, a small grain used here, as in parallel passages, for a minutely small self. The bodhisattva's birthplace is translated literally as "Stalk Village"; the Chinese version of this sūtra places Kāśyapa Bodhisattva in a village called Tāla, but the Tibetan word does not plainly render that name. The birds paired with the wild geese (Tibetan zal mo) could not be identified with confidence; the Chinese parallel names sārasa and kācilindika birds here. "The element that is hard to fathom" refers to the buddha nature, and "the half letter becoming complete" alludes to the sūtra's teaching on half and complete letters, taken up later in the text. The kata fruit is the water-clearing nut. The speaker, literally "the one of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa," is the bodhisattva Kāśyapa of this sūtra, distinct from the disciple Mahākāśyapa. The phrase rendered "even of an ant" reads literally "rgyu sogs pa" in the Tibetan, which makes little sense. It is taken here as a scribal slip for a word meaning ant or tiny creature, as in the parallel passage of the Chinese version. "Like toward the axe and the sandalwood" refers to the Buddha's equal regard for one who cuts him with an axe and one who anoints him with sandalwood. The interlocutor, literally "one of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa," is the bodhisattva Kāśyapa of this sūtra, not the elder Mahākāśyapa himself; he is rendered here as "kinsman of Mahākāśyapa." The river name Hiṅgula follows the Tibetan spelling and is not securely identified; it may correspond to the Sindhu or another river in the parallel Chinese lists. "Kāśyapa of the Mahākāśyapa clan" renders a Tibetan phrase meaning literally "one of the same clan as the great Kāśyapa." It refers to the bodhisattva interlocutor Kāśyapa, not the elder Mahākāśyapa. "A body that depends on poisons and medicines" is a literal rendering of a compressed phrase. The Chinese parallel speaks instead of the Tathāgata casting off a "poison-tree body." The interlocutor here is the bodhisattva Kāśyapa, addressed as "kinsman of Mahākāśyapa" (literally "of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa"). The Tibetan does not name him in this piece. I have supplied his name for the speaker, following the surrounding text. The phrase rendered "lacking nothing and exceeding nothing" (lhag chad med pa) could also be read as a noun, "the state without remainder," set beside "my state." The phrase "expression of disparagement" (smad pa'i tshig) is rendered literally. Its exact force in this Tibetan rendering, which was made from the Chinese, is unclear. "Need not take up the five bases of training" renders བླང་བར་མི་བྱའོ, literally "is not to take"; the sense, as in the parallel Chinese version, is that such a protector of the Dharma is exempted from these observances, not forbidden them. A few items in the long negative list (for example རྐང་པ་མེད་པ "without feet" and ཕུལ་བ་མེད་པ "nothing beyond it") are terse and admit other readings. The king's name སྲིད་སྦྱིན is rendered Bhavadatta from its Tibetan components (bhava, datta); the Sanskrit form is not certain. The sentence about Abhirati literally says that "one called the aspect (or form) of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya" appeared there; the sense is obscure, and it is rendered literally as "the Likeness of the Tathāgata Akṣobhya." The phrase དཀྲུགས་མ་བྱས is taken as the immoral "stirring up trouble" against the preacher, which is what the narrative requires. The questioner here is the bodhisattva Kāśyapa, addressed by the Buddha as being of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa. The terms rendered "great severity" and "lesser severity" are uncertain in sense; they appear to distinguish the irremediable offenses (the defeats) from remediable ones. The phrase rendered "a grave matter" is literally "a great basis," and its exact sense is unclear. The list of "eight sweets" as written contains nine items (permanence, stability, eternity, peace, coolness, agelessness, deathlessness, stainlessness, bliss); it is translated as it stands. "Saṃyukta sūtras" renders ཡང་དག་པར་ལྡན་པའི་མདོ, most likely the Saṃyuktāgama. The fourth category, "the force of a circumstance that has arisen concerning a matter," is a tentative rendering of an unusual phrase. The "final tantra" of the medical sciences renders རྒྱུད་ཕྱི་མ, the concluding section of a medical treatise. The recurring address ཆེ་ཞེ is not a normal Tibetan word; it appears to be a loan, probably reflecting a Chinese form of address for a woman, since this text was translated from Chinese. I have rendered it "Sister." In ཆེ་ཞེང་ལ I read ཆེ་ཞེ plus ང་ལ, "Sister, me." The phrase ཆབ་འཚལ་བར་འགྱུར, literally "would come to seek water," is rendered "would fall ill," since the context is the sickness of an infant who cannot digest. "The one of the same clan as Mahākāśyapa" most likely refers to the bodhisattva Kāśyapa, who is the interlocutor in this part of the sūtra. "The Vinaya and the Mātṛkā" renders འདུལ་བ་དང་མ་མོ; here མ་མོ is the Abhidharma matrix (mātṛkā), not the class of goddesses. "Like fowlers" renders བྱ་བ་འདྲ་བ, which is obscure as written; the Chinese parallel has "like hunters," and I read it as a word for a bird-catcher. "Prepare them as food" for སྣམས་སུ་སྦྱོར་བ is tentative; the Chinese parallel speaks of monks cooking food with their own hands. "Offenses requiring expiation" renders ལྟུང་བྱེད (pāyattika). The phrase rendered "heals with regard to both aims" is an etymological gloss on prātimokṣa whose exact sense in the Tibetan is unclear. The weaver simile ("discards whatever section of the weave on his loom has gone astray") is also obscure in the Tibetan. The rendering follows the evident point that faults are dealt with as they arise. The term rendered "by force of" translates the Tibetan word usually meaning "power" or "empowerment." Here it names one of four modes of teaching, so the glossary sense "empowerment" does not apply. The phrase rendered "'Iron' and 'lump of iron'" reads literally "iron and mdo lcags." The second term is unclear. It is taken here as referring back to the verse's "lump of iron" (lcags kyi gar bu), possibly through a scribal variant. The sense, that the two words do not differ in meaning, is unaffected. "A billion" renders ཐེར་འབུམ, a large number whose exact value varies in Tibetan sources; it is taken here as the count of suns and Jambudvīpas in a great trichiliocosm, which agrees with the Chinese parallel. "Juniper leaf" translates རྒྱ་ཤུག་གི་ལོ་མ as written; the Chinese version has a jujube leaf. "In the manner of the Śūraṅgama Samādhi" most likely refers to the Śūraṅgamasamādhi Sūtra. The phrase rendered "no circulation, no corresponding process, and no ripening" is obscure. "Ripening" may here mean the digestion of food rather than karmic maturation; the point is that the Buddha's body has no real bodily processes behind these acts. In "the divine māra and the māra of intrinsic nature," the second term probably answers to the māra of the aggregates in the usual list of four māras, but the Tibetan is terse. The quoted vows of the seven steps are rendered in the first person because the whole passage is the Buddha speaking of himself. The verb rendered "deliver" throughout is used here in its ordinary sense of saving beings, not in the wrathful-rite sense. The phrase about Brahmā and the gods, literally "showing obstruction" to their devotees, is somewhat obscure; it seems to mean the Buddha appears in their form to thwart wrong reliance and redirect them to the Dharma. The phrase rendered "since my passing into complete nirvāṇa is permanent" is ambiguous; it may instead mean "I do not pass into complete nirvāṇa in a final sense." The "perception of looking" at the charnel ground is also uncertain; it may mean "no notion of views." "Wonder-working sects" renders ཡ་མཚན་ཅན, literally "those possessing marvels"; context (going forth among them, crushing their pride, showing them to be coreless) indicates non-Buddhist ascetic sects (tīrthikas). The unnamed questioner is Kāśyapa, the interlocutor of this section. "Material things" renders ཟང་ཟིང (Skt. āmiṣa, worldly or carnal goods). The phrase translated "is the Tathāgata also to be likened to the vessel" contains the unclear form བས་པར; the sense is supplied from the following sentence. "Householder with servants and wives" renders ཁྱིམ་བདག་བྲན་གཟའ་ཅན, which recurs as a fixed phrase and might be intended as a name; it is translated descriptively. "A poison used as medicine" renders the terse དུག་སྨན་བཞིན་དུ, whose exact sense is uncertain. The verse is the familiar saying on those who hoard nothing and fully understand food. The second form of the verse, with its interrogatives, has been kept as questions. The word-explanation of "full" (yongs) and "accumulation" (bsags) depends on the Tibetan compound and has been rendered with the English words used in the verse. The phrase rendered "a term for what surpasses others" (literally "a term for what has a remainder or excess") is obscure. I took "excess" in the sense of surpassing, which fits the examples that follow. "Wounded by a falling stone" renders a phrase whose exact sense in this simile is unclear. "Those of great desire" renders the Tibetan term for the icchantikas, a key concept of this sūtra. The heading "afflicted, afflicted" is kept as the Tibetan gives it, although the section explains that liberation and the Tathāgata are not afflicted. The term rendered "not fragile" is uncertain; it seems to mean brittle or hollow, and the contrast drawn with a cracked clay pot supports "fragile." "Does not perish" renders a word that ordinarily means "unpacified." In this list of negations it most likely stands for "not ceasing." The precious object of the wheel-turning king is ཁར་བ, normally "bell-metal" or "bronze." Its exact sense here is unclear, and it is rendered literally. ཉེན་མེད་པ is rendered "free of danger." The images it is set against (cardamom colors, a lamp on its stand) leave its exact sense somewhat uncertain; it could also mean "free of disturbance." འདོད་ཆེན་པ ("those of great desire") is this sūtra's standard Tibetan rendering of icchantika. The sentence linking the icchantika to the Tathāgata's inexhaustibility is terse, and it is rendered closely. In the passage on "free of afflictions," the Tibetan literally says liberation is "like that" while contrasting it with a graspable myrobalan; it is rendered as a contrast ("by comparison"), which the sense requires. ཆང་པ is read as "fist, handful." In the final item ཆད་པ is rendered "severance" rather than the glossary's "annihilation," since it here denotes the cutting off of desire, not the nihilist extreme. "The essence of butter" as an example of the unstable is given as the text has it. "That which is ignorance is liberation" is translated as the Tibetan reads; the sense is presumably that the transformation or subduing of ignorance is liberation. "Liberation makes itself known to oneself" renders the obscure phrase rang la brda sprod pa; the meaning is uncertain. The plant name pa mkhal could not be identified and is left in phonetic form. The nāga tree is probably the nāgakesara. "Nothing equals the word of the teacher" renders the uncertain phrase bla ma'i tshig gcig tu med pa; it might also mean that the teacher's word is not one-sided. The passage on "cutting off paths" is syntactically ambiguous. It may mean that grasping at selflessness and grasping at a self are what is cut off, and that holding the existence of the buddha element is liberation. I have kept the Tibetan sequence as it stands. "Source" renders skye mched (āyatana), used here in its sense of "abode" or "source" rather than "sense base." "Foes that take the form of afflictions" reads དགྲ ("enemy") for the written སྒྲ ("sound"), which makes little sense in the hunter image. The kinsman's question at the start of Fascicle Seven is elliptical in the Tibetan and is rendered literally. ཤང་ཤང་ཏེའུ is taken as the jīvaṃjīvaka bird. "Ordinary being of the eighth rank" renders བརྒྱད་པའི་སོ་སོའི་སྐྱེ་བོ, which corresponds to the "eighth person" (aṣṭamaka) of the Chinese parallel, i.e. one counted eighth from the arhat, the candidate for stream-entry. "In the abode of the buddhas" renders སངས་རྒྱས་ཀྱི་གནས་སུ; it may also be understood as "to the rank of a buddha." The householder named གདངས་ཅན ("the one with a voice/melody") has been identified as Ghoṣila on the basis of the parallel in the Chinese version from which this Tibetan text was translated. The phrase "They have become a support for the world" reads a missing syllable རྟེན ("support") into the text as written, which says only "they have become the world"; the following lines speak of the persons being "a support," and this confirms the reading. Some phrases in the simile of the troops are unusual: the kind of sword named ཨུར་མོ, the expression "as if grinding to powder," and ཐེན་པ, rendered "hold their ground." They have been rendered by context. The grains ཅི་ཙེ, ཁྲེ and ཁྲེ་རྒོད are rendered "coarse grain, millet and wild millet." ཅི་ཙེ in particular is not securely identifiable; it denotes some inferior grain. ཁྲེད་རྒོད in one occurrence is taken as a slip for ཁྲེ་རྒོད, and the doubled བྱེད་བྱེད་པ is read as a single verb. In the one-Ganges case the Tibetan says these people will explain the teaching widely to all people. Some parallel versions say instead that they cannot yet explain it to others. The translation follows the Tibetan as it stands. The word rendered "frost" is uncommon; it may instead mean dense mist or dew, but the simile works the same way. The phrase rendered "by force together with other caṇḍālas" reads in the Tibetan as gdol pa dag deng de mthus, which does not construe as written; it has been translated as the evident reading gdol pa dag dang de'i mthus. The expression chad pa'i gnas in the monk's objection is rendered "an occasion for censure" rather than "annihilation," since the context, followed by talk of punishing the immoral, concerns blame or penalty, not the nihilist view. The list of those relied upon to remove the holders of the eight improper things (literally "accomplishers, chiefs, and grain gatherers") is obscure; it is rendered as "administrators, chiefs, and grain collectors," apparently referring to secular officials. The phrase rendered "join with them in granting leave, in the poṣadha, and in joining palms, while carrying out acts that afflict them" is likewise difficult; the sense seems to be that the bodhisattva outwardly takes part in communal acts with the immoral monks while working to suppress them, as the brahmin did with the caṇḍāla king. The questioner is not named in this piece; in this sūtra it is presumably Kāśyapa. The phrase rendered "acts of homage with joined palms and bowing" is literally "acts of tormenting with joined palms"; the verb is most likely a slip for the similar word meaning "to bow," which matches the sense of the parallel Chinese text from which this Tibetan version was made. The speaker of the questions is unnamed in this piece. "Invitation ceremony" renders the Tibetan term for pravāraṇā. "Māduraka" renders the transliterated incense name as written; its Sanskrit identity is uncertain. "The eight great matters" are, in this sūtra, the eight impure possessions improper for monks to accept. The text reads "these four are like reliances"; I have taken it as "these four are the reliances," the evident sense. In "those four persons are not authoritative, for they are the definitive meaning," the negative contradicts both the explanation that follows and the earlier statement that the four persons are authoritative, so I have translated "authoritative." In the final two sentences, the same word for "authoritative" is applied to the Dharma body and the body of skillful means. Here I have taken it in its sense of "measure," but the intended sense is uncertain. The phrase rendered "connected with discontent ... and flattery" reads literally "that become without (མེད་པར་འགྱུར་བ) ... flattery"; since the context (the alleged permission to keep possessions) shows these writings foster such faults, the rendering follows the sense, but the Tibetan construction is awkward. "The three bases" most likely refers to the Three Jewels, the Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha, whose permanence was just asserted. The simile "just as one conceals the signs of what one is doing" is obscure in the Tibetan; parallel versions speak of a hunter disguising himself. Throughout, ཚད་མ is rendered "valid authority," as it concerns which teachers and scriptures may be trusted (the four reliances), not epistemic instruments. The brahmin's name, literally "Sheep Glory," could not be securely identified with a Sanskrit form and is translated by meaning. The deity called "the Desiccator" is likewise rendered literally, as its Sanskrit identity is uncertain. Several items in the list of forbidden possessions and practices are obscure in the Tibetan, notably "the flower chariot," "satchels," "clubs," "fine refined powders" and "kissing heads"; these are rendered as literally as the words allow. "Heterodox orders" renders ཡ་མཚན་ཅན, literally "the strange or wondrous ones"; the sense of non-Buddhist sects seems intended but is not certain. "Later Section" and "Last of the Later Sections" render རྒྱུད་ཕྱི་མ and རྒྱུད་ཕྱི་མའི་ཡང་ཕྱི་མ; here རྒྱུད does not mean a tantra but a later portion of the scriptures, and the exact referent is unclear. In the final sentence, འཆབ་པ ("concealing") is read as འཆའ་བ ("claiming"), since a false claim to have attained the fruit is the defeat concerning superhuman states. The opening phrase "the Later of the Later" seems to name a section of the Vinaya literature, as in the preceding piece. The exact sense of "brought down in regard to the superhuman qualities" is uncertain; it appears to refer to the pārājika of falsely claiming superhuman attainments. In this translation of the sūtra, "those of great desire" renders icchantika, and it is translated that way here. It is distinct from "craving for greatness," which is the monk's vanity. The final long passage is the false teaching attributed to Māra, not the Buddha's own position. "Grave offense" renders ཉེས་པ་སྦོམ་པོ, the Vinaya category sthūlātyaya, which the following passage goes on to define. The sentence "In the Great Vehicle there is no destruction of self and life force either" is ambiguous in the Tibetan. It could also be read as "there is neither a self nor a destruction of life." The phrase rendered "together with whole companies of followers" (སྡེ་ཚན་བཅས) is uncertain. "Grave offense" renders ཉེས་པ་སྦོམ་པོ (Skt. sthūlātyaya), the class of serious but non-expulsive monastic transgressions. The phrase ཞེ་ནོམ་བྱེད་དུ་འཇུག་པ in both lists of austerities is obscure. It is rendered tentatively as "having others carry out malicious deeds"; the Chinese parallel at this point lists harming living beings. "The arts of concoction" for སྐྲུན་པའི་ལས is likewise an interpretive rendering, perhaps alchemical or occult preparations. The questioner's name is not given in this piece, so he is left unnamed. The term rendered "the heterodox" is literally "those who are strange or remarkable"; in context, and in light of the parallel Chinese version from which this Tibetan text was translated, it evidently refers to non-Buddhist teachers (tīrthikas). The list of the "four inversions" in the path section names only three items, as the Tibetan stands. The man's reply, "as soon as my son is born of you," is translated as the Tibetan stands; it differs from the Chinese parallel, where the man simply offers to show the son the treasure. The term rendered "the embryonic mass" in the list of bodily constituents is a Tibetan word for an early stage of the embryo; its place in a list alongside phlegm and colors is unusual, and the sense here is uncertain. The name of the elixir, "Bee Essence," translates the Tibetan literally; the parallel Chinese version names the medicine "Delightful Taste," so the Tibetan reading may reflect a different or corrupted source. "Gelpa" is a Tibetan name for a kind of tree or shrub that I could not identify with confidence. The phrase rendered "with different dispositions" reads གློ་ཐ་དད་པ, literally "different lungs" or "different sides," which makes little sense here; I have read it as བློ "mind, disposition," a similar-looking syllable. The verse about śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas and "cow's milk" is translated as it stands; its exact point is ambiguous in the Tibetan. The phrase rendered "the three entities that overcome fear" reads literally "the three entities that destroy"; the object is unstated, and "fear" is supplied from the preceding verse about the fearless. The simile of arrows passed "one after another" is obscure in the Tibetan; the rendering follows the literal wording. "Vast deeds" interprets the Tibetan word for "waves," taken in its sense of vast or magnificent activity. The Tibetan སྲིན་བུ་པད་པ normally means "leech." The image of seizing one spot and quickly letting go of the other may instead refer to an inchworm-like creature, as in other versions of this sūtra. "The Sūtra Gathering All Merit" may refer to the Sarvapuṇyasamuccayasamādhi Sūtra; this identification is not certain, so the title is translated. "The Light of Entering Nonduality" seems to name a section of the Perfection of Wisdom literature and is translated literally. The sentence about nirvāṇa having "no basis and no abode" is rendered as the Tibetan stands, though its syntax is compressed. The grass name rendered "Unctuous" translates the Tibetan word meaning "oily, rich"; I could not identify a Sanskrit name with confidence. The Chinese versions describe it with a similar word meaning "fatty, rich." The speaker of the questions to the Buddha is not named in this piece. It is presumably the bodhisattva interlocutor of this section, usually Kāśyapa. The phrase "they see it dimly, as though not seeing it" renders an idiom that literally means "seeing it as not seeing." The recurring term rendered "dimly" (Tibetan 'ol phyir) conveys indistinct, partial seeing; the point of each simile is that even tenth-ground bodhisattvas perceive buddha-nature only indistinctly. The phrase about the prince's night of music is obscure in the Tibetan and has been rendered by sense. The final sentence is phrased as a statement in the Tibetan and may introduce a question taken up in the next piece; the speaker is not named in this passage. "Akṣara," letter, is etymologized as "non-leaking" or "imperishable" (from kṣar, "to flow"), which explains the wordplay on non-leaking and the nine openings. The Tibetan says the succeeding king's son "found that sword," although the parable's sense, and the next sentence, imply that he did not. It is translated as written. "King's son of the far-off land" renders an unclear expression (literally "the king's son of the border"), which apparently refers to the prince who went to another country. "The three bases" renders a term that in this passage refers to the Three Jewels, Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha. "Those without discernment" takes the rare word bgrungs as "shrewd, discerning." The phrase rendered "weeds" literally reads "the face of the field," understood from context and the Chinese parallel as weeds or tares to be cleared. "Rāhula" here is the Buddha's son, the model of a parent's love. The word zalmo is left untranslated. It is not a common Tibetan word. The text itself explains one kind as a bird that keeps company with geese. This matches the parallel Chinese passage, which speaks of two kinds of birds that always go in pairs, but the exact identification is uncertain. The glossary gives རྒྱུད as "tantra", but in this sūtra context, a list with mantras and dharmas, "treatises" seems to be the intended sense. The questioner is not named in this piece; he is the interlocutor of the surrounding dialogue, so he is simply called "he." The phrase rendered "out of loving-kindness" (Tibetan byams pas) could in principle be read as the name Maitreya acting as subject. In context, the Buddha's love for beings as for his son Rāhula, the reading "loving-kindness" is the more natural one. The bird named here, alongside the goose, cannot be identified with confidence. It is rendered generically as "waterfowl." The phrase "like geese moving on" renders an obscure wording. Its sense, birds leaving one place for another, is inferred from the simile that follows. The birds named in the Buddha's reply are uncertain: the first term is taken as the shelduck, and the second, zalmo, could not be identified and is given phonetically. Parallel versions of this sūtra compare the Buddha to a pair of birds that are never separated. The phrase rendered "the various sects of wonder-workers" is literally "all those possessed of marvels"; parallel versions say the Buddha is seen going forth among non-Buddhist ascetics (tīrthikas). "A medicinal tree against poison" renders a compound literally meaning "poison-medicine tree." "The three foundations are ruined!" renders the obscure phrase gzhi gsum ma rung, which seems to be part of what beings think on seeing the Buddha bleed; its exact sense is uncertain (possibly the Three Jewels). In the physician simile, kha ba is taken as "the bitter one" (a medicinal taste) rather than "snow." "The three bases" most likely refers to the Three Jewels (Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha), as the preceding exhortation about the Tathāgata, Dharma and Saṅgha suggests. The Tibetan has "being like the sun is the nature of reality of the pratyekabuddhas," though the simile just given compares them to planets unseen by day; it is translated as written. The questioner is not named in this piece. The phrase rendered "hold generating the mind of awakening to be unnecessary" reads literally "having generated the mind for awakening, hold it unnecessary"; the context, which concerns those who have not generated the mind, favors the rendering given. The names of the legumes and grain in the seed simile are approximate. In the vajra simile the Tibetan states that the vajra does not cut a sheep's horn; this has been kept as written. The list of plants that sprout again after being cut is given largely in transliterated Indian names; several (doṅka, valmīki, karṇavalya, skandastava, vīraṇa, rocana) cannot be identified with certainty, so they are given in approximate romanized form. The phrase rendered "even though they do not hold that they must generate the resolve" is obscure in the Tibetan and may instead mean that they need not deliberately generate the resolve. The glosses beginning "'Well done' means awakening" comment on words not quoted in this piece. The paragraph beginning "One who is held to be an arhat" is ambiguous in the Tibetan as to whether it describes the icchantika or a genuine upholder of the Vaipulya. The following simile of the messenger suggests the latter. The kataka fruit is the clearing-nut used in India to purify muddy water. The phrase rendered "once it has sealed itself in" translates an unclear expression in the silkworm simile, apparently referring to the completed cocoon. The questioner in "He asked" is not named in this piece; in the surrounding dialogue it is the bodhisattva interlocutor. "The nāga called Bird-Choker" renders the Tibetan "bya gag", which I could not identify as a standard rendering of a Sanskrit name; the translation is literal. In this passage "the Later Section" refers to the concluding portion of a medical treatise that a skilled physician masters (compare the Uttaratantra of Āyurvedic works). "The old trunk of a poisonous plant" renders a terse Tibetan phrase whose exact sense is uncertain; it appears to describe the discarded body of skillful means as something worthless, like a poisonous stalk. The interlocutor addressed as "son of noble family" is not named in this piece. "Saindhava" renders the Tibetan transcription sen da pa, the Sanskrit word for things "from the Sindhu region" (rock salt, horses, and so on), famous from this simile in the Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra. The trees palāśa, aśoka and karṇikāra are identified from the Tibetan renderings and the parallel in the Chinese version; the Tibetan for the second tree is unusual. The terms rendered "multitudes upon multitudes" are two names for very large numbers whose exact values are uncertain. The item rendered "unfavorable times" is obscure in the Tibetan; it might instead refer to faulty writing materials. The tree called dongka could not be identified with confidence and is kept in its Tibetan form. The interlocutor who answers the Buddha is not named in this piece. The questioner is not named in this piece; in this section of the sūtra it is Kāśyapa. The phrase "they are, in my sense, supremely permanent" renders the elliptical ངའི་མཆོག་ཏུ་རྟག་པ, which could also be read "they are permanent through my supreme [nirvāṇa]." The wordplay on གནས་མེད ("no place" for others, and the Great Parinirvāṇa being "without place," that is, non-abiding) has been kept as literal as English allows. The name rendered "Utterly Joyful" (Tibetan rab tu dga' ba) is translated by meaning, since a Sanskrit form for this future tathāgata cannot be identified with confidence. "The three foundations" (gzhi gsum) here most likely refers to the Three Jewels, whose permanence this sūtra stresses. "Uphold the sacred Dharma" renders a verb that literally means "bring forth" or "draw out"; the sense of protecting or upholding the Dharma is taken from context. In the opening answer, the Tibetan grammar has the element mixed with afflictions as the subject that pervades the tathāgata element; this is translated as written. "The three permanent bases" most likely refers to the Three Jewels (Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha), whose permanence this sūtra teaches; it is rendered literally here. "Nalita" is kept as the name of a measure of grain; its exact Indic equivalent is uncertain. The phrase rendered "no larger than the tip of a splinter" is uncertain in its precise sense. "Three Jewels" renders the Tibetan "three bases" (gzhi gsum), taken here as the Buddha, Dharma and Saṅgha. "Meaning with remainder" and "meaning without remainder" render the Tibetan literally; in context they mark a statement that admits exceptions as against one that is complete without exception. "Passing dust" reads ལྷོ་བུར as the evident slip for གློ་བུར ("adventitious, sudden"). "Place to cling to" takes འཁྲ་ས as a variant of འཁྲི་ས, "support". "Just as one clears a field" is a reading of the obscure ཞིང་གི་ངོ་བཞིན་དུ, which probably refers to weeding a field. "The three bases" are presumably the Three Jewels. "Ninety-six heterodox schools" renders a Tibetan phrase literally meaning "the ninety-six possessing wonders"; it is taken as referring to the traditional ninety-six non-Buddhist (tīrthika) schools. The clause about the patient and the ghee uses a verb meaning "grant," which is somewhat awkward; it is read as the patient asking for a large amount. "Carries an unstated qualification" renders ལྷག་མ་ཡོད་པའི་དོན, a statement that is not exhaustive and requires qualification (sāvaśeṣa). In the final section ལྷག་པ is read as ལྷག་མ, matching the parallel phrases. The line ང་རྒྱལ་དབང་གྱུར་ཀུན་མི་ཟད is rendered literally as "endless"; its exact sense is obscure, and Mañjuśrī's discussion treats it as a phrase to be qualified rather than explaining it. Making defeated monks "wear white clothes" means returning them to lay status. The brahmin's name, ལུག་དཔལ, is translated literally as "Sheep Glory," since no secure Sanskrit form could be identified. This text was translated into Tibetan from Chinese.

Taking གྱེན་དུ་རྒྱལ ("victorious upward") as a calque of Ujjayinī is a reasoned identification, not a certain one. The same caution applies to the householder's name Kotaka.

The verse line about those "free of avarice" who "waver," and its explanation (holy beings "sparing toward themselves"), are obscure in the Tibetan and are rendered literally.

The large number in the closing sentence (ཕྲག་དཀྲིགས) is rendered loosely as "countless myriads." The figure "twenty-five thousand chapters" is translated as the Tibetan gives it. It reflects the traditional claim that the full Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra was vastly longer than the portion actually translated. Devacandra (Lha Dawa) was a Tibetan translator who is known by this Sanskrit form of his name, so it is kept here.
